8Block integration and polymer expansions

8.1Connected clusters with repeated labels

The abstract polymer expansion and its convergence criterion are developed in Kotecký and Preiss [KP86, the theorem on pp. 492–493]. The argument here also keeps a geometric bound at each occurrence number in the weighted expansion, for the later differentiated estimates.

Subsection 7.5 counted connected sets of distinct activity labels. A logarithmic expansion is different: its vertices are numbered occurrences, and the same activity label may occur several times. We now prove the bound for this expansion, including its factorials and the identity with the real logarithm of the gas. No positivity of the gas is assumed in obtaining the identity.

Let \(\Lambda\) be a finite periodic lattice. Each label \(i\) has a nonempty support \(X_i\), and write \(c (X) = C_B (X)\) and \(h_B = (2 B + 1)^4\). We use \(|X| \leqslant h_B c (X)\) and covering subadditivity for intersecting supports. The incompatibility relation \(R\) is symmetric and reflexive, and

\(\displaystyle R (i, j) \quad \Longrightarrow \quad X_i \cap X_j \ne \varnothing .\)

The converse is not required. Let \(w_i \geqslant 0\), \(a \geqslant 0\), \(\gamma > 0\), \(\delta > 0\), and suppose

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x \in \Lambda} \sum_{i : x \in X_i} w_i e^{(a + \delta) c (X_i)} \leqslant \gamma, \qquad q_0 = \frac{4 h_B \gamma}{\delta} \leqslant q < 1.\) (8.1)

In particular \(q > 0\).

For an ordered tuple \(z = (i_0, \ldots, i_m)\), let \(T (z)\) be the graph on \(\{0, \ldots, m\}\) with an edge \(\{r, s\}\) when \(r \ne s\) and \(R (i_r, i_s)\). Define its connected coefficient and majorant by

\(\displaystyle u (z) = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} G \subseteq T (z)\\ G \text{connected} \end{array}$}}} (- 1)^{|E (G) |}, \qquad W (z) = \frac{|u (z) |}{(m + 1) !} \prod_{r = 0}^m w_{i_r} .\)

For \(m = 0\) the graph with no edges is connected and \(u (z) = 1\). Repeated labels are allowed in every tuple sum below.

8.1.1Cancellation before taking absolute values

For any graph \(T\) on \(\{0, \ldots, m\}\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} G \subseteq T\\ G \text{connected} \end{array}$}}} (- 1)^{|E (G) |} \right| \leqslant \# \{ \text{spanning trees of } T\} .\) (8.2)

This is the Penrose tree-graph bound [FP07, Proposition 5, Eq. (4.3)]. Apply the identity to the finite simple graph on the occurrence indices. The alternating sum has absolute value equal to the number of Penrose trees, a subset of its spanning trees. For a disconnected graph both counts vanish. Repeated activity labels are permitted because different occurrences are different vertices, joined when the labels are incompatible.

8.1.2The weighted forest estimate

A parent map \(p : \{1, \ldots, m\} \to \{0, \ldots, m\}\) is called rooted if iterating it always reaches the external root zero. Place a fixed support \(Y\) at zero and a support \(X_{i_v}\) at vertex \(v \ne 0\). Only assignments in which each child support meets its parent support are retained. We claim

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{m!} \sum_{p \text{rooted}} \sum_{i_1, \ldots, i_m} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{ \text{all parent edges overlap} \}} \prod_{v = 1}^m w_{i_v} e^{ac (X_{i_v})} \leqslant e^{\delta c (Y)} q^m .\) (8.3)

The labels in this sum need not be distinct.

Put \(t = h_B \gamma / \delta > 0\), \(F (Y) = \max (\delta c (Y), 1)\), and introduce the nonnegative kernel

\(\displaystyle K (Y, i) = \frac{\boldsymbol{1}_{\{X_i \cap Y \ne \varnothing\}} w_i e^{(a + \delta) c (X_i)}}{tF (Y)} .\)

The site bound in (8.1) implies

\(\displaystyle \sum_i \boldsymbol{1}_{\{X_i \cap Y \ne \varnothing\}} w_i e^{(a + \delta) c (X_i)} \leqslant |Y| \gamma \leqslant h_B \gamma c (Y) \leqslant tF (Y) .\)

Thus every row of \(K\) sums to at most one. For a fixed rooted parent map, the sum over all label assignments of the product of its edge kernels is at most one: sum the label at a leaf first, apply its row bound, and repeat until no vertex remains. For infinite alphabets one first restricts to a finite alphabet; the row bounds still hold, and nonnegative convergence removes the restriction.

Let \(d_v\) be the number of children of vertex \(v\), including the external root. The exponential series gives

\(\displaystyle F (Z)^k \leqslant k!e^{\delta c (Z)} \quad (k \geqslant 0) .\)

Indeed if \(\delta c (Z) \geqslant 1\) this is one term in the exponential series; otherwise use \(F (Z) = 1\) and \(k!e^{\delta c (Z)} \geqslant 1\). Replacing each overlap weight with its kernel expression and collecting the factors at parents gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & \prod_{v = 1}^m \boldsymbol{1}_{\{X_{i_v} \text{ meets parent} \}} w_{i_v} e^{ac (X_{i_v})}\\ & \qquad \leqslant t^m e^{\delta c (Y)} \left( \prod_{v = 0}^m d_v ! \right) \prod_{v = 1}^m K (X_{i_{p (v)}}, i_v), \end{array}\)

where \(X_{i_0} = Y\). To check the reserve cancellation, before the inequality there is a factor \(e^{- \delta c (X_{i_v})}\) at every nonroot vertex. Applying the bound on \(F^{d_v}\) supplies exactly one inverse factor there, leaving only \(e^{\delta c (Y)}\) at the external root.

It remains to sum the child factorials. Summing over all parent maps, including those which are not rooted, can only increase the answer. For fixed child counts \(d_0 + \cdots + d_m = m\) there are \(m! / \prod_v d_v !\) maps. Therefore

\(\displaystyle \sum_{p : \{1, \ldots, m\} \to \{0, \ldots, m\}} \prod_{v = 0}^m d_v ! = m! \binom{2 m}{m} \leqslant m! 4^m .\)

The binomial coefficient counts weak compositions of \(m\) into \(m + 1\) parts. The inequality follows by comparing it with the sum of all binomial coefficients of order \(2 m\). After division by \(m\)!, the kernel bound and this count prove (8.3), since \((4 t)^m = q_0^m \leqslant q^m\). This also proves the case \(m = 0\), whose left side is one.

A distinguished root may carry a different nonnegative weight \(v_i\). If \(\sum_i v_i e^{(a + \delta) c (X_i)} \leqslant b\), multiply (8.3) with \(Y = X_{i_0}\) by \(v_{i_0} e^{ac (X_{i_0})}\) and sum \(i_0\). The result is the rooted bound \(bq^m\), with denominator \(m\)! for the other vertices.

8.1.3A root anywhere in the tuple

Let \(Q\) be any property of an activity label and assume

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i : Q (i)} w_i e^{(a + \delta) c (X_i)} \leqslant b.\)

Apply (8.2) to each occurrence graph and the rooted forest bound with \(v_i =\boldsymbol{1}_{Q (i)} w_i\) at a specified vertex. This gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_z \frac{|u (z) |}{m!} \hspace{0.17em} \boldsymbol{1}_{Q (i_r)} \prod_{s = 0}^m w_{i_s} e^{ac (X_{i_s})} \leqslant bq^m\)

for every root position \(r\). Relabeling occurrence positions preserves both the connected coefficient and the tuple sum, so the same bound holds for all \(r\). If a tuple contains at least one label satisfying \(Q\), its cluster majorant, with denominator \((m + 1)\)!, is bounded by \(1 / (m + 1)\) times the sum of the displayed rooted integrands over all \(r\). Summing yields

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z : \hspace{0.17em} \exists r \hspace{0.17em} Q (i_r)} \frac{|u (z) |}{(m + 1) !} \prod_{r = 0}^m w_{i_r} e^{ac (X_{i_r})} \leqslant bq^m .\) (8.4)

This is the normalization that pays for choosing a root; no additional factor \(m + 1\) remains.

If \(u (z) \ne 0\), its occurrence graph contains a connected spanning subgraph, hence a spanning tree. Incompatibility edges imply support overlap. Adding supports along this tree gives

\(\displaystyle c \left( \bigcup_{r = 0}^m X_{i_r} \right) \leqslant \sum_{r = 0}^m c (X_{i_r}) .\)

As \(a \geqslant 0\), the exponential weight of the actual union is therefore bounded by the product weight in (8.4). Take \(Q (i)\) to mean \(x \in X_i\) and \(b = \gamma\). The union contains \(x\) exactly when some label has this property. Finally fix an integer \(m_0 \geqslant 0\) and sum over \(m \geqslant m_0\) to obtain

\(\displaystyle \sum_{m \geqslant m_0} \sum_{i_0, \ldots, i_m} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{x \in \bigcup_r X_{i_r} \}} W (i_0, \ldots, i_m) e^{ac (\bigcup_r X_{i_r})} \leqslant \frac{\gamma q^{m_0}}{1 - q} .\) (8.5)

Thus the connected estimate (9.8) follows from a vertex site bound and the explicit smallness inequality in (8.1): use the exponent \(a + e\) there in place of \(a\), the vertex bound written \(t\) in (9.8) in place of \(\gamma\), the tail parameter \(\gamma\) of that section in place of \(q\), and its order threshold \(z\) in place of \(m_0\). (The auxiliary quantity \(t = h_B \gamma / \delta\) of the forest estimate above is unrelated.) The reserve \(\delta\) pays for tree branching and is distinct from the reserve \(e\) used later for spatial derivative weights.

8.1.4The exact logarithm identity

Now let \(v_i \in \mathbb{R}\) be possibly signed activities and apply the preceding estimates to \(w_i = |v_i |\). Define the gas and connected sum

\(\displaystyle Z = \sum_{A \text{finite,compatible}} \prod_{i \in A} v_i, \qquad \mathcal{L}= \sum_{m \geqslant 0} \frac{1}{(m + 1) !} \sum_{i_0, \ldots, i_m} u (i_0, \ldots, i_m) \prod_{r = 0}^m v_{i_r} .\)

A finite set is compatible if it contains no incompatible distinct labels. The empty set contributes one. We will prove

\(\displaystyle Z = e^{\mathcal{L}} > 0, \qquad \log Z =\mathcal{L}.\) (8.6)

First both series converge absolutely. Nonempty supports and (8.1) imply \(\sum_i |v_i | \leqslant | \Lambda | \gamma\), so the gas is dominated by \(\prod_i (1 + |v_i |) \leqslant e^{| \Lambda | \gamma}\). For the logarithm, use (8.4) with \(Q\) always true and \(b = | \Lambda | \gamma\), and drop the support weights. Its absolute sum at order \(m\) is at most \(| \Lambda | \gamma q^m\); hence the total is at most \(| \Lambda | \gamma / (1 - q)\).

Begin with a finite alphabet \(D\). Let \(P (t)\) be its gas polynomial, with each activity replaced by \(tv_i\), and let

\(\displaystyle L (t) = \sum_{m \geqslant 0} \ell_m t^{m + 1}, \qquad \ell_m = \frac{1}{(m + 1) !} \sum_{i_0, \ldots, i_m \in D} u (i_0, \ldots, i_m) \prod_r v_{i_r} .\)

The bound \(| \ell_m | \leqslant | \Lambda | \gamma q^m\) shows that \(L\) is differentiable for \(|t| < q^{- 1}\) by termwise differentiation. Indeed on \(|t| \leqslant u < q^{- 1}\) the derivative terms are bounded by the summable series \(| \Lambda | \gamma (m + 1) (qu)^m\). This interval contains \([0, 1]\).

We verify the coefficient identity \(P' = L' P\), rather than assuming a formal logarithm can be evaluated. On a tuple of \(n\) occurrences, the sum of \((- 1)^{|E (G) |}\) over all subgraphs of its incompatibility graph is \((1 - 1)^{|E (T) |}\). It is one if the graph has no edge and zero otherwise. Because \(R\) is reflexive, a tuple with repeated labels has an edge between equal occurrences and contributes zero. Thus summing this all-graph weight over tuples, multiplying by their activities, and dividing by \(n\)! gives exactly the coefficient \(p_n\) of \(P (t)\): each compatible \(n\)-element set has \(n\)! enumerations.

For \(n = m + 1\), distinguish occurrence zero in an arbitrary graph. Its connected component consists of zero and some \(k\) of the other \(m\) vertices. Choose those vertices in \(\binom{m}{k}\) ways. A graph is then uniquely specified by a connected graph on this component and an arbitrary graph on its complement, with no cross edges. Edge signs and label products factor. Summing the choices and dividing by \(m\)! gives the exact recurrence

\(\displaystyle (m + 1) p_{m + 1} = \sum_{k = 0}^m (k + 1) \ell_k p_{m - k} .\)

Absolute convergence of \(L'\) and the finiteness of \(P\) allow its coefficients to be multiplied and summed, proving \(P' = L' P\) on the interval. Hence

\(\displaystyle \frac{d}{dt} \{P (t) e^{- L (t)} \} = 0.\)

At zero, \(P (0) = 1\) and \(L (0) = 0\). The mean value theorem implies \(P (t) e^{- L (t)} = 1\) throughout the interval, and evaluation at \(t = 1\) proves (8.6) for the finite alphabet.

Finally let finite alphabets increase to all labels. Every gas term and every occurrence tuple is eventually retained. The absolute majorants already proved are independent of this restriction. Dominated convergence therefore gives \(P_D (1) \to Z\) and \(L_D (1) \to \mathcal{L}\). Continuity of the exponential yields \(Z = e^{\mathcal{L}}\), and its strict positivity gives the claimed real logarithm. For an arbitrary index set the same argument uses the directed family of all finite subsets; alternatively absolute summability leaves only countably many nonzero terms. This completes the proof, with no assumption of positivity or a choice of a complex logarithm branch.

Applied to the uniformly dominated tilted activities of Subsection 9.1, this result constructs their positive normalization and logarithmic cluster series once the vertex site bound (8.1) is established. The joint bounds proved there then justify differentiating it. Constructing the actual block-integral activities and proving their vertex bounds remain separate analytic tasks.

8.2Bounds for packets and marked connected components

The vertex bound used in Subsection 8.1 combines two populations: mandatory defect packets and optional local activities. We prove the counting and reserve estimates for this combination, then show how a single insertion is added to its actual alphabet. The analytic bound on the weight of one packet remains a separate input.

8.2.1Counting physical defect packets

Fix a block ratio \(L \geqslant 3\) and nonnegative integers \(\ell, B\). A seed is a fine plaquette, a coarse oriented bond, or a coarse site. These are distinct types even when they have the same location. Each seed has a coarse anchor and a finite set of points within max-distance one of its anchor. For a plaquette these points are the coarse parents of its touched fine sites; for a bond they are its two endpoints; for a site it is the site itself. For the plaquette, the parent-distance estimate is \(L \hspace{0.17em} \text{dist} (\text{parent} (x), \text{parent} (y)) \leqslant L + 2\). Since the distance is integral and \(L \geqslant 3\), it is at most one. The halo of radius \(\ell B\) around these points is its support, contained in the ball of radius \(\ell B + 1\) about the anchor. A packet is a nonempty finite set of seeds whose halo intersection graph is connected. Its support is the union of these halos, and its size \(n\) is the number of typed seeds.

The number of seeds with anchors in a set \(Y\) is at most \(T_L |Y|\), where

\(\displaystyle T_L = L^4 \# \{ \text{plaquette axes} \} + 4 + 1.\)

The fine site is determined by its coarse parent and one of \(L^4\) residues, which proves the plaquette count; the two other counts are immediate. Consequently at most

\(\displaystyle H_B = (2 (\ell B + 1) + 1)^4 T_L\)

seed halos can contain a given site. If two halos overlap, their anchors have distance at most \(2 (\ell B + 1)\), so each seed has at most

\(\displaystyle D_B = (4 (\ell B + 1) + 1)^4 T_L \geqslant 1\)

possible neighboring seeds. The root count \(H_B\), the adjacency count \(D_B\) and the per-seed cover cost \(c_{\ell}\) defined below are the quantities used under the same names in Subsection 8.25. These bounds remain valid when balls wrap around the periodic lattice, since wrapping only decreases the number of distinct sites.

There are at most \(H_B D_B^{2 n}\) packets of size \(n\) containing a fixed site in their support. To prove this, choose a seed whose halo contains that site; there are at most \(H_B\) choices. A connected \(n\)-seed packet has a spanning tree. Traversing every tree edge out and back gives a walk of length \(2 (n - 1)\), starting at the chosen seed, whose visited set is exactly the packet. There are at most \(D_B^{2 (n - 1)}\) such walks. Different visited sets cannot come from the same walk, so this counts packets without an injectivity assumption on their placements. Enlarging the bound to \(H_B D_B^{2 n}\) also covers \(n = 1\) uniformly. There is no packet with \(n = 0\).

A single halo has \(B\)-cover number at most \(c_{\ell} = (2 \ell + 3)^4\). For \(B \geqslant 1\) it lies in the ball of radius \((\ell + 1) B\), tiled by \(B\)-balls with centers on the \(B\)-spaced grid; there are at most \((2 \ell + 3)^4\) centers, forming a connected grid. For \(B = 0\) use at most \(3^4 \leqslant c_{\ell}\) singleton centers. Covering subadditivity along a spanning tree therefore bounds a packet's cover number by \(nc_{\ell}\).

Suppose its nonnegative weight obeys the analytic estimate

\(\displaystyle p_A \leqslant C \exp ((s - g) n (A)), \qquad C \geqslant 0,\) (8.7)

where \(s\) is the expense per seed and \(g\) the gain per seed. For a support exponent \(h \geqslant 0\), put

\(\displaystyle q_p = D_B^2 \exp (s - g + hc_{\ell}) .\)

If \(q_p \leqslant r_p < 1\), multiplication by the support weight, the packet count, and a geometric sum give

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} A : \hspace{0.17em} x \in X_A\\ n (A) \geqslant n_0 \end{array}$}}} p_A e^{hc (X_A)} \leqslant H_B C \frac{r_p^{\max (1, n_0)}}{1 - r_p} .\) (8.8)

Indeed the summand at fixed size \(n\) is at most \(H_B Cq_p^n\). The lower limit is \(\max (1, n_0)\) because packets are nonempty. This explains the single-seed extraction in the packet bound. If a packet has several analytic variants, the hypothesis (8.7) must bound their sum at that packet. It cannot be applied separately to every variant for free. A variant with smaller support has no larger support bound and is handled by the same fiberwise summation.

8.2.2Reserving the cost of a whole component

Let \(f_i \geqslant 0\) be weights on a combined alphabet, with supports \(X_i\), extra nonnegative costs \(d_i\), and nonnegative marks \(e_i\). Define

\(\displaystyle \tilde{f}_i = f_i \exp (2 \varepsilon d_i + e_i), \qquad D (A) = \sum_{i \in A} (c (X_i) + d_i), \qquad E (A) = \sum_{i \in A} e_i,\)

where \(\varepsilon > 0\). Assume the vertex condition (8.1) holds for \(\tilde{f}\) with support exponent \(a + 2 \varepsilon\), site bound \(\gamma\), tree reserve \(\delta\) and tail ratio \(q\). We will use the same tree bound for distinct connected sets.

For each connected finite set \(A\) containing a root label \(i\), choose a spanning tree rooted at \(i\). If \(|A| - 1 = m\), the \(m\)! enumerations of its other labels give \(m\)! different numbered trees. Their weights in (8.3) each contain \(1 / m\)!. Thus the sum over distinct sets and their chosen trees is bounded by the full numbered tree sum. Choose a root whose support contains \(x\), or bound by the sum over all such roots. The rooted bound established in Subsection 8.1, followed by the geometric sum in \(m\), proves

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} A \text{connected}, x \in \cup_{i \in A} X_i\\ |A| - 1 \geqslant m \end{array}$}}} \prod_{i \in A} \tilde{f}_i e^{(a + 2 \varepsilon) c (X_i)} \leqslant \frac{\gamma q^m}{1 - q} .\) (8.9)

No logarithmic coefficient is present in this distinct-set estimate.

Let \(k, m \geqslant 0\) be integers and restrict the sets further by \(D (A) \geqslant T\) and \(E (A) \geqslant U\), where \(T, U\) are real. The elementary polynomial estimate is

\(\displaystyle (1 + D)^k \leqslant P_{\varepsilon, k} e^{\varepsilon D}, \qquad P_{\varepsilon, k} = \varepsilon^{- k} k!e^{\varepsilon} \quad (D \geqslant 0) .\)

Since \(D \geqslant T\) and \(E \geqslant U\),

\(\displaystyle e^{\varepsilon D} \leqslant e^{- \varepsilon T - U} e^{2 \varepsilon D + E} .\)

For connected \(A\) we also have \(c (\cup_i X_i) \leqslant \sum_i c (X_i)\). Multiplying these inequalities, and then using (8.9), proves

\(\displaystyle \sum_A \left( \prod_{i \in A} f_i \right) (1 + D (A))^k e^{ac (\cup_{i \in A} X_i)} \leqslant P_{\varepsilon, k} e^{- \varepsilon T - U} \frac{\gamma q^m}{1 - q} .\) (8.10)

The sum on the left has all the preceding connectedness, site, cardinality, cost and mark restrictions. The two reserves have separate uses: one pays the polynomial, and the other leaves the explicit tail \(e^{- \varepsilon T}\).

8.2.3Combining the two vertex populations

For an optional label take its original weight \(w_i\), extra cost \(d_i \geqslant 0\) and no mark. Assume its site estimate with the full head exponent \(h = a + 2 \varepsilon + \delta\) is

\(\displaystyle \sup_x \sum_{i : x \in X_i} w_i e^{2 \varepsilon d_i} e^{hc (X_i)} \leqslant \Gamma_o .\)

For a packet of size \(n\), set extra cost \(bn\) and mark \(\mu n\), where \(b, \mu \geqslant 0\). Its modified weight is \(p_A e^{(2 \varepsilon b + \mu) n}\). Thus (8.8) applies with seed expense replaced by \(s + 2 \varepsilon b + \mu\), leaving the analytic packet bound (8.7) unchanged. If the resulting packet site bound is \(\Gamma_p\), the combined alphabet has site bound at most \(\Gamma_p + \Gamma_o\). Choose \(\gamma > 0\) with

\(\displaystyle \Gamma_p + \Gamma_o \leqslant \gamma, \qquad 4 h_B \gamma / \delta \leqslant q < 1.\)

This is exactly the vertex condition needed for (8.10).

If the allowed component contains at least \(r\) seeds in total, then \(E (A) \geqslant \mu r\). Hence the right-hand side becomes

\(\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon, k} e^{- \varepsilon T - \mu r} \frac{\gamma q^m}{1 - q} .\) (8.11)

This bound controls simultaneously excess label count \(m\), polynomial order \(k\), minimum seed count \(r\), and minimum total cost \(T\). In particular, \(r = 1\) may be used only for components known to contain a mandatory packet. The support of an output may be smaller than the component support. Summing all output majorants over each component fiber first, a bound by \(C_0\) times its component bound gives \(C_0\) times (8.11), by support monotonicity and nonnegative summation. This is the required rule for summing analytic variants without losing their multiplicities.

8.2.4Adding a normalized elementary insertion

The insertion changes one optional label already present in the alphabet. Its fine support has cover number \(c_f\), support exponent \(\nu_f\), and original scalar majorant \(w \geqslant 0\). Let \(g > 0\) denote the normalization gain and set

\(\displaystyle j = ge^{- (\nu_f - \varepsilon) c_f}, \qquad D = \frac{we^{\nu_f c_f} e^{- \varepsilon c_f}}{g} .\)

Then \(Dj = w\) exactly. Thus \(D\) is the insertion debt and \(j\) the normalized vertex weight, not two independent bounds.

Let \(X_{\ast}\) be this label's actual coarse footprint, and suppose its projection geometry gives

\(\displaystyle hc (X_{\ast}) \leqslant hP + \varepsilon c_f, \qquad \nu_f \geqslant 4 \varepsilon,\) (8.12)

where \(h = a + 2 \varepsilon + \delta\) is the head exponent and \(P\) the fixed halo constant. Its extra cost is \(c_f\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle je^{2 \varepsilon c_f} e^{hc (X_{\ast})} \leqslant g \exp \{hP + (4 \varepsilon - \nu_f) c_f \} \leqslant ge^{hP} = : R_{\ast} .\)

At a fixed site there is just this one modified label. Replacing its old nonnegative weight by \(j\) thus increases the optional site bound by at most \(R_{\ast}\). This follows even if \(j\) is smaller than the old weight, by bounding the new array by the old array plus the single new entry. Set the new optional bound and total vertex bound to

\(\displaystyle \Gamma_o' = \Gamma_o + R_{\ast}, \qquad \gamma' = \gamma + R_{\ast} .\)

The packet estimates are unchanged. Once \(4 h_B \gamma' / \delta \leqslant q' < 1\), the preceding mixed-component bound applies to the modified alphabet with these bounds. Retaining only components containing the marked label decreases each nonnegative sum. This produces the marked-molecule estimate used for the order-zero tail in Subsection 9.1.

For the actual projected footprint the sufficient geometric condition is \(h (10 P / L) \leqslant \varepsilon\), together with the comparison of fine and coarse cover radii: the coarse cover radius \(B_c\) is positive and the fine cover radius satisfies \(B_f \leqslant 2 B_c\). Under these two conditions the cover estimate for the enlarged inspected footprint \(X_{\ast}\), the projection of the fine support enlarged by the fixed halo, is \(c (X_{\ast}) \leqslant P + (10 P / L) c_f\), which gives (8.12). The fixed block ratio is chosen large enough for this condition before the coupling is increased. If \(g\) and \(\gamma\) have negative upper exponent \(q_{\ast} < 0\) in the fine coupling, then \(R_{\ast} = ge^{hP}\) and \(\gamma'\) have the same upper exponent, with \(L\) and the halo constants fixed. The factor \(h_B\) has upper exponent zero. The degree bound therefore has upper exponent \(q_{\ast}\), and is eventually bounded by the strictly larger power \(\textsf{h} (x)^{q_{\ast} / 2}\) of the coupling scale \(\textsf{h} (x) \to \infty\) used to define upper exponents in Subsection 8.25 (written \(h (x)\) there; here \(h\) is the head exponent). This power tends to zero. This proves affordability of the augmented tree bound under precisely these rate and geometric inputs.

The combinatorial part of the mixed and marked estimates is now proved. The remaining analytic input is the packet weight bound (8.7), including all its variants, together with the optional activity site bound and the quoted projection and rate estimates on the actual selected block step.

8.3Simultaneous Gaussian warnings and integrated activities

The expansion contains factors which are small only after integration. A warning factor, for example, need not be small on a configuration with a large fluctuation. What is small is the probability that all warnings in a given term are present. Their fluctuations are correlated, and several warnings may be witnessed by the same Gaussian coordinate. We first account for both effects explicitly.

8.3.1A bound for correlated coordinates

Let \(I\) be a finite coordinate set, and let \(Z\) be a centred real Gaussian vector with covariance matrix \(A\). Suppose

\(\displaystyle A \geqslant 0, \qquad v^{\textsf{T}} Av \leqslant M \sum_{i \in I} v_i^2 \quad (v \in \mathbb{R}^I), \qquad M \geqslant 0.\) (8.13)

The covariance may be singular. For \(J \subseteq I\), \(t \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(s \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} \{|Z_i | \geqslant t \text{ for every } i \in J\} \leqslant 2^{|J|} \exp \{(- st + Ms^2 / 2) |J|\} .\) (8.14)

Indeed, for each choice of signs \(\epsilon_i \in \{- 1, 1\}\) on \(J\), put \(v_i = \epsilon_i\) on \(J\) and \(v_i = 0\) elsewhere. On the corresponding orthant event, \(v \cdot Z \geqslant t |J|\). The Gaussian exponential identity gives

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}e^{sv \cdot Z} = e^{s^2 v^{\textsf{T}} Av / 2} \leqslant e^{Ms^2 |J| / 2} .\)

For completeness, write \(Z = A^{1 / 2} G\) with \(G\) a vector of independent standard real Gaussians. In each coordinate, completing the square in \((2 \pi)^{- 1 / 2} \int e^{hu - u^2 / 2} \hspace{0.17em} du\) gives \(e^{h^2 / 2}\); multiplication proves the identity, including zero eigenvalues of \(A\). Markov's inequality applied to \(e^{sv \cdot Z}\) bounds one orthant by \(e^{(- st + Ms^2 / 2) |J|}\), and the union of the \(2^{|J|}\) orthants contains the event in (8.14). This also proves the formula when \(J\) is empty.

Let \(\Lambda\) be the coarse periodic lattice, with its lattice distance. Write

\(\displaystyle b_D = (2 D + 1)^4 .\)

Every ball of radius \(D\) has at most \(b_D\) sites, including on small periods where some representatives coincide. Assign each Gaussian coordinate a site \(g (i) \in \Lambda\), with at most \(p\) coordinates at any site. Warning addresses form a finite set \(K\), with site map \(o : K \to \Lambda\) and at most \(m\) addresses at any site. For a set \(X \subseteq K\), define

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C} (X ; t, D) = \{z : \forall a \in X \exists i \in I, d (o (a), g (i)) \leqslant D, \quad |z_i | \geqslant t\} .\)

These are measurable sets, being finite intersections of finite unions of closed coordinate inequalities.

Proposition 8.1. If \(H \geqslant 0\) and

\(\displaystyle M / 2 + Hb_D m \leqslant t,\) (8.15)

then

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} \{Z \in \mathcal{C}(X ; t, D)\} \leqslant (2 b_D p \hspace{0.17em} e^{- H})^{|X|} .\) (8.16)

Proof. For each warning in \(X\), choose a witnessing coordinate within distance \(D\). There are at most \((b_D p)^{|X|}\) such assignments. For one assignment, let \(J\) be the set of distinct coordinates chosen. Then \(|J| \leqslant |X|\). Conversely, any fixed chosen coordinate can witness at most \(b_D m\) addresses, since all such addresses lie within distance \(D\) of its site. Thus \(|X| \leqslant b_D m |J|\). Applying (8.14) with \(s = 1\), the event belonging to this assignment has probability at most

\(\displaystyle 2^{|J|} e^{(- t + M / 2) |J|} \leqslant 2^{|X|} e^{- Hb_D m |J|} \leqslant 2^{|X|} e^{- H |X|} .\)

The union bound over assignments proves the assertion. If a warning has no possible coordinate, its event is empty; the empty set of warnings has probability one. Thus no nonemptiness exception is needed.\(\Box\)

8.3.2The scales used for warnings

Fix the block length \(L\). Let \(h = h (x) = \hat{\beta}_f\) be the fine reference coupling, so that \(h = x + c_L\) for a constant \(c_L\) depending on the fixed group and block length. For sufficiently large \(x\), put

\(\displaystyle B = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil, \qquad P = \exp \{(\log \log h)^2 \}, \qquad d = 1 + B + (\log h)^2, \qquad \tau = P / \sqrt{h} .\) (8.17)

The actual fine coupling satisfies \(h / 2 \leqslant \beta \leqslant 2 h\). The fluctuation is written \(\mu + \beta^{- 1 / 2} \text{erect} (Z)\); \(\mu\) is its mean and \(\text{erect}\) identifies colour coordinates with bond variables. For a fixed \(\xi > 0\), the coordinate warning threshold is

\(\displaystyle t = \xi \tau \sqrt{\beta} \geqslant \xi P / 2.\)

The weaker constant \(1 / 2\) is convenient and follows from \(\sqrt{\beta} \geqslant \sqrt{h} / 2\).

For the actual warning alphabet, its density per coarse site is bounded by the fixed integer

\(\displaystyle m = L^4 \# \{ \text{plaquette axes} \} + 2\# \{ \text{axes} \} + 1 + L^4 + L^4 \# \{ \text{axes} \} .\)

The five first types are fine plaquette masks, coarse bond masks, coarse bond likelihoods, coarse site likelihoods, and fine site contractions; the last type is a fine bond cutoff. The coordinate density \(p\) is the fixed bound for the tubular colour coordinates. Both bounds count distinct addresses, including different types at the same site.

Suppose that the physical covariance satisfies (8.13) with a constant \(M\) independent of the period and of \(x\). Set \(D = 2 B\), \(\varepsilon = \xi / 4\), and

\(\displaystyle E (x) = 2 b_{2 B} p \exp \{- \varepsilon \sqrt{P} \} .\) (8.18)

Then, for all sufficiently large \(x\), uniformly in the period and in \(X\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} \{Z \in \mathcal{C}(X ; t, 2 B)\} \leqslant E (x)^{|X|} .\) (8.19)

Here is the scale comparison needed to justify this assertion. First \(d \leqslant 7 (\log h)^2\) eventually, since \(h = x + c_L\), \(\log x \leqslant 2 \log h\) eventually, and \(B < (\log x)^2 + 1\). For every fixed \(a > 0\), integer \(r \geqslant 0\) and constant \(C \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle Cd^r \leqslant P^a \quad \text{eventually} .\) (8.20)

If \(C = 0\) there is nothing to prove. For \(C > 0\), taking logarithms, it suffices to compare a constant plus \(2 r \log \log h\) with \(a (\log \log h)^2\); the latter dominates. Since \(b_{2 B} m\) is bounded by a fixed multiple of \(d^4\), (8.20) implies \(b_{2 B} m \leqslant \sqrt{P}\) and \(M / 2 \leqslant \varepsilon P\) eventually. Consequently

\(\displaystyle M / 2 + \varepsilon \sqrt{P} \hspace{0.17em} b_{2 B} m \leqslant 2 \varepsilon P = \xi P / 2 \leqslant t.\)

Proposition 8.1, with \(H = \varepsilon \sqrt{P}\), gives (8.19).

In fact \(E\) decays faster than every negative power of \(h\). To see this, write \(y = \log \log h\). Then \(\log h = e^y\) and \(\sqrt{P} = e^{y^2 / 2}\). For each \(N > 0\),

\(\displaystyle \varepsilon e^{y^2 / 2} - Ne^y - \log (2 pb_{2 B}) \longrightarrow + \infty,\)

because \(\log b_{2 B} = O (y)\) and \(e^{y^2 / 2} / e^y \to \infty\). Thus \(E (x) \leqslant h^{- N}\) eventually. The thresholds may depend on \(L, \xi, M, p, m, N\); they do not depend on the lattice period.

8.3.3Why a nonzero warning forces an excursion

We spell out the deterministic step used to connect the preceding probability estimate with the actual factors. Let \(q\) be the number of colour coordinates on one bond. On the event that all coordinates within distance \(B\) of a site obey \(|Z_i | \leqslant \xi \tau \sqrt{\beta}\),

\(\displaystyle \| \beta^{- 1 / 2} \text{erect} (Z)_b \| \leqslant q \xi \tau, \qquad \| \mu_b + \beta^{- 1 / 2} \text{erect} (Z)_b \| \leqslant (C + q \xi) \tau\)

for the bonds in question, provided \(\| \mu_b \| \leqslant C \tau\) there. The first inequality follows by the triangle inequality in an orthonormal colour basis; it does not require a sharp Euclidean bound. Choose \(q \xi \leqslant 1\) and an outer cutoff plateau \(a \geqslant C + 1\). The bond cutoff is then identically one throughout this smaller event.

For the remaining masks we use the local fine and predictor estimates on the same radius-\(B\) event. At each coarse site at distance greater than \(R_p\) from the seed set, assume a local error bound \(\delta \tau\) for each fine or predictor reading, with guide bounds \(A \tau / L^2\) and \(A \tau\), respectively. The local error has three parts: the mean error, the change under the controlled fluctuation, and the nonlinear remainder. Each is bounded by \(\delta \tau / 3\). If the positive quadratic constants of the fine action and predictor penalty are \(c_f, c_p\), the resulting bounds are

\(\displaystyle \text{fine reading} \leqslant \frac{2}{\sqrt{c_f}} (A / L^2 + \delta) \tau, \qquad \text{predictor reading} \leqslant \frac{2}{\sqrt{c_p}} (A + \delta) \tau .\) (8.21)

Choose \(\delta = \sqrt{c_f} / 32\), then \(L\) large enough that \(A / L^2 \leqslant \delta\), and finally \(W > (2 / \sqrt{c_p}) (A + \delta)\). The first coefficient in (8.21) is at most \(1 / 8 < 1 / 4\), and the second is strictly less than \(W\). The corresponding fine and predictor masks therefore equal one; the local likelihood factors also equal one by the same local plateau estimates. Taking \(\xi\) smaller if necessary bounds the linear fluctuation error by \(\delta \tau / 3\); the two remaining errors are eventually below \(\delta \tau / 3\) by their strictly smaller scale estimates.

A warning factor is either a bounded cutoff times one of these factors minus one, or a bond cutoff minus one. Factors inside their compulsory seed region are defined to be zero. We now make the passage from these local statements to the warning precise; this requires the contraction type, which the preceding paragraphs do not treat, and the change of radius from \(B\) to \(2 B\).

For a coarse site \(j\) write \(\mathcal{W}_r (j)\) for the event that all coordinates within distance \(r\) of \(j\) obey \(|Z_i | \leqslant \xi \tau \sqrt{\beta}\). Let \(t_f\) be the fine chord threshold of Subsection 8.10 (written \(t\) there; it is unrelated to the coordinate threshold \(t\) of this section). Say that the plateau conclusion holds at \(j\) if: the site likelihood at \(j\) equals one; every fine plaquette whose anchor has parent \(j\) has chord less than \(t_f / 4\); and for every axis \(e\) the bond likelihood of the coarse bond \((j, e)\) equals one and its predictor chord is less than \(Wt_f\). The local estimates described above give the plateau conclusion at every coarse site \(j\) at distance greater than \(R_p\) from the seed set on the event \(\mathcal{W}_B (j)\); this is the form in which they enter, and it remains an analytic input. We assume that the compulsory seed region has radius \(R_c \geqslant R_p + B\).

The fine site contraction at a fine site \(y\), for the given finite list \(l\) of forced activities, is the factor equal to \(0\) if \(y\) is not covered by the supports in \(l\) and \(y\) is a fine flag of the current fine field at the flag threshold of the representation, and equal to \(1\) otherwise. Here \(y\) is a fine flag at threshold \(\theta\) if some fine plaquette having \(y\) as a corner has chord greater than \(\theta\). The representation's flag threshold is the fine threshold \(t_f\); this identification is part of the data of the representation.

The assigned coarse site of a warning is the parent of its fine address (fine plaquette masks, through the anchor, and fine site contractions), the base point of its coarse bond (bond masks, bond likelihoods and bond cutoffs), or its coarse site (site likelihoods).

Lemma 8.2. Let a warning have assigned coarse site \(i\) outside the compulsory seed region, and suppose \(\mathcal{W}_{2 B} (i)\) holds and \(\mu\) obeys the bound \(\| \mu_b \| \leqslant C \tau\) used above on the bonds within distance \(2 B\) of \(i\). Then the warning factor is zero.

Proof. Let \(j\) be a coarse site with \(\text{dist} (i, j) \leqslant B\). The ball of radius \(B\) about \(j\) lies in the ball of radius \(2 B\) about \(i\), so \(\mathcal{W}_B (j)\) holds. Since \(\text{dist} (i, \text{seeds}) > R_c \geqslant R_p + B\), we have \(\text{dist} (j, \text{seeds}) > R_p\). Hence the plateau conclusion holds at every such \(j\); in particular at \(j = i\).

Bond cutoff. On \(\mathcal{W}_B (i)\) the first paragraph of this subsection shows that the bond cutoff equals one on the bonds based at \(i\), so the warning, a bond cutoff minus one, vanishes.

Fine and predictor masks, bond and site likelihoods. The fine readings at plaquettes with parent \(i\) and the predictor readings on bonds based at \(i\) are below the mask plateaus by (8.21) and the choice of \(\delta, L, W\), and the likelihoods at \(i\) equal one, by the plateau conclusion at \(i\). Each such factor equals one, and the warning, a bounded cutoff times the factor minus one, vanishes.

Fine site contraction. Let the warning be the contraction at a fine site \(y\) with parent \(i\), and suppose its factor is \(0\). Then \(y\) is a fine flag at threshold \(t_f\), so some fine plaquette \(v\) having \(y\) as a corner has chord greater than \(t_f\). The anchor of \(v\) differs from \(y\) by at most one unit in each coordinate, and the parent map changes each coordinate by at most one when its argument changes by one, so the parent \(j\) of the anchor of \(v\) satisfies \(\text{dist} (i, j) \leqslant 1 \leqslant B\). The plateau conclusion at \(j\) gives chord\((v) < t_f / 4 < t_f\), a contradiction. Hence the contraction equals one and the warning vanishes.\(\Box\)

Taking the contrapositive: a nonzero warning outside the compulsory region implies that \(\mathcal{W}_{2 B} (i)\) fails, that is, a coordinate excursion within distance \(2 B\) of its assigned site \(i\); inside the compulsory region the warning is zero by definition. This is the implication used below, with the radius \(2 B\) of (8.19). The local estimates producing (8.21) and the plateau conclusion, the bound on \(\mu\), and the uniform physical covariance bound remain explicit analytic inputs here. The probability and integration arguments do not assume or replace their production.

8.3.4Integration before multiplication of bounds

Fix a tree sector. Its integration variables are a Haar variable \(Y\) and the single Gaussian vector \(Z\) above. Their reference measure \(\nu (dY) \gamma_A (dZ)\) is a product of probability measures. All factors in a term are evaluated at the same \((Y, Z)\).

A term is indexed by a finite set \(S\) of distinct vertices of four types: connected packets, forced activities, exponential logarithmic corrections, and warnings. Assume these factors are measurable and satisfy the following uniform pointwise bounds:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c|c} \text{vertex } i & |f_i (Y, Z) | \leqslant c_i\\ \hline \text{packet with } n_i \text{ seeds} & C_0 e^{(s - g) n_i}\\ \text{forced activity } i & v_i\\ \text{logarithmic correction } i & w_i\\ \text{warning } i & T \end{array}\)

Here all displayed bounds are nonnegative. The bound \(w_i\) bounds the actual factor \(e^{\ell_i} - 1\) as well as the logarithmic quantity when that additional estimate is used elsewhere. A disconnected packet is assigned factor and bound zero. Write \(X (S)\) for the distinct warning addresses in \(S\). Suppose each individual nonzero warning forces its coordinate excursion, as established under the local inputs above. Thus for every finite \(S\), a nonzero product forces \(Z \in \mathcal{C} (X (S) ; t, 2 B)\). Then

\(\displaystyle \int \left| \prod_{i \in S} f_i (Y, Z) \right| \hspace{0.17em} d \nu \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_A \leqslant \prod_{i \in S} \tilde{c}_i, \qquad \tilde{c}_i = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} E (x) T, & i \text{ a warning},\\ c_i, & \text{otherwise} . \end{array} \right.\) (8.22)

Indeed the integrand is bounded by \(\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{C} (X (S) ; t, 2 B)} (Z) \prod_{i \in S} c_i\). It is integrable, being measurable and uniformly bounded on a probability space. Fubini's theorem and (8.19) give a bound \(E (x)^{|X (S) |} \prod c_i\), which is exactly the right-hand side of (8.22). In particular, no product of marginal warning probabilities has been used.

The same calculation covers one marked insertion. Let its distinguished vertex be \(i_{\ast}\) and let its measurable factor \(F_{\ast}\) satisfy \(|F_{\ast} | \leqslant w_{\ast}\). Multiply it by a cutoff taking values in \([0, 1]\). Define the marked term to be zero unless \(i_{\ast} \in S\), and otherwise replace \(f_{i_{\ast}}\) by this cutoff times \(F_{\ast}\). Applying (8.22) to \(S \setminus \{i_{\ast} \}\) gives

\(\displaystyle \int | \text{marked term} | \hspace{0.17em} d \nu \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_A \leqslant \boldsymbol{1}_{\{i_{\ast} \in S\}} w_{\ast} \prod_{i \in S \setminus \{i_{\ast} \}} \tilde{c}_i .\) (8.23)

If the removed vertex was a warning, its probability bound is removed as well. The remaining warnings alone are used in the event bound. This is the marked vertex bound required for the connected sums of Subsection 8.2.

For the actual factors one may take \(T = 1 +\# \{ \text{frame tags} \} +\# \{ \text{point tags} \}\). Indeed the unmodified mask or likelihood lies in \([0, T]\), the enforcing cutoff lies in \([0, 1]\), and \(T \geqslant 1\), so its cutoff times the difference from one has absolute value at most \(T\). A bond cutoff minus one is bounded by one. Thus the small warning bound in (8.22) is supplied by the proved Gaussian estimate. The packet, forced, and logarithmic pointwise bounds in the table are separate analytic obligations; (8.22) is the precise passage from those bounds to integrated vertex products.

8.4Gauge comparison and the geometry of large defects

We now prove the geometric estimates used in the seed energy bound. The essential analytic point is a first-power estimate for the actual coarse predictor. It must hold on both branches of its definition and for every output with positive weight. Small-field uniqueness alone would not give the estimate needed here.

Write \(r (g) = D^{- 1} \|g - 1\|\) for the normalized matrix chord. Thus \(r \leqslant 2\), \(r (g^{- 1}) = r (g)\), \(r\) is conjugation invariant, and \(r (gh) \leqslant r (g) + r (h)\). For a finite lattice box \(X\), let

\(\displaystyle E_X (U) = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} (x, a)\\ x, x + e_a \in X \end{array}$}}} r (U_{x, a}), \qquad F_X (U) = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} (x, p)\\ \text{all corners in } X \end{array}$}}} r (U_p) .\)

The sums include positive bonds and one orientation for each plaquette. All boxes in the first part of the argument lie in the covering lattice \(\mathbb{Z}^4\); periodic multiplicities will be restored at the end.

8.4.1An axial gauge with a first-power bound

For the box of radius \(R\), with side \(q = 2 R + 1\), there is a coordinate path gauge \(H\) such that

\(\displaystyle E_X (H \cdot U) \leqslant 16 qF_X (U) .\) (8.24)

Here the gauge at \(x\) is parallel transport from a suitable base point to \(x\), moving along the coordinates in a prescribed order. We prove more generally the bound \(4 kqF_X\) on a \(k\)-dimensional coordinate slab. It is zero on a one-point slab. Suppose the assertion holds in \(k\) dimensions and add a coordinate \(j\). Choose a slice perpendicular to \(j\) with least plaquette sum; its sum is at most \(F_X / q\), since the plaquettes in different slices are distinct. Choose the base point there by induction, and extend the paths by moving last along \(j\). All \(j\)-bonds in the resulting gauge are the identity.

For a transverse bond, two neighbouring slice values differ in chord by at most the chord of the plaquette between them. Indeed, with the two vertical bonds equal to \(1\), that plaquette is \(ab^{- 1}\), and \(|r (a) - r (b) | \leqslant r (ab^{- 1})\). Summing over the transverse directions costs at most four times the plaquette sum between the slices. Telescoping in \(j\) shows that the bond sums of any two slices differ by at most \(4 F_X\). The total bond sum is consequently at most

\(\displaystyle q \left( 4 kq \frac{F_X}{q} + 4 F_X \right) = 4 (k + 1) qF_X .\)

This proves (8.24), with the required coordinate path form of the gauge. In particular the fallback rule which permits axial gauges with bound \(K_{\text{ax}} qF_X\) has an eligible choice whenever \(K_{\text{ax}} \geqslant 16\).

We also need a path counting estimate. If \(N = |X|\), then for any fixed coordinate order,

\(\displaystyle \sum_{x, y \in X} r (U_{x \to y}) \leqslant 4 NqE_X (U) .\) (8.25)

For each coordinate segment use the sum of the bond chords on its coordinate line. During that segment the other coordinates are taken from either \(x\) or \(y\), according to whether they have already been changed. Exchanging these coordinate slots between the two endpoints is a bijection of \(X \times X\). Thus the mixed point is uniformly distributed when the endpoints are uniformly distributed. Averaging the sum over its coordinate line gives \(qE_{X, a} / N\), where \(E_{X, a}\) is the bond sum in direction \(a\). Bounding each of the at most four segments by \(qE_X / N\) and multiplying by \(N^2\) proves (8.25). Reversed segments have the same bound because the chord is invariant under inversion.

8.4.2Synchronizing minimizing frames

Let \(I\) and \(A\) be nonempty finite sets, and suppose \(W_{ij \alpha} \in G\), with \(W_{ii \alpha} = 1\) and \(W_{ji \bar{\alpha}} = W_{ij \alpha}^{- 1}\) for a permutation \(\alpha \mapsto \bar{\alpha}\) of \(A\). A minimizing frame \(h : I \to G\) minimizes

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{E} (h) = \text{av}_{i, j, \alpha} \tfrac{1}{2} r (h_i W_{ij \alpha} h_j^{- 1})^2 .\)

Such a minimum exists by compactness. Put

\(\displaystyle b_i = \text{av}_{j, \alpha} \hspace{0.17em} r (W_{ij \alpha}), \qquad b = \text{av}_i \hspace{0.17em} b_i .\)

There is a constant \(C_G\), independent of both finite sets and of \(W\), such that some \(c \in G\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \text{av}_i \hspace{0.17em} r (h_i c^{- 1}) \leqslant C_G b.\) (8.26)

Here are the details which improve the square-root estimate to this first-power bound. Comparison with the constant frame gives \(\mathcal{E} (h) \leqslant b\), since \(r^2 / 2 \leqslant r\). The triangle inequality and \((u + v)^2 \leqslant 2 u^2 + 2 v^2\) therefore give

\(\displaystyle \text{av}_{i, j} \|h_i - h_j \|^2 \leqslant 8 D^2 b.\) (8.27)

Write \(m = \text{av}_j \hspace{0.17em} h_j\) for the matrix centroid and \(m_i = \text{av}_{j, \alpha} \hspace{0.17em} h_j W_{ij \alpha}^{- 1}\) for its local replacement. Then

\(\displaystyle \|m_i - m\| \leqslant Db_i, \qquad h_i \text{ is a nearest group element to } m_i .\) (8.28)

The norm bound follows by averaging matrix differences. For the second assertion, change only \(h_i\) in the minimizing energy. The two off-diagonal parts have equal costs by the reversal identity. The diagonal term in the actual energy remains zero. Freezing its second endpoint would add the nonnegative term \(r (kh_i^{- 1})^2 / 2\), which is zero at \(k = h_i\); hence \(h_i\) also minimizes the full frozen-neighbour cost. Expanding the squared Frobenius norms shows that this is exactly nearest-point minimization for \(m_i\).

Let \(\pi\) and \(\rho\) be the nearest-point projection and its radius from Lemma 8.4. Its derivative at \(1\) is the orthogonal projection \(\Pi\), of operator norm at most \(1\), and \(D \pi\) is continuous, so there is \(\delta \in (0, \rho]\) with \(\|D \pi \| \leqslant 2\) on the ball \(B (1, \delta)\). By part (b) of that lemma, every matrix \(A\) in this ball has exactly one nearest group element, \(\pi (A)\); integrating \(D \pi\) along segments in the convex ball gives \(\| \pi (A) - \pi (B)\| \leqslant 2 \|A - B\|\). Thus two matrices in the \(\delta\)-ball have nearest points at distance at most twice their distance. Set

\(\displaystyle \sigma = \frac{(\delta / 2)^2}{8 D^2}, \qquad a = \frac{\delta}{2 D} .\)

If \(b < \sigma\), choose \(c\) nearest to \(m\) and rotate the frame by \(c^{- 1}\). In the rotated coordinates \(1\) is nearest to its centroid. Choose \(k\) whose mean squared distance to the frame is at most the double average in (8.27). Jensen's inequality and the nearest-point property give

\(\displaystyle \|m - 1\|^2 \leqslant \|m - h_k \|^2 \leqslant \text{av}_j \|h_j - h_k \|^2 < (\delta / 2)^2 .\)

For \(b_i < a\), (8.28) puts \(m_i\) in the \(\delta\)-ball and gives \(Dr (h_i) \leqslant 2 Db_i\). For \(b_i \geqslant a\) use \(r (h_i) \leqslant 2 \leqslant 2 b_i / a\). Averaging proves the desired inequality in this case. If \(b \geqslant \sigma\), simply use the bound \(2 \leqslant 2 b / \sigma\). Thus (8.26) holds with \(C_G = \max \{2, 2 / a, 2 / \sigma\}\). Left and right rotations give the same chord, by conjugation invariance.

Apply this to the coordinate path transports on a patch \(X\) of side \(q\). The path orders supply \(A\), with reversed orders supplying the involution above. If \(f\) is the actual minimizing frame and \(H\) is any comparison gauge, then \(h_i = f_i H_i^{- 1}\) minimizes the path cost for \(H \cdot U\). This follows by substituting into every path product; normalizing a competitor at the patch centre is harmless because a common left rotation preserves the cost. Equations (8.25) and (8.26) yield

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i \in X} r (c^{- 1} f_i H_i^{- 1}) \leqslant 4 C_G qE_X (H \cdot U) .\)

For an active fallback frame, eligibility instead gives \(E_X (f \cdot U) \leqslant K_{\text{ax}} qF_X (U)\). The pure-gauge discrepancy \(d_i = f_i H_i^{- 1}\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle E_X (d \cdot 1) \leqslant E_X (f \cdot U) + E_X (H \cdot U) .\)

This is the bondwise triangle inequality, with conjugation removing \(H \cdot U\). Its path transport from \(i\) to \(j\) is \(d_i d_j^{- 1}\). Average (8.25) over \(j\) and select one \(j\) whose sum is no larger than the average. Taking \(c = d_j\) proves the same conclusion with the added axial term. Both branches therefore satisfy, for \(A_G = \max \{4 C_G, 4\}\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i \in X} r (c^{- 1} f_i H_i^{- 1}) \leqslant A_G q \{E_X (H \cdot U) + K_{\text{ax}} qF_X (U)\} .\) (8.29)

Only positivity of the selected frame weight is required; the fallback assertion would be false for an ineligible tag.

8.4.3The actual coarse predictor

Fix the smoothing order \(J \geqslant 4\) and the kernel constants. The odd block side \(L\) may vary. The patch has radius \((J + 3) L\), and the macro box has radius \((J + 7) L\). Fix a coarse site \(x\), lift the field around \(Lx\) to this macro box, and write \(P_x\) for its plaquette chord sum. All translated patches at coarse offsets in the radius-two box lie inside the macro box. Their connecting straight paths of length \(L\) lie there as well.

By (8.24) choose a macro gauge \(H\) with \(E_{\text{macro}} (H \cdot U) \leqslant D_0 LP_x\), where \(D_0\) depends only on \(J\). Applying (8.29) on each translated patch gives constants \(c_y \in G\) with

\(\displaystyle \sum_i r (c_y^{- 1} f_{x + y} (i) H_{i + Ly}^{- 1}) \leqslant C_0 L^2 P_x .\) (8.30)

Indeed each patch side is at most a constant times \(L\), and its bond and plaquette sums are bounded by the macro sums. The constants \(C_0, D_0\) are fixed before choosing \(L\).

The patch weights \(w_i\) are the distribution of the sum of \(J\) independent uniform points in the small box of side \(L\). They obey

\(\displaystyle w_i \leqslant L^{- 4} .\) (8.31)

Condition on all but the first point. For a prescribed sum there is at most one possible first point, among \(L^4\) equiprobable choices. This proves (8.31), including coincidences among different tuples of points.

For a coarse bond from \(x + y\) to \(x + y + e_a\), let \(Y_i\) be its connector in the two selected frames. Gauging the middle straight path by \(H\) expresses \(c_y^{- 1} Y_i c_{y + e_a}\) as the product of the two frame errors and that path transport. At each of its \(L\) steps, translation of \(i\) is injective; hence the sum of the path chords is at most \(LE_{\text{macro}} (H \cdot U)\). Equations (8.30) and (8.31) give

\(\displaystyle \sum_i w_i r (c_y^{- 1} Y_i c_{y + e_a}) \leqslant (2 C_0 + D_0) L^{- 2} P_x .\) (8.32)

The predictor \(Q\) either minimizes the weighted squared chord to the connectors, or selects a connector when that minimum is at least the fixed positive cutoff \(\zeta\). For every group element \(b\) its output satisfies

\(\displaystyle r (Qb^{- 1}) \leqslant 2 \max \{1, \zeta^{- 1} \} \sum_i w_i r (Y_i b^{- 1}) .\)

On the minimizing branch, \(Q\) is nearest to the weighted matrix centroid; comparison with \(b\) and two triangle inequalities prove the factor \(2\). On the other branch, \(\zeta \leqslant \sum_i w_i r (Y_i b^{- 1})^2 / 2 \leqslant \sum_i w_i r (Y_i b^{- 1})\), while \(r (Qb^{- 1}) \leqslant 2\). This proves the claim for every output tag. Using \(b = c_y c_{y + e_a}^{- 1}\) in (8.32) bounds each gauged coarse bond by \(C_1 L^{- 2} P_x\). The four corner gauges cancel around a plaquette, so

\(\displaystyle r (Q_p) \leqslant CL^{- 2} P_x, \qquad r (V_p) \leqslant \sum_{e \in \partial p} r (V_e Q_e^{- 1}) + CL^{- 2} P_x, \quad C = 4 C_1 .\) (8.33)

The second inequality follows by replacing the four factors of the plaquette one at a time, conjugating the intervening products. No smallness assumption on \(U\) was used. The hypothesis is that the frames at the radius-two coarse offsets have positive weight.

8.4.4Periodic multiplicity and the large-defect witness

On a periodic lattice, let \(m (x, p)\) count the lifts of the fine plaquette \(p\) wholly contained in the macro box centred at \(Lx\). Then, with \(r_p = r (U_p)\),

\(\displaystyle P_x = \sum_p m (x, p) r_p .\) (8.34)

This equality counts lifts, not merely distinct periodic plaquettes. If the fine period is greater than \(2 (J + 7) L\), different anchors in one macro box cannot wrap to the same point, and \(m (x, p) \leqslant 1\). Furthermore, if \(m (x, p) > 0\), the coarse parent of \(p\) lies at periodic sup distance at most \(R = J + 8\) from \(x\): the fine anchor is within \((J + 7) L\) of \(Lx\), its distance to its block centre is at most \(L / 2\), and distances between embedded coarse sites are \(L\) times their coarse distances. Consequently, under the same nonwrapping condition,

\(\displaystyle \sum_x m (x, p) \leqslant (2 R + 1)^4 .\) (8.35)

The periodic ball bound holds even when the ball wraps, since reducing an integer box modulo the period is surjective onto that ball.

A seed collection consists of fine plaquettes \(\mathcal{F}\), coarse predictor bonds \(\mathcal{I}\) and coarse flags \(\mathcal{G}\). Its size is \(n = |\mathcal{F}| + |\mathcal{I}| + |\mathcal{G}|\). Associate to it the coarse parents of the fine plaquette corners, the endpoints of the predictor bonds and the flag sites. Their union has size at most \(4 n\). A radius-\(b\) neighbourhood of this union therefore contains at most

\(\displaystyle v (b) n, \qquad v (b) = 4 (2 b + 1)^4 N_p,\)

coarse plaquette addresses, where \(N_p\) is the number of plaquette orientations. For \(B \geqslant 1\), \(v (2 B) \leqslant v (2) B^4\). This proves the outer-region bound used in Subsection 8.10.

Let \(t\) be the fine threshold, \(T\) the very large threshold, and \(W\) the predictor cutoff parameter. Retain the seeds in \(\mathcal{F}\) with \(r_p > T\) and those in \(\mathcal{I}\) with \(r (V_e Q_e^{- 1}) > T\), and discard the flags. Call this collection the very large seeds, of size \(d_A\).

Definition 8.3. (Local sector conditions) Let \(\mathcal{N}\) be the set of coarse sites at distance at most \(5 B\) from the associated coarse sites of the seed collection. A fine plaquette meets \(\mathcal{N}\) if the coarse parent of at least one of its four corners lies in \(\mathcal{N}\); a coarse bond meets \(\mathcal{N}\) if at least one of its two endpoints lies in \(\mathcal{N}\). Write \(r_p\) for the chord of the fine plaquette \(p\) and \(z_e = r (V_e Q_e^{- 1})\) for the predictor chord of the coarse bond \(e\). The local sector conditions are:

    (S1)

    for every fine plaquette \(p\) meeting \(\mathcal{N}\): \(r_p \geqslant t / 4\) if \(p \in \mathcal{F}\), and \(r_p \leqslant t / 2\) if \(p \not\in \mathcal{F}\);

    (S2)

    for every coarse bond \(e\) meeting \(\mathcal{N}\): \(z_e \geqslant Wt\) if \(e \in \mathcal{I}\), and \(z_e \leqslant 2 Wt\) if \(e \not\in \mathcal{I}\);

    (S3)

    for every \(i \in \mathcal{N}\): \(i \in \mathcal{G}\) if and only if \(i\) is a coarse flag of \(V\) at the coarse threshold \(s\);

    (S4)

    for every \(i \in \mathcal{N}\): the selected frame weight (the retained site likelihood) of the patch at \(i\) is strictly positive.

These are the conditions defining the packet sector; they are part of the hypotheses of every estimate below and in Sections 8.10 and 8.6 that refers to them. Every fine plaquette whose anchor has its parent in \(\mathcal{N}\) meets \(\mathcal{N}\), and every coarse bond whose base point is in \(\mathcal{N}\) meets \(\mathcal{N}\), so the conditions imply the weaker anchor-based statements; the arguments of this section only use those. The incidence form matters in Subsection 8.10, where the data are transformed by signed coordinate maps: such a map sends the corners (endpoints) of an address to the corners (endpoints) of the image address and commutes with the parent map, so it preserves the class of addresses meeting \(\mathcal{N}\) once \(\mathcal{N}\) is replaced by its image, whereas it changes anchors.

Suppose

\(\displaystyle R \leqslant \lfloor 4 B / 5 \rfloor, \quad t / 2 \leqslant T, \quad 2 Wt \leqslant T, \quad (4 + CL^{- 2} (2 (J + 7) L + 1)^4 N_p) T \leqslant \delta .\) (8.36)

Then every coarse plaquette of chord greater than \(\delta\) whose anchor lies in the radius-\(2 B\) seed neighbourhood is within distance \(R\) of a very large seed. To prove this, suppose no such seed exists. Every fine plaquette counted by \(P_x\) has parent within \(R\) of \(x\). If listed, its chord is at most \(T\), or it would be a witness; if unlisted, its chord is at most \(t / 2 \leqslant T\). The same reasoning applies to the four predictor discrepancies, using \(2 Wt \leqslant T\). All these addresses and the required radius-two frames lie in the radius-\(5 B\) neighbourhood, since \(x\) starts within \(2 B\) and the added radii are at most \(3 B\). Thus (8.33) applies and gives \(r (V_p) \leqslant \delta\), a contradiction.

It follows that these large coarse plaquettes belong to the hit region, defined using the radius-\(\lfloor 4 B / 5 \rfloor\) neighbourhood of the very large seeds, and their number is at most \(v (R) d_A\). The whole hit region has at most \(v (2) B^4 d_A\) plaquettes. A coarse flag is, by definition, a corner of a coarse plaquette with chord above the coarse threshold \(s\); each such plaquette has at most four corners. Hence

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{G}| \leqslant 4\# \{p \text{ in the outer region} : r (V_p) > s\} .\)

Splitting these plaquettes at \(\delta\) gives precisely the flag count and the \(K_0 d_A\) large-plaquette count used in the energy allocation.

All constants in these geometric counts can be chosen before \(L\) and \(W\): take, for example, \(K_0 = v (R) + v (2) + R + (2 R + 1)^4\). For each fixed \(L, W\), the conditions (8.36) hold eventually along the coupling parameter. Indeed \(B \to \infty\), \(T = h^{- 49 / 100} \to 0\), and \(t / T = P (h) h^{- 1 / 100} \to 0\), by the threshold estimates already proved. The allowed coarse periods are at least \(100 (1 + B)\), which also ensures the nonwrapping condition for the macro box. Thus the geometric constants are independent of both the volume and the subsequent choice of block side. The guide bounds and the hit and unhit energy comparison estimates are separate analytic inputs; this argument establishes the geometric part of their allocation.

8.5Kernel sums and the bounded guide fields

The energy argument uses kernels after parallel transport, truncation and periodization. We derive their row and column bounds from the cover estimates, and then bound the clipped guide fields. The inputs are the tower strip bounds and the exponentially summable path expansions at the two adjacent stages. The passage to the physical kernels does not require a small-field assumption.

8.5.1Counting types before periodization

Let \(K_{ab} (z)\) be a scalar cover kernel, with finite type sets \(\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}\), satisfying, for every fixed pair of types,

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z \in \mathbb{Z}^4} |K_{ab} (z) | e^{\eta |z|_1} \leqslant C, \qquad \eta \geqslant 0.\)

where \(|z|_1\) is the \(\ell^1\) norm of the cover displacement (the same norm is used in the strip hypotheses below; neighbourhoods are still measured in the periodic supremum distance). A physical entry from \((x, a)\) to \((y, b)\) is a sum of selected terms \(K_{ab} (z) \text{Ad} (U_{\gamma_{x, z}})\) with \(y = x + \text{wrap} (z)\). Adjoint transport is an isometry of the real colour space, including along winding paths, so the operator norm of each term is at most \(|K_{ab} (z) |\). Absolute convergence therefore permits the following rearrangements:

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x, a} \sum_{y, b} \|F_{(x, a), (y, b)} \| \leqslant |\mathcal{B}| C, \qquad \sup_{y, b} \sum_{x, a} \|F_{(x, a), (y, b)} \| \leqslant |\mathcal{A}| C.\) (8.37)

For the first, fix \(z, b\): exactly one \(y\) can contribute. For the second, fix \(z, a\): exactly one \(x = y - \text{wrap} (z)\) can contribute. Dropping the truncation only increases the absolute sum. Thus different lifts of one periodic displacement are all paid.

The wide kernel \(\Phi\) has fine doubled plaquette rows and coarse doubled plaquette columns. In coarse coordinates a fine type includes its residue in the block. There are \(L^4\) residues. Its fixed-type strip cost is \(C / L^2\), so (8.37) gives, after absorbing only fixed orientation counts,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c|cc} & \text{row sum} & \text{column sum}\\ \hline \Phi & a / L^2 & aL^2\\ \Phi' & a & a\\ N & a & a \end{array}\) (8.38)

with \(a\) fixed before \(L\). Here \(\Phi'\) has predictor-bond rows and coarse doubled-plaquette columns; \(N\) has auxiliary coarse rows and the same columns. Their fixed-type strip costs are bounded independently of \(L\). The tower estimates provide precisely these three strip inputs; no residue factor is present in the narrow two kernels. In particular the column bound for \(\Phi\) cannot be replaced by its row bound. The bounds (8.37) hold for arbitrary isometric transports. When these kernels are identified with those of the energy comparison in Subsection 8.10, all transports come from one coarse field \(V\): the narrow kernels \(\Phi'\) and \(N\) are transported in \(V\), and the wide kernel \(\Phi\) in the fine replica \(V^{\uparrow}\) of the same \(V\), whose links are identities or links of \(V\) (as in Subsection 8.16), along the path from the residue \(r\) to \(Lz\) described below.

All three kernels are cut at path length at most \(R_0 = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\) in fine units. The endpoint of the wide path beginning at a fine site of residue \(r\) and ending at coarse displacement \(z\) is the embedded coarse point at parent plus \(z\): this is the path from \(r\) to \(Lz\) on the cover, reduced modulo the fine period. The parent changes by at most one coarse step on every fine bond. For the narrow paths the ordinary endpoint bound applies. Thus every nonzero entry has anchor separation at most \(B / 50\) in coarse units. This is an incidence assertion for these kernels, and does not bound all auxiliary reads of a larger operation.

8.5.2Absolute path expansions and their transposes

At one stage write the path expansion of a plaquette field in the form

\(\displaystyle \ell_p (U) = \sum_{i \geqslant 0} c_{a, i} \hspace{0.17em} \text{Ad} (U_{\gamma_{p, i}}) F (U_{p + s_{a, i}}), \qquad p = (x, a) .\)

The label \(i\) includes the target plaquette orientation and the transport path. Its length \(\ell_{a, i}\) is the number of bonds of the transport path \(\gamma_{p, i}\) plus four, the length of the boundary of the target plaquette; in particular it bounds the \(\ell^1\) distance from \(x\) to the target anchor. The truncation below uses this length, and the clipped field depends on this convention. The stage input is

\(\displaystyle \sum_i |c_{a, i} | e^{\sigma \ell_{a, i}} \leqslant P, \qquad \sigma > 0, \quad P > 0,\) (8.39)

uniformly in \(a\), with clipping relaxation radius \(r_0 > 0\) satisfying \(\sigma r_0 \geqslant 1\). Both stages have such bounds, with constants fixed before the step side \(L\). We retain the full label set when different labels land at the same periodic plaquette.

Keep terms with \(\kappa \ell_{a, i} \leqslant R_0\), where the stage factor is \(\kappa = 1\) on the fine stage and \(\kappa = L\) on the coarse stage. (It is unrelated to the guide constant \(d\) introduced at the end of this section.) Let \(\ell_p^{\text{cut}}\) be their sum and let

\(\displaystyle k_{pu} = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} i : \kappa \ell_{a, i} \leqslant R_0\\ p + s_{a, i} = u \end{array}$}}} |c_{a, i} | .\)

Absolute convergence follows from (8.39). The curvature bound \(\|F (U_u)\| \leqslant r (U_u)\) gives

\(\displaystyle \| \ell_p^{\text{cut}} (U)\| \leqslant \sum_u k_{pu} r (U_u), \quad \sum_u k_{pu} \leqslant P, \quad \sum_p k_{pu} \leqslant N_p P.\) (8.40)

For the column bound, fix an input orientation \(a\) and a label \(i\). Translation by its offset is a permutation of the periodic sites, so at most one anchor can land at the specified \(u\). Sum first over labels and then over the \(N_p\) possible input orientations. This argument also explains why merging repeated targets before taking absolute values would not justify the bound.

A retained site satisfies \(r (U_u) \leqslant t \exp \{d (p, u) / r_0 \}\) for every target \(u\). Since \(d (p, u) \leqslant \ell_{a, i}\) and \(1 / r_0 \leqslant \sigma\), (8.39) implies

\(\displaystyle \| \ell_p^{\text{cut}} (U)\| \leqslant Pt.\) (8.41)

The direct component is \(\sqrt{a_0} F (U_p)\), where \(a_0 > 0\) is the stage quadratic coefficient. Its clipped version multiplies it by \(\min \{1, t / r (U_p)\}\), with multiplier \(1\) at zero. Its norm is at most \(\sqrt{a_0} t\). The second component is \(\ell_p^{\text{cut}}\) at retained sites and zero at discarded sites. Thus the two-component clipped field \(v\) satisfies the pointwise bound

\(\displaystyle \|v_{p, \varepsilon} \| \leqslant A_0 t, \qquad A_0 = (N_p + 1) P + \sqrt{a_0} .\) (8.42)

This same \(A_0\) bounds both row and column sums in (8.40).

The selected and discarded rows have the locality needed for the energy proof. For a cut path, the parent separation is at most its length plus one, hence at most \(R_0 + 1\). A witness to discarding at lattice distance \(k\) obeys \(te^{k / r_0} < r (U_u) \leqslant 2\). For \(t = P (h) / \sqrt{h} \geqslant h^{- 1 / 2}\) this implies \(k < r_0 \log (2 \sqrt{h})\). With \(h = x + c_L\), \(c_L\) fixed, this is at most \(r_0 \log x\) eventually. Since \(B = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\), both \(R_0 + 1\) and \(k + 1\) are at most \(B / 50\) eventually. The parent map increases distances by at most one when compared with fine lattice distance; therefore these bounds give the asserted coarse anchor locality too. All tails are uniform in the period.

Finally let \(s = t (x)\) be the coarse threshold and \(t = t (x + c_L)\) the fine threshold. The shift \(c_L\) is nonnegative: its group factor is a positive multiple of a sum of squares of adjoint matrix entries, and \(\log L \geqslant 0\). For large \(x\), \(s \leqslant 2 t\): the numerator \(P (x) = \exp \{(\log \log x)^2 \}\) is increasing once \(\log x \geqslant 1\), and \(\sqrt{x + c_L} \leqslant 2 \sqrt{x}\) for \(x \geqslant c_L\). Choose \(A\) larger than the two stage constants \(A_0\). Then (8.42), applied on the coarse stage, and (8.38) imply

\(\displaystyle \|v_j \| \leqslant dt, \qquad \|(\Phi v)_i \| \leqslant dL^{- 2} t, \qquad \|(\Phi' v)_b \| \leqslant dt, \qquad d = 2 A (1 + a) .\) (8.43)

Indeed a matrix with operator-norm row sum \(r\) sends a field bounded by \(u\) to a field bounded by \(ru\). These are exactly the guide bounds used for the fine and predictor energy rows in Subsection 8.10, with constants fixed before \(L, W\). The derivation uses the stated tower and path-expansion inputs; the construction of the path expansions remains a separate input to be proved below.

8.6Paying for the affected comparison terms

The hit region consists of coarse plaquettes near the very large seeds. Its size is controlled by Subsection 8.4. We now bound the comparison terms on this region, including the negative coarse energy of large plaquettes. The decisive distinction is between a small contribution at every tested row and a summable excess. The former may have constants depending on \(L, W\); the latter must retain constants chosen before \(L\).

Keep the notation \(s, t\) for coarse and fine thresholds, with \(s \leqslant 2 t\). Let \(a, d\) be the kernel and guide constants in (8.38) and (8.43). Let \(D\) be the predictor-curvature constant in (8.33), and let \(N\) bound the macro column count and the hit-region size. These constants are fixed before \(L, W\). Write \(g = \sqrt{p}\), where \(p > 0\) is the predictor penalty, and

\(\displaystyle u = \sqrt{a_f} + d, \qquad v_0 = \sqrt{a_c} + d.\)

Here \(a_f, a_c\) are the direct quadratic coefficients. All kernels and fields below are real; a star denotes the physical Hilbert-space adjoint, after transport and truncation.

8.6.1A small part and a summable excess

For a matrix \(F_{ij}\) with operator-norm row bound \(r\) and column bound \(c\), suppose \(\|z_i \| \leqslant b + e_i\) on the inputs of the tested columns \(j \in S\), with \(b, e_i \geqslant 0\). Then

\(\displaystyle \sum_{j \in S} \|(F^{\ast} z)_j \| \leqslant |S| cb + r \sum_i e_i .\) (8.44)

Indeed \(\|(F^{\ast} z)_j \| \leqslant \sum_i \|F_{ij} \| \|z_i \|\) because an operator and its adjoint have the same norm. The constant part is bounded using the column sum. In the excess part exchange the two finite sums and enlarge \(S\) to all columns; the row sum then gives \(r \sum_i e_i\). Inputs outside the nonzero stencil need no estimate.

For the unclipped two-component plaquette field \(X\), define the excess

\(\displaystyle E^{\sharp}_p = \sqrt{a_0} E_p + \sum_q k_{pq} E_q,\)

where \(k\) is the nonnegative path matrix of (8.40). If \(r (U_q) \leqslant b + E_q\) on the local stencil, those bounds give, for either component \(\varepsilon\),

\(\displaystyle \|X_{p, \varepsilon} \| \leqslant (\sqrt{a_0} + d) b + E^{\sharp}_p, \qquad \sum_{p, \varepsilon} E^{\sharp}_p \leqslant 2 (\sqrt{a_0} + d) \sum_p E_p .\) (8.45)

For the direct component use the curvature bound. For the path component use the row bound for the constant part. For the total excess use the column bound. The factor \(2\) is the actual number of components, even though a sharper componentwise estimate is possible.

Freeze the following excess arrays:

\(\displaystyle E_p =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{p \in \mathcal{F}, r (U_p) > t\}} r (U_p), \qquad F_e =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{e \in \mathcal{I}\}} r (V_e Q_e^{- 1}), \qquad \mathcal{B}= L^{- 2} \sum_p E_p + \sum_e F_e .\)

The threshold in \(E\) is \(t\), and \(F\) includes every listed predictor bond. The larger threshold \(T\) is used only to count the hit region. The sector conditions (S1) and (S2) of Definition 8.3 give, for every address meeting the radius-\(5 B\) neighbourhood,

\(\displaystyle r (U_p) \leqslant t + E_p, \qquad r (V_e Q_e^{- 1}) \leqslant 2 Wt + F_e .\) (8.46)

For a listed fine plaquette outside the excess set its chord is at most \(t\); an unlisted one is at most \(t / 2\). Listed predictor bonds are included entirely in \(F\), while unlisted ones are at most \(2 Wt\).

Let \(M_L = (2 (J + 7) L + 1)^4 N_p\) bound the number of lifted plaquettes in a macro box. Put

\(\displaystyle r_{\ast} = 8 W + DL^{- 2} M_L, \qquad H_p = \sum_{e \in \partial p} F_e + DL^{- 2} \sum_q m (x_p, q) E_q, \qquad H_{\ast} = 4 N_p + DN_p N.\)

Equations (8.33) and (8.46) imply

\(\displaystyle r (V_p) \leqslant r_{\ast} t + H_p \quad \text{in the radius-$3 B$ neighbourhood}, \qquad \sum_p H_p \leqslant H_{\ast} \mathcal{B}.\) (8.47)

For the local assertion, the four boundary bonds contribute \(8 Wt\) and their excesses; the macro row contributes \(DL^{- 2} M_L t\) and its excess. All input anchors remain within radius \(5 B\) once \(B \geqslant N + 1\), and the required nearby frames have positive weight. For the total assertion, each of the four boundary-bond maps, for a fixed plaquette orientation, is injective on the periodic lattice; thus its sum is at most \(\sum_e F_e\). The macro column sum is at most \(N\) for each fine plaquette, and summing over coarse plaquette orientations contributes \(N_p\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle \sum_p H_p \leqslant 4 N_p \sum_e F_e + DN_p NL^{- 2} \sum_q E_q \leqslant H_{\ast} \mathcal{B}.\)

In contrast to \(r_{\ast}\), the coefficient \(H_{\ast}\) is independent of \(L, W\).

8.6.2The two comparison rows

Let \(S\) be the hit plaquettes, \(m = |S|\), and \(\tilde{S} = S \times \{0, 1\}\), so \(| \tilde{S} | = 2 m\). Put \(Z_e = gF (V_e Q_e^{- 1})\) for the predictor curvature field. The three norm totals entering the linear comparison satisfy

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \sum_{j \in \tilde{S}} \|(\Phi^{\ast} X_f)_j \| & \leqslant 2 m \hspace{0.17em} aL^2 ut + 2 au\mathcal{B}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.48)} \\ \sum_{j \in \tilde{S}} \|((\Phi')^{\ast} Z)_j \| & \leqslant 2 m \hspace{0.17em} ag (2 Wt) + ag\mathcal{B}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.49)} \\ \sum_{j \in \tilde{S}} \|(X_c)_j \| & \leqslant 2 m \hspace{0.17em} v_0 r_{\ast} t + 2 v_0 H_{\ast} \mathcal{B}. \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.50)} \end{array}\)

For (8.48), use (8.45) with \(b = t\) and then (8.44) with row \(a / L^2\) and column \(aL^2\). The total excess is at most \(2 u \sum E\), giving \(2 auL^{- 2} \sum E \leqslant 2 au\mathcal{B}\). The two successive stencils extend the tested radius-\(2 B\) region first to radius \(3 B\) and then to radius \(5 B\), so the assumed local envelope is sufficient. For (8.49), use \(\|Z_e \| \leqslant g (2 Wt + F_e)\) and the row and column bounds \(a\) for \(\Phi'\). For (8.50), apply (8.45) to (8.47) and sum its excess. Again the stencil lies within the region where that inequality is valid.

The linear comparison row is

\(\displaystyle L_p = \sum_{\varepsilon = 0}^1 \langle v_{p, \varepsilon}, (\Phi^{\ast} X_f + (\Phi')^{\ast} Z - X_c)_{p, \varepsilon} \rangle .\)

By \(\|v_j \| \leqslant dt\) and Cauchy–Schwarz, the sum over \(S\) is bounded below by \(- dt\) times the sum of the three right sides above. The quadratic defect row is

\(\displaystyle D_p = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{\varepsilon = 0}^1 \langle v_j, (\{1 - \Phi^{\ast} \Phi - (\Phi')^{\ast} \Phi' - N^{\ast} N\}v)_j \rangle, \qquad j = (p, \varepsilon) .\)

There is no assertion here that the bracket is zero at a curved field. From (8.43) and the column bounds,

\(\displaystyle \| \Phi^{\ast} \Phi v\|_{\infty} \leqslant adt, \quad \|(\Phi')^{\ast} \Phi' v\|_{\infty} \leqslant adt, \quad \|N^{\ast} Nv\|_{\infty} \leqslant a^2 dt.\)

In the first inequality the factor \(L^2\) in the column cancels the \(L^{- 2}\) in the guide bound. Thus

\(\displaystyle D_p \geqslant - d_0 t^2, \qquad d_0 = d (d + ad + ad + a^2 d) .\) (8.51)

The factor \(1 / 2\) cancels the two component slots.

Let \(\mathcal{C}_2\) be the coarse plaquettes in the outer region with chord greater than \(\delta\). By Subsection 8.4, \(\mathcal{C}_2 \subset S\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \sum_{p \in \mathcal{C}_2} r (V_p) \leqslant mr_{\ast} t + H_{\ast} \mathcal{B}.\)

Writing \(\lambda_c\) for the coarse linear coefficient and using \(s \leqslant 2 t\), combine this with (8.48)–(8.51) to obtain

\(\displaystyle \sum_{p \in S} (L_p + D_p) \geqslant \lambda_c s \sum_{p \in \mathcal{C}_2} r (V_p) - C_1 t\mathcal{B}- Z_0 t^2 m,\) (8.52)

where the explicit nonnegative constants are

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} C_1 & = d (2 au + ag + 2 v_0 H_{\ast}) + 2 \lambda_c H_{\ast},\\ Z_0 & = 2 d \{aL^2 u + 2 agW + v_0 r_{\ast} \} + d_0 + 2 \lambda_c r_{\ast} . \end{aligned}\)

This is just addition of the bounds; in particular \(C_1\) is chosen before \(L, W\), while \(Z_0\) may depend on them. The geometric estimate \(m \leqslant NB^4 d_A\) now changes the last term into \(- Zt^2 B^4 d_A\), with \(Z = NZ_0 + 1\). Since \(\mathcal{B}= L^{- 2} \sum_{\mathcal{S}} r (U_p) + \sum_{\mathcal{I}} r (V_e Q_e^{- 1})\), (8.52) is the hit-region input used in Subsection 8.10. Its proof retains the two distinct thresholds and all transposed-kernel multiplicities. The unhit-region comparison still requires the local flat-field approximation.

8.7A common small gauge away from very large seeds

The remaining energy comparison needs a gauge in which both the fine and coarse fields are small near an unhit plaquette. The fine field is given on its actual block-tree slice: its tree bonds equal \(1\). We use that tree, including paths crossing a periodic seam.

8.7.1Comparing one fine bond with the coarse replica

In each block the rooted forest gives a path from a fine site to its block centre, of length at most \(4 L\). On the tree slice its transport is \(1\), since every step is a tree bond or its inverse. For a fine bond \(e = (x, a)\), concatenate the reverse path from \(x\) to its root, the bond itself, and the path from \(x + e_a\) to its root. This gives a walk \(w\) between the two roots with

\(\displaystyle |w| \leqslant 8 L + 1, \qquad U_w = U_e .\)

Lift this entire walk to the covering lattice starting at zero, with origin the first root. Its length fits in the macro radius \((J + 7) L\) because \(J \geqslant 4\). Its endpoint \(z\) lies in that macro box. For fine period at least \(100 (J + 7) L\), wrapping is injective on the macro box. Hence \(z = 0\) if the two roots coincide, and \(z = Le_a\) if the bond crosses to the next block. The latter is true even at a periodic seam: the periodic endpoints agree with the two stated cover endpoints, and injectivity identifies their lifts.

Use the macro gauge \(H\) of Subsection 8.4 at the first coarse root \(i\), and write \(P_i\) for its macro plaquette sum. If its bond sum is at most \(D_0 LP_i\), the chord of the transport of \(w\) is at most its length times that bond sum. This deliberately allows repeated steps and gives

\(\displaystyle r (H_0 U_e H_z^{- 1}) \leqslant (8 L + 1) D_0 LP_i .\) (8.53)

The normalized local frames take value \(1\) at their own centres. Thus the centre term of (8.30) gives

\(\displaystyle r (H_{Ly} c_y) \leqslant C_0 L^2 P_i \quad (y = 0, e_a) .\)
