Together with the gauged predictor bond bound this yields

\(\displaystyle r (H_0 Q_{i, a} H_{Le_a}^{- 1}) \leqslant (2 C_0 L^2 + C_1 L^{- 2}) P_i .\)

Indeed insert \(c_0 c_0^{- 1}\) and \(c_{e_a} c_{e_a}^{- 1}\) and apply the triangle inequality to the two centre errors and the predictor term.

The coarse replica \(\bar{V}\) is \(1\) on internal fine bonds and \(V_{i, a}\) on crossing bonds. If \(e\) is internal, (8.53) has \(z = 0\) and conjugation removes \(H_0\). If it crosses, compare the last two displayed bounds in the same macro gauge and then compare \(Q_{i, a}\) with \(V_{i, a}\). Thus, with

\(\displaystyle D_L = (8 L + 1) D_0 L + 2 C_0 L^2 + C_1 L^{- 2},\)

we obtain the shell comparison

\(\displaystyle r (U_e \bar{V}_e^{- 1}) \leqslant D_L P_i + \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0, & e \text{ internal to a block},\\ r (V_{i, a} Q_{i, a}^{- 1}), & e \text{ crossing from } i. \end{array} \right.\) (8.54)

The constant is allowed to depend on the fixed block side. The only frame hypothesis is the same positive-weight condition in the macro comparison. No replacement of the prescribed forest was made.

8.7.2Constructing the gauge in an unhit neighbourhood

Fix an outer plaquette with anchor \(o\) which is not in the hit region. Let \(T\) be the very large threshold. Throughout distance \(\lfloor 4 B / 5 \rfloor\) from \(o\), all fine plaquette chords and all predictor discrepancies are at most \(T\). For listed defects this follows from being unhit: any value above \(T\) would place a very large seed in that neighbourhood. For unlisted defects it follows from the sector bounds \(t / 2 \leqslant T\) and \(2 Wt \leqslant T\).

Write \(M = (J + 7) L\) and \(K_L = (2 M + 1)^4 N_p\). For \(B\) sufficiently large after fixing \(L, W\), every macro box whose coarse centre lies within \(r = \lfloor B / 2 \rfloor\) of \(o\) is contained, in coarse parent distance, in the above neighbourhood. Its macro sum is at most \(K_L T\), with lifted multiplicities included. The necessary frames are active there as well. By (8.33), every coarse plaquette with anchor within \(r\) has chord at most

\(\displaystyle F_L T, \qquad F_L = 4 + CL^{- 2} K_L .\)

The allowed coarse period is at least \(100 (1 + B)\), so the cover box of radius \(r\) is nonwrapping. Apply the axial gauge construction (8.24) on this box. Its plaquette sum is at most \((2 r + 1)^4 N_p F_L T\), so the total gauged bond sum is at most \(16 (2 r + 1)^5 N_p F_L T\). Descend the cover gauge by the centred lift \(y \mapsto \text{lift} (y - o)\). For a bond whose anchor is within \(\lfloor 2 B / 5 \rfloor\) of \(o\), both endpoints lie in the box; the descended gauge therefore agrees with the cover gauge at both endpoints. Each such coarse bond has chord at most

\(\displaystyle H_L B^5 T, \qquad H_L = 16 \hspace{0.17em} 3^5 N_p F_L .\) (8.55)

This follows by bounding one nonnegative summand by the total and using \(2 r + 1 \leqslant 3 B\).

Call the resulting coarse gauge \(z\) and use \(z \circ \text{parent}\) on the fine lattice. Under this block-constant gauge, the replica of \(V\) becomes the replica of \(z \cdot V\): odd \(L\) ensures the prescribed parent changes across a positive fine bond either not at all or by the corresponding positive coarse step. Thus its bond chord is either zero or bounded by (8.55). On the other hand, the chord of \(U_e \bar{V}_e^{- 1}\) is unchanged by the simultaneous gauge, since that product is conjugated at the initial endpoint. Equation (8.54) consequently gives

\(\displaystyle r ((z \circ \text{parent}) \cdot U)_e \leqslant (D_L K_L + 1) T + H_L B^5 T.\)

With \(C_L = H_L + D_L K_L + 1\) and \(B \geqslant 1\), both fine and coarse fields therefore satisfy the common bound

\(\displaystyle r ((z \circ \text{parent}) \cdot U)_e \leqslant h (x), \qquad r (z \cdot V)_b \leqslant h (x), \qquad h (x) = C_L B^{10} T,\) (8.56)

on the radius-\(\lfloor 2 B / 5 \rfloor\) neighbourhood of \(o\). The fine bound uses parent distance for the anchor. All choices, including the gauge, refer to the same original fields, tree and tags. The threshold for \(x\) may depend on \(L, W\) and the fixed construction parameters, but not on the torus volume or on the chosen unhit plaquette. This proves the common small-gauge input needed for the local comparison.

8.8The minimizing branches and the predictor derivative

We establish the branch properties used in the local comparison. The cutoffs for uniqueness can be chosen using only the group, before choosing a patch. Analyticity near the identity is then a local finite-dimensional statement, and its derivative includes the root normalization explicitly.

8.8.1The nearest-point projection onto the group

The gauge group \(G\) is a closed subgroup of \(U (N)\). We regard \(\mathcal{M}= M_N (\mathbb{C})\) as a real vector space with the Frobenius inner product \(\langle A, B \rangle = \text{Retr} (A^{\ast} B)\) and norm \(\| \cdot \|\). Left and right multiplication by a unitary matrix are isometries of \(\mathcal{M}\). Let \(\mathfrak{g}= \{X \in \mathcal{M}: \exp (sX) \in G \text{ for all } s \in \mathbb{R}\}\) be the Lie algebra of \(G\), a real subspace of the skew-Hermitian matrices, let \(\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\) be its orthogonal complement in \(\mathcal{M}\), and let \(\Pi\) be the orthogonal projection of \(\mathcal{M}\) onto \(\mathfrak{g}\). A point \(u \in G\) is nearest to \(Z \in \mathcal{M}\) if \(\|Z - u\| \leqslant \|Z - v\|\) for all \(v \in G\). Since \(G\) is compact, every \(Z\) has at least one nearest point. The group may be disconnected, and it may be finite, in which case \(\mathfrak{g}= 0\).

Lemma 8.4. There are \(\rho > 0\) and a real-analytic map \(\pi : B (1, 2 \rho) \to G\), defined on the open Frobenius ball of radius \(2 \rho\) about \(1\) in \(\mathcal{M}\), with the following properties.

    (a)

    \(\pi (u) = u\) for \(u \in G \cap B (1, 2 \rho)\), and \(D \pi (1) = \Pi\).

    (b)

    For every \(Z \in B (1, \rho)\), \(\pi (Z)\) is the only point of \(G\) nearest to \(Z\).

    (c)

    If \(Z \in \mathcal{M}\) and \(\|Z - v\| < \rho\) for some \(v \in G\), then \(Z\) has exactly one nearest point in \(G\), namely \(v \pi (v^{- 1} Z)\).

    (d)

    The first and second derivatives of \(\pi\) are bounded on the closed ball \(\bar{B} (1, \rho)\).

Proof. Since \(U (N)\) is closed in \(GL (N, \mathbb{C})\), \(G\) is a matrix Lie group in the sense of [Hal00, Definition 2.2]. Its Lie algebra uses the one-parameter subgroup criterion of [Hal00, Definition 3.13]. By [Hal00, Theorem 3.23], the exponential maps a neighborhood of zero in \(\mathfrak{g}\) homeomorphically onto a neighborhood of \(1\) in \(G\). The ambient logarithm is its inverse by [Hal00, Theorem 3.6]. Thus, on a sufficiently small ambient ball of radius \(\varepsilon_0 < \log 2\), \(\exp X \in G\) if and only if \(X \in \mathfrak{g}\). Indeed, if \(\exp X \in G\), it is the exponential of a small algebra element, which the ambient logarithm identifies with \(X\). The ambient maps are analytic by their power series. Every \(u \in G\) with \(\|u - 1\| < \theta\), for sufficiently small \(\theta > 0\), therefore has a unique logarithm in this ball, tending to zero as \(u \to 1\).

The normal chart. Define \(\Phi : \mathfrak{g} \times \mathfrak{g}^{\perp} \to \mathcal{M}\) by \(\Phi (X, N) = \exp (X) (1 + N)\). It is real-analytic, \(\Phi (0, 0) = 1\), and \(D \Phi (0, 0) (X, N) = X + N\). Since \(\mathcal{M}=\mathfrak{g} \oplus \mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\), this derivative is a linear isomorphism. Complexify the finite-dimensional real spaces. The convergent power series of \(\Phi\) gives a holomorphic extension whose derivative at zero is the complexification of \((X, N) \mapsto X + N\), hence an isomorphism. After translating the base point and composing with the inverse of the derivative, the complexified map has the form \(z \mapsto z + h (z)\) with \(h (0) = 0\) and \(Dh (0) = 0\). On a sufficiently small closed complex ball of radius \(r_0\), we have \(\sup \|Dh\| \leqslant 1 / 2\). For \(\|w\| < r_0 / 2\), the iteration \(z_{n + 1} = w - h (z_n)\), starting at zero, stays in the ball and converges uniformly there as a function of \(w\) by the contraction estimate. Its limit is holomorphic in \(w\) and is the unique preimage in the ball. This gives the local holomorphic inverse. Conjugation invariance of \(\Phi\) and uniqueness of that inverse imply that it maps real points to real points. Its restriction is therefore real-analytic. Thus there is \(r \in (0, \varepsilon_0)\) such that, with \(O_r = \{(X, N) : \|X\|< r, \|N\|< r\}\), the set \(\Phi (O_r)\) is an open neighbourhood of \(1\) and \(\Phi : O_r \to \Phi (O_r)\) is a real-analytic diffeomorphism. Choose \(\rho > 0\) so small that \(\bar{B} (1, 3 \rho) \subset \Phi (O_r)\), \(2 \rho < \theta\), \(\rho \leqslant r\), and every \(u \in G\) with \(\|u - 1\| < 2 \rho\) is \(\exp X\) with \(X \in \mathfrak{g}\), \(\|X\| < r\). For \(Z \in B (1, 2 \rho)\) write \(\Phi^{- 1} (Z) = (X (Z), N (Z))\) and put \(\pi (Z) = \exp X (Z)\). Then \(\pi\) is real-analytic and takes values in \(G\), because \(X (Z) \in \mathfrak{g}\).

Property (a). If \(u \in G \cap B (1, 2 \rho)\), then \(u = \exp X\) with \(X \in \mathfrak{g}\), \(\|X\| < r\), so \(u = \Phi (X, 0)\) with \((X, 0) \in O_r\). Injectivity of \(\Phi\) on \(O_r\) gives \(X (u) = X\) and \(\pi (u) = u\). Differentiating \(\pi (\Phi (X, N)) = \exp X\) at \((0, 0)\) gives \(D \pi (1) (X + N) = X\), that is \(D \pi (1) = \Pi\).

Property (b). Let \(Z \in B (1, \rho)\) and let \(u\) be a nearest point. Since \(1 \in G\), \(\|Z - u\| \leqslant \|Z - 1\| < \rho\) and therefore \(\|u - 1\| < 2 \rho\), so \(u = \exp X\) with \(X \in \mathfrak{g}\), \(\|X\| < r\). For \(Y \in \mathfrak{g}\), \(s \mapsto \|Z - u \exp (sY)\|^2\) is differentiable and minimal at \(s = 0\), because \(u \exp (sY) \in G\). Its derivative there is \(- 2 \langle Z - u, uY \rangle = - 2 \langle u^{\ast} Z - 1, Y \rangle\), using that \(u\) is unitary. Hence \(N := u^{\ast} Z - 1 \in \mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\), and \(\|N\| = \|Z - u\| < \rho \leqslant r\). Thus \(Z = \Phi (X, N)\) with \((X, N) \in O_r\), so \((X, N) = \Phi^{- 1} (Z)\) and \(u = \pi (Z)\). As a nearest point exists, \(\pi (Z)\) is the unique one.

Property (c). Left multiplication by \(v^{- 1} \in G\) is an isometry of \(\mathcal{M}\) mapping \(G\) onto \(G\). Hence \(u\) is nearest to \(Z\) if and only if \(v^{- 1} u\) is nearest to \(v^{- 1} Z\), and \(\|v^{- 1} Z - 1\| = \|Z - v\| < \rho\). By (b), \(v^{- 1} u = \pi (v^{- 1} Z)\).

Property (d). The derivatives of \(\pi\) are continuous on the compact set \(\bar{B} (1, \rho) \subset B (1, 2 \rho)\).\(\Box\)

In the rest of this chapter and in Sections 8.4 and 8.10, \(\pi\) denotes this map. Its construction uses only \(G\); no other part of the data enters.

8.8.2Uniform uniqueness of minimizing frames

Use the finite path family \(W_{ij \alpha}\) and minimizing energy from Subsection 8.4. Let \(\pi\) and \(\rho\) be as in Lemma 8.4. By continuity of \(D \pi\) at \(1\) and \(D \pi (1) = \Pi\), there is a convex ball about \(1\), contained in \(B (1, \rho)\), on which \(\|D \pi - \Pi \| \leqslant \tfrac{1}{4}\); for all \(A, B\) in that ball,

\(\displaystyle \| \pi (A) - \pi (B) - \Pi (A - B)\| \leqslant \tfrac{1}{4} \|A - B\| .\) (8.57)

This follows by integrating \(D \pi - \Pi\) along the segment. Choose \(\delta > 0\) for such a ball, and choose \(0 < \varepsilon \leqslant \min \{\delta / 4, 1 / 4\}\). Suppose

\(\displaystyle \|W_{ij \alpha} - 1\| \leqslant \varepsilon \quad \text{for every } i, j, \alpha .\)

We claim that any two minimizing frames differ by a common left multiplication, independently of the number of points or paths.

Comparison with the constant frame and the squared triangle inequality give

\(\displaystyle \text{av}_{i, j} \|h_i - h_j \|^2 \leqslant 4 \varepsilon^2 .\)

For clarity, the mean squared transported discrepancy \(\|h_i W_{ij \alpha} - h_j \|^2\) is at most \(\varepsilon^2\) by minimality. Each untransported discrepancy squared is at most twice this term plus \(2 \varepsilon^2\); averaging gives the claim. Rotate each minimizing frame separately so that \(1\) is nearest to its centroid \(m\). Choosing a point with mean squared distance no larger than the double average and applying Jensen gives \(\|m - 1\| \leqslant 2 \varepsilon\). Its feedback matrix \(m_i = \text{av}_{j, \alpha} \hspace{0.17em} h_j W_{ij \alpha}^{- 1}\) satisfies \(\|m_i - m\| \leqslant \varepsilon\), so \(\|m_i - 1\| \leqslant 3 \varepsilon < \delta\). By (8.28), \(h_i\) is a nearest group element to \(m_i\), and \(m_i\) lies in the \(\delta\)-ball, which is contained in \(B (1, \rho)\). Hence Lemma 8.4(b) identifies \(h_i = \pi (m_i)\).

Let \(h, k\) be the two rotated minimizers and set \(v = \max_i \|h_i - k_i \|\). Their feedback differences \(D_i\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \|D_i \| \leqslant v, \qquad \|D_i - (m_h - m_k)\| \leqslant v \varepsilon .\)

The first follows by averaging and unitary invariance. For the second, subtract the centroid difference to obtain the average of \((h_j - k_j) (W_{ij \alpha}^{- 1} - 1)\) and use the matrix product bound. Since \(1\) is nearest to each centroid, \(\Pi (m_h - 1) = \Pi (m_k - 1) = 0\): differentiate squared distance along \(\exp (tX)\) for each tangent vector \(X\). Thus \(\| \Pi D_i \| \leqslant v \varepsilon\). At an index attaining the maximum, (8.57) yields

\(\displaystyle v \leqslant v \varepsilon + \tfrac{1}{4} v \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} v,\)

so \(v = 0\). Undoing the rotations proves uniqueness up to a common left multiplier. If both frames are normalized at their centre, that multiplier is \(1\).

The actual frame branch tests that, for every rooted axial gauge centred at the patch centre (every coordinate order of the axial tree), every coordinate path transport has chord less than a fixed cutoff. Take this cutoff at most \(\varepsilon / D\). Choose one of those axial gauges; the resulting \(W\) satisfies the preceding matrix bound. Changing variables by that gauge in the minimizing energy proves uniqueness of the normalized frame on the entire tested branch. These cutoffs do not depend on the patch size or smoothing order. At the identity all tested chords vanish, so this branch is an open neighbourhood of the identity field.

For the predictor branch, let \(w_i \geqslant 0\), \(\sum_i w_i = 1\), and let \(m = \sum_i w_i Y_i\). Expanding the squares shows that minimizing \(\sum_i w_i r (Y_i u^{- 1})^2 / 2\) is equivalent to choosing a nearest group element to \(m\). If the weighted cost at some \(u\) is less than \(\zeta\), Jensen gives

\(\displaystyle \|m - u\|^2 \leqslant \sum_i w_i \|Y_i - u\|^2 < 2 D^2 \zeta .\)

Choose \(\zeta\) so that \(\sqrt{2 D^2 \zeta} < \rho\). Then \(\|m - u\| < \rho\) with \(u \in G\), and Lemma 8.4(c), the uniform nearest-point tube obtained by left translation, shows that \(m\) has exactly one nearest group element. Then the predictor minimum is unique on its tested branch, uniformly over all finite weight distributions. Finally the fallback frame rule has an eligible choice by (8.24) with axial factor \(16\). Together these facts construct the required branch constants for any fixed positive predictor penalty.

8.8.3Analyticity of the normalized frame

Fix a patch and a finite torus for the moment. Write \(a\) for the fine logarithmic bond field and \(h_i (a)\) for the actual frame matrices. At \(a = 0\) they are all \(1\). The chosen minimizer is continuous on the uniqueness branch. To verify this without assuming regularity of a choice function, consider a convergent sequence of input fields. Compactness of the finite product of groups gives a convergent subsequence of any sequence of minimizers. Continuity of the energy and the centre constraint makes every limit a minimizer at the limiting field. Uniqueness makes that limit the selected one. A sequence staying outside a fixed neighbourhood of that value would contradict this argument, which proves continuity.

Let \(o\) be the patch centre and let \(W_{ij \alpha} (a)\) be its actual coordinate path transports. Define an ambient matrix-valued system

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{F}_i (a, H) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} H_o - 1, & i = o,\\ H_i - \pi \left( \text{av}_{j, \alpha} \hspace{0.17em} H_j W_{ij \alpha} (a)^{- 1} \right), & i \ne o. \end{array} \right.\)

It is analytic near \((0, \boldsymbol{1})\). Continuity of the actual frame and its feedback, together with self-consistency, shows that \(\mathcal{F} (a, h (a)) = 0\) near zero. The derivative in the ambient frame variable is

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{D}v)_o = v_o, \qquad (\mathcal{D}v)_i = v_i - \Pi \bar{v} \quad (i \ne o), \qquad \bar{v} = \text{av}_j \hspace{0.17em} v_j .\)

It is invertible. Indeed if \(\mathcal{D}v = 0\), put \(A = \Pi \bar{v}\). Then \(v_o = 0\) and \(v_i = A\) for \(i \ne o\). If the patch has \(n\) points, \(\bar{v} = (n - 1) A / n\), so applying \(\Pi\) gives \(A = (n - 1) A / n\) and \(A = 0\). The derivative is an injective endomorphism of a finite -dimensional space and is therefore bijective. The analytic implicit function theorem gives a unique analytic local solution. Here this theorem follows from the same inverse theorem: for an equation \(F (x, y) = 0\) with invertible \(D_y F\), apply it to \((x, y) \mapsto (x, F (x, y))\) and restrict the inverse to \((x, 0)\). Continuity places the actual frame in that local solution neighbourhood, so \(h (a)\) is analytic. This argument works in ambient matrices; it does not require choosing coordinates for a complement at every frame.

8.8.4The root correction in the derivative

For a Lie-algebra-valued bond perturbation \(a\), write \(\int_{\gamma} a\) for its signed sum along a path. Define

\(\displaystyle \tau_y (i ; a) = - \text{av}_{j, \alpha} \int_{i \to j, \alpha} a,\)

where the paths are in the patch centred at the coarse site \(y\). Throughout, a coarse site \(y\) is embedded in the fine lattice as \(Ly\), and the patch at \(y\) consists of the fine points \(Ly + i\), with \(i\) ranging over the patch offsets in fine units and periodic reduction understood; thus \(\int_{i \to j, \alpha} a\) is the sum of \(a\) along the path from \(Ly + i\) to \(Ly + j\) of order \(\alpha\). Differentiate the feedback equation at zero. If \(\dot{h}_i\) is the matrix derivative and \(\overline{\dot{h}}\) its uniform average, differentiation of the inverse transport gives

\(\displaystyle \dot{h}_i = \Pi \overline{\dot{h}} + \text{col} (\tau_y (i ; a)) .\)

The feedback identity holds also at the centre: the cost is invariant under a common left multiplication, so the centre-normalized minimizer is an unconstrained minimizer and (8.28) applies at \(o\) as well (see Subsection 8.4). Since the normalized frame has \(h_o (a) = 1\), its centre derivative is zero. Subtraction therefore yields

\(\displaystyle \dot{h}_i = \text{col} \{\tau_y (i ; a) - \tau_y (o ; a)\} .\) (8.58)

In particular the frame derivative uses uniform averages over patch points and path orders, before the separate connector weights enter.

For a coarse bond \(e = (y, b)\), the connector at patch point \(i\) consists of its frame at \(y\), the length-\(L\) fine path, and the inverse frame at \(y + e_b\). Its colour derivative is consequently

\(\displaystyle \tau_y (i ; a) - \tau_y (o ; a) + \int_{Ly + i \to L (y + e_b) + i} a - \tau_{y + e_b} (i ; a) + \tau_{y + e_b} (o ; a) .\)

At zero all connectors equal \(1\). Nearby the predictor is on its minimizing branch and equals \(\pi\) of their weighted matrix centroid. This follows from the weighted nearest-point identity above; the branch remains open since its minimum cost is at most the continuous cost of comparison with \(1\), which vanishes at zero. Thus the predictor is analytic near zero. The derivative of \(\pi\) is \(\Pi\), which fixes the above colour derivatives, so the predictor derivative is their weighted sum.

Define the bond averaging map and the site correction by

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} (\mathcal{A}a)_{y, b} & = \sum_i w_i \int_{Ly + i \to L (y + e_b) + i} a,\\ (\mathcal{R}a)_y & = \tau_y (o ; a) - \sum_i w_i \tau_y (i ; a) . \end{aligned}\)

The weighted connector derivative is exactly

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Q}a =\mathcal{A}a + d_c^{(0)} \mathcal{R}a.\) (8.59)

The sign is determined by the subtraction of the frame at the final endpoint: the remaining root terms are \((\mathcal{R}a)_{y + e_b} - (\mathcal{R}a)_y\). For a fine gradient \(a = d_f^{(0)} v\), path sums telescope, giving

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{R}d_f^{(0)} v)_y = v (Ly) - \sum_i w_i v (Ly + i), \qquad \mathcal{Q}d_f^{(0)} v = d_c^{(0)} \{v (Ly)\}_y .\)

This checks the root normalization directly. Equations (8.58) and (8.59) prove the analytic predictor law and derivative used in Subsection 8.9. Its transplantation argument then supplies a uniform fixed-patch Taylor radius for all periods.

The covariance needed there follows from the same definitions. A gauge change turns a frame into \(f'_i = g_o f_i g_i^{- 1}\). On a minimizing branch it preserves the energy and centre normalization, so uniqueness identifies this with the selected frame; on a fallback branch the identity follows by telescoping its rooted paths. Connector endpoint factors then reduce to \(g_{Ly} Y_i g_{L (y + e_b)}^{- 1}\). The weighted cost is invariant under this change of variables. Uniqueness identifies the minimizing predictor, and direct substitution handles a selected connector on the other branch. The original tags are retained in a gauge change. These identities give the block-constant covariance used in the unhit comparison.

8.9The comparison error on the unhit region

We compare the actual cut kernels with the full linear kernels at the identity. The linear cancellation uses the derivative of the actual rooted predictor. Its gradient correction disappears only after the coarse curl is applied. Throughout this section \(L\) and the patch are fixed; constants may depend on them but are uniform in the period, the truncation length, and the selected tags.

8.9.1The full linear identities

Let \(B_f, B_c\) be the two stacked curvature maps: their first components are \(\sqrt{a_f}\) and \(\sqrt{a_c}\) times the identity, and their second components are the full path-root kernels. Path coefficients are aligned with these root kernels by summing all labels with the same displacement and target orientation. Absolute convergence in (8.39) permits periodization and grouping. Consequently the linearizations of the two plaquette fields are

\(\displaystyle X_f^0 = B_f d_f a, \qquad X_c^0 = B_c d_c b,\)

where \(a, b\) are fine and coarse logarithmic bond fields and \(d_f, d_c\) are the discrete exterior derivatives from bonds to plaquettes. Let \(F_0, I_0, N_0\) be the full real kernels corresponding to \(\Phi, \Phi', N\) at the identity. The flat stack identities needed here are

\(\displaystyle \ \begin{aligned} F_0^{\ast} F_0 + I_0^{\ast} I_0 + N_0^{\ast} N_0 & = 1,\\ \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} I_0^{\ast} & = B_c d_c,\\ F_0^{\ast} B_f d_f & = \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} I_0^{\ast} \mathcal{A}. \end{aligned}\) (8.60)

Here \(\mathcal{A}\) is the unrooted linear averaging map. These are identities of full finite matrices in physical counting coordinates, for the same step and the same two stages. We state their exact role because none of them is asserted for the transported cut kernels. Their production, together with the aligned path expansion, is the flat-stack input to this comparison.

The derivative \(\mathcal{Q}\) of the actual predictor has the form \(\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{A}+ d_c^{(0)} \mathcal{R}\), where \(d_c^{(0)}\) sends site fields to bond gradients. Thus, since \(d_c d_c^{(0)} = 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} I_0^{\ast} \mathcal{Q}= B_c d_c \mathcal{A}.\)

The discrete identity \(d_c d_c^{(0)} = 0\) follows by cancellation of the four endpoint differences around each plaquette. With \(Z^0 = \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} (b -\mathcal{Q}a)\), the last two identities in (8.60) therefore give

\(\displaystyle F_0^{\ast} X_f^0 + I_0^{\ast} Z^0 - X_c^0 = 0.\) (8.61)

The first identity in (8.60) also gives \((1 - F_0^{\ast} F_0 - I_0^{\ast} I_0 - N_0^{\ast} N_0) w = 0\) for every doubled coarse field \(w\). No closedness assumption on \(w\) or removal of harmonic modes is used.

8.9.2Uniform Taylor bounds for local reads

For a real logarithmic field \(a\), put \(U_e = \exp (a_e)\) and use its supremum norm. On a fixed small group chart,

\(\displaystyle r (\exp v) \leqslant C \|v\|, \qquad \| \log g\| \leqslant Cr (g), \qquad \exp (\log g) = g.\)

These follow by integrating the first derivative of the analytic exponential and local logarithm on smaller closed balls. Likewise, Taylor's formula for the product of the four exponentials around a plaquette gives

\(\displaystyle \|F (U_p) - (da)_p \| \leqslant C \|a\|_{\infty}^2, \qquad \|(da)_p \| \leqslant C \|a\|_{\infty} .\) (8.62)

Indeed differentiating the product at zero gives the signed sum of the four logarithms; the derivative of the curvature coordinate is the identity on the Lie algebra. Bound the second derivative on the fixed four-slot ball and integrate the remainder. Repeated periodic bonds simply repeat coordinates in that four-slot map and do not change the constant.

For the actual predictor we use its local analytic law at the identity, for each allowed tag, with derivative \(\mathcal{Q}\) above. The law is part of the branch construction: it includes uniqueness of the normalized minimizing frame on the branch where that frame is used. It implies the uniform estimate

\(\displaystyle \|Z - Z^0 \|_{\infty} \leqslant Ch^2, \qquad \|Z^0 \|_{\infty} \leqslant Ch, \quad h = \max \{\|a\|_{\infty}, \|b\|_{\infty} \},\) (8.63)

with a radius independent of the torus. Here is why finite-dimensional Taylor estimates give that uniformity. A predictor bond reads only two frame patches, their coordinate paths, and their length-\(L\) connectors. This is a fixed finite set of cover bond anchors. Choose one reference torus large enough that these anchors have their actual cover lifts and its two coarse endpoints are distinct. Transplant the local fine logarithms to it by reading the original periodic field at each corresponding cover anchor. Transplant the coarse logarithm at the tested bond as a constant field, and copy the actual two frame tags and connector tag. The transplanted sup norm does not increase. All path products, frame scores, selected frames and connectors agree. On a minimizing branch, equality of scores gives equality of the selected normalized frame by its uniqueness; on the other branch the chosen axial paths agree directly.

There are only finitely many tags and tested directions on this reference torus. Take the minimum of their positive Taylor radii and the maximum of their Taylor bounds. Equality of the transplanted reads along every ray \(v \mapsto v (a, b)\) gives equality of their derivatives at zero as well, so these bounds prove (8.63) on every torus, including short ones. This proves the uniformization; the stated analytic branch law and identification of its derivative are required inputs.

8.9.3Truncation and transport errors

For cutoff \(R\) in fine units define

\(\displaystyle \omega (R, h) = (1 + R) h + e^{- \tau R}, \qquad \tau > 0.\)

The kernel strip width here is strictly positive, \(\eta > 0\). The value of \(\tau\) can be decreased a finite number of times below. If all logarithms have norm at most \(h\) in the chart, every link has chord at most \(Ch\), and a path of length \(k\) has chord at most \(Ckh\) by the product triangle inequality. On the colour space,

\(\displaystyle \| \text{Ad} (g) - 1\| \leqslant 2 Dr (g) .\) (8.64)

Here \(D\) is the fixed colour normalization. To see this, write \(gXg^{\ast} - X = (g - 1) Xg^{\ast} + X (g^{\ast} - 1)\), use the Frobenius product bound and unitary invariance, then divide by the colour normalization.

Consider a transported kernel term with cover displacement \(z\) and physical path length \(k \leqslant D_L (1 + |z|_1)\), with \(|z|_1\) the \(\ell^1\) norm as in Subsection 8.5. This bound holds for the actual paths: a narrow path has fine length \(L |z|_1\), while the wide path from residue \(r\) to \(Lz\) has length at most \(L|z|_1 + 4 L\). If \(k \leqslant R\), (8.64) bounds the difference from the scalar flat term by \(CRh |K_{ab} (z) |\). If \(k > R\), the flat term is omitted, and

\(\displaystyle 1 \leqslant e^{\eta} e^{\eta |z|_1} e^{- \eta R / D_L} .\)

Paying both cases with the weighted strip sum and using the same row and column counting as (8.37) proves

\(\displaystyle \|F\|_{\text{row}}, \|F\|_{\text{col}}, \|F_0 \|_{\text{row}}, \|F_0 \|_{\text{col}} \leqslant C, \qquad \|F - F_0 \|_{\text{row}}, \|F - F_0 \|_{\text{col}} \leqslant C \omega (R, h) .\) (8.65)

This holds for each of the three kernels, after increasing \(C\) and choosing \(\tau \leqslant \eta / D_L\). The type counts, including residues, are absorbed after \(L\) is fixed. Every cover term is estimated before periodization, so winding paths cause no exception.

The same argument applies to the two plaquette fields. For a retained path term, first use (8.62), then (8.64) on its transport. Its error is at most \(C (1 + R) h^2\) times its coefficient. For an omitted term use its full linear value, bounded by \(Ch\), and \(1 \leqslant e^{\sigma \ell} e^{- \sigma R / \kappa}\) when \(\kappa \ell > R\), where \(\kappa \in \{1, L\}\) is the stage factor of Subsection 8.5. The weighted absolute path sum therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \|X^0 \|_{\infty} \leqslant Ch, \qquad \|X - X^0 \|_{\infty} \leqslant Ch \omega (R, h)\) (8.66)

for both stages, with \(\tau \leqslant \min \{\sigma_f, \sigma_c / L\}\). Absolute convergence justifies subtracting and summing the path series, even if the set of labels below the length cutoff is infinite. Equation (8.63) has the same form since \(h \leqslant \omega (R, h)\).

If \(F, F_0\) satisfy (8.65) and \(z, z^0\) satisfy (8.66), then

\(\displaystyle F^{\ast} z - F_0^{\ast} z^0 = F^{\ast} (z - z^0) + (F - F_0)^{\ast} z^0\)

has sup norm at most \(2 C^2 h \omega (R, h)\). For an arbitrary bounded field \(w\), the same decomposition gives

\(\displaystyle \|(F^{\ast} F - F_0^{\ast} F_0) w\|_{\infty} \leqslant 2 C^2 \|w\|_{\infty} \omega (R, h) .\)

Insert these bounds in the two full linear cancellations. After increasing \(C\), the actual defect vectors satisfy

\(\displaystyle \| \Phi^{\ast} X_f + (\Phi')^{\ast} Z - X_c \|_{\infty} \leqslant Ch \omega (R, h), \qquad \|(1 - \Phi^{\ast} \Phi - (\Phi')^{\ast} \Phi' - N^{\ast} N) w\|_{\infty} \leqslant C \|w\|_{\infty} \omega (R, h) .\) (8.67)

The argument keeps \(w\) arbitrary; it requires no regularity of the clipping indicators that will later define \(w\).

8.9.4Restriction to a neighbourhood and summation

A cut kernel entry has endpoints within distance \(R\) in coarse anchor units and reads only the transport path of that length. The cut path field reads through radius \(R + 4\). A predictor bond reads through a fixed patch radius \(R_{\text{pred}}\). Thus the first defect at anchor \(o\) depends only on fine and coarse bonds through radius \(3 R + 4 + R_{\text{pred}}\), using parent distance for fine bonds. For example, the outer transpose moves the input anchor by at most \(R\), its kernel path adds at most \(R\), and the input field adds \(R + 4\) or the predictor radius. The second defect, as an operator applied to a fixed \(w\), reads its kernels through radius \(2 R\). Equality of these reads gives equality term by term in each cut series and finite matrix sum.

Suppose a coarse gauge \(z\) makes the fine and coarse bond chords at most \(h\) on this neighbourhood. Take the local logarithms there and set them to zero outside. Their global sup norms are at most \(Ah\) and their exponentials agree with the gauged fields on all required reads. Apply (8.67) to these extensions. Replacing \(h\) by \(Ah\) changes only the constant, since for \(A \geqslant 1\), \(\omega (R, Ah) \leqslant A \omega (R, h)\).

To remove the gauge, every transported plaquette term transforms by \(\text{Ad} (z_o)\) at its anchor; the endpoint gauge factors cancel. The same endpoint calculation for kernels gives

\(\displaystyle F'_{ij} = \text{Ad} (z_i) F_{ij} \text{Ad} (z_j)^{- 1},\)

with the parent anchor on fine rows. Taking physical adjoints preserves this identity, because adjoint transport is orthogonal. The actual predictor is covariant with the same tags under the block-constant fine gauge. Hence both defect vectors transform by \(\text{Ad} (z_o)\), provided the free field \(w\) is transformed at its anchors. Its norm is unchanged. Thus the local version of (8.67) holds for the original fields and for any bounded \(w\). In particular we may use the original clipped field \(v\) without changing its indicators in the extension argument.

Take \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\) and \(h = C_L B^{10} T\) from (8.56). For large \(B\), \(3 R + 4 + R_{\text{pred}} \leqslant \lfloor 2 B / 5 \rfloor\), so this estimate applies to every unhit outer plaquette. As \(\|v\|_{\infty} \leqslant dt\), its two comparison rows are bounded below by minus

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}= 2 dt \hspace{0.17em} Ch \omega (R, h) + dt \hspace{0.17em} Cdt \hspace{0.17em} \omega (R, h) .\) (8.68)

There are two components in the linear row, while the factor \(1 / 2\) in the quadratic row cancels its two components.

For completeness, the required delay is stronger than \(h \to 0\). Write \(H = x + c_L\), so \(T = H^{- 49 / 100}\) and \(t = P (H) H^{- 1 / 2} \geqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\). Any fixed power of \(B = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\) is smaller than any positive power of \(H\) eventually. Consequently, for every fixed integer \(n\),

\(\displaystyle B^n h \longrightarrow 0, \qquad B^n \frac{h^2}{t} \longrightarrow 0,\)

the latter because \(T^2 / t \leqslant H^{- 48 / 100}\). Also \(e^{- \tau R} \leqslant H^{- 1}\) eventually: \(R\) grows quadratically in \(\log x\), whereas \(\log H\) grows linearly. It follows that

\(\displaystyle B^n \omega (R, h) \longrightarrow 0, \qquad B^n \frac{h \omega (R, h)}{t} \longrightarrow 0.\)

Expanding \(\omega\) proves the second assertion from the \(h^2 / t\) bound and \(e^{- \tau R} / t \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\). The outer region has at most \(NB^4 n\) plaquettes for \(n\) seeds. Given \(\epsilon > 0\), choose the coupling threshold so that \(NB^4 \mathcal{L} \leqslant \epsilon t^2\). Summing (8.68) gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_{p \text{ outer, unhit}} (L_p + D_p) \geqslant - \epsilon t^2 n.\) (8.69)

This is the unhit-region estimate used in the energy allocation. Its remaining construction inputs are the full flat stack and aligned path expansions, and the analytic law and derivative of the actual predictor. The estimates, localization, common-gauge use and delay from those inputs have all been given above.

8.10The energy gain assigned to defect seeds

The exponential gain in Subsection 8.25 comes from a lower bound on the energy assigned to a finite set of defects. Fine plaquette defects, predictor defects and coarse flags require different arguments. In particular, some coarse terms can be negative. The proof retains a reserve from the very large fine and predictor defects to pay for these terms. We give the local algebra and the final allocation, keeping the geometric and local comparison inputs explicit.

8.10.1A quartic bound for the curvature defect

Use the real Frobenius inner product in the defining unitary matrix representation of the group. Write \(\mathfrak{g}\) for its tangent space at the identity and \(\Pi\) for orthogonal projection onto \(\mathfrak{g}\). Let \(D > 0\) be the colour normalization, so that

\(\displaystyle \|U - 1\| = D \hspace{0.17em} r (U), \qquad \| \text{col} (v)\| = D \|v\| .\)

Here \(r (U)\) is the group chord and \(\text{col}\) is the isometric identification up to the factor \(D\). The curvature coordinate \(F (U)\) is obtained by projecting \((U - U^{\ast}) / 2\) onto \(\mathfrak{g}\). Since the Hermitian part of \(U - 1\) is orthogonal, for the real Frobenius inner product, to every skew-Hermitian matrix,

\(\displaystyle \text{col} (F (U)) = \Pi (U - 1) .\)

To check the orthogonality, if \(H^{\ast} = H\) and \(X^{\ast} = - X\), cyclicity of the trace shows that \(\text{Retr} (HX^{\ast})\) is its own negative. Pythagoras therefore gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle D^2 \{r (U)^2 -\|F (U)\|^2 \} = \|(1 - \Pi) (U - 1)\|^2 .\) (8.70)

In particular \(\|F (U)\| \leqslant r (U)\) for every group element.

The nearest-point projection \(\pi\) of Lemma 8.4 is not part of the group data; it is constructed from \(G\) there. It is real-analytic on \(B (1, 2 \rho)\), equals the identity on \(G \cap B (1, 2 \rho)\), and has derivative \(\Pi\) at \(1\). For \(U \in G\) with \(\|U - 1\| \leqslant \rho\) we therefore have \((1 - \Pi) (U - 1) = \pi (U) - \pi (1) - D \pi (1) (U - 1)\), and Taylor's formula with integral remainder along the segment from \(1\) to \(U\), which lies in the convex closed ball \(\bar{B} (1, \rho)\), gives

\(\displaystyle \|(1 - \Pi) (U - 1)\| \leqslant C \|U - 1\|^2, \qquad C = \tfrac{1}{2} \sup_{\bar{B} (1, \rho)} \|D^2 \pi \|,\)

which is finite by part (d) of the lemma. (If \(C = 0\) replace it by \(1\).) Combining it with (8.70) gives constants \(K, \delta > 0\), depending only on the group data, such that

\(\displaystyle r^2 - Kr^4 \leqslant \|F (U)\|^2 \leqslant r^2, \qquad \tfrac{3}{4} r^2 \leqslant \|F (U)\|^2 \quad \text{when } r = r (U) \leqslant \delta .\) (8.71)

One can take \(K = C^2 D^2\) and choose \(\delta\) with \(D \delta \leqslant \rho\) (so that \(\|U - 1\| = Dr (U) \leqslant \rho\) and the Taylor bound applies) and \(K \delta^2 \leqslant 1 / 4\). Thus the nonnegative defect \(\mathfrak{f} (U) = (r (U)^2 -\|F (U)\|^2) / 2\) is at most \(Kr (U)^4 / 2\) in that neighbourhood.

This proves the group estimate needed for the coarse energy row. Let \(a_c > 0\) be its quadratic coefficient and \(\lambda_c > 0\) its linear coefficient, with \(\lambda_c \leqslant a_c / 8\). At coarse threshold \(s \geqslant 0\), the clipping function and the radial attenuation are

\(\displaystyle q_s (r) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} a_c r^2 / 2, & r \leqslant s,\\ \lambda_c sr, & r > s, \end{array} \right. \qquad \alpha_s (r) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1, & r = 0,\\ \min \{1, s / r\}, & r > 0. \end{array} \right.\)

Cancellation of the other coarse quadratic components gives the row

\(\displaystyle C_s (U) = a_c (\alpha_s - \alpha_s^2 / 2) \|F (U) \|^2 - q_s (r (U)) .\) (8.72)

For \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant \delta\) this has three useful bounds:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{ll} r \leqslant s : & C_s (U) \geqslant - B_0 s^4, \quad B_0 = a_c K / 2,\\ s < r \leqslant \delta : & C_s (U) \geqslant (a_c / 4) sr,\\ r > \delta : & C_s (U) \geqslant - \lambda_c sr. \end{array}\) (8.73)

For the first, \(\alpha_s = 1\) and use the quartic defect. In the other two ranges \(\alpha_s = s / r \in [0, 1]\), so \(\alpha_s - \alpha_s^2 / 2 \geqslant s / (2 r)\). In the middle range this gives \(C_s \geqslant (3 a_c / 8 - \lambda_c) sr \geqslant a_c sr / 4\); in the last range drop the nonnegative curvature term. These arguments include \(s = 0\).

8.10.2The fine row and the discarded quadratic component

Let the fine threshold be \(t > 0\). Write \(a_f, \lambda_f > 0\) for the fine quadratic and linear coefficients, and let \(r_i\) be the fine plaquette chords. The direct part of its energy row is

\(\displaystyle D_i = q_t (r_i) + \tfrac{1}{2} \|v_i \|^2 - \langle v_i, y_i \rangle, \qquad \|y_i \| \leqslant \sqrt{a_f} \hspace{0.17em} r_i,\)

where \(q_t\) uses coefficients \(a_f, \lambda_f\). The second part is

\(\displaystyle H_i =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{i \text{ retained} \}} \tfrac{1}{2} \|u_i \|^2 + \tfrac{1}{2} \| v'_i \|^2 - \langle v'_i, u_i \rangle .\)

Their sum is the fine row. The guide bounds are \(\|v_i \|, \|v'_i \| \leqslant \epsilon t\), with \(\epsilon = C / L^2\). The guide constant \(C \geqslant 0\), the coefficients \(a_f, \lambda_f > 0\), and the fine clipping radius \(R > 0\) are fixed before \(L\) is chosen.

Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be the listed fine defects and \(\mathcal{S}= \{i \in \mathcal{F}: r_i > t\}\) their large part. The local sector conditions (Definition 8.3, part (S1)) ensure \(r_i \geqslant t / 4\) on \(\mathcal{F}\) and \(r_i \leqslant t / 2\) on unlisted plaquettes in the relevant neighbourhood. For \(r_i \leqslant t\), Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\(\displaystyle D_i \geqslant \tfrac{1}{2} (\sqrt{a_f} \hspace{0.17em} r_i -\|v_i \|)^2 \geqslant 0.\)

If additionally \(i \in \mathcal{F} \setminus \mathcal{S}\) and \(\epsilon \leqslant \sqrt{a_f} / 8\), the last square is at least \(a_f t^2 / 128\). For \(i \in \mathcal{S}\), the linear part of the clipping and \(\epsilon \sqrt{a_f} \leqslant \lambda_f / 4\) give \(D_i \geqslant (3 \lambda_f / 4) tr_i\). Summing over the fine rows in the \(2 B\) neighbourhood of the seeds therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_i D_i \geqslant \frac{3 \lambda_f}{4} t \sum_{i \in \mathcal{S}} r_i + \frac{a_f}{128} t^2 |\mathcal{F} \setminus \mathcal{S}| .\) (8.74)

The second component is nonnegative on retained rows, since there it is \(\|u_i - v'_i \|^2 / 2\). It is not necessarily nonnegative on discarded rows. We count these rows on the original fine lattice. A discarded row at \(i\) has a witness \(j\) with

\(\displaystyle t \exp \{d (i, j) / R\} < r_j .\)

The finite-range locality conditions ensure that each such witness belongs to \(\mathcal{S}\). For \(y \geqslant 1\), the number of fine plaquette addresses within distance \(R \log y\) of a point is at most

\(\displaystyle C_R y, \qquad C_R = (8 R + 1)^4 \# \{ \text{plaquette axes} \} .\)

Indeed \(1 + (\log y) / 4 \leqslant y^{1 / 4}\) implies \(2 R \log y + 1 \leqslant (8 R + 1) y^{1 / 4}\), and the lattice ball has at most \((2 \lfloor R \log y \rfloor + 1)^4\) sites. A union bound over witnesses therefore gives, for the discarded set \(\mathcal{D}\),

\(\displaystyle t |\mathcal{D}| \leqslant C_R \sum_{j \in \mathcal{S}} r_j .\) (8.75)

There is no factor \(L^4\) in this count.

For the shortened path field \(u_i\), use the nonnegative finite-range coefficient matrix \(k_{ij}\), whose row and column sums are at most \(C\) and which satisfies \(\|u_i \| \leqslant \sum_j k_{ij} r_j\). Locality and the sector conditions imply \(r_j \leqslant t\) for a contributing \(j\) outside \(\mathcal{S}\). Thus

\(\displaystyle \|u_i \| \leqslant Ct + \sum_{j \in \mathcal{S}} k_{ij} r_j, \qquad \sum_{i \in \mathcal{D}} \|u_i \| \leqslant C (C_R + 1) \sum_{j \in \mathcal{S}} r_j .\)

The second inequality uses (8.75) for the constant part and the column bound for the large part. Since \(H_i \geqslant - \epsilon t \|u_i \|\) on discarded rows, choosing \(L\) so large that \(\epsilon C (C_R + 1) \leqslant \lambda_f / 4\) and combining with (8.74) proves

\(\displaystyle F_A := \sum_i (D_i + H_i) \geqslant \frac{\lambda_f}{2} t \sum_{i \in \mathcal{S}} r_i + d_f t^2 |\mathcal{F} \setminus \mathcal{S}|, \qquad d_f = a_f / 128 > 0.\) (8.76)

The geometric row and column estimates and the guide bound used here are explicit inputs; the discarded-row argument shows precisely how they enter the energy estimate.

For predictor defects the argument is simpler. Let \(p > 0\) be the predictor penalty and \(z_i\) its bond chord. A predictor energy row is at least \((\sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} z_i -\|w_i \|)^2 / 2\), with \(\|w_i \| \leqslant Ct\). On each listed predictor defect \(i \in \mathcal{I}\), the sector condition (S2) of Definition 8.3 is \(z_i \geqslant Wt\). Choose \(W\) after \(L\) so that \(C \leqslant \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} W / 2\). The unlisted rows are nonnegative, and hence

\(\displaystyle I_A \geqslant \frac{p}{8} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{I}} z_i^2 .\) (8.77)

8.10.3The reserve for very large defects

Let \(T \geqslant t\) be a larger threshold and put

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{F}_T = \{i \in \mathcal{F}: r_i > T\}, \quad \mathcal{I}_T = \{i \in \mathcal{I}: z_i > T\}, \quad d_A = |\mathcal{F}_T | + |\mathcal{I}_T | .\)

The use of \(T\) here does not change the seed set. Let \(m \geqslant 0\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle m \leqslant \lambda_f / 8, \qquad m \leqslant d_f, \qquad m \leqslant p / 32.\)

If \(c_F \leqslant \lambda_f / 4\), then (8.76) implies

\(\displaystyle F_A \geqslant mt^2 |\mathcal{F} | + mtT| \mathcal{F}_T | + c_F t \sum_{i \in \mathcal{S}} r_i .\) (8.78)

To check this, both \(t |\mathcal{S}|\) and \(T |\mathcal{F}_T |\) are at most \(\sum_{\mathcal{S}} r_i\). Their two charges and the \(c_F\) charge use at most \((2 m + c_F) t \sum_{\mathcal{S}} r_i \leqslant (\lambda_f / 2) t \sum_{\mathcal{S}} r_i\). The remaining fine seeds are paid by \(d_f t^2\).

Similarly suppose \(W \geqslant 1\) and \(C_1 \leqslant pW / 16\). Then (8.77) implies

\(\displaystyle I_A \geqslant mt^2 |\mathcal{I} | + mtT| \mathcal{I}_T | + C_1 t \sum_{i \in \mathcal{I}} z_i .\) (8.79)

Indeed \(z_i \geqslant Wt \geqslant t\) on every listed bond, so \(t^2 |\mathcal{I}| \leqslant \sum z_i^2\) and \(tT |\mathcal{I}_T | \leqslant \sum z_i^2\). Also \(C_1 tz_i \leqslant (p / 16) z_i^2\). The two first charges use at most \(2 m \sum z_i^2 \leqslant (p / 16) \sum z_i^2\), leaving the required linear charge within the total \(p / 8\).

8.10.4Coarse flags and the affected region

Let \(\mathcal{O}\) be the coarse plaquette rows in the \(2 B\) neighbourhood of all seeds, and let \(\mathcal{H} \subseteq \mathcal{O}\) be the rows affected by a very large defect. Split the coarse plaquettes into

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}_0 = \{r_i \leqslant s\}, \quad \mathcal{C}_1 = \{s < r_i \leqslant \delta\}, \quad \mathcal{C}_2 = \{r_i > \delta\}, \qquad i \in \mathcal{O}.\)

The geometry supplies a constant \(K_0\), fixed before \(L, W\), such that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}_2 \subseteq \mathcal{H}, \quad |\mathcal{C}_2 | \leqslant K_0 d_A, \quad n_g \leqslant 4 (|\mathcal{C}_1 | + K_0 d_A), \quad |\mathcal{O}| \leqslant K_0 B^4 n,\) (8.80)

where \(n_g\) is the number of coarse flag seeds and \(n = |\mathcal{F}| + |\mathcal{I}| + n_g\) is the total number of seeds. These are the precise geometric counting inputs used below. For \(t \leqslant 2 s\) and \(m \leqslant a_c / 64\), the middle bound in (8.73) gives at least \(4 mt^2\) on each row of \(\mathcal{C}_1\). Summing all three ranges yields

\(\displaystyle C_A \geqslant 4 mt^2 |\mathcal{C}_1 | - \lambda_c s \sum_{i \in \mathcal{C}_2} r_i - B_0 s^4 |\mathcal{O}| .\) (8.81)

The remaining two comparison rows, denoted together by \(L_A\), are split over \(\mathcal{H}\) and its complement. Their local estimates have the form

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} L_{\mathcal{H}} & \geqslant \lambda_c s \sum_{i \in \mathcal{C}_2} r_i - \frac{C_1}{L^2} t \sum_{i \in \mathcal{S}} r_i - C_1 t \sum_{i \in \mathcal{I}} z_i - Zt^2 B^4 d_A, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.82)} \\ L_{\mathcal{O} \setminus \mathcal{H}} & \geqslant - \eta t^2 n. \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.83)} \end{array}\)

The constant \(C_1\) is fixed before \(L, W\), whereas \(Z\) may depend on both. The second estimate is available for every fixed \(\eta > 0\) by taking \(x\) sufficiently large after the other parameters. The row estimates producing these inequalities remain inputs here. The positive normalization row, denoted \(N_A\), satisfies \(N_A \geqslant 0\). The total assigned energy is exactly \(F_A + I_A + C_A + N_A + L_{\mathcal{H}} + L_{\mathcal{O} \setminus \mathcal{H}}\).

Choose \(L\) large enough for the fine estimate and \(C_1 / L^2 \leqslant \lambda_f / 4\), and use (8.78) with \(c_F = C_1 / L^2\); this is the coefficient of the fine linear expense in (8.82). Choose \(W\) large enough for the predictor estimate, \(W \geqslant 1\) and \(C_1 \leqslant pW / 16\). Choose

\(\displaystyle 0 < m \leqslant \min \{\lambda_f / 8, d_f, p / 32, a_c / 64\}, \qquad \eta = m / 4.\)

Suppose finally that

\(\displaystyle (4 mK_0 + ZB^4) t^2 \leqslant mtT, \qquad B_0 s^4 K_0 B^4 \leqslant (m / 4) t^2 .\) (8.84)

Adding (8.78)–(8.83) cancels the three explicit linear expenses: the term in \(\mathcal{C}_2\) against its counterpart in (8.81), and the fine and predictor terms against the \(c_F\) and \(C_1\) charges of (8.78) and (8.79). The first inequality in (8.84) leaves \(4 mK_0 t^2 d_A\) from the very-large-defect reserve, and the second pays the small coarse rows. Thus the assigned energy is at least

\(\displaystyle mt^2 (|\mathcal{F}| + |\mathcal{I}|) + 4 mt^2 (|\mathcal{C}_1 | + K_0 d_A) - \tfrac{m}{4} t^2 n - \tfrac{m}{4} t^2 n \geqslant \tfrac{m}{2} t^2 n.\)

The last step is the flag count in (8.80). This is the required positive gain per seed, with \(\mu = m / 2\).

8.10.5The order of choices and orientation average

For the actual thresholds put

\(\displaystyle t = \frac{P (h)}{\sqrt{h}}, \qquad s = \frac{P (x)}{\sqrt{x}}, \qquad T = h^{- 49 / 100}, \qquad h = x + c_L .\)

As \(x \to \infty\) at fixed \(L\),

\(\displaystyle s \to 0, \quad t / s \to 1, \quad B^r \hspace{0.17em} t / T \to 0, \quad B^r s \to 0 \quad \text{for every fixed } r \geqslant 0.\)

For the ratio \(t / s\), subtract the logarithms: \(\log t = (\log \log h)^2 - \frac{1}{2} \log h\) and the corresponding formula at \(x\). Their difference tends to zero under a fixed shift, by the mean value theorem, since the derivative of \((\log \log x)^2 - \frac{1}{2} \log x\) tends to zero. The other statements follow from \(B\) and \(P\) growing slower than every positive power, using \(- 1 / 2 + 49 / 100 = - 1 / 100\) for \(t / T\). It follows that \(0 < s \leqslant \delta\), \(t \leqslant 2 s\), \(t \leqslant T\), and both inequalities (8.84) hold eventually. For the second, divide by \(t^2\) and use \(s / t \to 1\) and \(B^4 s^2 \to 0\). All choices have therefore been made in the order

\(\displaystyle \text{fixed group and row data} ; L \text{ large} ; W \text{ large} ; \mu > 0 ; x \text{ large},\)

with no dependence on the lattice period in the last threshold.

The packet construction uses an average of the assigned energies over signed coordinate transformations. Here the block side is odd, so the parent map commutes with the signed coordinate action. We use the patch's signed covariance laws for the predictor and the local likelihoods. Turn the tree, seed set and tag array together with the fields. Tree edges remain equal to the group identity; the predictor and likelihood transform covariantly; distances, chords and the number of seeds are preserved. The local sector conditions of Definition 8.3, including the strict positivity (S4) of the retained site likelihoods, therefore hold in every transformed channel. Here the incidence convention of that definition is used: a signed coordinate map sends the corners of a fine plaquette (the endpoints of a coarse bond) to the corners (endpoints) of its image and commutes with the parent map, so an address meets the transformed seed neighbourhood if and only if its preimage meets the original one. An anchor-based formulation would not be invariant, because a reflection changes the anchor of a positively oriented plaquette or bond. Applying the same energy bound there and averaging with normalized weights yields \(Q_A^{\text{core}} \geqslant \mu t^2 n\). This supplies the core input to (8.184) once the geometric, guide and comparison inputs specified in this section have been established. The average is of energies on transformed copies of the same data; there is no independent choice of trees or tags between channels.

8.11The local annular remainder

This section proves the annular estimate used in Subsection 8.25. The comparison is made before Gaussian integration, on the actual fine configuration, coarse configuration and predictor tags. In particular, removing a remote cutoff does not permit us to replace the predictor by a newly chosen one. The argument needs only the local conditions stated below.

Fix the block length \(L\), the predictor patch, the positive direct coefficients \(a_f, a_c\), and the predictor penalty \(p > 0\). Retain the exponent convention of Subsection 8.25, with large parameter \(h = h (x)\). Put \(t = P (h) / \sqrt{h}\); this is the fluctuation scale written \(\tau\) in (8.17) and in Subsection 8.25 (not the coordinate warning threshold called \(t\) there). Let \(s\) be the coarse threshold, and let \(B\) and \(R\) be the shell and truncation radii. We use \(s \leqslant 2 t\), \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\), and the eventual geometric inequality

\(\displaystyle 10000 (\rho + R_{\text{macro}} + L + 10) \leqslant B,\)

where \(\rho\) is the predictor reach. In addition, the coarse period \(c\) is assumed to satisfy

\(\displaystyle 100 (1 + B) \leqslant c.\)

This is not an eventual inequality: \(c\) is arbitrary, and the condition is a standing hypothesis on the period, part of the funded-step condition under which the packet estimates are applied. Both \(B\) and \(R\) have upper exponent zero, whereas \(e^{- \sigma R}\) has every negative upper exponent for fixed \(\sigma > 0\). These properties follow from \(B \asymp (\log x)^2\). All thresholds in this section are chosen after the fixed local data; all estimates are uniform over all periods \(c\) with \(100 (1 + B) \leqslant c\).

The incoming linear data are the path and kernel bounds of Subsection 8.5, the flat identities of (8.60), and the additional orthogonality

\(\displaystyle N_0 B_c d_c = 0.\) (8.85)

The last identity is the null-row part of the same flat decomposition. Its construction, like that of the flat identities, belongs to the flat-stack input. We will identify exactly where it is used.

8.11.1A common local chart

Call a coarse patch clean when no coarse flag occurs there. On a clean patch every plaquette chord is at most \(s\). The axial gauge argument in Subsection 8.7, applied on a box of radius \(d + 1\) with \(d \leqslant 32 B\), gives a gauge in which the bond chords on the smaller box are bounded by

\(\displaystyle u_0 = 16 \{2 (32 B + 1) + 1\}^5 N_{\text{plaq}} (2 t) .\)

Thus \(u_0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). For large \(x\) these bonds are in the logarithm chart. Take their logarithms and set all other bond logarithms to zero. This gives a full array \(b\) with \(\|b\|_{\infty} \leqslant Cu_0\) whose exponential agrees with the gauged coarse configuration on the required patch. No assertion about the coarse configuration outside that patch is needed.

Write \(V^{\uparrow}\) for the block replica of \(V\). Suppose, on a patch about \(o\), the fine configuration and a colour array \(u\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle U_e = \exp (u_e) V^{\uparrow}_e, \qquad \|u_e \| \leqslant w (x),\) (8.86)

where \(w \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Under the block-constant lift of the coarse gauge, \(u_e\) is conjugated at its initial point; its norm is unchanged. Since a replica bond is either the identity or one coarse bond, the chord triangle inequality gives

\(\displaystyle r (U_e) \leqslant Cw + Cu_0 .\)

Consequently both fields have small bonds in this same gauge, with a bound \(H (x) \geqslant w (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The coarse logarithm extension also has norm at most \(H\) after enlarging \(H\).

For any prescribed \(\delta > 0\), all predictor reads on a sufficiently small such patch lie in the minimizing branch and have chord at most \(\delta\). Here is the uniformity argument. At the identity configuration the normalized minimizing frames and the best connector are the identity. Their continuity was proved in Subsection 8.8. The branch inequalities are strict there. The finitely many frame tags, connector tags and axes therefore have a common neighbourhood on which these inequalities hold and the predictor chord is less than \(\delta\). In the product topology on cover configurations, this neighbourhood contains a cylinder restricting finitely many bonds. Enclose these bonds in one fixed patch and choose one positive chord radius for all of them. Every torus configuration whose lifted patch satisfies these restrictions belongs to the same neighbourhood, independently of its period. For large \(x\) the patch fits within \(B / 2\) and its bonds satisfy the restrictions. In particular, the normalized tag likelihoods are exactly one there: in the minimizing branch the frame is the selected minimizer, and each frame tag has probability the reciprocal of the total number of frame tags. The four-link triangle inequality also makes every touched fine plaquette chord at most \(\delta\). These statements hold for the original tags, including tags retained after remote masks were erased.

8.11.2Affine approximation at the actual coarse field

Let \(\mathcal{C} (U, V)\) be the row field consisting of the two fine curvature rows and the predictor row:

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C} (U, V) = \left( X_f (U), \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} F (VQ (U)^{- 1}) \right) .\)

Here \(X_f\) includes the truncated path row and the direct curvature row, and \(Q\) is the actual predictor. Each row norm is the physical colour norm at one address; an array norm is its supremum over addresses. No whole-volume Euclidean norm is used in these estimates. Its full linearization is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}^0 (a, b) = \left( B_f d_f a, \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} (b -\mathcal{Q}a) \right) .\)

The chart estimates of Subsection 8.9 give, for \(\|a\|, \|b\| \leqslant H\),

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{C}^0 (a, b)\|_{\infty} \leqslant CH, \qquad \|\mathcal{C}(e^a, e^b) -\mathcal{C}^0 (a, b)\|_{\infty} \leqslant CH \{(1 + R) H + e^{- \sigma R} \} .\) (8.87)

The predictor term has a quadratic error; the path row has the displayed additional truncation error. The right side has upper exponent \(- 1\).

Define \(A_{\infty} a =\mathcal{C}^0 (a, 0)\), and let \(A_R\) be its truncated version. Absolute exponential summability of the path coefficients and the four-bond curl estimate give

\(\displaystyle \|A_{\infty} a\|_{\infty} + \|A_R a\|_{\infty} \leqslant C \|a\|_{\infty}, \qquad \|(A_{\infty} - A_R) a\|_{\infty} \leqslant C \|a\|_{\infty} e^{- \sigma R} .\) (8.88)

Indeed, each face curl has norm at most \(4 \|a\|_{\infty}\). For a discarded path of length \(\ell > R\), insert \(e^{- \sigma R} e^{\sigma \ell} \geqslant 1\) into its absolute coefficient bound and sum. The predictor part is local and is not truncated.

Let \(A_R^V\) be the actual transported row: input colours are parallel transported along the prescribed coarse routes before the same cut linear row is applied. If the coarse bonds along these routes have chord at most \(h_0\), and the routes have length at most \(d\), then

\(\displaystyle \|(A_R^V - A_R) v\| \leqslant 2 CD_{\text{col}} \hspace{0.17em} dh_0 \hspace{0.17em} \|v\|_{\infty} .\) (8.89)

The route product has chord at most \(dh_0\) by the triangle inequality. The bound \(\| \text{Ad} (g) - 1\| \leqslant 2 D_{\text{col}} r (g)\) then bounds each changed input, and (8.88) bounds the resulting row. Only inputs within distance \(R + \rho + 4\) occur; \(4 (R + \rho + 4) \leqslant d\) ensures the routes remain in the controlled patch. One may set all other input differences to zero to apply the supremum-norm bound.

We claim that, under (8.86) on a clean patch of radius \(B / 2\), there are nonnegative functions \(M, E\), of upper exponents \(- 1 / 2\) and \(- 1\) respectively, such that at its central row

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{C}(U, V)\| \leqslant M, \qquad \|\mathcal{C}(U, V) -\mathcal{C}(V^{\uparrow}, V) - A_R^V u\| \leqslant E.\) (8.90)

To prove this, work in the common gauge above and restrict to the smaller radius \(d = \lfloor B / 4 \rfloor\). Extend \(u\) by zero outside this patch, obtaining \(v\) with \(\|v\|_{\infty} \leqslant H\). Let \(a\) be the linear replica of the coarse logarithm extension \(b\); then \(\|a\|_{\infty} \leqslant H\) and \(e^a = (e^b)^{\uparrow}\).

The local product logarithm satisfies

\(\displaystyle e^{w_e} = e^{v_e} e^{a_e}, \qquad \|w - (v + a)\|_{\infty} \leqslant JH^2, \qquad \|w\|_{\infty} \leqslant J' H.\)

For completeness, \((v, a) \mapsto \log (e^v e^a)\) is analytic near zero, vanishes at zero and has derivative \((v, a) \mapsto v + a\). Integrate its second derivative on a smaller closed ball to obtain the quadratic remainder. The first-derivative bound gives the last inequality, bond by bond, with constants independent of the number of bonds. Locality identifies the actual central rows with those at \((e^w, e^b)\) and \((e^a, e^b)\).

Put \(z =\mathcal{C}^0 (w, b)\) and \(z_0 =\mathcal{C}^0 (a, b)\). Linearity gives the exact decomposition

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \mathcal{C} (U, V) -\mathcal{C} (V^{\uparrow}, V) - A_R^V u = & \mathcal{C} (U, V) - z + A_{\infty} (w - v - a)\\ & + z_0 -\mathcal{C} (V^{\uparrow}, V) + (A_{\infty} - A_R) v + (A_R - A_R^V) v. \end{array}\)

The equality uses locality to replace \(u\) by \(v\) in the transported row. Equations (8.87)– (8.89) bound these five terms by

\(\displaystyle 2 E_0 + CJH^2 + CHe^{- \sigma R} + C' BH^2,\)

where \(E_0\) has upper exponent \(- 1\). This proves the error bound in (8.90); the bound on the row itself follows from its linear bound plus \(E_0\). Gauge covariance of all three actual rows and invariance of the colour norm return the estimate to the original gauge.

8.11.3Subtracting the selected squares

Let \(g\) be the coarse guide row and assume the already established guide bound \(\|g\| \leqslant At\). Define the actual and affine residuals

\(\displaystyle v =\mathcal{C} (U, V) - g, \qquad v_0 =\mathcal{C} (V^{\uparrow}, V) - g + A_R^V u.\)

They satisfy \(\|v\| \leqslant m = M + At\) and \(\|v - v_0 \| \leqslant E\). The elementary identity for a difference of squares implies

\(\displaystyle \left| \frac{\|v\|^2}{2} - \frac{\|v_0 \|^2}{2} \right| \leqslant Z := \frac{E (2 m + E)}{2} .\) (8.91)

Indeed \(|\|v\|-\|v_0 \|| \leqslant E\) and \(\|v_0 \| \leqslant m + E\). Hence \(Z\) has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\).

Write \(\text{flaw} (q) = (r (q)^2 -\|F (q)\|^2) / 2\). The curvature argument in Subsection 8.10 gives

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant \text{flaw} (q) \leqslant Kr (q)^4 / 2, \qquad \|F (q)\|^2 \geqslant 3 r (q)^2 / 4\)

for small \(r (q)\). The branch and plaquette bounds above put the required arguments in this neighbourhood. Since the appropriate direct component of \(\mathcal{C}\) has norm at most \(M\),

\(\displaystyle r (q_f) \leqslant 2 M / \sqrt{a_f}, \qquad r (q_I) \leqslant 2 M / \sqrt{p} .\)

Thus their flaw contributions are bounded by

\(\displaystyle X = a_f (K / 2) (2 M / \sqrt{a_f})^4, \qquad Y = p (K / 2) (2 M / \sqrt{p})^4,\)

both of upper exponent \(- 2\).

On a kept fine row below its threshold the fine energy is exactly

\(\displaystyle a_f \text{flaw} (q_f) + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{j = 0}^1 \|v_{f, j} \|^2 .\)

Expand the squares to verify this: their cross terms are the guide pairings, and their direct curvature square together with \(a_f \text{flaw}\) restores \(a_f r (q_f)^2 / 2\). The predictor energy has, without a kept condition, the exact form

\(\displaystyle p \text{flaw} (q_I) + \|v_I \|^2 / 2.\)

It follows from (8.91) that subtracting the two affine fine squares costs at most \(X + 2 Z\) in absolute value, and subtracting the predictor square costs at most \(Y + Z\). One common nonnegative bound is \(X + Y + 2 Z\), of upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). At an unselected fine or predictor address no square is subtracted. Its corresponding displayed energy is nonnegative. This gives the required one-sided bound there, without asserting an absolute bound on an unselected row.

8.11.4The remaining coarse rows

Three further terms occur in the assigned energy. First, on a coarse plaquette with \(r \leqslant s\), the coarse counterterm is

\(\displaystyle \frac{a_c}{2} \{\|F (q)\|^2 - r (q)^2 \} .\)

It is nonpositive and its absolute value is at most \(a_c Ks^4 / 2\), of upper exponent \(- 2\).

Second, consider a guide kernel \(K (V)\) at one central row. On the clean patch its clipped coarse field agrees with the ordinary truncated curvature field at every address touched by this row: the curvature is below threshold, and the exponential kept test is automatic outside the smaller radius where it needs checking. Kernel locality and the local agreement \(V = e^b\) then give the exact central-row equality

\(\displaystyle [K (V) \text{vision} (V)]_i = [K (e^b) X_c (e^b)]_i .\)

This equality is taken before any supremum norm is used. Add and subtract \(K (e^b) X_c^0 (b)\) to obtain

\(\displaystyle | [K (V) \text{vision} (V) - K_0 X_c^0 (b)]_i | \leqslant C \|X_c (e^b) - X_c^0 (b)\|_{\infty} + C \omega (R, H)\|X_c^0 (b)\|_{\infty},\)

where \(\omega (R, H) = (1 + R) H + e^{- \sigma R}\). The row bounds in (8.65) justify this inequality. The globally small extension \(b\) permits both supremum estimates on the right; no global bound on the original \(V\) is required. The right side has upper exponent \(- 1\). For the null row \(K = N\), its full linear comparator vanishes by (8.85). The null energy, half the square of this row, consequently has upper exponent \(- 2\). This is the only extra flat identity used here.

Finally, the linear and quadratic mismatch rows are precisely the gaps estimated in (8.67). The common local gauge and locality argument used there apply to the present grafted configuration, with smallness bound \(H\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Pairing the linear gap with a guide of size \(At\) gives at most \(At \hspace{0.17em} CH \omega (R, H)\) for each of the two fine-row channels. Pairing the quadratic gap with that guide gives at most \(At \hspace{0.17em} CAt \hspace{0.17em} \omega (R, H) / 2\) per channel. Hence their combined absolute cost is at most

\(\displaystyle 2 At \hspace{0.17em} CH \omega (R, H) + (At)^2 C \omega (R, H),\)

of upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). Adding all row costs gives one nonnegative function \(r (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\).

8.11.5The protected annulus of an actual packet

We verify the local hypotheses on the packet itself. Its fine configuration is formed in a fixed tree slice. On active free bonds it is \(\exp (u_e) V^{\uparrow}_e\); on frozen free bonds it is the fixed slice value \(Y_e\); on tree bonds it is the identity. Let \(K > 0\) be a fixed shell constant and define

\(\displaystyle \ell_K (q) = \chi ((r (q) / (Kt))^2) \log q,\)

where the smooth cutoff equals one for \(r (q) \leqslant Kt\) and vanishes for \(r (q) \geqslant 2 Kt\). The local logarithm estimate gives \(\| \ell_K (q)\| \leqslant 2 \gamma Kt\) everywhere. This assertion also holds outside the chart because the cutoff is zero there; choose \(x\) so that the closed ball \(r (q) \leqslant 2 Kt\) lies in the logarithm chart. The resulting map is smooth wherever its group argument varies smoothly, by the same two local cases.

Define the actual colour signal \(\tilde{u}\) to be \(u\) on active bonds, \(\ell_K (Y_e (V^{\uparrow}_e)^{- 1})\) on frozen free bonds, and zero on tree bonds. On a weak interior patch the fine plaquettes have chord at most \(t / 2\), the predictor mismatch at most \(2 Wt\), and the selected frames have positive likelihood. The tree comparison (8.54) then gives

\(\displaystyle r (U_e (V^{\uparrow}_e)^{- 1}) \leqslant Kt, \qquad K = C \hspace{0.17em} N_{\text{macro}} / 2 + 2 W,\)

where \(N_{\text{macro}}\) counts the fine plaquettes in the fixed macro patch. The frozen cutoff is therefore one and its logarithm exponentiates to the original mismatch. On tree bonds both \(U_e\) and \(V^{\uparrow}_e\) are the identity. Thus on this patch

\(\displaystyle U_e = \exp (\tilde{u}_e) V^{\uparrow}_e .\)

If the active variables there obey \(\|u_e \| \leqslant w (x)\) and \(2 \gamma Kt \leqslant w (x)\), the signal obeys the same bound on all bonds. This proves (8.86) for the actual signal.

Let \(A\) be the packet's seed set, and consider an energy address whose coarse anchor belongs to its \(200 B\) neighbourhood but not its \(2 B\) neighbourhood. Retain the weak fine, predictor and coarse flag conditions through \(300 B\), and the bounds on active variables through \(304 B\). For a bond within \(B\) of this anchor, its \(B / 4\) interior patch lies within the \(300 B\) neighbourhood and outside the seeds. This follows from the triangle inequality: the outer distance is at most \(201 B + B / 4 < 300 B\), whereas entering the seeds would put the anchor within \(B + B / 4 < 2 B\) of them. The retained conditions therefore give the weak interior just used. The active-variable condition is available since \(201 B < 304 B\). The same argument supplies a clean coarse patch at the anchor.

Signed changes of orientation preserve these conclusions, allowing one additional bond of radius for reversed bonds. Norms are unchanged by the associated conjugations. Finally, the fine kept test holds on this annulus: a plaquette whose test is nontrivial has distance at most \(B / 25\) by the exponential threshold bound; the weak interior makes its chord at most \(t / 2 \leqslant te^{\text{dist} / r_0}\). At all other plaquettes the right side is at least the universal chord bound \(2\). Thus the actual fine energy equals the square expression used above, including at threshold equality.

At every selected address the extracted affine square is the square of the actual signal row with the same tags, orientations and coarse field. At every unselected address it is zero. We have therefore proved, on the protected annulus,

\(\displaystyle \text{actual assigned energy at the address} \geqslant - r (x) .\) (8.92)

Here \(r \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). Summing over its at most \(CB^4\) addresses per seed and averaging over the signed channels gives exactly the annular input of (8.184). This proves the value estimate; derivative bounds for the density, covariance and chart factors remain separate inputs to the packet jet estimate.

8.12The Gaussian quadratic form in a sector

The quadratic reference must remain nondegenerate after the large-field bonds have been frozen. A lower bound for a flat Hessian alone would not establish this. We prove the lower bound for the transported-flat selected rows \(A^V_{s, a}\) defined below (not for the curved derivative rows, which are treated in Subsection 8.16), using a separate local gauge around each active bond. All norms in the quadratic inequalities below are physical counting norms: squared colour norms are summed over bonds without dividing by the volume.

Fix the tree, seed set, coarse field and original predictor tags of Subsection 8.11. Let \(\mathcal{H}\) be the active free bonds and let \(P_{\mathcal{H}}\) denote restriction to them, extended by zero on all other bonds. The seed store's coarse-flag component equals the actual coarse flags; it may have other seed components. An active bond has both coarse endpoints outside the \(10 B\) seed neighbourhood. A row is selected when its anchor is outside the \(6 B\) seed neighbourhood. These are fixed selections for this quadratic form.

Let \(q\) be the number of signed channels and write \(\lambda = q^{- 1 / 2}\). For a channel \(s\) and a fine or predictor row \(a\), set

\(\displaystyle D_{s, a} u = \boldsymbol{1}_{\{(s, a) \text{selected} \}} A^V_{s, a} P_{\mathcal{H}} u.\)

The map \(A^V_{s, a}\) is the flat linear cut row transported using \(V\), including the signed displacement of input colours. It is not, in general, the derivative at the replica of \(V\) of the nonlinear curvature or predictor. In orthonormal colour coordinates, define \(M\) by

\(\displaystyle (Mu)_{s, a} = \lambda D_{s, a} u.\)

Here and below a colour array and its scalar coordinate vector are identified. Define the completed precision matrix

\(\displaystyle A = M^{\ast} M + P_{\mathcal{H}^c} .\) (8.93)

Every column of \(M\) indexed by an inactive bond is zero. Consequently \(A\) acts as the identity on the inactive coordinates and has no cross terms between them and the active coordinates. The completion supplies no energy on the active subspace.

If \(b\) is the vector of selected affine rows at zero fluctuation, including the same factor \(\lambda\), linearity of the transported rows gives

\(\displaystyle Q_{\text{quad}} (u) = \tfrac{1}{2} \|b + Mu\|_2^2 .\)

This is exactly the orientation average of the extracted squares: \(\lambda^2 = 1 / q\) supplies its normalization. In particular,

\(\displaystyle Q_{\text{quad}} (v + u) - Q_{\text{quad}} (v) = \langle u, M^{\ast} b + M^{\ast} Mv \rangle + \tfrac{1}{2} \langle u, M^{\ast} Mu \rangle .\)

The same fine field, tags and orientation choices are used in every term. We have not averaged independently chosen Gaussian matrices.

8.12.1A local linear inequality from the tree comparison

Let \(w\) be a fine colour array which vanishes on the fixed tree. The tree comparison of Subsection 8.7, with coarse configuration equal to the identity, implies

\(\displaystyle \|w_e \| \leqslant K \sum_{z \in \mathcal{B}} \sum_{\alpha} \|(d_f w)_{\bar{z} + Lo, \alpha} \| + \|(\mathcal{Q}w)_{o, a} \|, \qquad o = \text{parent} (e),\) (8.94)

where \(a\) is the axis of \(e\), \(\mathcal{B}\) is the fixed macro box, and \(\mathcal{Q}\) is the derivative of the actual predictor. The box sum may count periodic images more than once; this only enlarges its nonnegative right side.

Here is the passage to the linear inequality. Apply the nonlinear comparison to \(U_e (\varepsilon) = \exp (\varepsilon w_e)\). It remains in the same tree slice since \(w\) vanishes on the tree. For sufficiently small \(\varepsilon\) all required frame likelihoods are positive: at zero every frame is in the minimizing branch, and the finitely many conditions are open. Divide the comparison by \(| \varepsilon |\) and let \(\varepsilon \to 0\) through nonzero real values. If \(g (0) = 1\) and its matrix derivative is the colour matrix of \(v\), then

\(\displaystyle \lim_{\varepsilon \to 0} \frac{r (g (\varepsilon))}{| \varepsilon |} = \|v\| .\)

This follows directly from differentiability and the physical normalization of the chord. The derivatives of the fine links, plaquettes and predictor are respectively \(w\), \(d_f w\) and \(\mathcal{Q}w\), the last by Subsection 8.8. Passing through the finite box sum proves (8.94). The limiting neighbourhood can depend on the finite torus; the resulting constant \(K\), inherited from the nonlinear tree inequality, does not.

8.12.2A lower bound for the transported selected rows

For an anchor \(o\), define

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} J_o (u)^2 & = \sum_{\text{dist} (o, \text{parent} (e)) \leqslant 3 B} \|(P_{\mathcal{H}} u)_e \|^2,\\ R_o (u)^2 & = \sum_{\text{dist} (o, \text{anchor} (s, a)) \leqslant R_{\text{macro}} + 2} \|D_{s, a} u\|^2 . \end{aligned}\)

The square roots are nonnegative. An active bond at \(o\) is more than \(10 B\) from the seeds. Since the macro radius is much smaller than \(B\), every row with anchor within \(R_{\text{macro}} + 2\) of \(o\) is selected in the identity signed channel. Also the coarse patch of radius \(2 B + 1\) is clean, because the coarse flags belong to the seeds. The local axial construction gives a gauge \(g\) with

\(\displaystyle r ((g \cdot V)_e) \leqslant h_0 (x) \quad \text{on radius } 2 B,\)

where \(h_0 \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\).

Apply (8.94) to \(w = \text{Ad} (g) P_{\mathcal{H}} u\). This array still vanishes on the tree. For every required row, the transport estimate (8.89) and gauge covariance give

\(\displaystyle \|(A_R w)_a \| \leqslant R_o (u) + \varepsilon (x) J_o (u), \qquad \varepsilon (x) = C \hspace{0.17em} 2 D_{\text{col}} (B + 1) h_0 (x) .\) (8.95)

Indeed the transported row in the chosen gauge has the same norm as its selected original row and is at most \(R_o (u)\). Every input used in the comparison lies within \(3 B\) of \(o\), so its norm is at most \(J_o (u)\). All comparison routes lie inside the radius \(2 B\) patch. The identity signed channel suffices for this lower bound; the sum defining \(R_o\) may include all the other channels.

The direct fine component of \(A_R w\) is \(\sqrt{a_f} \hspace{0.17em} d_f w\), and its predictor component is \(- \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{Q}w\). Writing \(N_{\text{macro}} = |\mathcal{B}| N_{\text{plaq}}\), insert (8.95) into (8.94). We obtain

\(\displaystyle \|(P_{\mathcal{H}} u)_e \| \leqslant ZR_o (u) + E (x) J_o (u), \quad Z = KN_{\text{macro}} / \sqrt{a_f} + 1 / \sqrt{p}, \quad E = Z \varepsilon .\) (8.96)

This also holds on inactive bonds because its left side is zero. \(Z\) is fixed after \(L\); \(E\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\).

There are \(L^4 N_{\text{axis}}\) fine positive bonds per coarse site. Let

\(\displaystyle S_L (d) = (2 d + 1)^4 L^4 N_{\text{axis}} .\)

For every nonnegative family \(f_a\) anchored on coarse sites, changing the order of the two finite sums shows

\(\displaystyle \sum_e \sum_{\text{dist} (\text{parent} (e), \text{anchor} (a)) \leqslant d} f_a \leqslant S_L (d) \sum_a f_a .\)

For fixed \(a\), at most \((2 d + 1)^4\) coarse sites can occur, each with at most \(L^4 N_{\text{axis}}\) bonds. This remains valid when the neighbourhood wraps around the torus: wrapping reduces the number of distinct sites.

Square (8.96), using \((r + s)^2 \leqslant 2 r^2 + 2 s^2\), and sum over bonds. The counting bound gives

\(\displaystyle \|P_{\mathcal{H}} u\|_2^2 \leqslant 2 Z^2 S_L (R_{\text{macro}} + 2) \sum_{s, a} \|D_{s, a} u\|^2 + 2 E (x)^2 S_L (3 B) \|P_{\mathcal{H}} u\|_2^2 .\)

The last coefficient has upper exponent \(- 1\) and is eventually at most \(1 / 2\). Move it to the left to get

\(\displaystyle \|P_{\mathcal{H}} u\|_2^2 \leqslant a_{\ast} \sum_{s, a} \|D_{s, a} u\|^2, \qquad a_{\ast} = 1 + 4 Z^2 S_L (R_{\text{macro}} + 2) .\)

The added one ensures a strictly positive constant in every case. Since \(\|Mu\|_2^2 = \lambda^2 \sum \|D_{s, a} u\|^2\), the orthogonal active/inactive decomposition proves

\(\displaystyle \langle u, Au \rangle \geqslant \kappa \|u\|_2^2, \qquad \kappa = \min \{\lambda^2 / a_{\ast}, 1\} > 0.\) (8.97)

This includes an empty active set, in which case \(A = 1\). No globally small gauge for \(V\) has been constructed or assumed.

8.12.3Upper bounds with spatial weights

We also need a bound independent of the period on the matrix entries and their weighted sums. The cut row \(A_R\) vanishes on an impulse at a bond farther than \(R + \rho + 4\) from its anchor. Together with (8.88), this implies

\(\displaystyle e^{\sigma d} \|(A_R \delta_e v)_a \| \leqslant 2 Ce^{\sigma (\rho + 6)} \|v\|, \qquad d = \text{dist} (\text{anchor} (a), \text{parent} (e)) .\)

To see this, if \(d \leqslant \rho + 5\) use the uniform cut-row bound. Otherwise take \(r = d - (\rho + 5)\), so that \(A_r \delta_e v = 0\). If \(R < r\) locality gives zero as well. For \(R \geqslant r\), compare both \(A_R\) and \(A_r\) with \(A_{\infty}\); the two tails are at most \(C \|v\| e^{- \sigma r}\) each. This proves the stated bound.

Signed displacement and parallel transport send a single impulse to a single impulse with unchanged colour norm. The coarse anchor may move by one under reversal of a bond. Thus the preceding constant becomes \(2 Ce^{\sigma (\rho + 7)}\) for the transported oriented row. Selection and restriction to active bonds only set entries to zero. In scalar coordinates,

\(\displaystyle e^{\sigma \text{dist} (i, j)} |M_{ij} | \leqslant b_{\ast} = \lambda \hspace{0.17em} 2 Ce^{\sigma (\rho + 7)} .\)

The anchors of input coordinates have multiplicity at most \(m = L^4 N_{\text{axis}} q_{\text{col}}\), where \(q_{\text{col}}\) is the colour dimension. Output-coordinate anchors have multiplicity at most

\(\displaystyle n = q \hspace{0.17em} (2 L^4 N_{\text{plaq}} + N_{\text{axis}}) q_{\text{col}} .\)
