For \(\eta > 0\) put \(P_0 (\eta) = \sum_{k \geqslant 0} (2 k + 1)^4 e^{- \eta k} < \infty\). Grouping coordinates by their distance from an anchor gives, for \(0 \leqslant r \leqslant \sigma / 2\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_i \sum_j e^{r \text{dist} (i, j)} |M_{ij} | \leqslant b_{\ast} mP_0 (\sigma / 2), \qquad \sup_j \sum_i e^{r \text{dist} (i, j)} |M_{ij} | \leqslant b_{\ast} nP_0 (\sigma / 2) .\)

Choose one constant \(C_{\ast}\) larger than these two bounds and one. The triangle inequality for anchor distances proves the product rule for such weighted row and column sums. Therefore

\(\displaystyle \sup_i \sum_j e^{r \text{dist} (i, j)} |A_{ij} | \leqslant C_{\ast}^2 + 1.\) (8.98)

The extra one is the inactive diagonal. Symmetry gives the same column bound. The usual finite Cauchy–Schwarz argument gives \(\|A\|_{2 \to 2} \leqslant C_{\ast}^2 + 1\): bound \(| (Au)_i |^2\) by the absolute row sum times \(\sum_j |A_{ij} | |u_j |^2\) and then sum over \(i\). Also \(M_{ij} = 0\) beyond distance \(B / 4\) by its stencil, so \(A_{ij} = 0\) beyond \(B / 2\).

Combining (8.97) and (8.98) proves a strictly positive, uniform spectral interval for the transported-flat completed precision matrix. In particular its inverse exists and satisfies \(0 < A^{- 1} \leqslant \kappa^{- 1} 1\). For \(\beta > 0\), the centered finite-dimensional Gaussian with covariance \(\beta^{- 1} A^{- 1}\) is consequently well defined, including when there are no active coordinates. Restricting to the active subspace gives the physical Gaussian; the inactive identity coordinates are an algebraic completion. Spatial locality of the inverse, comparison of local Gaussian normalizations, and control of the affine mean require the additional estimates developed at their respective steps.

8.13Local inverses and Gaussian normalization

For comparison, Demko, Moss and Smith [DMS84, Theorem 2.4] prove exponential inverse decay for band matrices from spectral bounds. A fixed bandwidth in a linear ordering is unavailable here. We use the distance between anchors and exponential row and column sums instead.

The Gaussian covariance used in the expansion is assembled from local principal inverses. It is not the full inverse of the precision matrix. We prove its positivity, estimate the resulting error, and express the normalization through local terms and convergent correction series. This follows the real interpolation from the identity used in the construction.

Let \(I\) be a finite coordinate set, with anchors \(a_i\) on a coarse four-dimensional torus. Assume at most \(m\) coordinates have any given anchor. For a real matrix \(T\) define

\(\displaystyle \|T\|_{\eta} = \max \left\{ \sup_i \sum_j e^{\eta \text{dist} (a_i, a_j)} |T_{ij} |, \sup_j \sum_i e^{\eta \text{dist} (a_i, a_j)} |T_{ij} | \right\} .\)

On an empty coordinate set both suprema are zero. The triangle inequality for distance and an interchange of finite sums give \(\|ST\|_{\eta} \leqslant \|S\|_{\eta} \|T\|_{\eta}\) for \(\eta \geqslant 0\). Transposition preserves this norm, and decreasing \(\eta\) cannot increase it. It dominates the Euclidean operator norm by the row-and-column Cauchy–Schwarz estimate used above.

Let \(A = A^{\ast}\) and suppose

\(\displaystyle A \geqslant r 1, \qquad \|A\|_{\eta} \leqslant Q, \qquad 0 < r \leqslant 1, \quad Q \geqslant 1, \quad \eta > 0.\) (8.99)

Subsection 8.12 supplies these constants for the actual completed sector precision, after fixing \(L\) and then taking the large parameter sufficiently large. Every principal submatrix has the same lower bound and weighted estimate: extend a test vector by zero for the first assertion, and restrict the nonnegative row and column sums for the second.

8.13.1Exponential decay of the inverse

The qualitative exponential decay of the inverse is a special case of Jaffard's inverse-decay theorem [Jaf90, Proposition 2, p. 464]. His hypothesis requires exponential distance sums to be uniformly finite. For the finite coordinate set here, put \(d' (i, j) = \text{dist} (a_i, a_j) +\boldsymbol{1}_{\{i \ne j\}}\). This is a metric, and bounded anchor multiplicity gives

\(\displaystyle \sup_i \sum_j e^{- \varepsilon d' (i, j)} \leqslant m \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (2 n + 1)^4 e^{- \varepsilon n} < \infty \qquad (\varepsilon > 0) .\)

Moreover (8.99) gives \(|A_{ij} | \leqslant Qe^{\eta} e^{- \eta d' (i, j)}\) and makes \(A\) invertible. We prove the explicit estimate below because we need constants uniform in the period and in all principal windows; the empty case is immediate. Set

\(\displaystyle \theta = \min \{1, r / (2 Q)\}, \qquad \gamma = \theta \eta, \qquad c_0 = \min \{1, r / 2\} .\)

For every \(u \geqslant 0\), the matrix \(A + u 1\) is invertible and

\(\displaystyle e^{\gamma \text{dist} (a_i, a_j)} | (A + u 1)^{- 1}_{ij} | \leqslant \{c_0 (1 + u)\}^{- 1} .\) (8.100)

We give the weighted argument, which is uniform in the number of coordinates. Fix \(j\) and put \(s_i = e^{\gamma \text{dist} (a_i, a_j)}\). For \(0 \leqslant \theta \leqslant 1\) and \(|v| \leqslant \theta D\) with \(D \geqslant 0\), convexity of the exponential gives

\(\displaystyle |e^v - 1| \leqslant \theta e^D .\)

For positive \(v\), use \(e^{\theta D} \leqslant 1 + \theta (e^D - 1)\); for negative \(v\), use \(1 - e^v \leqslant e^{- v} - 1\). The reverse triangle inequality for distances now implies

\(\displaystyle |s_i / s_k - 1| \leqslant \theta e^{\eta \text{dist} (a_i, a_k)} .\)

Conjugate \(A + u 1\) by the positive diagonal matrix with entries \(s_i\). Its difference \(F\) from \(A + u 1\) has entries \((A + u 1)_{ik} (s_i / s_k - 1)\). The diagonal contribution is zero, so both absolute row and column sums of \(F\) are at most \(\theta Q \leqslant r / 2\). Thus \(| \langle w, Fw \rangle | \leqslant (r / 2) \|w\|_2^2\).

Let \(y = (A + u 1)^{- 1} e_j\) and \(w_i = s_i y_i\). The conjugated equation is \((A + u 1 + F) w = e_j\), since \(s_j = 1\). It follows that

\(\displaystyle (r / 2 + u) \|w\|_2^2 \leqslant w_j \leqslant \|w\|_2 .\)

If \(w \ne 0\), division gives \(\|w\|_2 \leqslant (r / 2 + u)^{- 1} \leqslant \{c_0 (1 + u)\}^{- 1}\); the case \(w = 0\) is immediate. Each \(|w_i |\) is bounded by this norm, proving (8.100). Positive definiteness gave invertibility before this computation. The same proof applies to every principal submatrix.

Put \(k = \gamma / 3\) and

\(\displaystyle S = c_0^{- 1} mP_0 (\gamma - 2 k), \qquad P_0 (t) = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (2 n + 1)^4 e^{- tn} .\)

Summing (8.100) at \(u = 0\) with weight \(e^{2 k \text{dist}}\) gives \(\|A^{- 1} \|_{2 k} \leqslant S\). The summation uses the anchor multiplicity \(m\) and the same distance-shell count as in Subsection 8.12.

8.13.2Principal windows and their error

For each coordinate \(j\), choose a window \(W_j\) containing every coordinate within distance \(B\) of \(a_j\) and no coordinate farther than \(B + 2\). Define \(P\) by its columns:

\(\displaystyle P_{ij} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (A_{W_j}^{- 1})_{ij}, & i \in W_j,\\ 0, & i \not\in W_j . \end{array} \right.\)

Indices inside a window mean the corresponding principal coordinates. All these inverses exist by (8.99). Applying (8.100) separately to each column yields the same pointwise exponential bound for \(P\), hence \(\|P\|_{2 k} \leqslant S\). This also controls column sums even though the windows vary with the column.

Let \(E = AP - 1\). If \(i \in W_j\), the principal inverse equation gives \(E_{ij} = 0\): the column \(P_{\cdot j}\) is zero outside \(W_j\), so the full product at \(i\) is exactly the principal product. In particular \(E_{ij} = 0\) when the anchor distance is at most \(B\). The product bound gives \(\|E\|_{2 k} \leqslant QS + 1\). On its remaining entries the weight can be reduced from \(2 k\) to \(k\) at cost \(e^{- kB}\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \|E\|_k \leqslant \epsilon_B := e^{- kB} (QS + 1), \qquad \|P - A^{- 1} \|_k \leqslant S \epsilon_B .\) (8.101)

The second inequality uses the exact identity \(P - A^{- 1} = A^{- 1} E\). Notice the order of the factors.

The physical covariance is the symmetric matrix

\(\displaystyle C = \tfrac{1}{2} (P + P^{\ast}) .\) (8.102)

Symmetry of \(A^{- 1}\) and (8.101) give

\(\displaystyle \|C\|_k \leqslant S, \qquad \|C - A^{- 1} \|_k \leqslant \delta_B := S \epsilon_B, \qquad \|AC - 1\|_k \leqslant Q \delta_B .\)

The upper bound \(A \leqslant Q 1\) implies \(A^{- 1} \geqslant Q^{- 1} 1\). It can also be checked directly: for \(w = A^{- 1} v\) and \(x = Q^{- 1} v\), positivity of \(\langle w - x, A (w - x) \rangle\) and \(\langle x, Ax \rangle \leqslant Q \|x\|^2\) give \(\langle v, A^{- 1} v \rangle \geqslant Q^{- 1} \|v\|^2\). Consequently, once \(\delta_B \leqslant 1 / (2 Q)\),

\(\displaystyle C \geqslant (2 Q)^{- 1} 1.\) (8.103)

The local activity bond windows are colour independent: a coordinate belongs to \(W_j\) when some coarse endpoint of its bond is within \(B\) of some endpoint of the bond of \(j\). Each endpoint is within one of its bond's parent anchor. Choosing the two anchors proves the inner bound; the triangle inequality proves the outer bound \(B + 2\). Thus complete colour blocks occur together, and \(C\) has range at most \(B + 2\).

A different panel is used for the global source-row estimates in Subsection 8.16. There membership requires every pair of coarse endpoints to be within an integer threshold \(R = \lfloor \text{radiusB} (x) / 20 \rfloor\). For \(R \geqslant 2\), it contains the anchor ball of radius \(R - 2\) and is contained in the anchor ball of radius \(R\): use the same endpoint-to-anchor bound for the first inclusion, and take the anchors themselves for the second. It too is colour independent. The conservative inner radius \(\lfloor \text{radiusB} (x) / 60 \rfloor\) used in the global estimates is valid on the stated sufficiently late tail. The two window prescriptions need not coincide. In either case, the closeness of the symmetrized window inverse to \(A^{- 1}\) is a conclusion of the preceding estimates, not an identification of the two matrices.

For the sector precision, inactive rows and columns of \(A\) are those of the identity. This property passes to every principal inverse, hence to \(P\) and \(C\). Also \(M^{\ast} b\) vanishes on inactive coordinates, so the chosen local mean \(- P^{\ast} M^{\ast} b\) vanishes there. These observations justify restricting the local Gaussian to the active coordinates without changing its physical entries.

8.13.3Ordered inverse series

If a matrix \(E\) satisfies \(\|E\|_k \leqslant \epsilon < 1\), then

\(\displaystyle (1 + E)^{- 1} = \sum_{n = 0}^{\infty} (- E)^n .\)

Indeed the partial geometric identity has remainder \((- E)^N\), whose norm tends to zero; the series converges absolutely in the finite matrix space. For \(E = AP - 1\) this gives

\(\displaystyle A^{- 1} = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} P (- E)^n .\) (8.104)

For \(D = AC - 1\) the corresponding identity is

\(\displaystyle C^{- 1} = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (- D)^n A, \qquad C^{- 1} - A = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (- D)^{n + 1} A.\) (8.105)

Multiply these formulas by \(A\) on the left and by \(C\) on the right, respectively, to verify them. No commutation of \(P\), \(C\) and \(A\) is asserted. Since \(B \asymp (\log x)^2\) and \(k > 0\) is fixed after \(L\), \(\epsilon_B\) and \(\delta_B\) have every negative upper exponent. Both convergence conditions can thus be met on one sufficiently late tail, uniformly over sectors and periods.

8.13.4Logarithms as local terms and correction series

For \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant 1\) put \(A_s = 1 + s (A - 1)\). The hypotheses (8.99) hold for \(A_s\) with the same \(r, Q, \eta\), since \(r \leqslant 1 \leqslant Q\). Let \(P_s\) be its window inverse and \(E_s = A_s P_s - 1\). All bounds above are uniform in \(s\). Define the local diagonal terms and their correction terms by

\(\displaystyle \ell_i = \int_0^1 [(A - 1) P_s]_{ii} \hspace{0.17em} ds, \qquad \ell_{i, n} = \int_0^1 [(A - 1) P_s (- E_s)^n]_{ii} \hspace{0.17em} ds.\)

Thus \(\ell_i = \ell_{i, 0}\). We have the exact identity

\(\displaystyle \log \det A = \sum_i \ell_i + \sum_i \sum_{n \geqslant 1} \ell_{i, n} .\) (8.106)

To justify it, the determinant's linear term at the identity is the trace, as follows by expanding the finite determinant by permutations. Factoring

\(\displaystyle A_{s + t} = (1 + t (A - 1) A_s^{- 1}) A_s\)

therefore gives \(\frac{d}{ds} \log \det A_s = \text{tr} ((A - 1) A_s^{- 1})\). All \(A_s\) are positive definite. Their inverses and principal inverses vary continuously, by the adjugate formula and the nonvanishing determinant. Integrate the derivative from zero to one and substitute (8.104). Its diagonal terms are bounded, uniformly in \(s\), by \((Q + 1) S \epsilon_B^n\). This summable geometric bound justifies termwise integration for each \(i\). The coordinate sum is finite. In particular,

\(\displaystyle | \ell_i | \leqslant (Q + 1) S, \qquad \sum_{n \geqslant 1} | \ell_{i, n} | \leqslant \frac{(Q + 1) S \epsilon_B}{1 - \epsilon_B} .\)

These are per-coordinate bounds. Summing them over the entire torus introduces its coordinate count; it does not yield a per-seed estimate without the further support assignment.

There is a second determinant correction for the covariance \(C\). With \(D = AC - 1\) and \(\|D\|_k < 1\), the path \(1 + sD\) is invertible for \(s \in [0, 1]\) by its Neumann series. Its real determinant starts at one and never vanishes, so it remains positive. The same determinant derivative and geometric domination give

\(\displaystyle \log \det A + \log \det C = \sum_i \sum_{n \geqslant 0} \int_0^1 [D (- sD)^n]_{ii} \hspace{0.17em} ds.\) (8.107)

Here \(1 + D = AC\) and both \(A, C\) have positive determinant, so multiplicativity of the determinant justifies the sum of logarithms. Although \(D\) need not be symmetric, no spectral logarithm of \(D\) is used. The integral of its \(n\)th term is \((- 1)^n (D^{n + 1})_{ii} / (n + 1)\), with absolute value at most \(\|D\|_k^{n + 1} / (n + 1)\).

8.13.5Local comparison of normalization terms

For a physical bond \(v\), group its colour coordinates and set \(J_v (A) = \sum_{i : \hspace{0.17em} i \text{ lies over } v} \ell_i\). The term \(\ell_i\) reads only the principal window \(W_i\):

\(\displaystyle [(A - 1) P_s]_{ii} = [(A_{W_i} - 1) (1 + s (A_{W_i} - 1))^{- 1}]_{ii} .\)

This follows because column \(i\) of \(P_s\) vanishes outside \(W_i\). Suppose \(A\) and \(Z\) both satisfy (8.99), use the same windows, and differ entrywise by at most \(e\) on \(W_i \times W_i\). The window has at most \(\Delta = m (2 B + 5)^4\) coordinates. Thus the unweighted row and column norms of \(A_{W_i} - Z_{W_i}\) are at most \(\Delta e\). The same bound holds for the difference of their interpolants. The inverse identity, with its order retained, is

\(\displaystyle (Z_s)_{W_i}^{- 1} - (A_s)_{W_i}^{- 1} = (Z_s)_{W_i}^{- 1} ((A_s)_{W_i} - (Z_s)_{W_i}) (A_s)_{W_i}^{- 1} .\)

Each inverse has row-and-column norm at most \(S\). Splitting the difference of the two local products into a difference of first factors and a difference of inverses bounds its norm by

\(\displaystyle \{S + (Q + 1) S^2 \} \Delta e.\)

Integration and the colour sum give

\(\displaystyle |J_v (A) - J_v (Z) | \leqslant q_{\text{col}} \{S + (Q + 1) S^2 \} \Delta e,\) (8.108)

provided the entrywise hypothesis holds on the window for each colour over \(v\).

Finally \(J_v\) is invariant under bondwise orthogonal changes of colour frame. The windows contain whole colour blocks, so such a change restricts to an orthogonal matrix on every window and commutes with the projection onto the colours over \(v\). Principal interpolants and their inverses are conjugated by this matrix. The sum of diagonal entries over \(v\) is the trace after that projection; cyclicity of the finite trace proves invariance before integration. This is why one compares the whole colour trace, rather than assigning independent gauge-invariant meanings to its individual diagonal terms.

8.14The Gaussian mean away from seeds

The local covariance is accompanied by a local mean. Its defining formula alone does not make the affine energy small: a uniform bound on the inverse would only preserve the size of the original source. The additional gain comes from the stationary linear response. We first prove its cancellation, and then compare it with the actual mean on one common clean neighbourhood.

8.14.1The stationary response cancels every linear row

Fix the step, forest and full flat kernels of Subsection 8.9. Write \(\mathcal{U}_F b\) for the fine stationary field in tree coordinates with coarse input \(b\). It is the tree-straightened installed linear response. Its relative field is

\(\displaystyle v_F (b) =\mathcal{U}_F b - \bar{b},\)

where \(\bar{b}\) is the linear coarse replica. On every forest bond this difference is zero. We use the actual diagonalization identity for this response: if \(K_f, K_c\) are the fine and coarse quadratic forms and \(\mathcal{Q}\) is the rooted linear predictor,

\(\displaystyle K_c =\mathcal{U}_F^{\ast} K_f \mathcal{U}_F + p (1 -\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{U}_F)^{\ast} (1 -\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{U}_F) .\) (8.109)

These are the full physical forms, including the prescribed root correction. This is an incoming flat construction identity; it does not follow merely from positivity of the forms.

Let \(b_s\) and \(a_s\) be the pullbacks, by one signed lattice symmetry, of \(b\) and \(\mathcal{U}_F b\). Set

\(\displaystyle w = B_c d_c b_s, \qquad u = B_f d_f a_s, \qquad z = \sqrt{p} \hspace{0.17em} (b_s -\mathcal{Q}a_s), \qquad U = F_0 w, \quad V = I_0 w.\)

All norms in this paragraph are counting Euclidean norms, summed over their physical sites and colours. The two curvature squares are the corresponding quadratic forms. Indeed expand each colour component in the curvature factorization \(K = d^{\ast} B^{\ast} Bd\); the predictor square is exactly the last term in (8.109). Thus that identity gives

\(\displaystyle \|u\|_2^2 + \|z\|_2^2 = \|w\|_2^2 .\) (8.110)

The same equality holds after the signed pullback: the two full quadratic forms are invariant, and the nonlinear predictor energy is invariant with the transported tags. Apply that last invariance to \(\exp (tb)\) and \(\exp (t\mathcal{U}_F b)\), divide by \(t^2\) and let \(t \to 0\). The derivative proved in Subsection 8.8 gives the stated predictor square. This also explains why the rooted response need not itself commute with the chosen symmetry.

The full stack identities give

\(\displaystyle N_0 w = 0, \qquad F_0^{\ast} U + I_0^{\ast} V = w, \qquad F_0^{\ast} u + I_0^{\ast} z = w.\)

The first is the extra identity \(N_0 B_c d_c = 0\); the second follows from the stack isometry and the first, and the third is (8.61). Taking inner products with \(w\) gives

\(\displaystyle \|U\|_2^2 + \|V\|_2^2 = \|w\|_2^2, \qquad \langle U, u \rangle + \langle V, z \rangle = \|w\|_2^2 .\)

Together with (8.110), these imply \(\|U - u\|_2^2 + \|V - z\|_2^2 = 0\). Every summand is nonnegative, so \(U = u\) and \(V = z\) at every address. In the notation of affine energy rows, this says

\(\displaystyle C_0 (\overline{b_s}, b_s) - G_0 (b_s) +\mathcal{A}_{\infty} (v_F (b))_s = 0.\) (8.111)

Here \(C_0\) is the linear fine-curvature/predictor pair, \(G_0\) the linear guide, and \(\mathcal{A}_{\infty}\) the full fluctuation row; the subscript \(s\) includes the signed action on bonds. Linearity of \(C_0\) in its fine input and commutation of the replica with the signed action give the displayed equation. In particular the cancellation holds for each row, not just after summing the energy.

We will also need a volume-independent bound

\(\displaystyle \|v_F (b)\|_{\infty} \leqslant C_{\text{resp}} \|b\|_{\infty} .\) (8.112)

Here is the quantitative tree estimate behind this bound. Put \(r_0 = (J + 3) L\) and let the installed cross kernel have weighted row bound \(C / L\) and width \(\eta \geqslant 0\). At fine sites within \(tL\) of a fixed origin, moving the exponential weight costs at most \(e^{\eta t}\). Consequently its action on a scalar coarse field bounded by that weight is at most

\(\displaystyle A / L, \qquad A = e^{\eta (12 (J + 3) + 5)} e^{\eta} \hspace{0.17em} 4 \max (C, 1) .\)

A tree hike has length at most \(4 L\). Each coordinate route in the root patch has length at most \(8 r_0\); the root correction is the difference of two averages of such path integrals. It is therefore bounded by \(16 r_0 A / L\). Combining the hike and root correction gives \(C_1 = (4 + 16 (J + 3)) A\) for the straightening potential. Taking its bond gradient costs \(2 C_1\), so the extracted response costs at most \(A + 2 C_1\). Subtracting the replica costs another \(e^{\eta}\) in the weighted estimate. For a field of sup norm one all its colour coordinates satisfy the weighted premise, since the weight is at least one. Summing the absolute colour coordinates bounds the physical colour norm. Thus one may take

\(\displaystyle C_{\text{resp}} = \max \{1, q \max (0, A + 2 C_1 + e^{\eta})\},\)

where \(q\) is the colour dimension. Homogeneity proves (8.112) for arbitrary \(b\). All tree and path lengths were bounded before periodization, so the estimate is uniform in the period and in the forest.

8.14.2A common logarithmic comparison field

Retain the upper-exponent convention of Subsection 8.25. Away from the seed set the coarse field \(V\) is clean: its plaquette chords are bounded by the small threshold. If the ball of radius \(d + 1\) about \(o\) is clean, \(d \leqslant 32 B\), the axial construction gives a gauge with bond chords, on the radius-\(d\) ball, at most

\(\displaystyle 16 \{2 (32 B + 1) + 1\}^5 N_p \hspace{0.17em} 2 \tau_c .\)

Here \(\tau_c\) is the coarse small threshold and has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Apply the local logarithm there and extend the logarithmic field by zero elsewhere. We obtain a single global field \(b\) and a coarse gauge \(g\) such that

\(\displaystyle g \cdot V = \exp b \text{on the radius-$d$ ball}, \qquad \|b\|_{\infty} \leqslant w (x),\) (8.113)

where \(w \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The period and chart-radius conditions hold at one threshold depending only on the fixed step parameters. The zero extension is used for the full linear kernels; no smallness of \(V\) outside the ball is asserted.

Consider a row whose anchor is at distance greater than \(20 B\) from all seeds, and suppose (8.113) holds on its radius-\(2 B\) ball. In the original gauge use the candidate

\(\displaystyle v = \text{Ad} (g^{- 1} \circ \text{parent}) v_F (b) .\)

It vanishes on tree bonds and has norm at most \(W = C_{\text{resp}} w\). Within the stencil of this remote row all non-tree bonds are active. Thus the sector's affine signal agrees there with \(v\), including its tree zeros. Gauge covariance transfers its actual affine row to the same \(b\) and \(v_F (b)\).

Let \(C (V)\) be the fine-curvature/predictor pair at the coarse replica, \(G (V)\) the guide, and \(\mathcal{A}_R^V\) the transported cut fluctuation row. Their residual is \(C (V) - G (V) +\mathcal{A}_R^V v_F (b)\) in this common gauge. Subtract (8.111). The four errors are

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} & C (V) - C_0 (\bar{b}, b), \qquad - (G (V) - G_0 (b)),\\ & (\mathcal{A}_R^V -\mathcal{A}_R) v_F (b), \qquad (\mathcal{A}_R -\mathcal{A}_{\infty}) v_F (b) . \end{aligned}\)

The first two have upper exponent \(- 1\), by the local Taylor and guide comparisons of Sections 8.9 and 8.11: their bounds are fixed multiples of \(w \{(1 + R) w + e^{- \sigma R} \}\). The clean condition makes the guide's clipping inactive on precisely the read stencil; it is removed before the comparison with the globally extended logarithm. For the third error, all coarse transport bonds have chord at most \(Cw\) on the common hull. The adjoint estimate and the absolute path bound give a bound \(C (B + 1) wW\). The fourth is at most \(CWe^{- \sigma R}\). The cutoff \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\) has an exponentially small tail of every prescribed negative upper exponent. We have therefore proved, for one \(a (x) \geqslant 0\) of upper exponent \(- 1\),

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{R}_j (v)\| \leqslant a (x)\) (8.114)

on every row whose comparison hull is included in the common clean ball. Here \(\mathcal{R}_j\) is the actual unscaled affine row, with its original orientation and tags. One field \(b\) is used for all rows in the ball, so these estimates can be inserted into a matrix identity without changing the candidate from row to row.

8.14.3Comparing the candidate with the chosen local mean

Use counting coordinates and the matrices of Sections 8.12 and 8.13. Write \(M\) for the selected row matrix, \(A = M^{\ast} M + \Pi_{\text{inactive}}\), \(\ell = M^{\ast} r (0)\) for the linear source, and

\(\displaystyle r (v) = Mv + r (0), \qquad P = \text{the padded principal inverse}, \qquad m = - P^{\ast} \ell, \quad E = AP - 1.\)

The row \(r_j (v)\) is \(s\mathcal{R}_j (v)\) when selected, and zero otherwise; \(s > 0\) is the fixed signed-channel normalization. The chosen physical mean \(\mu\) is obtained from \(m\) by reassembling its colour coordinates. We have the exact identity

\(\displaystyle m - v = E^{\ast} v - P^{\ast} (Av + \ell), \qquad Av + \ell = M^{\ast} r (v) + \Pi_{\text{inactive}} v.\) (8.115)

For the first equality use \(E^{\ast} = P^{\ast} A - 1\) and cancel \(P^{\ast} Av\). The second follows directly from the definition of \(A\) and \(\ell\).

Let \(S\) bound both unweighted row and column sums of \(P\), let \(\varepsilon_B\) bound those of \(E\), and let \(Y\) bound those of \(M\). These constants are supplied by the weighted estimates already proved; \(\varepsilon_B\) has every negative upper exponent. If the normal residual \(Av + \ell\) is bounded by \(b_0\) on the principal window attached to coordinate \(i\), (8.115) gives

\(\displaystyle |m_i - v_i | \leqslant Sb_0 + \varepsilon_B \|v\|_{\infty} .\) (8.116)

Indeed the \(i\)th row of \(P^{\ast}\) is supported on that very window, and the column bounds give both estimates. This uses \(P^{\ast}\) in the mean; replacing it by the symmetrized covariance would change the identity.

Fix a target row at anchor \(o\) farther than \(30 B\) from every seed. Choose (8.113) on the radius-\(8 B\) ball. For each coordinate within \(B\) of \(o\), its principal window lies within \(3 B\) of \(o\), since the window radius is at most \(B + 2\). At such window coordinates the inactive term vanishes: a non-tree bond is active there, and a tree bond has candidate value zero. A nonzero entry of \(M^{\ast}\) reads a row within \(B / 4\), so all its affine residuals obey (8.114), using subballs of the same radius-\(8 B\) comparison. Hence \(b_0 = Ysa\), and

\(\displaystyle |m_i - v_i | \leqslant U (x) := SYs \hspace{0.17em} a (x) + \varepsilon_B W (x) \quad \text{when } d (o, i) \leqslant B.\)

The function \(U\) has upper exponent \(- 1\).

The target row is selected and reads only coordinates within \(B / 4\). Its difference on \(\mu\) and \(v\) is linear, so the row bound on \(M\) and the passage from colour coordinates to the colour norm imply

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{R}_o (\mu) -\mathcal{R}_o (v)\| \leqslant qs^{- 1} YU.\)

Combining this with (8.114) proves

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{R}_o (\mu)\| \leqslant a (x) + qs^{- 1} YU (x), \qquad d (o, \text{seeds}) > 30 B.\) (8.117)

The right-hand side has upper exponent \(- 1\), uniformly in the period, forest, tags and all sectors satisfying the fixed construction conditions. This is the required extra order of smallness of the actual affine mean. The argument uses the flat stationary response and its diagonal identity; it makes no assertion about derivatives of the mean or a nonlinear global stationary solution.

8.15Producing the warning plateaus

We prove the deterministic input to the warning estimate of Subsection 8.3. Fix the patch and the positive fine-action and predictor-penalty constants \(c_f, c_p\). We retain the funded path bounds, the clean-sector precision bounds and the stationary response of Sections 8.12 and 8.14. Let \(t = \text{fineCut} (x)\), \(B = \text{radius} (x)\) and \(q = \dim \mathfrak{g}\). We use \(\delta_0\) for a fixed small error allowance, to distinguish it from the field scale \(t\).

The guide estimate supplies a constant \(A > 0\), chosen before \(L\), such that the fine guide has norm at most \(At / L^2\) and the predictor guide at most \(At\). Choose

\(\displaystyle \delta_0 = \frac{\sqrt{c_f}}{32}, \qquad \frac{A}{L^2} \leqslant \delta_0, \qquad W > \frac{2}{\sqrt{c_p}} (A + \delta_0) .\) (8.118)

For example, any integer lower bound strictly larger than \(A / \delta_0\) suffices for \(L\), since \(L \geqslant 1\). We will choose the remaining constants after \(L\) and \(W\), and finally one lower bound for \(x\). None of these choices depends on the lattice period or on the seed allocation.

8.15.1A uniform bound for the affine mean

First we need \(\| \mu_e \| \leqslant Ct\) with a fixed post-\(L\) constant \(C\). A bound with upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) alone would not imply this stronger comparison with the particular scale \(t\).

On a clean ball of fixed radius \(n\), the axial gauge construction of Subsection 8.4 bounds every coarse bond chord by \(K_n t\), where one may take

\(\displaystyle K_n = 32 (2 (n + 1) + 1)^5 \# \{ \text{plaquette axes} \} .\)

The number \(n\) is fixed before \(x\). For a fine plaquette in the coarse replica, use \(n = 4\): each of its four links has chord at most \(K_4 t\) in that gauge, so its curvature norm is at most \(4 K_4 t\). Every path term retained by the fine cutoff leaves the required radius-five margin at its endpoint inside the clean \(B\)-ball: its endpoint separation plus five is at most \(B\). Apply the same radius-four argument separately at that endpoint. Unitary transport preserves its norm; summing the absolute path coefficients bounds the entire fine base stroke by

\(\displaystyle 4 K_4 (\sqrt{c_f} + P_f) t,\)

where \(P_f\) is the funded absolute fine-path bound. This is a sum of individual fixed-radius estimates; no gauge on the whole growing \(B\)-ball is required for this bound.

For a predictor base stroke, take \(n\) equal to the fixed predictor read radius. The logarithm chart extends the locally gauged coarse field to a global logarithmic field of norm at most \(K'_n t\), while preserving the actual links on that stencil. Its linear replica has no larger norm. The fixed-patch Taylor estimate from Subsection 8.8 bounds the predictor by \(J (\|w\|+\|w\|^2)\) for the pair of logarithmic fields \(w\). Eventually \(\|w\| \leqslant K'_n t \leqslant 1\), so this is at most \(2 JK'_n t\). Locality and gauge covariance identify it with the original predictor base stroke for every original tag choice. We have therefore proved a common bound \(A_1 t\) for both base-stroke types on clean balls.

Let \(s > 0\) be the fixed row normalization, \(N_1\) the row operator bound, and \(\gamma, K\) the shell-access constants. The saved frozen logarithmic field is bounded by \(2 \gamma Kt\). A selected affine row at zero is the base stroke minus its guide plus the row applied to this saved field. Unselected rows vanish. Hence all normalized affine rows have norm at most \(b_1 t\), where

\(\displaystyle b_1 = s (A_1 + A + 2 N_1 \gamma K) .\)

Let \(Y\) bound the absolute column sums of the row matrix, and let \(P_0\) bound both row and column sums of the unsymmetrized local inverse \(P\). The affine linear coefficient \(\ell\) and the mean \(m = - P^T \ell\) then satisfy coordinatewise

\(\displaystyle | \ell_i | \leqslant Yb_1 t, \qquad |m_i | \leqslant P_0 Yb_1 t, \qquad \| \mu_e \| \leqslant qP_0 Yb_1 t.\)

Each inequality is the elementary bound \(| \sum_j M_{ij} v_j | \leqslant (\sum_j |M_{ij} |) \sup_j |v_j |\); the last sums the \(q\) colour coordinates. Choose once and for all

\(\displaystyle C = 1 + \max \{0, b_1, Yb_1, qP_0 Yb_1 \} > 0.\) (8.119)

The same algebra gives the residual identity \(Am + \ell = - A (P - A^{- 1})^T \ell\). Its entries are bounded by a fixed multiple of \(\text{distPay} (B) t\), and hence have every prescribed negative upper exponent by the local inverse tail estimate. The mean and its residual thus come from the same affine system.

8.15.2The genuine local minimizing branches

We also need exact likelihood values, before restoring other masks. Fix any \(\varepsilon > 0\). There exist a radius \(r > 0\) and a finite read distance \(d\), depending on the patch and \(\varepsilon\), such that if all fine bond chords within distance \(d\) of a coarse site are at most \(r\), then its site likelihood equals one, every outgoing bond likelihood equals one, and the predictor bonds have chord at most \(\varepsilon\).

Here is the uniformity argument. On the covering lattice, the identity configuration lies in the open unique-minimizer branches for both the frame and the connector. Their values there are the identity. The continuity and uniqueness proved in Subsection 8.8 imply a neighbourhood on which these branches remain selected and each predictor chord is below \(\varepsilon\). Intersect these neighbourhoods over the finitely many axes and frame-tag pairs. A product-topology neighbourhood contains one obtained by restricting finitely many covering bonds to chord balls of a common positive radius \(r\). Enclose their anchors in a ball of finite read distance \(d\).

Pulling a periodic configuration to the cover preserves all these finite reads, even when some periodic bonds coincide. Thus the same \(r, d\) work for every period and every tag array. On a minimizing branch the chosen tag is uniform: its mass is the reciprocal of the number of tags. The physical likelihood multiplies by that number, so its value is exactly one. This proves both likelihood identities, not merely their convergence to one.

Suppose now that the coarse field is clean on the \(B\)-ball about \(o\) and that, on its half-ball, the actual fine field is \(U = \exp (v) \text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V\) with \(\|v_e \| \leqslant w (x)\), where \(w\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The local axial gauge and logarithm extension put both coarse and fine chords below a common bound \(W_1 (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Indeed the coarse logarithm has that bound, the exponential is locally Lipschitz, and

\(\displaystyle \text{chord} (\exp (v_e) \text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V_e) \leqslant \text{chord} (\exp (v_e)) + \text{chord} (\text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V_e) .\)

Choose \(x\) so that \(B \geqslant 2 d + 12\) and \(W_1 (x) \leqslant \min \{r, \varepsilon / 4\}\), applying the preceding branch result with target \(\varepsilon / 2\). The predictor/coarse relative chord is at most \(\varepsilon / 2 + W_1 (x) \leqslant \varepsilon\). Every fine plaquette chord is at most \(4 W_1 (x) \leqslant \varepsilon\). Gauge covariance transfers these statements and the likelihood identities to the original fields.

8.15.3The three errors on a small fluctuation event

Let \(o\) have distance greater than \(30 B\) from the seeds. The exact flag condition implies that the coarse field is clean on its needed hull. For \(\beta > 0\), assume

\(\displaystyle |z_i | \leqslant \xi t \sqrt{\beta} \quad \text{for every coordinate whose anchor lies within } B \text{ of } o.\) (8.120)

With \(v = \mu + \beta^{- 1 / 2} z\), interpreted in the colour basis, (8.119) gives

\(\displaystyle \|v_e - \mu_e \| \leqslant q \xi t, \qquad \|v_e \| \leqslant (C + q \xi) t.\)

Away from the seeds the actual fine field agrees with this increment of the coarse replica. Thus the small-chart branch result just proved applies to it, with \(w = (C + q \xi) t\).

For a fine or predictor row based at \(o\), write \(B_p (U, V)\) for its actual stroke minus its guide, and \(R_p (v)\) for the transported affine row with the same coarse field, tags and saved variables. There is a nonnegative error \(H (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1\) such that

\(\displaystyle \|B_p (U, V) - R_p (v)\| \leqslant H (x) .\) (8.121)

To detail the estimate, use the preceding common local gauge and extend its two logarithmic fields by zero outside the read hull. Let \(h\) bound their norms; it has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The local product logarithm differs from the sum by at most \(Jh^2\). Taylor expansion of each genuine fine/predictor stroke at zero leaves a remainder \(E_0\) of upper exponent \(- 1\). Subtract the corresponding Taylor formula at the replica. The five resulting errors are bounded by

\(\displaystyle 2 E_0 + N_1 Jh^2 + N_1 he^{- \sigma R} + 2 N_1 c_G BA_2 h^2 .\) (8.122)

Here \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\) is the retained path cutoff, and \(\sigma > 0\) is the funded path decay exponent. They are, respectively, the two Taylor remainders, the product-log error acted on by the linear row, the cutoff tail of that row, and the change from flat to coarse-field transport. For the last term, each transport path has length at most \(B\), each coarse chord is at most \(A_2 h\), and the adjoint perturbation bound is \(2 c_G\) times the path chord; multiply by the input norm \(h\) and the row bound \(N_1\). The retained read radius is at most \(B / 4\), so truncating the extended fields does not alter the actual row. Since \(B\) has upper exponent zero and \(e^{- \sigma R}\) has every negative exponent, every term in (8.122) has upper exponent \(- 1\). This proves (8.121) with the original tags.

The affine row matrix has absolute row bound \(Y\) after normalization by \(s\). Therefore (8.120) implies

\(\displaystyle \|R_p (v) - R_p (\mu)\| \leqslant qs^{- 1} Y \xi t.\) (8.123)

The local mean comparison in (8.117) gives a nonnegative \(E (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1\) with \(\|R_p (\mu)\| \leqslant E (x)\). Choose

\(\displaystyle \xi = \min \left\{ (q + 1)^{- 1}, \frac{\delta_0}{3 qs^{- 1} |Y| + 1} \right\} > 0.\) (8.124)

Then \(q \xi \leqslant 1\) and \(qs^{- 1} Y \xi \leqslant \delta_0 / 3\). An upper-exponent-\(- 1\) function is eventually at most \(\delta_0 t / 3\): multiplying it by \(\hat{\beta}_f^{1 / 2}\) gives a quantity tending to zero, while \(t = \hat{\beta}_f^{- 1 / 2} \text{fineMonitor} \geqslant \hat{\beta}_f^{- 1 / 2}\). Hence \(H, E \leqslant \delta_0 t / 3\) eventually. The triangle inequality proves

\(\displaystyle \|B_p (U, V)\| \leqslant \delta_0 t.\) (8.125)

Choose the earlier small-chart target \(\varepsilon\) below the fixed curvature-comparison radius. In that neighbourhood, \(\text{chord} (g) \leqslant 2 \| \text{curvature} (g)\|\). Adding the guide bounds to (8.125), and retaining the square-root penalty normalizations, gives

\(\displaystyle \text{toll} (U, p) \leqslant \frac{2}{\sqrt{c_f}} (A / L^2 + \delta_0) t < t / 4, \qquad \text{wire} (Q (U), V, (o, a)) \leqslant \frac{2}{\sqrt{c_p}} (A + \delta_0) t < Wt.\)

The strict inequalities follow from (8.118); the first coefficient is at most \(1 / 8\). Both physical likelihoods are already exactly one by the local minimizing-branch argument.

8.15.4The cutoff margin and the final order of choices

Set \(a = \max \{C + 1, \gamma K\}\). The same event has \(\|v_e \| \leqslant (C + q \xi) t \leqslant at\), so the broad bond cutoff is one. For derivatives, use the event with \(\xi / 2\). It gives the strict margin \(C + q \xi / 2 < a\), while the fine and predictor inequalities above are strict as well. Continuity makes these masks constant on a neighbourhood of the base point. This is exactly the plateau required in Subsection 8.28; no derivative is taken through a chosen gauge or a varying seed assignment.

The order is now explicit: choose the guide bound \(A\) and error allowance before \(L\); choose \(L\), then \(W\); obtain the fixed shell and precision constants, then \(C, a, \xi\); finally enlarge the \(x\) threshold for the finite-read branches, the two order-\(- 1\) errors, the chart radius and the inverse-series bounds. This supplies the previously displayed safety input on every funded sector whose adjacent quadratic data and paths have the compatibility specified in Subsubsection 13.10.6. Lemma 13.25 supplies that compatibility for the stored tubes. Funding, the stationary response and the guide bounds retain their separate construction hypotheses.

8.16Global row and source bounds on the actual sector

The palette comparison needs estimates on the whole selected row matrix, including the source created by frozen links. A small covariance error on a posed clean panel does not supply these estimates. We establish the required global bounds using the actual sector precision and the selected background rows.

Fix the gauge group, a one-step patch with block length \(L\), its kernel, and its funded tower and path bounds. Assume the patch satisfies the predictor branch conditions. All constants below are chosen from these fixed data. Put

\(\displaystyle B = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil, \qquad t = \text{fineCut} (x, L), \qquad R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor .\)

Here \(t\) is the fine small-field threshold of Subsection 8.5, while \(\text{fieldCut} (x)\) is the coarse threshold; eventually \(\text{fieldCut} (x) \leqslant 2 t\). The coarse period satisfies \(c \geqslant 100 (1 + B)\). Use arbitrary stores, forest, predictor tags and coarse field \(V\) with the stores' flags equal to the actual coarse flags at threshold \(\text{fieldCut} (x)\). No assertion about their fine costs or other noise entries is required. The large-\(x\) threshold is uniform over all these choices and all funded towers having the fixed bounds.

Let \(I\) be the active link-color coordinates and \(J\) the row-color coordinates, with unselected rows set to zero. An input coordinate is anchored at the coarse parent of its fine link. For a row in signed channel \(s\), apply \(s\) to the row's coarse root: the parent of the fine plaquette base for a curvature row, or the initial coarse site for a predictor row. This is the output anchor used in the weighted bounds. The selector here retains a row exactly when its prescribed stencil meets an active bond; that stencil contains all fine-direction inputs of the row. The anchor-distance selector of Subsection 8.12 uses a different rule; no equality of selected sets is assumed.

We make the selection rule and its consequence for flags explicit. For \(k \geqslant 0\), the \(k\)-neighbourhood of the seeds is the set of coarse sites within distance \(k\) of the seed place of the stores; the coarse flags belong to the seed place. As in Subsection 8.12, the active bonds are the free bonds whose coarse endpoints lie outside the \(10 B\)-neighbourhood of the seeds; below we use only that the coarse parent of an active bond lies outside it. Let \(r_{\text{pred}}\) be the fixed reach of the predictor and put

\(\displaystyle \text{span} (x) = R + 5 + r_{\text{pred}} .\)

The stencil of a row in signed channel \(s\) consists of the fine bonds whose coarse parent is within distance \(\text{span} (x)\) of its output anchor \(o\). Here \(R\) is the cutoff on the length of the retained paths, and the stencil contains every fine-direction input of the row. Since \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\), one has \(\text{span} (x) \leqslant \lfloor B / 50 \rfloor\) as soon as \(B \geqslant 100 (5 + r_{\text{pred}})\); this and \(B \geqslant 7\) hold eventually.

Clearance of selected rows. Suppose a row with output anchor \(o\) is selected, so that its stencil contains an active bond \(a\) with coarse parent \(l\). Then \(\text{dist} (o, l) \leqslant \lfloor B / 50 \rfloor\). If a coarse site \(m\) and \(k \geqslant 0\) satisfy \(k + \text{dist} (m, l) \leqslant 10 B\), then \(m\) is outside the \(k\)-neighbourhood of the seeds: otherwise some seed point \(y\) has \(\text{dist} (y, m) \leqslant k\), hence \(\text{dist} (y, l) \leqslant 10 B\), contradicting that \(a\) is active. Applying this with \(m = o\) and \(k = 9 B + \lfloor 9 B / 10 \rfloor\) (note \(\lfloor 9 B / 10 \rfloor + \lfloor B / 50 \rfloor \leqslant B\)) shows that \(o\) is outside the \((9 B + \lfloor 9 B / 10 \rfloor)\)-neighbourhood of the seeds. For a bond \(e\) of the stencil, its coarse parent \(m\) satisfies \(\text{dist} (m, l) \leqslant \text{dist} (m, o) + \text{dist} (o, l) \leqslant 2 \lfloor B / 50 \rfloor\), and the coarse parent \(n\) of its other endpoint is within one more. With \(k = 9 B + \lfloor 8 B / 10 \rfloor\) and \(\lfloor 8 B / 10 \rfloor + 1 + 2 \lfloor B / 50 \rfloor \leqslant B\) (valid for \(B \geqslant 7\)), both \(m\) and \(n\) lie outside the \((9 B + \lfloor 8 B / 10 \rfloor)\)-neighbourhood of the seeds. Since the flags lie in the seed place, every coarse ball of radius at most \(9 B\) about the output anchor or about an endpoint of the stencil is free of coarse flags. All the neighbourhoods used below for a selected row (the branch neighbourhood of a predictor read, the clean \(B\)-ball of the base-row estimate, and the chart boxes of the plaquettes reached by retained paths) have radius at most \(B\) for large \(x\), so they are clean. This is the meaning of the phrase “row selection clears the required neighbourhood of flags” below. Unselected rows are set to zero and need no clearance. Write

\(\displaystyle M : \mathbb{R}^I \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}^J, \qquad Q = M^{\ast} M\)

for the actual selected derivative matrix and its Gram matrix, including the fixed row normalization \(\lambda > 0\). Thus \(M\) is the matrix obtained by applying the selected row derivative to each active coordinate impulse, not a new linearization at the identity. These derivatives are taken at the actual replica; the predictor derivative applies curvature to the predictor at the displaced field multiplied by the inverse predictor at zero displacement. They need not equal the transported flat rows used in Subsection 8.12; their coercivity requires the separate comparison below. For matrices between either pair of anchor sets, use the weighted maximum of absolute row and column sums with exponential weight \(w \geqslant 0\), denoted \(\| \cdot \|_w\); this is the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{\eta}\) of Subsection 8.13 with \(\eta = w\). (The letter \(Q\) below is the Gram matrix, not the amplitude constant of (8.99); when that section is applied to \(Q\), its amplitude constant is supplied by (8.127).) For each \(j \in I\), let \(W_j\) be its actual panel. Let \(N\) be the matrix whose \(j\)th column is the inverse of \(Q_{W_j}\), extended by zero, and let \(C = (N + N^{\ast}) / 2\).

Proposition 8.5. (Uniform global bounds) There are \(\eta > 0\), \(K \geqslant 1\), \(u, v > 0\), and \(L_0 > 0\), depending only on the fixed data, such that eventually in \(x\), uniformly over the choices just specified,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} Q \geqslant \eta I, \qquad C \geqslant \eta I, \qquad \|Q\|_0, \|C\|_0, \|N\|_0 \leqslant K, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.126)} \\ \|N - Q^{- 1} \|_0 \leqslant ue^{- v \lfloor B / 60 \rfloor}, \qquad \|M\|_{\sigma / 2} \leqslant L_0, \qquad \|Q\|_{\sigma / 2} \leqslant L_0^2, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.127)} \end{array}\)

where \(\sigma > 0\) is the fixed path width. In particular \(Q\) and \(C\) are positive definite. Given any fixed \(r \geqslant 0\), there is also \(Z > 0\) such that, for \(0 < d \leqslant rt\), the actual source vector \(s (Y) \in \mathbb{R}^J\) created by an arbitrary frozen field \(Y\) obeys

\(\displaystyle |s (Y)_i | \leqslant Zt \qquad (i \in J) .\) (8.128)

The same tail in \(x\) may be used for all these inequalities. The source constant and tail may additionally depend on \(r\).

8.16.1The actual matrix and its principal windows

The actual derivative has a uniform exponentially decaying impulse bound. For a plaquette derivative, the product rule gives a fixed sum over its four input bonds; path transports preserve colour norms and the funded absolute path coefficients pay the remaining distance. The predictor derivative reads a fixed stencil and has a uniform bound on its actual branch by the finite-read argument of Subsection 8.21. That finite-read argument uses the fixed patch and predictor branch conditions; it does not use the precision bounds assumed for other parts of that section. Row selection clears the required neighbourhood of flags, by the clearance argument above, so this branch estimate applies at \(V\). Reversing a bond changes its parent anchor by at most one, which costs only a fixed factor in the exponential weight. Restrictions to selected rows or active coordinates delete nonnegative terms from the absolute sums. This is the actual-derivative counterpart of the impulse estimate used in Subsection 8.12.

More explicitly, the resulting actual entry estimate has the form

\(\displaystyle e^{\sigma \text{dist} (a_i, a_j)} |M_{ij} | \leqslant C_0 .\)

Let \(m_I, m_J\) bound the numbers of input and output coordinates per coarse anchor. They are finite constants fixed after \(L\), including all color, orientation and block-residue factors. For \(\mathcal{W} (v) = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (2 n + 1)^4 e^{- vn}\), grouping by anchor distance gives

\(\displaystyle \|M\|_{\sigma / 2} \leqslant C_0 \max \{m_I, m_J \} \mathcal{W} (\sigma / 2) = : L_0 .\)

Increasing \(L_0\) if needed makes it positive. This is a global row and column bound; its proof does not introduce the torus cardinality. The same product estimate gives \(\|Q\|_{\sigma / 2} \leqslant L_0^2\): the weighted norm is submultiplicative, because the triangle inequality \(\text{dist} (a_i, a_k) \leqslant \text{dist} (a_i, a_j) + \text{dist} (a_j, a_k)\) through the intermediate anchor splits the weight, and the adjoint has the same weighted norm since row and column sums are interchanged. This is the last bound in (8.127). We prove a uniform lower bound for these actual derivatives. Let \(a\) be an active-coordinate vector and extend it by zero to a fine-bond field \(u\), including zeros on tree and frozen bonds. At each active bond choose a coarse gauge on a fixed macro patch around its parent; the active-bond clearance and the flag identity make this patch clean. The tree comparison's fixed macrobox fits on the fine torus: eventually \(B\) dominates its fixed radius, while the fine period \(f = Lc\) satisfies \(f \geqslant c \geqslant 100 (1 + B)\) and \(c > 1\). The axial estimate makes every replica link read by the comparison arbitrarily close to the identity, uniformly in the period and in the subsequently chosen sector. Use one such gauge for the whole comparison at that bond, including every row in its macro patch.

Fix \(\varepsilon > 0\). On a sufficiently small identity neighbourhood, the derivatives of a plaquette and of the selected predictor differ from their flat derivatives by at most \(\varepsilon\) times the sum of the norms of the input directions on their respective finite stencils. For a plaquette this follows by differentiating its finite link product and using continuity of its derivative. For the predictor, use the finite-read analytic branch of Subsection 8.21; its derivative at the identity is the flat predictor row. Continuity of that derivative on the fixed reference patch gives the required \(\varepsilon\) bound for each axis and local tag choice. Their finite intersection is one neighbourhood, independent of the period. The read pullback has supremum norm at most one even when periodic reads coincide, and the supremum of the input directions is bounded by their sum on the stencil. Gauge covariance and invariance of the colour norm return the estimates to the original actual derivatives. The tree inequality (8.94), applied in the chosen gauge, therefore bounds each active \(\|u_e \|\) by a fixed linear combination of the norms of the actual direct plaquette and predictor derivatives, plus \(\varepsilon\) times sums of \(\|u\|\) over fixed-radius stencils. The gauge may depend on \(e\); it is fixed while differentiating the rows.

Square this inequality and sum over active bonds. Reordering the finite neighbourhood sums, as in Subsection 8.12, gives fixed constants \(K_f, K_p, H_f, H_p \geqslant 0\), independent of the period, such that

\(\displaystyle \|a\|_2^2 \leqslant 2 K_f \sum_p \|D_p u\|^2 + 2 K_p \sum_b \|D_b u\|^2 + 2 \varepsilon^2 (K_f H_f + K_p H_p) \|a\|_2^2,\)

where \(D_p, D_b\) are the actual direct plaquette and predictor derivatives in the identity signed channel. These constants include the macrobox and stencil multiplicities: Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the square of each local input sum by the stencil cardinality times its sum of squares, and each input bond occurs in only a fixed number of these sums per coarse anchor. The number of fine bonds over an anchor is \(L^4\) times the number of axes. The same finite counting applies to the plaquette and predictor terms, including on a wrapped torus. Choose \(\varepsilon\) before the large-\(x\) threshold so that the final coefficient is at most \(1 / 2\). The row-selection rule retains a row whenever its read stencil meets an active bond. Conversely, if the row is omitted, the zero-extended direction vanishes on its entire stencil, so its derivative is zero. For a path row the stencil contains the reads of every retained path; for a predictor row it contains the finite branch reads. Thus inserting row selection does not change the derivative on the zero-extended active space. The normalized Gram energy obeys

\(\displaystyle \langle a, Qa \rangle \geqslant \lambda^2 \left( c_f \sum_p \|D_p u\|^2 + c_p \sum_b \|D_b u\|^2 \right),\)

with the positive direct and predictor coefficients \(c_f, c_p\). The remaining signed channels and path rows contribute nonnegative squares. Absorbing the error gives

\(\displaystyle \|a\|_2^2 \leqslant 4 (K_f + K_p + 1) \left( \sum_p \|D_p u\|^2 + \sum_b \|D_b u\|^2 \right) .\)

Thus one may take \(\eta_0 = \lambda^2 \min (c_f, c_p) / \{4 (K_f + K_p + 1)\} > 0\), uniformly in all the stated sector choices. The empty active space is included. This argument uses the same tree inequality as Subsection 8.12, but does not identify its transported-flat matrix with \(M\).

Set \(K_0 \geqslant \max \{1, L_0^2 \}\). Apply the inverse estimates of Subsection 8.13 to this actual \(Q\) and its principal submatrices. They give \(w > 0\) and \(S > 0\), independent of the period, for which

\(\displaystyle \|Q^{- 1} \|_w \leqslant S, \qquad \|N\|_w \leqslant S.\)

For the latter assertion the windows vary by column, but every principal inverse has the same entrywise exponential bound; summing that bound over either anchor set proves both sums. This is why a bound on each column's Euclidean norm alone would be insufficient. We may decrease \(w\) to ensure \(w \leqslant \sigma / 2\).

Each actual panel contains every coordinate within anchor distance \(\rho = \lfloor B / 60 \rfloor\) of its center, once \(B \geqslant 120\). Indeed the actual panel requires every pair of coarse bond endpoints to be at distance at most \(\lfloor B / 20 \rfloor\). Each endpoint is at distance at most one from its anchor, and \(2 + \lfloor B / 60 \rfloor \leqslant \lfloor B / 20 \rfloor\) for \(B \geqslant 120\). The triangle inequality proves the asserted containment. Equivalently one may use any family of panels with this ball-containment property. The principal-inverse equation implies

\(\displaystyle E := QN - I, \qquad E_{ij} = 0 \quad \text{if } \text{dist} (a_i, a_j) \leqslant \rho .\)

The product norm gives \(\|E\|_w \leqslant K_0 S + 1\). Remove its exponential weight only on the remaining entries to obtain

\(\displaystyle \|E\|_0 \leqslant (K_0 S + 1) e^{- w \rho}, \qquad \|N - Q^{- 1} \|_0 \leqslant S (K_0 S + 1) e^{- w \rho} .\)

The second inequality follows from the ordered identity \(N - Q^{- 1} = Q^{- 1} E\).

Since \(Q^{- 1}\) is symmetric, averaging \(N\) with its transpose does not increase this error. Also \(Q \leqslant K_0 I\), so \(Q^{- 1} \geqslant K_0^{- 1} I\). Because \(\rho \to \infty\), enlarge the \(x\) threshold until \(S (K_0 S + 1) e^{- w \rho} \leqslant (2 K_0)^{- 1}\). Then \(C \geqslant (2 K_0)^{- 1} I\). Take

\(\displaystyle \eta = \min \{\eta_0, (2 K_0)^{- 1} \}, \quad K \geqslant \max \{1, K_0, S\}, \quad u = S (K_0 S + 1) + 1, \quad v = w.\)

These choices prove (8.126) and (8.127). The argument works on an empty active set as well, with all matrix inequalities interpreted on the zero space. No local mean or source bound was used to prove these matrix estimates.

8.16.2Selected background rows

The first subsection of Subsection 8.15 proves the needed bound on the affine base strokes by fixed-radius endpoint gauges, absolute path sums, the global guide bound and the local predictor estimate for the original tags. We check its application to the actual selected rows here. This uses only that subsection's base-stroke argument, not its subsequent mean calculation.

A selected row has no coarse flags in its required surrounding ball, by the clearance argument following the definition of the stencil. For large \(B\), this contains every fixed chart box needed by a predictor read or by a plaquette reached by a retained path. The actual flag identity in the proposition therefore gives the local small plaquette condition used by the axial gauge. Denote the fixed box constant by \(c_{\ast}\). Its coarse gauge and the parent-lifted replica gauge obey

\(\displaystyle r (V^g_e) \leqslant c_{\ast} \text{fieldCut} (x), \qquad r ((V^{\uparrow})^{g \circ \text{parent}}_e) \leqslant c_{\ast} \text{fieldCut} (x) .\) (8.129)

A replica link is either the identity or a coarse link. In a signed channel, pull back the gauge; reversal moves an anchor by at most one and preserves chord under inversion. The extra layer in each fixed box pays this displacement. The period condition makes the boxes nonwrapping.

The cited argument may consequently be applied separately at each path target, since only color norms are compared. Four-link curvature costs at most \(4 c_{\ast} \text{fieldCut} (x) \leqslant 8 c_{\ast} t\); its absolute path sum has a fixed bound. The predictor uses its fixed local logarithm chart, with size \(O (t)\), and the same original tags. The subtracted fine and predictor guides are precisely those of (8.43); their global bounds do not require flags to vanish at an unselected site. Thus the cited proof gives, for the unscaled affine base row,

\(\displaystyle \|b_i (V)\| \leqslant C_b t \quad \text{on every selected row } i,\) (8.130)

with \(C_b > 0\) fixed before the stores, period and field. Selection uses no assumption about the stores' fine costs or their other noise entries. This identifies the actual base rows to which the canonical argument applies without equating them to their flat approximations.

8.16.3Directions that include frozen links

The source contains the derivative evaluated on the frozen displacement, which need not be supported on the active links. We require a separate supremum estimate on all such directions. Let \(a\) be any fine-link Lie-algebra field with \(\sup_e \|a_e \| \leqslant A\), \(A \geqslant 0\). At a plaquette, the directional derivative is a sum of four transported link directions, followed by the fixed curvature projection. Hence its norm is bounded by \(C_f A\), uniformly in the base configuration. One can equivalently sum the norms over the fixed neighbouring fine bonds; their number is bounded in terms of \(L\).

For a transported path row, the differentiated curvature inputs are summed with its absolutely summable path coefficients and isometric adjoint transports. Thus its bound is the coefficient row sum times \(C_f A\). The direct row is bounded by its fixed square-root coefficient times \(C_f A\). These are the two components of the selected linear curvature row. For the predictor derivative, use the local branch estimate at (8.129). It is bounded by a constant times the sum of \(\|a_e \|\) on the fixed predictor stencil, hence by \(C_p A\). Conjugating directions by the local gauge or applying a signed channel preserves their norms. The same constants therefore apply to the original direction, including its values on frozen links. There is a \(C_a > 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{D}_i (V) a\| \leqslant C_a A \quad \text{on every selected row } i,\) (8.131)

where \(\mathcal{D}_i (V)\) is the derivative used in the actual row construction. Its path transports are held as prescribed in that construction; the estimate does not differentiate a newly chosen gauge.

Finally the frozen input \(a (Y)\) is the patched displacement on frozen links and zero on the other links. Its clipping gives \(\|a (Y)_e \| \leqslant 2 d \leqslant 2 rt\) for every \(Y\) and every link. The exact source is the selected restriction of \(\lambda \{b (V) +\mathcal{D}(V) a (Y)\}\) in scalar color coordinates. Projection onto a color coordinate has norm at most one. Equations (8.130) and (8.131) therefore give

\(\displaystyle |s (Y)_i | \leqslant \lambda (C_b + 2 rC_a) t.\)

Taking \(Z = \lambda (C_b + 2 rC_a) + 1\) proves (8.128). Intersect the finitely many eventual tails used for the matrix, base, branch and source bounds. All their constants were fixed before the period, stores, field, tags and frozen input, proving the asserted uniformity. No differentiability of the frozen field as a function of \(V\) was used or concluded.

8.17Uniform matrix bounds and the local mask test

This section isolates the warning input used by the opacity construction. There are two different estimates here. The matrix estimate is a global row-and-column bound at spatial rate zero for the actual screened Gaussian matrix. The mask identity is deterministic: on a prescribed local small-noise event, every factor in the local mask product equals one. A comparison for one posed reader does not imply the first estimate, and a Gaussian probability estimate does not by itself imply the second.

For \(x > 1\) write \(\text{radius} (x) = \text{radiusB} (x) = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\); both names denote this radius below.

Write \(\mathcal{I}\) for the active link-colour coordinates of a fixed forest and \(\mathcal{R}\) for the retained response-row coordinates. A move matrix \(M = (M_{ri})_{r \in \mathcal{R}, i \in \mathcal{I}}\) maps active-coordinate vectors to response-row vectors; \(a (r)\) and \(a (i)\) denote their anchor sites. In the selected application, row anchors include the signed channel action specified in Subsection 8.16. For a rectangular matrix define its exponential row-and-column bound at rate \(\sigma \geqslant 0\) by

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{p}_{\sigma} (M) = \max \left\{ \sup_{r \in \mathcal{R}} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{I}} e^{\sigma d (a (r), a (i))} |M_{ri} |, \sup_{i \in \mathcal{I}} \sum_{r \in \mathcal{R}} e^{\sigma d (a (r), a (i))} |M_{ri} | \right\} .\)

The bounds below are uniform in the torus size, but their constants may depend on the fixed patch, tube, and interaction range. Let \(Q\) be the Gram matrix of the actual row family, let \(W\) be a family of principal panels, each containing its own coordinate, and put

\(\displaystyle C = \text{clouded} (W, Q), \qquad N = \text{per} (W, Q) .\)

Here \(s \in \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{R}}\) is the source row, \(b = M^{\textsf{T}} s \in \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{I}}\) its transported row and \(m = - N^{\textsf{T}} b\) the selected mean. The corresponding direct and auxiliary rows are

\(\displaystyle r = Mm + s, \qquad z^{\text{aux}} = - (\text{coupling} (\beta))^{- 1} Q (Q^{- 1} - N^{\textsf{T}}) b.\)

These are the finite-dimensional identities behind the estimates; the transpose is the ordinary real transpose. The windowed covariance \(C\) and its inverse comparison are taken on the same active coordinate set; each column of \(N\) uses the inverse on its own panel, extended by zero.

Proposition 8.6. (Weighted response implies a uniform matrix bound) Suppose that, for all sufficiently large \(x\), uniformly in the period, forest, tube, choices and configuration under consideration, the following bounds hold:

  1. \(Q\) and \(C\) are positive definite and \(\mathfrak{p}_0 (Q), \mathfrak{p}_0 (C), \mathfrak{p}_0 (N) \leqslant K\);

  2. \(\mathfrak{p}_0 (M) \leqslant L\);

  3. \(|s_r | \leqslant Z \hspace{0.17em} \varphi (x)\) at every source coordinate \(r \in \mathcal{R}\), where \(K, L, Z > 0\), \(\varphi (x) \geqslant 0\), and \(\varphi (x) \to 0\); the local width satisfies \(0 \leqslant d \leqslant r \varphi (x)\) for a fixed \(r \geqslant 0\);

  4. the window defect obeys

    \(\displaystyle \mathfrak{p}_0 (N - Q^{- 1}) \leqslant u \exp \{- v \hspace{0.17em} \text{radius} (x) / 60\}\)

    for fixed \(u, v > 0\);

  5. \(0 < \beta \leqslant 4 x\) and \(x \geqslant 4\). Since \(g = \text{coupling} (\beta) = \beta^{- 1 / 2}\), we have \(g^{- 1} = \sqrt{\beta} \leqslant 2 \sqrt{x} \leqslant x\). The local interface product is a product of broad factors and obeys this implication: if it is nonzero, then \(|m_i + g \zeta_i | \leqslant 1\) whenever \(|m_i | \leqslant 1\) and \(2 d \leqslant 1\), where \(g = \text{coupling} (\beta)\) and \(\zeta\) is the noise vector.

Then there is a fixed \(\ell \geqslant 1\) such that, eventually in \(x\), the following four bounds hold for every configuration row in this family:

\(\displaystyle |m_i | \leqslant 1, \qquad |r_i | \leqslant 1, \qquad |z_i^{\text{aux}} | \leqslant x^{- 4}, \qquad \text{interface product} \ne 0 \Longrightarrow | \zeta_i | \leqslant x^2 .\)

Moreover the screened palette matrix \(C\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{p}_0 (C) \leqslant \ell .\)

Proof. For any rectangular matrix \(A\) with \(\mathfrak{p}_0 (A) \leqslant c\) and any vector \(u\) on its column set with \(\|u\|_{\infty} \leqslant b_0\), the triangle inequality gives

\(\displaystyle | (Au)_i | \leqslant \sum_j |A_{ij} | \hspace{0.17em} |u_j | \leqslant cb_0 .\)

The move-map bound first gives \(|b_i | = | (M^{\textsf{T}} s)_i | \leqslant LZ \varphi (x)\) by its column bound. Apply the row-and-column estimate to \(N^{\textsf{T}} b\). The column bound for \(N\) is the row bound for its transpose, so \(|m_i | \leqslant KLZ \varphi (x)\). Applying the same calculation to \(Mm + s\) gives

\(\displaystyle |r_i | \leqslant (L^2 KZ + Z) \varphi (x) .\)

For the auxiliary row, factor the displayed identity exactly as written:

\(\displaystyle |z_i^{\text{aux}} | \leqslant x \hspace{0.17em} KuLZ \varphi (x) e^{- v \text{radius} (x) / 60} .\)

Because \(\text{radius} (x)\) grows quadratically in \(\log x\), the exponential factor is eventually at most \(x^{- 5}\). The right side is then at most \(KuLZ \varphi (x) x^{- 4}\). Since \(K, u, L, Z\) are fixed and \(\varphi (x) \to 0\), its coefficient is eventually at most one; hence \(|z_i^{\text{aux}} | \leqslant x^{- 4}\). The first two row bounds also tend to zero, hence are eventually at most one. The width condition and \(\varphi (x) \to 0\) ensure \(2 d \leqslant 1\) eventually. If the interface product is nonzero, its broad-span implication gives \(|m_i + g \zeta_i | \leqslant 1\). Thus \(|g \zeta_i | \leqslant 2\), and the coupling-bound hypothesis gives

\(\displaystyle | \zeta_i | \leqslant 2 g^{- 1} \leqslant 2 x \leqslant x^2\)

for \(x \geqslant 2\). Finally, the rate-zero bound of \(C\) is already at most \(K\); take \(\ell = \max (1, K)\). This proves all conclusions with one threshold, independent of the period and of the subsequently chosen configuration.\(\Box\)

The four conclusions, together with \(\mathfrak{p}_0 (C) \leqslant \ell\), are the complete response receipt needed here: they give a positive matrix bound at rate zero, a mean bound, a direct-row bound, an auxiliary-row bound of order \(x^{- 4}\), and the implication from a nonzero interface product to the noise bound \(x^2\). Subsection 8.16 supplies the distinct global matrix, source-row, and move-row estimates used in this calculation.

In the selected application, Subsection 8.16 supplies the matrix bound and gap for \(Q\), its panelized covariance \(C\), the windowed matrix \(N\), and the exponentially small bound for \(N - Q^{- 1}\). It also supplies the source-row bound \(Z \text{fineCut} (x)\) and the move-row bound \(L\) when the local width is strictly positive. Its panel error is stated with \(\lfloor B / 60 \rfloor\); since \(\lfloor B / 60 \rfloor \geqslant B / 60 - 1\), enlarging \(u\) by the fixed factor \(e^v\) gives the form used in the proposition. The calculation above is included because it shows exactly how these different receipts combine, with no factor depending on the number of sites. The local covariance comparison of Subsection 14.10 is a distinct input; its entrywise estimate cannot replace these global row-and-column bounds.

8.17.1Uniformity over the matched configurations

For a pilot \(p\), let \(\Pi_p\) be its endpoint palette, namely the normalized Gaussian matrix on the forest coordinates after retaining the panel addresses. A palette has a \(\sigma\)-bound \(C\) when its two weighted sums above are at most \(C\). The endpoint convention identifies \(\Pi_p\) exactly with the clouded panel Gram matrix \(C\); it is not an approximate identification and involves no limiting field.

For each matched pilot, specialize the uniform response estimate to that pilot's store, forest, configuration and tag. The endpoint palette then satisfies

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{p}_0 (\Pi_p) \leqslant \ell \qquad \text{for every matched } p.\)

Because the matrix bounds were obtained uniformly before selecting the pilot, the same constant works for the entire matched family. This universal quantifier comes from all-configuration screened-kernel bounds, not from testing a dense set of readers or passing from one posed pilot by continuity.

8.17.2The local mask identity

Fix a matched pilot \(p\) and a mask address \(i\), and put \(q_i = \text{toes} (i)\) and \(B = \text{radius} (x)\). Write \(z_p\) for its local fluctuation vector and \(g = \text{coupling} (\beta) > 0\). For a site \(o\), define \(\textsf{Toned} (R, o, z, h)\) to mean \(|z_j | \leqslant h\) for each active coordinate \(j\) whose anchor is at distance at most \(R\) from \(o\). The local small-noise condition is

\(\displaystyle \textsf{Toned} (2 B, q_i, z_p, \varepsilon \hspace{0.17em} t / g), \qquad t = \text{fineCut} (x) .\)

By definition, the socket is zero when the support foot meets the store's radius-\(300 B\) dome. It remains to treat the branch in which the foot and that dome are disjoint. The foot contains its toe \(q_i\). Since the radius-\(50 B\) dome is contained in the radius-\(300 B\) dome, disjointness immediately gives \(q_i \not\in A.dome (50 B)\).

The beacon receipt is evaluated on the actual selected configuration, store, tag and mean-adjusted field. At every site \(y\) outside the radius-\(40 B\) store dome, it gives active tape coordinates of norm below the chart radius, fine-face toll at most \(t / 4\), coarse-bond wire at most \(Wt\), and both normalized likelihoods equal to one, provided \(\textsf{Toned} (B, y, z_p, \varepsilon t / g)\) holds. The toe is outside the radius-\(50 B\) dome. Every site within distance \(B\) of the toe is therefore outside the radius-\(40 B\) dome. Restricting the radius-\(2 B\) toned bound gives the required radius-\(B\) bound at these sites. The face, bond-wire and frame parts of the beacon receipt consequently give the local outpost condition.

The port estimate turns this local condition into the interface bound. Let \(N_{\text{port}}\) be the number of plaquettes in the fixed macrobox for the patch (the macrobox site count times the number of plaquette orientations). The port inequality sums at most \(N_{\text{port}}\) face tolls, and the braid inequality bounds the connector by \(Wt\). Thus the chord of the relative link at any active row is at most \((C_0 N_{\text{port}} + W) t\), where \(C_0\) is the fixed port constant. On the chart ball, the logarithm has norm at most \(K_0\) times this chord. The beacon places the actual relative link in the injectivity ball, so its logarithm is the actual tape coordinate. Therefore the outpost condition and the distant-interface estimate give

\(\displaystyle \| \ell_{p, j} \| \leqslant C_{\ast} t, \qquad C_{\ast} = K_0 (C_0 N_{\text{port}} + W),\)

for each active interface row. An inactive interface factor equals one by its definition. The selected width has \(C_{\ast} t \leqslant \text{arc}\), so the broad factor equals one for active rows too. The integer \(N_{\text{port}}\) is fixed by the patch and does not grow with the torus.

The mask has five coordinate types: fine-face share, coarse-bond wire share, site likelihood, bond likelihood, and interface. The first two equal one because their nonnegative tolls are below their full-share thresholds; the likelihoods equal one by the beacon receipt; and the interface equals one by the preceding argument. Thus the local ask is one, so the socket (ask minus one) is zero. Every estimate is on the same matched pilot; there is no perturbation or replacement of its store or tag.

Proposition 8.7. (Exact assembly of the palette condition) Assume \(\varepsilon \geqslant 0\) and \(\beta > 0\), and the uniform rate-zero response bounds of Proposition 8.6; these give a common bound bound \(\ell \geqslant 1\). Let \(\mathcal{P}\) be the set of matched pilots. Assume the selected field and forest satisfy the common-trajectory response conditions of Subsection 8.16, and that the following uniform local receipts hold for every \(p \in \mathcal{P}\) and mask address: the disjoint-foot branch geometry, beacon estimates, port estimate, and distant-interface estimate just stated. These receipts are on the same field, forest, tag and store, with thresholds uniform over the matched family. Then for

\(\displaystyle q = \varepsilon \hspace{0.17em} \text{fineCut} (x) / \text{coupling} (\beta)\)

the endpoint palette has a uniform Pours bound at rate zero, with a positive bound constant, for every matched pilot. Its socket vanishes whenever the specified \(\textsf{Toned} (2 B, q_i, z_p, \varepsilon t / g)\) condition holds. These are the two components of the all-matched palette condition used by the opacity step.

Proof. The rate-zero matrix estimate is uniform over the matched family, so its constant is the common Pours bound \(\ell\). For each matched pilot and each mask address, the local argument above gives zero socket under its toned hypothesis. Since neither conclusion changes the pilot, the quantified statements hold for the full matched family.\(\Box\)

8.17.3The combined matrix and mask estimates

The geometric and local receipts are selected together. For a fixed patch, a permitted chart ratio, a positive mask width \(W\), and the reserve and wedge hypotheses of the sector construction, there is a lower scale \(L_0\) and a beacon parameter \(\varepsilon > 0\). For each fixed chosen width \(\lambda_{\text{w}} \geqslant L_0\), the chart and size conditions, the annular response at width \(\lambda_{\text{w}}\), the local beacon, and the distant-interface estimate all hold eventually in \(x\). Their cutoff is uniform over legal stores, forests, tags, configurations and mask addresses, hence over matched pilots. The port/log-chart and distant-interface route gives the fixed constant \(C_{\ast}\) above; choose \(L_0 \geqslant C_{\ast}\), so \(C_{\ast} t \leqslant \lambda_{\text{w}} t = \text{arc}\). The annular estimate is a separate component of the selected package.

Here is the mean-switch argument that makes the beacon a statement about the actual sewn field. The centered affine background (CAB) calibration is an auxiliary calculation; its matrices must not be identified with the actual-reader matrices \(Q, C, N\) above. Fix one selected store, forest, tag, field \(V\), and a site \(o\) outside the radius-\(30 B\) store dome, where \(B = \text{radiusB} (x)\). Write \(\varepsilon_0 = 2 \varepsilon\), where \(\varepsilon\) is the final all-matched beacon fee and \(\varepsilon_0\) is the CAB plateau threshold. The based chart construction supplies a gauge and a chart vector \(b\) with \(\|b\| \leqslant \text{clearance} (x) / 2\), agreeing with that gauge on the radius-\(20 B\) room. Write \(v\) for the corresponding local affine direction. For the auxiliary finite calibration, write \(H\) for its sheet, \(P_{\text{CAB}}\) for its within-sheet map, \(E = HP_{\text{CAB}} - I\) for its error, and \(s_{\text{CAB}}\) for its source. The exact finite-dimensional subtraction identity is

\(\displaystyle \mu_{\text{CAB}} - v = - P_{\text{CAB}}^{\textsf{T}} (Hv + s_{\text{CAB}}) + E^{\textsf{T}} v.\) (8.132)

It follows by expanding \(E = HP_{\text{CAB}} - I\) and the definitions of the centered calibration mean. To state the paid rates without source shorthand, put

\(\displaystyle \rho_{r, k} (x) = \text{radiusB} (x)^k \hspace{0.17em} \text{fineCut} (x)^r .\)

The bought central residual and error-sheet estimates are, respectively, the rates \(\rho_{2, 25}\) and \(\rho_{2, 26}\); the selected scale schedule includes \(\rho_{2, 27} (x) \leqslant x^{- 7 / 8}\) eventually. In the first term of (8.132), the central residual bound \(\rho_{2, 25}\) is multiplied by the fixed within-sheet row bound; one additional factor of the radius is absorbed into the next rate, giving \(\rho_{2, 26}\). The second term is the error-sheet tail, also at that rate. The reserve's allowance growth then gives, coordinatewise,

\(\displaystyle | (\mu_{\text{CAB}} - v)_j | \leqslant \rho_{2, 27} (x) \leqslant x^{- 7 / 8} .\) (8.133)

These are the two matrix terms in (8.132); no equality between the auxiliary calibration law and the actual palette law is used.

The chart comparison of the actual portrait with this same affine direction has a second, separately paid error. The finite chart estimate and the identity between the local face read and its short-row read give a per-color error at most \(x^{- 7 / 8}\); summing over the fixed color coordinates and combining with (8.133) gives

\(\displaystyle \|D'_j - \mu_{\text{CAB}, j} \| \leqslant 2 \dim (G_{\text{color}}) x^{- 7 / 8} \leqslant x^{- 3 / 4}\) (8.134)

for all sufficiently large \(x\). Here \(D'\) is the portrait of the original selected field, in the local chart, and the last inequality is just a fixed-dimension power comparison. If the local fluctuation is \(z\) and \(g = \text{coupling} (\beta) > 0\), the switched active coordinate is exactly

\(\displaystyle (D' + gz - \mu_{\text{CAB}})_j .\)

For any point in the radius-\(B\) neighborhood of \(o\), (8.134) and the Toned bound \(|z_j | \leqslant \varepsilon_0 t / (2 g)\) imply

\(\displaystyle | (D' + gz - \mu_{\text{CAB}})_j | \leqslant x^{- 3 / 4} + \varepsilon_0 t / 2 \leqslant \varepsilon_0 t\)

eventually; the final step uses the bought threshold \(x^{- 3 / 4} \leqslant \varepsilon_0 t / 2\). Thus the shifted affine field is Awake with threshold \(\varepsilon_0 t\) on that neighborhood.

The charged plateau estimate is applied to this auxiliary affine field. Write \(W_{\text{wire}} = W\), \(a_{\ast} = \sqrt{\text{direct}}\), and \(b_{\ast} = \sqrt{\text{penalty}}\). The reserve and wedge purchases give \(a_{\ast} > 0\), \(b_{\ast} > 0\), and the uniform row bound satisfies \(W_{\text{row}} > 0\); these are the positive quadratic and penalty floors in the selected calibration.

\(\displaystyle \frac{C}{L^2} \leqslant \frac{a_{\ast}}{64}, \qquad C + \frac{a_{\ast}}{64} \leqslant \frac{W_{\text{wire}} b_{\ast}}{4} .\) (8.135)

Let \(W_{\text{row}}\) be the bought uniform row bound for the selected CAB system and let \(\omega_{\text{CAB}} > 0\) be its fixed quadratic-response weight. Set \(\delta = a_{\ast} / 64\) and choose the plateau threshold

\(\displaystyle \varepsilon_0 = \min \left\{ 1, \frac{\delta \sqrt{\omega_{\text{CAB}}}}{4 (1 + \sqrt{\dim (G_{\text{color}})}) W_{\text{row}}} \right\} .\) (8.136)

The local target estimate gives target size at most \(\delta t\) under this shift. Here is its payment.
