The action of the CAB mean is bounded by the rate \(\rho_{2, 29}\), which is eventually at most \(W_{\text{row}} \varepsilon_0 t\); the sparse shifted action has the same bound. Thus the full shifted action is at most \(2 W_{\text{row}} \varepsilon_0 t\). In the target expansion, the linear term is at most \(\delta t / 2\) by the choice in (8.136), and the nonlinear term is at most \(\rho_{2, 40} \leqslant \delta t / 2\) by the paid rate comparison. Their sum is at most \(\delta t\). Decreasing a permitted shift threshold preserves these inequalities, so the minimum with one in (8.136) is harmless.

The first inequality in (8.135) bounds the unshifted face response by \(a_{\ast} t / 64\). The paid target response changes it by at most \(a_{\ast} t / 64\), so the resulting row has norm at most \(a_{\ast} t / 32\). The face read is \(a_{\ast}\) times the curvature norm of the face holonomy, so that curvature norm is at most \(t / 32\). On the purchased chart the face holonomy has chord at most the radius \(\delta_G\) of the local curvature estimate \(\tfrac{3}{4} \text{chord} (U)^2 \leqslant \| \kappa_G (U)\|^2\) recalled in Subsection 14.8; hence its chord is at most twice its curvature norm. The face toll is the chord of the face holonomy, so it is at most \(t / 16 < t / 4\). For a bond row the unshifted term is at most \(Ct\) and the response term at most \(a_{\ast} t / 64\). Its normalized read is \(b_{\ast}\) times its curvature norm, so the second inequality in (8.135) gives curvature at most \(W_{\text{wire}} t / 4\). Chord is at most twice curvature on this chart; hence wire is below \(W_{\text{wire}} t\). The likelihood plateau estimate sets the site and bond factors equal to one.

For the broad chart, the reserve bounds each CAB-mean color coordinate by \(\rho_{1, 12}\). The shift coordinates are at most \(\varepsilon_0 t\). Because \(\varepsilon_0 \leqslant 1\) and \(\text{radiusB} (x) \geqslant 1\), \(\varepsilon_0 t \leqslant \rho_{1, 13}\) and \(\rho_{1, 12} \leqslant \rho_{1, 13}\). The Euclidean color norm of the shifted row is at most \(2 \sqrt{\dim (G_{\text{color}})} \rho_{1, 13}\), which the purchased chart budget bounds by the chart radius. Thus the single threshold \(\varepsilon_0\) ensures the target, face, wire and chart conclusions for every allowed calibration and every local shift. The coordinate-to-color step is the elementary inequality \(\|a\| \leqslant \sqrt{\dim (G_{\text{color}})} \max_j |a_j |\).

Finally the CAB construction is sewn back without changing the physical field. Let \(\mathcal{B}\) be the auxiliary configuration map built from the original field \(V\) and its frozen-link data, and let \(a = D' + gz\) be the desired active read. Define \(\chi (a)\) to be \(a - \mu_{\text{CAB}}\) on active coordinates and zero on frozen coordinates. The sewing identity is, link by link,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B} (\mu_{\text{CAB}} + \chi (a)) = V_{\text{sewn}} (a),\) (8.137)

where \(V_{\text{sewn}} (a)\) uses the prescribed tape \(a\) on active links and the original stored values on frozen links. On a trunk both sides are the identity. On an active link the auxiliary mean cancels and leaves exactly the prescribed tape \(a\). On a frozen link the correction is zero and the stored link is unchanged. Thus the plateau bounds apply to the actual sewn configuration. This proves the local beacon receipt used in the socket argument; its uniformity in all stores, forests, tags and periods is the uniformity of the chart, reserve and wedge purchases, before the pilot is chosen.

Now fix one such selected field, with \(0 < \beta \leqslant 4 x\), local width \(0 < d \leqslant ut\), chosen broad-response width \(\lambda_{\text{w}} \geqslant L_0\), and arc \(\lambda_{\text{w}} t\). Suppose its budget certificate gives both the chart compatibility needed for the beacon and the auxiliary sector comparison, and suppose the all-matched response estimate above holds. For each matched pilot the actual response receipt gives the endpoint Pours bound with the common constant \(\ell\). The deterministic argument above gives zero socket under \(\textsf{Toned} (2 B, q_i, z_p, \varepsilon t / \text{coupling} (\beta))\). The fee \(\varepsilon t / \text{coupling} (\beta)\) is nonnegative. Thus one and the same selected field satisfies both palette clauses for all matched pilots. This is the exact two-clause output used in the later opacity estimate; it is not obtained by selecting a different field for each pilot.

In Proposition 8.7 these inputs enter as follows. An enlarged patch supplies the local chart, while the separate port/log-chart estimate and the distant-interface estimate supply the far-interface bound. The annular response supplies its own fixed-scale response receipt; it does not supply the port constant \(C_0\). Combining these with the all-configuration rate-zero matrix estimate gives the two hypotheses of the proposition on one selected sector. The correlated-warning bound of Subsection 8.3 bounds the probability of violating many such local conditions, while Subsection 8.15 proves the deterministic likelihood and row plateaus on the small-fluctuation event. The annular argument in Subsection 8.11 controls the energy outside the packet core. None of these three statements alone is the all-matched Pours estimate; that part remains tied to the global kernel-bound supplier.

8.18The actual Haar density in exponential coordinates

The chart factor in the packet is the density of probability Haar measure. We construct it from the given local exponential and logarithm, keeping its scalar normalization. Write \(\mathfrak{g}\) for the physical colour space, with its counting Lebesgue measure, and \(\exp\) for the group exponential. The local logarithm is inverse to \(\exp\) near zero. After shrinking the radius, \(\exp\) is a homeomorphism from a ball \(D\) about zero onto an open neighbourhood \(U\) of the identity. To check openness, if \(b\) is in a sufficiently small open set \(O\), continuity of the local logarithm gives a neighbourhood of \(\exp b\) whose logarithms lie in \(O\); the local right-inverse identity places that neighbourhood in \(\exp O\).

For a group element \(u\), where the expression stays in the chart set

\(\displaystyle T_u (b) = \log (u \exp b), \qquad H (b) = DT_{(\exp b)^{- 1}} (b), \qquad J (b) = \det H (b) .\)

In the left trivialization, \(H (b) = (dL_{\exp (- b)})_{\exp b} D \exp (b)\). The exponential differential formula [DK00, Theorem 1.5.3, Eq. (1.5.1)] gives

\(\displaystyle H (b) = \int_0^1 e^{- t \text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} b} \hspace{0.17em} dt.\)

Thus \(J\) is analytic with \(J (0) = 1\). Shrink \(D\) so that \(1 / 2 < J < 2\) there. Transporting Lebesgue density at the identity by left translations defines a smooth left-invariant density on all of \(G\). It has finite nonzero total mass by compactness, so its normalization is probability Haar measure by uniqueness. Its pullback to \(D\) has density \(J (b)\) by the left trivialization above. In particular, with \(m_G\) denoting probability Haar measure and \(c_{\text{chart}} = m_G (U) / \int_D J (b) \hspace{0.17em} db\), we have

\(\displaystyle \exp_{\ast} \left( 1_D c_{\text{chart}} J (b) \hspace{0.17em} db \right) = m_G |_U .\) (8.138)

The normalization uses the entire compact group, including all components if \(G\) is disconnected.

8.18.1The protected chart correction

Fix \(a > 0\) and use the prescribed small fluctuation scale \(\tau = P / \sqrt{h} > 0\) from (8.17), where \(h\) is the fine reference coupling. (In (8.17) the letter \(t\) denotes the larger coordinate warning threshold \(\xi \tau \sqrt{\beta}\); it does not occur in this section.) The scale \(\tau\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and \(\tau^{- 1} \leqslant \sqrt{h}\) because \(P \geqslant 1\); thus its reciprocal has upper exponent \(1 / 2\). Let \(\chi : [0, \infty) \to [0, 1]\) be the smooth cutoff, equal to one on \([0, 1]\) and zero on \([4, \infty)\). For a fluctuation bond \(u_i\) set

\(\displaystyle \gamma_a (u_i) = \chi \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{\|u_i \|^2}{a^2 \tau^2} \right), \qquad j_i (u) = \gamma_{2 a} (u_i) \log J (u_i) .\)

Outside the chart this last expression can use any specified measurable extension of \(J\) and the total real logarithm convention; the cutoff makes its value zero there once \(4 a \tau\) is smaller than the chart radius. Where \(\gamma_a (u_i) \ne 0\), we have \(\|u_i \| \leqslant 2 a \tau\), and hence \(\gamma_{2 a} (u_i) = 1\). Positivity of \(J\) then proves

\(\displaystyle e^{j_i (u)} = J (u_i) \quad \text{where } \gamma_a (u_i) \ne 0.\) (8.139)

Thus the doubled cutoff preserves the true Haar factor on the support of the narrower protected integral.

Since \(\log J\) is analytic near zero and vanishes there, the fundamental theorem of calculus on a smaller closed ball gives \(| \log J (v) | \leqslant C \|v\|\). If \(\gamma_{2 a} (u_i)\) is nonzero, \(\|u_i \| \leqslant 4 a \tau\); if it is zero, \(j_i = 0\). Therefore, globally in \(u\),

\(\displaystyle |j_i (u) | \leqslant 4 aC \tau .\) (8.140)

In particular the bound \(|j_i | \leqslant 1\) used in the packet estimate holds at one sufficiently large coupling threshold, uniformly in the period and in the number of chart bonds.

We record the differentiated version with its exact incoming premise. Suppose a variation \(u_i (y)\) is \(C^k\) near \(y_0\), and its increment \(u_i (y) - u_i (y_0)\) has derivatives through order \(k\) bounded by \(v (x)\), where \(v\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). This is the compensated variation supplied by the Gaussian change of variables; its production is separate from the chart calculation here. If \(\gamma_b (u_i (y))\) is zero on a neighbourhood of \(y_0\), all its jets vanish. Otherwise continuity gives \(\|u_i (y_0)\| \leqslant 2 b \tau\). Thus every derivative, including order zero, of \(u_i\) at \(y_0\) is bounded by \(v + 2 b \tau\). For \(g = u_i / (b \tau)\) these bounds are at most

\(\displaystyle K_b = (b \tau)^{- 1} (v + 2 b \tau),\)

which has upper exponent zero. The derivatives of \(\|g\|^2\) are bounded by a fixed polynomial in \(K_b\); this follows directly by the product rule for the inner product. Derivatives of \(\chi\) are bounded on \([0, 4]\), and its value is at most one. The chain rule, indexed by partitions of the derivative arguments, therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \|D^r \gamma_b (u_i (y_0))\| \leqslant L_b (x)^r, \qquad 0 \leqslant r \leqslant k,\) (8.141)

for an \(L_b \geqslant 1\) of upper exponent zero. The zero-order inequality uses the range of \(\chi\); a uniform amplitude from the positive-order bounds can be absorbed into \(L_b\).

Apply this to the broader cutoff \(b = 2 a\). If its germ vanishes, so do all jets of \(j_i\). Otherwise \(\|u_i (y_0)\| \leqslant 4 a \tau\), and all jets of \(u_i\) are bounded by \(V = v + 4 a \tau\), which tends to zero. All derivatives of \(\log J\) through order \(k\) are bounded on a fixed smaller chart ball. For positive derivative order, each partition term in the chain rule contains at least one derivative of \(u_i\); since \(V \leqslant 1\), its product is at most \(V\). At order zero use \(| \log J (u_i (y_0)) | \leqslant CV\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \|D^r (\log J \circ u_i) (y_0)\| \leqslant C_k V \quad (0 \leqslant r \leqslant k) .\)

Leibniz's formula with (8.141) yields

\(\displaystyle \|D^r j_i (y_0)\| \leqslant C_k V (1 + L_{2 a})^k \quad (0 \leqslant r \leqslant k) .\) (8.142)

The common right-hand side has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The argument covers points on the mask boundary and arbitrary Gaussian inputs: smallness is inferred from the nonvanishing germ of the protected factor, not assumed for the Gaussian field globally.

8.19Local normalization and its derivatives

The local scalar correction is a difference between the actual sector and its flat reference. The reference uses the same forest, tags, cutoff radius and windows; its coarse field is the identity and its seed sets are empty. Write \(A^{\circ}\) for its completed precision matrix. With the local colour trace \(J_e (A)\) of Subsection 8.13, the correction at a physical bond is

\(\displaystyle c_e = \frac{1}{2} \{J_e (A^{\circ}) - J_e (A)\} .\) (8.143)

We first establish its scalar bounds and then differentiate the actual window formula. A bound on the full determinant would not provide these local estimates.

8.19.1Uniform and remote scalar bounds

Let \(\|A\|_{\eta}, \|A^{\circ} \|_{\eta} \leqslant Q\) and let \(S\) be the uniform row and column bound for every principal inverse along \(A_s = 1 + s (A - 1)\) and the corresponding flat interpolation, \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant 1\). The local formula is

\(\displaystyle J_e (A) = \sum_{\alpha = 1}^q \int_0^1 [(A - 1) P_s]_{(e, \alpha), (e, \alpha)} \hspace{0.17em} ds.\)

Thus \(|J_e (A) | \leqslant q (Q + 1) S\), and the same holds for \(A^{\circ}\). By (8.143),

\(\displaystyle |c_e | \leqslant q (Q + 1) S.\) (8.144)

Both precisions meet the hypotheses of the Gram estimate: the flat reference has no flags, and gauge transformations preserve the flags of the actual sector. Hence the same post-\(L\) constants can be used for the actual, flat and gauge-transformed sectors.

If the anchor of \(e\) is farther than \(30 B\) from the seeds, choose a single gauge making the coarse bond chords at most \(Cw (x)\) on the radius-\(6 B\) ball, as in (8.113). Here \(w\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). For any coordinate in the window of \((e, \alpha)\), its anchor lies within \(B + 2\) of that of \(e\). On these coordinates the inactive diagonal of the gauged sector agrees with the flat reference: exactly the tree bonds are inactive. If an entry of the selected row matrix meets such a coordinate, either its row anchor lies within \(B / 4\) or both entries are zero. In the former case that row is selected in both sectors. The gauge transport comparison, applied to the same unit coordinate input, gives

\(\displaystyle |M'_{pi} - M^{\circ}_{pi} | \leqslant d (x), \qquad d (x) = C_1 (B + 1) w (x) .\)

The same estimate holds for the other tested coordinate. All transport paths lie in the common \(6 B\) hull. Expanding the two products in the Gram entry gives

\(\displaystyle |M'_{pi} M'_{pj} - M^{\circ}_{pi} M^{\circ}_{pj} | \leqslant d (x) |M'_{pj} | + d (x) |M^{\circ}_{pi} | .\)

Sum over \(p\) and use the column bound \(Y\) for both row matrices:

\(\displaystyle |A'_{ij} - A^{\circ}_{ij} | \leqslant 2 Yd (x) = : \delta (x) \quad \text{on each tested principal window} .\) (8.145)

No count of all rows enters this bound.

The window contains at most \(\Delta_B = m (2 B + 5)^4\) coordinates. The local resolvent comparison of Subsection 8.13 now yields

\(\displaystyle |J_e (A') - J_e (A^{\circ}) | \leqslant q \{S + (Q + 1) S^2 \} \Delta_B \delta (x) .\)

The complete colour trace is gauge invariant; the endpoint windows are invariant under colour rotations. Therefore \(J_e (A') = J_e (A)\), and

\(\displaystyle |c_e | \leqslant q \{S + (Q + 1) S^2 \} \Delta_B \delta (x) = : a_0 (x), \qquad d (e, \text{seeds}) > 30 B.\) (8.146)

We dropped a harmless factor \(1 / 2\). Since \(B\) has upper exponent zero, \(a_0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\).

8.19.2Matrix derivative bounds with spatial weights

For a \(C^k\) matrix-valued map \(A (y)\) near \(y_0\), a convenient quantitative statement, for \(f, v, \gamma \geqslant 0\), is the existence of a nonnegative matrix \(H\) such that

\(\displaystyle \|D^r A_{ij} (y_0)\| \leqslant H_{ij} v^r \quad (0 \leqslant r \leqslant k), \qquad \|H\|_{\gamma} \leqslant f.\) (8.147)

The norm on the right is the maximum of the weighted row and column sums. Thus it controls a common entrywise majorant of all jets, not just the norm of each derivative separately. If \(A\) and \(B\) have bounds \((f, v)\) and \((g, z)\) with \(g, z \geqslant 0\), Leibniz's rule gives the envelope \(HK\) for their product with bounds \((fg, v + z)\): the binomial sum is \((v + z)^r\), and the triangle inequality for distance proves \(\|HK\|_{\gamma} \leqslant \|H\|_{\gamma} \|K\|_{\gamma}\). Addition, transpose and restriction to a principal set preserve the corresponding bounds. These operations introduce no volume factor.

Suppose \(A (y_0)\) is invertible and its inverse has weighted bound \(g\). Set \(F = A (y_0)^{- 1}\) and \(V (y) = 1 - FA (y)\). Then \(V (y_0) = 0\), and, wherever \(A (y)\) is invertible,

\(\displaystyle A (y)^{- 1} = \sum_{r = 0}^k V (y)^r F + V (y)^{k + 1} A (y)^{- 1} .\) (8.148)

Multiplying the finite geometric identity on the right by \(A (y)^{- 1}\) proves this without commuting any factors. Invertibility holds on a neighbourhood by continuity of the determinant. The inverse is \(C^k\) there by the adjugate formula. Every derivative of order at most \(k\) of \(V^{k + 1}\) vanishes at \(y_0\): in each Leibniz term at least one of the \(k + 1\) factors is undifferentiated and equals zero. The remainder in (8.148) consequently has zero jets through order \(k\).

The envelope for \(V\) has bounds \((1 + fg, v)\). Applying the product rules to the finite sum proves an inverse envelope with bounds

\(\displaystyle T_k (f, g) = \sum_{r = 0}^k (1 + fg)^r g, \qquad kv.\) (8.149)

Only the inverse at the base point was priced. A uniform inverse bound on an entire neighbourhood was not assumed.

Apply this argument separately in every prescribed principal window. Each restriction of \(A (y)\) retains the envelope \((f, v)\), and each base inverse has weighted bound \(S\). Its \((i, j)\) entry has majorant \(T_k (f, S) e^{- \gamma d (i, j)}\). Padding it by zero outside the window gives a common envelope for \(P (y)\), because window membership is fixed. Both its row and column sums are bounded by

\(\displaystyle F_B = \Delta_B T_k (f, S), \qquad \text{with derivative scale } kv.\) (8.150)

For the row bound, even though different columns use different windows, any nonzero entry has \(d (i, j) \leqslant B + 2\), and there are at most \(\Delta_B\) such \(j\). The same count proves the column bound.

The error \(A (y) P (y) - 1\) vanishes, as a germ, whenever \(d (i, j) \leqslant B\). Indeed each such coordinate belongs to the window, and its principal inverse solves the corresponding row equation for all nearby \(y\). Erasing these zero jets and decreasing the spatial weight from \(\gamma\) to \(\gamma / 2\) gives an envelope with bound

\(\displaystyle e^{- \gamma B / 2} (fF_B + 1), \qquad \text{derivative scale } v + kv.\) (8.151)

This is the differentiated version of the boundary error. Multiplying by the full inverse envelope also bounds \(P - A^{- 1}\); taking the symmetric part and then multiplying by \(A\) bounds the covariance defect \(A (P + P^{\ast}) / 2 - 1\). For fixed \(k\) all these bounds are a polynomial in \(B\) times \(e^{- \gamma B / 2}\), hence have every prescribed negative upper exponent. The derivative scales are fixed multiples of \(v\).

8.19.3The actual sector matrix supplies the premise

Freeze the seeds, tree, tags, windows and active sets while varying the coarse field \(U (y)\). Suppose every link matrix has jets

\(\displaystyle \|D^r U_e (y_0)\|_{\text{F}} \leqslant R_G v^r, \qquad R_G = \sqrt{\dim (\text{matrix representation})}, \quad 0 \leqslant r \leqslant k.\)

The same bound holds for inverses by taking the adjoint, for signed pullbacks, and for replicas. Constant gauge transformations preserve it by unitary invariance of the Frobenius norm.

A product along a walk of length \(n\) has amplitude \(R_G\) and derivative scale \(nR_G v\). To verify the absence of an exponential amplitude, split its first factor as \(U (y_0) + (U (y) - U (y_0))\). Multiplication by \(U (y_0)\) is an isometry; in every positive-order term from the difference, its bound \(R_G v^r\) is at most \((R_G v)^r\). The binomial formula therefore adds \(R_G v\) to the derivative scale while preserving the amplitude of the remaining product. Induction proves the claim, including inverse letters and repeated physical links.

For the selected sector row matrix, a single coordinate input is transported from its physical bond to the row anchor. The coordinate path has length at most \(4 B\). A reversed signed bond adds its replica adjoint, whose derivative scale is \(2 v\). The two sides of the transported adjoint add twice the path scale. Thus the transported input has fixed amplitude and derivative scale

\(\displaystyle z = 8 BR_G v + 2 v.\) (8.152)

The remaining cut flat row is a fixed linear map. The weighted impulse estimate from Subsection 8.12, before periodization, bounds its operator norm by \(Ce^{- \eta d (p, i)}\). The extra one-step shift from a reversed bond is absorbed into this fixed \(C\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \|D^r M_{pi} (y_0)\| \leqslant Ce^{- \eta d (p, i)} z^r .\)

Inactive columns, unselected rows and entries outside the cutoff are identically zero throughout the variation; their indicators are not differentiated.

For \(0 < \gamma < \eta\), sum the displayed envelope using the bounded number of row and column coordinates per coarse site. The shell sum \(\sum_{n \geqslant 0} (2 n + 1)^4 e^{- (\eta - \gamma) n}\) is finite. It follows that \(M\) has an envelope \((C_{\gamma}, z)\), with \(C_{\gamma}\) independent of the volume and of \(B\). The identity \(A = M^{\ast} M + \Pi_{\text{inactive}}\) therefore supplies (8.147) with

\(\displaystyle f = C_{\gamma}^2 + 1, \qquad v_A = 2 z.\)

The inactive projection is constant. If the incoming link scale \(v\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), so do \(z\) and \(v_A\).

8.19.4Differentiating the local logarithmic trace

Choose \(\gamma\) also below the retained inverse width. The glide \(A_s (y) = 1 + s (A (y) - 1)\) has envelope \((f + 2, v_A)\) uniformly for \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant 1\). Its principal inverses at \(y_0\) satisfy the same uniform bound \(S\) as before. Multiplication by \(A (y) - 1\) and (8.150) consequently give, for every local diagonal entry, the amplitude

\(\displaystyle (f + 1) \Delta_B T_k (f + 2, S)\)

and derivative scale \((k + 1) v_A\), uniformly in \(s\).

To justify differentiation under the integral, note that the principal determinants are nonzero at every \((y_0, s)\) on the compact interval. There are finitely many windows. Joint continuity gives one neighbourhood of \(y_0\) on which all these determinants remain nonzero for all \(s \in [0, 1]\). The adjugate formula makes the integrand jointly \(C^k\) there. On a smaller closed parameter ball times the interval, all derivatives are bounded by compactness. Difference quotients and the fundamental theorem of calculus, followed by dominated convergence, now permit differentiation under the integral, successively through order \(k\). For this argument the variation space is finite dimensional, as it is for each finite-volume field variation. The local bounds at \(y_0\) are unaffected by the auxiliary compactness bound. Summing the \(q\) colour entries proves

\(\displaystyle \|D^r J_e (A (y_0))\| \leqslant q (f + 1) \Delta_B T_k (f + 2, S) \{(k + 1) v_A \}^r, \quad 0 \leqslant r \leqslant k.\) (8.153)

The flat-reference term in (8.143) is constant in \(y\). Combining its scalar bound with (8.153), and enlarging the harmless factor \(1 / 2\), gives an envelope for \(c_e (y)\) with amplitude \(b (x)\) of upper exponent zero and derivative scale \(z_1 (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). It follows that all its jets are bounded by \(b (x) (1 + z_1 (x))^k\), again of upper exponent zero. At a remote bond we can do better: its value is at most \(a_0 (x)\) by (8.146), while for positive orders, once \(0 \leqslant z_1 \leqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \|D^r c_e (y_0)\| \leqslant b (x) z_1 (x)^r \leqslant b (x) z_1 (x) .\)

Thus the common bound \(a_0 + bz_1\), of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), controls all jets through order \(k\) at remote bonds. This obtains small normalization jets without differentiating a chosen local gauge. The needed link variation and its coherent Gaussian compensation remain distinct inputs to the eventual packet variation.

8.20Varying the coarse field while holding the fine field fixed

Differentiating a packet directly in its coarse field would also change the fine configuration represented by its fluctuation variable. The resulting derivatives would meet the fine masks and branch choices. We instead make the actual compensated change of variables. It is a global bijection on the fluctuation space near the chosen coarse configuration, and agrees with the required logarithmic adjustment where the protected integrand can be nonzero.

8.20.1A globally defined adjustment of one logarithm

We first specify the logarithm on nearby ambient matrices. As in Subsection 8.8, regard \(\mathcal{M}= M_N (\mathbb{C})\) as a real inner-product space with \(\langle A, B \rangle = \text{Retr} (A^{\ast} B)\), and split it as the colour space \(\mathfrak{g}\) and its orthogonal complement \(\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\). The map \((u, n) \mapsto \exp (u) \exp (n)\) on \(\mathfrak{g} \times \mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\) has derivative \((u, n) \mapsto u + n\) at \((0, 0)\), which is invertible. By the inverse function theorem there is a neighbourhood \(O\) of \((0, 0)\) in \(\mathfrak{g} \times \mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\) such that this map is a diffeomorphism of \(O\) onto an open neighbourhood \(V\) of the identity \(1\) in \(\mathcal{M}\). Its inverse \(V \to O\) is smooth; let \(\text{Log}_{\ast} : V \to \mathfrak{g}\) be the colour component of this inverse. In particular, \(\text{Log}_{\ast}\) is colour-valued even when its matrix argument is not a group element.

For a group element \(g\) near the identity, \(\text{Log}_{\ast} (g)\) is the local group logarithm. This uses one fact from the theory of matrix Lie groups, already used in Subsection 8.8: by Cartan's closed-subgroup theorem in the form of [Hal15, Corollary 3.44], there is \(\theta > 0\) such that every \(g \in G\) with \(\|g - 1\| < \theta\) equals \(\exp X\) for some \(X \in \mathfrak{g}\), with \(X \to 0\) as \(g \to 1\). For such \(g\) close enough to \(1\), the pair \((X, 0)\) lies in \(O\) and is mapped to \(g\), so \(g \in V\) and, the map being injective on \(O\), \(\text{Log}_{\ast} (g) = X\). Without this theorem one would know only that \(g\) is a matrix near \(1\), and its local preimage could have a nonzero \(\mathfrak{g}^{\perp}\) component.

Choose fixed \(d_{\ast}, A > 0\) so small that

\(\displaystyle \text{Log}_{\ast} (\exp u) = u \quad (u \in \mathfrak{g}, \|u\| \leqslant 2 d_{\ast})\)

and this extension is smooth at \(\exp (u) M\) whenever \(\|u\| \leqslant 2 d_{\ast}\) and \(\|M - 1\| \leqslant A\). Also require

\(\displaystyle \exp \text{Log}_{\ast} (\exp (u) g) = \exp (u) g \qquad (u \in \mathfrak{g}, \|u\| \leqslant d_{\ast}, g \in G, \|g - 1\| \leqslant A) .\)

This identity is asserted only for group elements \(g\): for a matrix \(X\) outside \(G\) the left-hand side lies in \(G\) and the right-hand side does not. Such constants exist by continuity of multiplication and the exponential at \((0, 1)\), after choosing a smaller product neighbourhood inside the given logarithm domain; for the last identity note that \(\exp (u) g \in G\) is close to \(1\), so the previous paragraph applies to it. The constants are chosen before the coupling threshold and before the particular cutoff. The fixed chart radius \(d_{\ast}\) is not the growing scale \(d = 1 + B + (\log h)^2\) of (8.17).

Using the same smooth cutoff as before, define

\(\displaystyle \psi (M, u) = \chi (\|u\|^2 / d_{\ast}^2) \{ \text{Log}_{\ast} (\exp (u) M) - u\}, \qquad F_M (u) = u + \psi (M, u) .\)

Here group elements are represented by their unitary matrices in the logarithm expression. The correction is zero for \(\|u\| \geqslant 2 d_{\ast}\). On the closed ball it is smooth in \((M, u)\); outside the ball it vanishes locally, so these formulas define a smooth map everywhere for \(M\) sufficiently near \(1\). At \(M = 1\) the correction and all its \(u\) derivatives vanish.

Compactness of \(\|u\| \leqslant 2 d_{\ast}\) now supplies one neighbourhood of \(M = 1\) on which, for every \(u\),

\(\displaystyle \| \psi (M, u)\| < d_{\ast} / 2, \qquad \|D_u \psi (M, u)\| < 1 / 2.\) (8.154)

Indeed these inequalities hold at \((1, u)\), and their continuous left-hand sides are uniformly small on that compact set; off it they are zero. Integration of the derivative on line segments shows that \(\psi (M, \cdot)\) is \(1 / 2\)-Lipschitz. Thus \(F_M\) is injective, since \(F_M (u) = F_M (v)\) implies \(\|u - v\| \leqslant \|u - v\| / 2\). For every \(z\), iteration of \(u \mapsto z - \psi (M, u)\) is a contraction on the complete colour space. Its iterates are Cauchy because successive distances form a geometric series; their limit is its unique fixed point and solves \(F_M (u) = z\). Hence \(F_M\) is surjective as well.

Its derivative is \(1 + D_u \psi\), which is invertible by the convergent geometric series. The determinant

\(\displaystyle p (M, u) = \det (1 + D_u \psi (M, u))\)

is positive for all \(u\) after a further uniform restriction on \(M\): it equals one at \(M = 1\), is continuous on the compact \(u\) ball, and is one outside it. Thus \(F_M\) is a smooth bijection with positive Jacobian. Its local smooth inverses agree by injectivity, giving a global smooth inverse.

For an actual group element \(g\) near \(1\) and \(\|u\| \leqslant d_{\ast}\), the cutoff equals one. Consequently

\(\displaystyle \exp (F_g (u)) = \exp (u) g.\) (8.155)

The map is global, but this exact group identity is used only within that smaller ball.

8.20.2The coherent coarse and fluctuation variables

Fix a sector with coarse field \(V_0\), and keep its seeds, forest, tags, frozen fine bonds and active sets fixed. In the affine source replace the clipped coarse guide by its unmasked path-field formula; let \(\tilde{\ell} (V)\) be the resulting linear source and put

\(\displaystyle \nu (V) = - P (V)^{\ast} \tilde{\ell} (V)\)

in physical colour coordinates. This specifies the extension used in the change of variables. At a valid sector, every selected row has a clean neighbourhood. The clipping is then inactive at every read of that row, as proved in the guide comparison. Its unmasked source equals the original source, and hence \(\nu (V_0) = \mu\). The same statement holds at any other valid sector. Away from valid sectors the unmasked expression is retained as its definition.

Let \(g_{\beta} = \beta^{- 1 / 2} > 0\) and identify a counting Gaussian coordinate \(z\) with its colour vector on each fine bond. The extended fluctuation field is

\(\displaystyle u_0 = \nu (V_0) + g_{\beta} z.\)

For a nearby coarse field \(V\), define separately at each fine bond

\(\displaystyle M_i (V) = \bar{V}_{0, i} \bar{V}_i^{- 1}, \quad u_i (V) = F_{M_i (V)} (u_{0, i}), \quad z_i (V) = g_{\beta}^{- 1} \{u_i (V) - \nu_i (V)\} .\) (8.156)

The replica is the prescribed one. It follows identically that \(\nu (V) + g_{\beta} z (V) = u (V)\), and at \(V = V_0\) the map is the identity on \(z\).

The fine field on an active bond is \(\exp (u_i) \bar{V}_i\); on an inactive bond it is the fixed original fine field. Suppose \(8 a \tau \leqslant d_{\ast} / 2\) and the varied active fluctuation satisfies \(\|u_i (V)\| \leqslant 8 a \tau\). By (8.154),

\(\displaystyle \|u_{0, i} \| \leqslant \|u_i (V)\| + \| \psi (M_i (V), u_{0, i})\| < d_{\ast} .\)

Equation (8.155) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \exp (u_i (V)) \bar{V}_i = \exp (u_{0, i}) \bar{V}_{0, i} .\) (8.157)

Inactive bonds agree by definition. On the protected support all fine reads are consequently fixed, including their masks, likelihoods and selected branch tags. The larger \(8 a \tau\) allowance is deliberate: at a point where the broader cutoff germ is not identically zero, continuity first gives \(\|u_i (V_0)\| \leqslant 4 a \tau\), then gives the strict \(8 a \tau\) bound on a neighbourhood. A finite set of protected bonds has one common such neighbourhood. No condition is imposed on the unprotected Gaussian coordinates.

8.20.3Uniform derivative bounds for the compensation

Fix the derivative order \(k\). On \(\{1\} \times \{u : \|u\| \leqslant 2 d_{\ast} \}\), all derivatives in \(M\) of \(\psi\) and \(p\) through order \(k\) have fixed bounds, by smoothness and compactness. Outside this set their \(u\)-sections are respectively zero and one as functions of \(M\). Hence these bounds hold for every \(u \in \mathfrak{g}\), with constants independent of the Gaussian coordinate. Moreover \(\psi (1, u) = 0\) and \(p (1, u) = 1\).

Suppose the coarse variation \(V (y)\) has the link derivative bounds of Subsection 8.19, with \(V (y_0) = V_0\) and scale \(v (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Inversion, replication and multiplication by the fixed unitary replica show that \(M_i (V (y))\) has the same bound. In a chain-rule term of positive order \(r \leqslant k\), the product of derivatives of \(M_i\) has total scale \(v^r\); its amplitude is bounded by a constant depending only on \(k\) and the representation. There are finitely many partitions of \(r\). Thus, for every \(u_{0, i}\),

\(\displaystyle \|D^r_y \psi (M_i (V (y_0)), u_{0, i})\| \leqslant C_k v^r, \qquad \|D^r_y p (M_i (V (y_0)), u_{0, i})\| \leqslant C'_k v^r \quad (1 \leqslant r \leqslant k) .\)

Their zero-order values are zero and one. Since \(v \to 0\), enlarge constants and obtain

\(\displaystyle \|D^r_y (u_i (y) - u_{0, i}) |_{y = y_0} \| \leqslant s (x), \qquad \|D^r_y p (M_i (V (y)), u_{0, i}) |_{y = y_0} \| \leqslant D (x)^r \quad (0 \leqslant r \leqslant k),\) (8.158)

where \(s \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and \(D \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent zero. For the first bound use \(C_k v\) for positive orders and zero at order zero; for the second take \(D = \max (1, C'_k) v\), increasing the fixed constant if necessary. For \(r \geqslant 1\), \(\max (1, C'_k)^r \geqslant C'_k\). At order zero the second bound is equality, with the convention \(D^0 = 1\). These are precisely the compensated increment bounds used in Subsection 8.18. They are uniform over all Gaussian samples; they do not require a bound on \(z_i (y)\) or on the derivatives of the mean extension.

The incoming link-variation premise itself holds for the prescribed scaled exponential variations. For example, let \(V_i (y) = \exp (\epsilon L_i y) V_{0, i}\), where the linear maps \(L_i\) have uniformly bounded norm and \(y\) stays in a fixed bounded set. The derivatives of the matrix exponential are uniformly bounded on the resulting fixed colour ball. The linear chain rule contributes \(\epsilon^r\) at order \(r\). Right multiplication by \(V_{0, i}\) is an isometry. Enlarging one fixed constant therefore gives the link bound with \(v = C_k \epsilon\). In particular the required scale holds when \(\epsilon\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), as for the prescribed inverse-square-root coupling normalization. Finite collections of marked directions are handled by taking the maximum of their norms.

8.20.4The exact finite-dimensional Jacobian

For a fixed nearby \(V\), the map in (8.156) acts separately on the colour coordinate of every fine bond. On a finite set \(P\) of bonds it is conjugate, by the fixed coordinate regrouping, to the product of maps

\(\displaystyle z_i \longmapsto g_{\beta}^{- 1} \{F_{M_i (V)} (\nu_i (V_0) + g_{\beta} z_i) - \nu_i (V)\} .\)

Each is a bijection. Its derivative is exactly \(DF_{M_i (V)} (u_{0, i})\): the two scalar factors \(g_{\beta}^{- 1}\) and \(g_{\beta}\) cancel. The derivative of the product is block diagonal, so its determinant is

\(\displaystyle \prod_{i \in P} p (M_i (V), u_{0, i}) > 0.\) (8.159)

A fixed regrouping of counting coordinates conjugates the derivative and does not change its determinant. This remains true if the regrouped spaces are temporarily given different finite-dimensional norms. For the empty set the map is the identity of the zero-dimensional space and the product is one.

The ordinary change-of-variables formula therefore gives, for every nonnegative measurable test \(f\) on these coordinates (and hence for every integrable test),

\(\displaystyle \int f (z') \hspace{0.17em} dz' = \int f (z (V)) \prod_{i \in P} p (M_i (V), u_{0, i}) \hspace{0.17em} dz.\)

Together with (8.157) and (8.158), this supplies the coherent protected variation and its exact Jacobian. Differentiating a Gaussian density under this substitution still requires its own covariance and mean score bounds; those do not follow from this Jacobian calculation.

8.21Derivatives of the mean and of the local action

We now prove the differentiated estimates needed for the logarithm of a packet. The pointwise comparison with a stationary response is not differentiated: its gauge was chosen from the base configuration. Instead we use that one fixed gauge to express nearby configurations in logarithmic coordinates, estimate the smooth unmasked expressions there, and finally restore the original gauge. This distinction keeps the argument valid at the boundary of a cutoff.

Fix a derivative order \(j\), a patch, the branch constants, the forest, tags and sector indicators. Assume the uniform path bounds, clean-sector conditions and positive precision bounds of Sections 8.12–8.19. All these choices precede the large-\(x\) limit. A coarse variation \(V (y)\) is measured by its matrix derivatives on each bond:

\(\displaystyle \|D^r \rho (V_e) (y_0)\| \leqslant R_G v (x)^r \quad (0 \leqslant r \leqslant j), \qquad v \geqslant 0,\)

where \(R_G\) is the fixed representation bound and \(v\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). We use the derivative notation \(\mathcal{B}_j\) of Subsection 8.25; the same definition applies to colour-valued and operator-valued functions.

8.21.1Uniform bounds for the actual predictor branch

The analytic branch of Subsection 8.8 gives bounds of every fixed finite order on a neighbourhood of the identity. We need that neighbourhood to be independent of the torus size. Here is the finite-read argument that ensures this.

For a fixed patch and direction, take the union of the sites visited by all frame paths, their translates to the neighbouring coarse root, and all the straight connectors of length \(L\). This is a finite subset of the covering lattice. Choose a reference torus large enough that lifting after wrapping fixes every site in this union, and with distinct neighbouring coarse roots. There are only finitely many tag choices and output bonds on this reference torus. For each, analyticity gives a positive logarithmic radius and a bound on derivatives through order \(k\). Take the minimum of the radii and one plus the sum of the bounds.

For a bond on any other torus, pull its logarithmic coordinates to the reference torus by translation and wrapping. This linear map has norm at most one in the supremum norm, even when some input coordinates coincide. Assign the two reference frame tags the actual tags at the two endpoints, and assign the actual predictor tag to the reference bond. Every read path has the same transport. Thus the two frame score functions coincide; uniqueness on the minimizing branch, and the explicit axial formula on its complement, identify the frames. The connector and predictor then coincide as well. Composition with the norm-one linear map transfers the derivative bound without a dimension factor.

Consequently, for each \(k\) there are \(r, C > 0\), independent of the period, such that the actual predictor on logarithmic fields of norm at most \(r\) belongs to \(\mathcal{B}_k (C, 1)\) on each output bond. Shrink \(r\) once more to use the logarithm and exponential charts. The chain rule shows that a near-identity group variation with derivative scale \(v\) has predictor derivative scale \(C_k v\). This statement includes the original tags and the branch tests.

In particular, on a fixed ball in pairs \((b, u)\) of coarse and fine logarithmic fields, the four fields

\(\displaystyle V = \exp b, \qquad U = \exp u \hspace{0.17em} \text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V, \qquad Q (U), \qquad Q (\text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V)\)

have uniform derivatives of every prescribed finite order. Indeed both coordinate projections have norm one; differentiation of the exponential on a fixed compact chart, replication, and the unitary product rule give uniform bounds for \(V, U\). Their chords are bounded by a fixed multiple of \(\max (\|b\|, \|u\|)\), so the predictor estimate just proved applies after shrinking the ball.

Let \(f (b, u)\) be one unmasked affine residual at zero fluctuation, namely the fine or predictor base stroke minus its transported coarse guide, with coarse field \(\exp b\). It does not depend on \(u\), but retaining the pair space allows the same chart for all local actions. The preceding bounds and the absolute path bounds imply

\(\displaystyle f \in \mathcal{B}_k (A (x), 1 ; (b, u)) \quad \text{for } \|(b, u)\| \leqslant r, \qquad f (0, 0) = 0,\) (8.160)

where \(A\) has upper exponent zero. To see the rate assertion, each cut path has length at most a fixed multiple of \(R + 1\) and bounded absolute coefficient sum. Transport adds a derivative scale \(C (R + 1)\); a guide sums over at most \(C (R + 1)^4\) inputs with bounded coefficients. At fixed order, the product rule therefore costs only a polynomial in \(R + 1\). The predictor bounds are fixed constants. Here \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\) has upper exponent zero. At the identity all curvature strokes vanish, the predictor equals the identity, and the guides are linear in zero coarse strokes, which proves the last equality in (8.160).

8.21.2Recovering small derivatives by flat interpolation

We give the interpolation step explicitly because its constants are independent of the dimension of the logarithmic field space. Let \(f : E \to F\) be \(C^k\) near the closed ball of radius \(B_0\) in a finite-dimensional normed space, with \(k \geqslant 3\). Suppose

\(\displaystyle f \in \mathcal{B}_k (A, 1 ; z) \quad (\|z\| \leqslant B_0), \qquad \|f (z)\| \leqslant u \quad (\|z\| \leqslant 3 t), \quad 0 < t \leqslant 1, \quad 3 t \leqslant B_0,\)

where \(A, u \geqslant 0\). Put

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} W & = 1000 (u / t^2 + AB_0), & Y & = 1000 u / t + 3 WB_0,\\ H & = u + YB_0 + Yt + Wt^2 + At^3 . & & \end{array}\)

Then, throughout the larger ball,

\(\displaystyle f \in \mathcal{B}_k (H, t^{- 1}) .\) (8.161)

First, if \(\|Df (z) - Df (a)\| \leqslant ed\) and \(\|f (z)\| \leqslant u\) on \(\bar{B} (a, d)\), integration along a segment of length \(d\) in any unit direction gives

\(\displaystyle \|Df (a)\| \leqslant 2 u / d + ed.\)

Write \(H_0 = \|D^2 f (0)\|\). The third derivative bound gives \(\|D^2 f (z)\| \leqslant H_0 + A \|z\|\). Apply the preceding inequality on balls of radius \(t / 4\) centred in \(\bar{B} (0, 2 t)\). These balls lie in \(\bar{B} (0, 3 t)\), and hence

\(\displaystyle \|Df (z)\| \leqslant A_1 := 8 u / t + (H_0 + 3 At) t / 4 \quad (\|z\| \leqslant 2 t) .\)

Apply the same inequality to \(Df\) on the radius-\(2 t\) ball, using the third derivative bound to control the variation of \(D^2 f\). It yields \(H_0 \leqslant A_1 / t + 2 At\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle H_0 \leqslant \tfrac{32}{3} u / t^2 + \tfrac{11}{3} At \leqslant 50 (u / t^2 + At) .\)

Integration out to radius \(B_0\) now gives \(\|D^2 f\| \leqslant W\), \(\|Df\| \leqslant Y\), and \(\|f\| \leqslant u + YB_0\). For orders \(r \geqslant 3\), the original bound gives \(t^r \|D^r f\| \leqslant At^3\). Each of the bounds for orders zero, one, two and at least three is a summand of \(H\), proving (8.161).

Apply this with \(k = j + 3\) and \(t = h^{- 1 / 2}\). This \(t\) is the flat probing radius, not the larger fluctuation threshold \(\tau = P (h) h^{- 1 / 2}\). In a clean ball, choose one gauge from the base field \(V (y_0)\) and keep it fixed. The small-log construction and the smooth local logarithm give a logarithmic variation \(b (y)\), zero outside the required ball, with

\(\displaystyle b \in \mathcal{B}_j (F (x), 1 ; y_0), \qquad F \text{ of upper exponent } - 1 / 2.\)

For positive derivatives this follows by composing the bounded derivatives of the logarithm with the bond derivative scale \(v\); for the value it is the small-log bound at the base field. Finitely many strict chart inequalities give a common neighbourhood on which exponentiating \(b\) agrees with the gauge-transformed field on the whole read stencil. No derivative of the chosen gauge is taken.

Set \(B_0 = F + 3 t\), which eventually lies in the fixed ball of (8.160). Since \(f (0) = 0\), integration of its first derivative gives \(\|f\| \leqslant 3 At\) on the radius-\(3 t\) ball. Thus take \(u = 3 At\) above. The upper exponents of \(W, Y, H\) are, respectively, \(1 / 2, 0, - 1 / 2\): each term follows from the displayed formulas, \(B_0, t\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and \(t^{- 1}\) of upper exponent \(1 / 2\).

The chain rule for \(f (b (y), 0)\) costs at most a fixed factorial times \(H\) and a fixed-order polynomial in \(1 + 2 F (1 + t^{- 1})\). To verify this directly, in a term indexed by a partition into \(m\) blocks, the outer derivative contributes \(Ht^{- m}\) and each inner derivative contributes at most \(2 F\); there are at most \(j\)! partitions. The displayed polynomial has upper exponent zero. Thus every derivative through order \(j\) of the residual has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Locality identifies this expression with the actual unmasked residual in the fixed gauge; gauge covariance restores the original field by a fixed isometry.

8.21.3The source and the mean

The selected affine source also contains frozen cut logarithms. They obey the same upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) derivative bound. For completeness, write such a logarithm as

\(\displaystyle \chi (\text{chord} (U)^2 / Z^2) \hspace{0.17em} \log U, \qquad Z = K \tau,\)

with fixed \(K > 0\). If the base chord is strictly larger than \(2 Z\), the function is locally zero. Otherwise normalize the matrix displacement by \(R_G Z\). Its value is at most two and its positive derivatives have size bounded by constants times \(v / Z\) after enlarging the scale. Since \(Z^{- 1}\) has upper exponent \(1 / 2\), the derivatives of the cutoff have upper exponent zero. On the fixed logarithm chart, the value of \(\log U\) is at most \(CZ\); its positive derivatives are at most \(C_j v\) after \(v \leqslant 1\). The product rule proves the assertion, including the closed boundary \(\text{chord} (U) = 2 Z\).

On a selected row, the zero-fluctuation affine source is the fixed row scale times the sum of the unmasked residual and the transported frozen logarithm. The latter transport reads only a radius-\(B\) neighbourhood and has bounded absolute row bound. Its derivative scale is bounded by \(C (B + 1) v + 1\), of upper exponent zero. Combining the preceding estimates gives

\(\displaystyle r (0) \in \mathcal{B}_j (F_0 (x), 1) \quad \text{coordinatewise}, \qquad F_0 \text{ of upper exponent } - 1 / 2.\) (8.162)

On unselected rows the function is identically zero. The selection is held fixed throughout this differentiation.

Use the same counting-coordinate matrices \(M, A, P\) as before, and put \(\ell = M^{\ast} r (0)\) and \(\nu = - P^{\ast} \ell\). Weighted row and column derivative bounds for \(M\), together with (8.162), show that each coordinate of \(\ell\) has derivatives of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The padded inverse derivative bounds have upper exponent zero, so the same is true of \(\nu\). Reassembling a colour vector costs at most its fixed colour dimension. On the clean selected sector \(\nu\) equals the actual mean \(\mu\), by the local clipping identity already proved.

There is a stronger estimate for the normal residual. Since \(A\) is symmetric, on a neighbourhood where it is invertible,

\(\displaystyle A \nu + \ell = - A (P - A^{- 1})^{\ast} \ell .\) (8.163)

This follows by substituting \(\nu = - P^{\ast} \ell\) and using \((A^{- 1})^{\ast} = A^{- 1}\). Every prescribed finite derivative of \(P - A^{- 1}\) has every prescribed negative upper exponent, by Subsection 8.19. The weighted product rule therefore proves the same assertion for (8.163). For \(0 < \beta \leqslant 2 h\), multiplication by \(\sqrt{\beta}\) loses at most upper exponent \(1 / 2\), so the normalized linear Gaussian score \(- \sqrt{\beta} (A \nu + \ell)\) still has any prescribed upper exponent. This proves the score bound without replacing the local mean by the exact global inverse mean.

8.21.4Local action derivatives with a held fine field

We also require a cruder bound that holds for every fixed fine configuration \(U\). Keep \(U\) and its actual predictor \(Q (U)\) fixed while varying the coarse field. Since \(v \leqslant 1\) eventually, all coarse matrix derivatives may be bounded with scale one.

The cut curvature strokes and predictor-noise stroke have a common constant amplitude \(C_0\) and derivative scale \(D_0 = C (R + 1)\). For curvature strokes this follows by differentiating their cut path sums, whose absolute coefficients are bounded; the unextended plaquette alternative is a product of four unitary bonds. For the noise stroke use the product \(VQ (U)^{- 1}\) and the bounded curvature map. A transported guide adds a finite input sum and transport: its amplitude is at most \(C (R + 1)^4 C_0\) and its derivative scale at most \(D_0 + C (R + 1)\). A second application of the transported operator has the corresponding squared polynomial bound.

All unmasked local action terms now have upper exponent zero in every derivative through order \(j\). Indeed they are finite sums of inner products of the above strokes and guides, their squared norms, and the plaquette discrepancy

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} (\text{chord} (g)^2 -\| \text{curv} (g)\|^2) .\)

An inner product multiplies amplitudes and adds derivative scales; this is precisely the bilinear product rule. The discrepancy has bounded derivatives because the chord squared is a fixed multiple of \(\| \rho (g) - 1\|^2\), and the curvature is a fixed linear projection of the matrix displacement. At fixed \(j\), converting a polynomial derivative scale to scale one preserves upper exponent zero.

The fine energy requires one separate observation: its clipped part need not be smooth in the fixed fine input. It is constant in the variable being differentiated. The chord is at most two and \(\tau \leqslant 1\), so the direct and linear pieces of that clipped energy are bounded by twice their fixed coefficients. If \(C_{\text{path}}\) is the fixed absolute path bound, the complete fine constant is

\(\displaystyle F = \tfrac{1}{2} (C_{\text{path}} R_G^2)^2 + 2 c_{\text{direct}} + 2 c_{\text{linear}} .\)

The remaining fine terms are, for the two curvature components, \(\|G\|^2 / 2 - \langle G, X_f \rangle\), where \(G\) is the varying guide and \(X_f\) is fixed and bounded. The preceding product estimates apply. Thus no smoothness of the arbitrary held fine configuration is assumed.

Finally consider an extracted affine square. If a varying fluctuation \(w\) has derivatives bounded by \(p (x)\) of upper exponent zero on the hot bonds in its read ball, then

\(\displaystyle r (w) = r (0) + Mw\)

has coordinatewise derivatives of upper exponent zero. Rows of \(M\) vanish off the hot bonds and outside radius \(B / 4\), so only these local bounds on \(w\) enter. Squaring, dividing by twice the square of the fixed row normalization, and summing over the at most two parent rows at an energy address preserves that exponent. This applies both to the compensated fluctuation on protected bonds and to the mean \(\nu\).

8.21.5The logarithmic packet estimate

Let \(n\) be the number of seeds. If the product of protected masks is not locally zero, their closed support bounds and the compensated variation of Subsection 8.20 give derivatives of the fluctuation of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) on the protected \(304 B\) region. The mean has the same bound by the preceding argument. Thus the local action and both extracted-square costs just estimated are available at every address of the \(200 B\) region. There are at most \(nb_{200 B + 1} s_L\) such addresses in each signed channel. Taking the normalized signed average preserves this bound.

Let \(C_a, C_q, C_c, C_J \geqslant 0\) bound respectively the derivatives of the unmasked action, one extracted-square cost, the Gaussian normalization correction and the chart correction, all at scale one through order \(j\). Their upper exponents are zero, zero, zero and \(- 1 / 2\), respectively. With \(\rho_L\) the fine-bond density, put

\(\displaystyle S (x) = 2 h \hspace{0.17em} b_{200 B + 1} s_L (C_a + 2 C_q) + b_{200 B + 2} \rho_L C_c + b_{304 B + 2} \rho_L C_J .\)

It has upper exponent one. The formula (8.185), the local bounds, and the bond census show that its nonconstant part belongs to \(\mathcal{B}_j (nS, 1 ; y_0)\). The term \(- N_A \log m_{\beta}\) is constant in this variation. Subtracting the value at \(y_0\) costs at most a factor two, so

\(\displaystyle \Phi_A - \Phi_A (y_0) \in \mathcal{B}_j (2 nS (x), 1 ; y_0) .\) (8.164)

This proves the first bound in (8.189), with the original local covariance, chart and compensated fluctuation. If the protected mask product is locally zero, the packet derivatives vanish and this bound on its logarithm is not needed.

8.22Small derivatives of the remote action

The packet estimate permits a polynomial expense in its logarithm. Outside the packets we need a stronger conclusion: each local logarithmic activity, together with its derivatives, must be small. We obtain the extra power from the local quadratic comparison and the flat interpolation argument already proved. Throughout this section the derivative order, block length, patch, forest and tags are fixed. The path bounds, flat identities and positive precision estimates retain the hypotheses of the preceding sections.

8.22.1A cubic remainder in a flat chart

For a local energy address \(p\), let \(\mathcal{E}_p (U, V)\) denote its unmasked energy, with the original predictor tags. Let \(I (p)\) be its set of extracted quadratic rows; \(|I (p) | \leqslant 2\). Write \(f_i (V)\) for the zero-fluctuation residual and \(M_i (V) u\) for the transported cut linear row. Define

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} R_p (U, V, u) & =\mathcal{E}_p (U, V) - \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i \in I (p)} |f_i (V) + M_i (V) u|^2, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.165)} \\ F_p (b, u) & = R_p (\exp (u) \text{replica} (\exp b), \exp b, u) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.166)} \end{array}\)

The four address types are the fine curvature, predictor penalty, coarse curvature with its two mismatch terms, and the null-row square. For the last two, the parent set is empty. These are the same unmasked expressions as in Subsection 8.11.

For every fixed \(k\), there are a radius \(r > 0\) independent of the period and a function \(A\) of upper exponent zero such that

\(\displaystyle F_p \in \mathcal{B}_k (A (x), 1 ; (b, u)), \qquad \max (\|b\|_{\infty}, \|u\|_{\infty}) \leqslant r.\) (8.167)

Here and below the same bound holds at every address. To verify it, use the four dressed fields and their predictor bounds in Subsection 8.21. Each cut path has length \(O (R + 1)\), its absolute coefficients have bounded sum, and its transport derivatives cost a polynomial in \(R + 1\). The local energy is a sum of squared curvature rows and inner products of the coarse stroke with linear guide rows. Its derivatives therefore cost only another polynomial in \(R + 1\). Each affine row in (8.165) is the sum of the residual already estimated there and a cut linear map applied to \(u\). On the unit ball, the coordinate map \(u \mapsto u_e\) has value and first derivative at most one and all higher derivatives zero. The product rule bounds its transported image by a polynomial in \(B + 1\). Squaring and summing at most two such rows proves (8.167). This is a bound on the unmasked formula; it makes no small-field assertion on that whole fixed ball.

Put \(t = qh^{- 1 / 2}\) with \(q > 0\) fixed and sufficiently small. Every coarse field \(\exp b\) with \(\|b\|_{\infty} \leqslant 3 t\) is clean on all its plaquettes. Indeed the exponential chart gives \(\text{chord} (\exp b_e) \leqslant C \|b_e \|\), and the four-link triangle inequality gives plaquette chord at most \(12 Cqh^{- 1 / 2}\). Choose \(q \leqslant (12 C)^{- 1}\) and use \(h^{- 1 / 2}\) no larger than the coarse flag threshold. This check applies on every period.

On the radius-\(3 t\) ball, the fields in (8.166) have the exact local graft \(U = \exp u \text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V\). The annular comparison then gives

\(\displaystyle |F_p (b, u) | \leqslant E (x), \qquad E \text{ of upper exponent } - 3 / 2.\) (8.168)

More explicitly, the fine and predictor rows have affine error of upper exponent \(- 1\) and amplitude of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\); the difference of their squares consequently has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). Their extracted rows agree with \(f_i + M_i u\), since cleanliness removes the clipping on the complete read stencil. The coarse curvature remainder and null-row square have upper exponent \(- 2\). Each mismatch term is an inner product of a coarse stroke of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) with an error of upper exponent \(- 1\). Their sum has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). Taking the sum of these four nonnegative bounds gives one \(E\) for all address types. These are precisely the estimates proved in the annular argument; no inequality is differentiated in obtaining (8.168).

Let \(Q \geqslant 0\) have upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and put \(B_0 = Q + 3 t\). Apply (8.161) to \(F_p\), with \(k = j + 3\), value bound \(E\) and fixed-ball derivative bound \(A\). Its constants satisfy

\(\displaystyle W = 1000 (E / t^2 + AB_0), \qquad Y = 1000 E / t + 3 WB_0, \qquad H = E + YB_0 + Yt + Wt^2 + At^3 .\)

Their upper exponents are \(- 1 / 2, - 1, - 3 / 2\), respectively. For example \(E / t^2\) and \(AB_0\) have upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and each of \(YB_0, Yt, Wt^2, At^3\) has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). Thus

\(\displaystyle F_p \in \mathcal{B}_j (H, t^{- 1} ; z) \quad (\|z\| \leqslant Q), \qquad H \text{ of upper exponent } - 3 / 2.\) (8.169)

The reciprocal \(t^{- 1}\) has upper exponent \(1 / 2\) and is paid by the small derivatives of the chart coordinates in the next step.

8.22.2Returning to a protected remote address

Suppose the anchor of \(p\) is farther than \(200 B\) from the seeds. Let \(V (y)\) be a coarse variation of derivative scale with upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and let \(u (y)\) be its coherently compensated fine increment. Hold the underlying fine configuration \(U_{\ast}\) fixed, as in Subsection 8.20. Let \(\psi_p (y)\) be the product of the doubled protective cutoffs on the local read region. If \(\psi_p\) vanishes on a neighbourhood of \(y_0\), the protected remainder has zero jets there. Otherwise the closed support argument bounds the increment on that read region by \(q_0 + 4 a \tau\), with \(q_0\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\).

After a signed pullback, the relative increment is \(\text{Ad}\) of this increment by the appropriate replica link. The product rule and the unitary bounds give amplitude

\(\displaystyle R_G^2 (q_0 + 4 a \tau) \{1 + (v + 1 + v)\}^j\)

for its jets through order \(j\); this still has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). On tree bonds the increment is identically zero. All other bonds in the radius-\(B\) read region are active because the address is remote from the seeds. The compensated identity therefore gives, on a neighbourhood of \(y_0\),

\(\displaystyle U_{\ast} = \exp u (y) \text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V (y) \quad \text{on the radius-} \lfloor 7 B / 8 \rfloor \text{ patch},\)

with the signed pullback understood on both sides.

Choose one coarse gauge from \(V (y_0)\) on this patch. The coarse logarithmic coordinates \(b (y)\) have jets of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) by the fixed-gauge construction in Subsection 8.21. Conjugate \(u (y)\) by this fixed gauge and set it to zero outside the patch. This preserves its derivative bound. Denote the resulting pair of arrays by \(z (y)\). For one nonnegative \(Q\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), we have \(z \in \mathcal{B}_j (Q, 1 ; y_0)\).

All terms in (8.165) are determined by this patch. To check the radius, the cut guide and transported linear rows read within \(B / 4\) of their address; the local energy and its predicted coarse field read within \(B / 2\), including the fixed predictor reach. Both fit inside \(7 B / 8\) for the previously chosen geometric threshold. The row transports, predictor tags and quadratic extraction decisions are unchanged there. Gauge covariance and the local equality of the fields consequently identify the actual remainder with \(F_p (z (y))\) throughout a neighbourhood.

For a derivative of order \(k \leqslant j\), the chain rule has at most \(k\)! partition terms. A term with \(m\) blocks is bounded by \(Ht^{- m} Q^m\). Hence every derivative of \(F_p (z (y))\) is bounded by

\(\displaystyle H \max (1, j!) (1 + Qt^{- 1})^j .\)

The second factor has upper exponent zero. We have proved that the remote unmasked remainder has jets of upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\) whenever its protector is not locally zero. The protector has amplitude one and derivative scale bounded by a function of upper exponent zero times its support cover. Multiplying it back in preserves the amplitude exponent \(- 3 / 2\), allowing this support factor in the derivative scale.

8.22.3The remote mean square and other short terms

The local mean residual \(m_p\) has remote value bound of upper exponent \(- 1\), proved in Subsection 8.14. Its jets have the same exponent. Here is why the scalar estimate suffices in this application. On a flat probe ball all sectors are clean and the actual mean agrees with its unmasked expression. The derivative estimate on the larger shrinking ball requires an additional check, since the intermediate probes need not satisfy the sector flag conditions. Let \(B_0 = Q + 3 t\) as above and write \(A (z)\) for the empty-seed precision at logarithmic probe \(z\). The common-trajectory comparison at the identity supplies \(A (0) \geqslant r_0 I\) for a fixed \(r_0 > 0\). On \(\|z\| \leqslant B_0\), the transported-row estimates give an entry derivative bound \(Cv_1\), where \(C\) is fixed and \(v_1\) has upper exponent zero. Integrating this derivative along the segment from \(0\) to \(z\) gives \(|A (z)_{ik} - A (0)_{ik} | \leqslant Cv_1 B_0\). The matrices have range at most \(B\) and coordinate density at most \(\rho\) per site. Both the row and column sums of this difference are therefore at most \(b_B \rho Cv_1 B_0\), which tends to zero. The elementary row-column bound on the Euclidean operator norm follows by Cauchy–Schwarz: \(\|Hv\|_2^2 \leqslant (\sup_i \sum_k |H_{ik} |) (\sup_k \sum_i |H_{ik} |) \|v\|_2^2\). Consequently \(A (z) \geqslant (r_0 / 2) I\) throughout the shrinking ball for large \(x\). Every principal restriction has the same floor. The exponentially weighted entry estimates remain uniform there; the inverse and padded-inverse jet bounds proved earlier thus apply with fixed reduced floor and width. They cost only a polynomial in \(B\) at fixed derivative order.

The empty-seed residual is the zero-fluctuation row residual minus the transported row applied to its padded-inverse response, that is \(f - MPM^{\ast} f\) after the fixed row normalization. All four factors now have jet bounds of upper exponent zero on this shrinking ball. The product and row-sum estimates give the required upper exponent zero bound for the residual there. On the smaller radius-\(3 t\) ball every empty-seed probe is clean, so the same-history stationary comparison applies separately to each probe and gives the remote value exponent \(- 1\). No flag condition was assumed on the entire larger ball. Apply the same interpolation above with this probe value exponent \(- 1\). Then \(W, Y, H\) have upper exponents \(0, - 1 / 2, - 1\). The fixed-gauge composition again costs only upper exponent zero. Finally the mean residual depends only on the radius-\(24 B\) patch and the unchanged windows; a point farther than \(30 B\) from the seeds has the same local formula as the empty-seed comparison. This gives the claimed exponent \(- 1\) for every fixed jet order, with no derivative of a gauge choice or stationary minimizer.

At most two such residuals enter a local mean-square term, so its jets have upper exponent \(- 2\). The remote logarithmic action is \(- \theta \beta\) times the sum of this mean-square term and the protected remainder, where \(0 \leqslant \theta \leqslant 1\) is the fixed channel weight and \(0 < \beta \leqslant 2 h\). Its amplitude therefore has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). This uses the same \(\beta\) as in the original action.

The other two short logarithmic terms are the remote normalization correction and the protected chart logarithm. Their jets have upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) by Sections 8.19 and 8.18. Each term is set to zero on its prescribed packet neighbourhood; this indicator depends only on the frozen sector. Consequently the three short families have one common nonnegative amplitude \(b (x)\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and derivative scale

\(\displaystyle v_0 (x) \{1 + \text{cov}_B (S_p)\}, \qquad v_0 \text{ of upper exponent zero} .\) (8.170)

Here \(S_p\) is the full enlarged read support of the term. For fixed enlargement \(\ell\), these supports lie in radius \((\ell + 1) B\) balls about their anchors and have uniformly bounded cover number. The number of anchors meeting a given site is at most \(b_{(\ell + 1) B}\) times the fixed density of the three families. Their exponentially cover-weighted site sum is therefore bounded by this polynomial count times \(b (x)\) and a fixed exponential in the cover bound. It still has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), uniformly in the period. This supplies the short-activity part of the logarithmic estimate.

8.23Derivative bounds for the Gaussian correction chains

The corrections in Subsection 8.13 are sums over ordered itineraries of physical bonds. Their operator bounds alone do not bound each activity in the connected expansion. We now keep the itinerary labels and their full read supports while differentiating and summing them.

8.23.1Products along a fixed itinerary

Write \(q\) for the number of colour coordinates. An itinerary of length \(N\) consists of physical bonds \(e_0, \ldots, e_N\); colour indices are summed within its matrix product. Suppose the entry jets of its \(r\)th matrix have amplitude \(a_r \geqslant 0\) and common derivative scale \(d \geqslant 0\). Repeated matrix multiplication and the product rule give

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_j \hspace{-0.17em} \left( q^N \prod_{r = 0}^{N - 1} a_r, Nd \right)\) (8.171)

for any entry of the colour-block product. This follows by induction on \(N\): multiplying the next entry adds \(d\) to the derivative scale, multiplies the amplitude by \(a_r\), and introduces at most \(q\) choices for the next colour. The estimate deliberately overcounts when \(N = 1\). Taking a colour trace adds a factor \(q\). Integrating such a trace over \(s \in [0, 1]\) preserves this bound when the entry estimates are uniform in \(s\).

For the present matrices, this last differentiation under the integral is valid by the compact-glide argument of Subsection 8.19: all principal determinants are nonzero on one common parameter neighbourhood for \(s \in [0, 1]\), and the finite matrix products are jointly smooth there. A closed itinerary contributes its diagonal trace; a nonclosed itinerary contributes zero to that trace.

All the matrices in these words have range at most \(3 B\). If some consecutive pair of bonds has all endpoint distances greater than \(3 B\), the corresponding matrix block vanishes identically as the coarse field varies. Thus the whole word is a zero germ. Call an itinerary admissible when every consecutive pair has endpoints at distance at most \(3 B\). This condition is fixed by its geometry, and is retained in every bound below.

Let \(a\) be the common weighted jet amplitude of the actual precision \(A\), let \(h_0\) bound the padded inverse of the glide, and let \(t_0\) bound its error \(A_s P_s - 1\). These quantities were constructed in Subsection 8.19, under other names. There \(A\) has the envelope \((f, v)\) of (8.147), and the glide \(A_s = 1 + s (A - 1)\) has the envelope \((f + 2, v_A)\) uniformly in \(s \in [0, 1]\); we take \(a = f + 2\), which covers both. The padded principal inverse \(P_s\) of the glide has row and column bound (8.150) with \(f\) replaced by \(f + 2\), so \(h_0 = \Delta_B T_k (f + 2, S)\). Its error \(E_s = A_s P_s - 1\) has the bound (8.151) with the same replacement, so \(t_0 = e^{- \gamma B / 2} \{(f + 2) h_0 + 1\}\). The derivative scales are the fixed multiples of \(v_A\) recorded there. The amplitudes \(a, h_0\) have upper exponent zero; \(t_0\) has every negative upper exponent. The first logarithmic correction has word

\(\displaystyle (A - 1) P_s (- E_s)^{n + 1}, \qquad E_s = A_s P_s - 1.\)

It has \(n + 3\) matrix factors. If \(d_A\) bounds their derivative scales, (8.171) and the colour trace give

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_j (C_A Q_A^n, (n + 3) d_A), \qquad C_A = q^4 (a + 1) h_0 t_0, \quad Q_A = qt_0 .\) (8.172)

The same argument applies to the flat-reference correction. Its coarse field is constant and satisfies the same entry bounds.

For the second correction put \(D = AC - 1\), where \(C\) is the symmetric padded covariance. Its entry jets have amplitude \(w_0\) with every negative upper exponent, obtained by multiplying the padded-inverse error by the full inverse envelope and then by \(A\). If \(d_D\) is their derivative scale, the word \(D (- sD)^n\) has \(n + 1\) factors. Since \(0 \leqslant s \leqslant 1\), its integrated trace has bound

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_j (C_S Q_S^n, (n + 1) d_D), \qquad C_S = q^2 w_0, \quad Q_S = qw_0 .\) (8.173)

These formulas bound the actual trace words, including their endpoint identifications. Discarding a factor \(1 / 2\) or another coefficient of absolute value at most one only enlarges the bounds.

8.23.2The two protected Gaussian endpoints

The inverse correction is a quadratic form with word \((- D)^{n + 1} A\), of length \(n + 2\), and a Gaussian coordinate at each endpoint. Those coordinates are not bounded on the whole sample space. Their protective cutoffs supply the needed local bound.

Let \(u (y) = \nu (y) + g_{\beta} z (y)\), \(g_{\beta} = \beta^{- 1 / 2}\), be the compensated increment on an active physical bond. If its doubled cutoff is not a zero germ, the increment jet bound has amplitude \(s_0 + 4 a_0 \tau\), where \(s_0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) and \(a_0 > 0\) is the fixed cutoff parameter. The mean jets have amplitude \(m_0\) of upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Hence

\(\displaystyle z_e \in \mathcal{B}_j (p, 1), \qquad p = \sqrt{\beta} \hspace{0.17em} (s_0 + 4 a_0 \tau + m_0),\) (8.174)

which has upper exponent zero since \(\beta \leqslant 2 h\). If either cutoff is a zero germ, the protected quadratic activity is a zero germ instead. If an endpoint is inactive, the inactive block of the completed precision and covariance is the identity; the corresponding defect block is zero. These block identities persist on the neighbourhood where the fixed principal windows remain invertible. The quadratic word therefore vanishes as a germ unless both endpoints are active. This justifies using (8.174) only where it applies.

Suppose the cutoff jets have amplitude one and scale \(d_0\). The word has entry amplitude \(q^{n + 2} aw_0^{n + 1}\). The two endpoint colour sums add \(q^2 p^2\), and the product rule adds two endpoint scales and two cutoff scales. Enlarging the resulting scale gives

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_j (C_Q Q_Q^n, (n + 2) d_Q), \qquad C_Q = p^2 q^4 aw_0, \quad Q_Q = qw_0, \quad d_Q = d_D + d_0 + 1,\) (8.175)

after enlarging \(d_D\) to include the scale of \(A\). All derivative scales here have upper exponent zero. The unprotected linear score is handled separately: its coefficient has every negative upper exponent by the mean identity (8.163), \(A \nu + \ell = - A (P - A^{- 1})^{\ast} \ell\), and the bound of \(P - A^{- 1}\) that follows (8.151), while its one protected endpoint has the bound just proved. Summing its \(q\) colours and multiplying its cutoff produces one local bond activity with every negative upper exponent, even after its fixed-radius support is counted.

8.23.3Paying the derivative order before the chain length

All three itinerary families have the form

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_j (CQ^n, (n + u) d), \qquad u \in \{3, 1, 2\},\)

on admissible itineraries and zero germs otherwise. To give every jet a common amplitude and unit derivative scale, multiply the amplitude by \((1 + (n + u) d)^j\). The elementary inequality \(n + 1 \leqslant 2^n\) implies

\(\displaystyle 1 + (n + u) d \leqslant 2^n \{1 + (1 + u) d\} .\)

It is therefore enough to replace

\(\displaystyle C \longmapsto C' = C \{1 + (1 + u) d\}^j, \qquad Q \longmapsto Q' = 2^j Q.\) (8.176)

No factor depending on \(n\) remains outside the geometric power. For fixed \(j\), \(C'\) retains every negative upper exponent of \(C\), and \(Q'\) retains every negative upper exponent of \(Q\).

8.23.4The rooted support sum

Fix a halo multiplier \(m \geqslant 4\), also large enough to contain the previously established mean, window and compensated-variation read radii, a support exponent \(a_1 \geqslant 0\), and a length reserve \(\varepsilon > 0\). The inequality \(m \geqslant 4\) alone is used in the count below; the additional read-radius requirement is needed when applying it to the actual activities. The support of an itinerary is the union of the radius-\(mB\) halos of the endpoint sets of all its bonds. Put

\(\displaystyle \rho_L = L^4 \# \{ \text{axes} \}, \quad K = b_{3 B + 2} \rho_L, \quad s_m = b_{m + 1} .\)

There are at most \(K\) choices for a next bond in an admissible step: its anchor is within \(3 B + 2\) of the preceding anchor. Thus there are at most \(K^{n + u}\) admissible itineraries of length \(n + u\) with prescribed initial bond.

Each bond halo has \(B\)-cover number at most \(s_m\). Consecutive halos intersect because \(3 B + 2 \leqslant mB\). Subadditivity along the ordered chain gives support cover at most \((n + u + 1) s_m\). If this support contains a fixed site \(x_0\), the initial anchor is within \((n + u + 1) (mB + 1)\) of \(x_0\): follow the chain to a halo containing \(x_0\) and sum the step distances. Since \(n + u + 1 \leqslant (u + 1) 2^n\), the number of possible initial bonds is at most

\(\displaystyle b_{(u + 1) (mB + 1)} 16^n \rho_L .\)

The factor \(16^n\) is the fourth power of \(2^n\); it also bounds the additive one in the lattice ball formula. Wrapping on a torus can only reduce these counts.

Multiplying the bound \(C' (Q')^n\), the count, the support weight and the extra length weight \(e^{2 \varepsilon n}\) gives the bound

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\lambda : x_0 \in S_{\lambda}} p_{\lambda} e^{a_1 \text{cov}_B (S_{\lambda})} e^{2 \varepsilon n (\lambda)} \leqslant HC' \sum_{n \geqslant 0} [Q' 16 Ke^{a_1 s_m + 2 \varepsilon}]^n,\) (8.177)

where

\(\displaystyle H = b_{(u + 1) (mB + 1)} \rho_L K^u e^{a_1 (u + 1) s_m} .\)

All sums have nonnegative terms, so grouping first by length and then by initial bond is justified without a convergence assumption. At fixed \(m, L, j\), both \(K\) and \(H\) have upper exponent zero. The geometric ratio in (8.177) is eventually at most \(1 / 2\), and its sum is at most \(2 HC'\). Consequently every prescribed negative upper exponent is available for the rooted bound of these correction chains. This pays the actual full read supports and every itinerary label, including repeated physical bonds; it does not assume disjoint paths or independent Gaussian coordinates.

8.24Bounds for the inherited activities

The remaining logarithmic activities come from the incoming representation: its regular activities, cubic and quartic path terms, quadratic loop terms, and the true long-path tails on the fine and coarse levels. We convert their existing coefficient bounds into bounds on the full coarse read supports. This section assumes those incoming coefficient bounds on the same representation and paths as the selected step. It proves the bound conversion; maintenance of the incoming bounds along the renormalisation run is a separate construction requirement.

8.24.1Coarsening a connected cover

Let \(\pi\) be the parent map with block length \(L\), and let \(c_M (X)\) be the connected covering number at radius \(M\). Thus \(X\) is contained in the union of \(M\)-balls around \(c_M (X)\) centres, and that union is connected by unit lattice steps. For \(B \geqslant 1\), \(M \leqslant 2 B\) and integer \(\ell \geqslant 0\), put

\(\displaystyle P_{\ell} = b_{\ell + 6}, \qquad v_{\ell} = 10 P_{\ell} / L, \qquad Y = \text{halo}_{\ell B} (\pi X) .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle c_B (Y) \leqslant P_{\ell} + v_{\ell} c_M (X) .\) (8.178)

Here is a proof retaining the factor \(L^{- 1}\). Choose a minimal cover with centre set \(D\). The graph joining centres at distance at most \(2 M + 1\) is connected. Traverse a spanning tree out and back, replacing each edge by steps of max-distance at most one (diagonal steps are allowed). This gives a walk visiting all centres, of length at most \((4 M + 2) |D|\). Sample it every \(LB\) steps and apply \(\pi\). If its length is \(N\), the resulting centre set \(D'\) satisfies \(LB |D' | \leqslant N + LB\). The parent-distance inequality

\(\displaystyle L \hspace{0.17em} d (\pi x, \pi y) \leqslant d (x, y) + L\)

shows that every projected walk point is within \(2 B\) of a sampled centre and that consecutive sampled centres are within \(2 B\). Every point of \(X\) is within \(M \leqslant 2 B\) of an original centre; its parent is therefore within \(3 B\) of that centre's parent. Thus \(Y\) is covered by the \((\ell + 5) B\)-balls about \(D'\), with connected union. Tile their slightly enlarged \((\ell + 6) B\)-balls by \(B\)-balls with centres on a \(B\)-spaced four-dimensional grid. There are at most \(P_{\ell}\) tiles per centre. These enlarged halos overlap along the sampled walk, so the resulting cover remains connected. Finally \((4 M + 2) |D| \leqslant 10 B |D|\) proves (8.178). For an empty set the inequality is immediate. Wrapping any of these walks or grids only decreases the number of distinct centres.

If \(o \in X\) and \(y \in Y\), the same spanning walk gives

\(\displaystyle d (y, \pi o) \leqslant (\ell + 20) B \{1 + c_M (X)\} .\) (8.179)

Indeed two points of \(X\) are at distance at most \((4 M + 2) c_M (X) + 2 M\); apply the parent-distance inequality and then add the \(\ell B\) halo. The displayed larger constant bounds the result for \(M \leqslant 2 B\), \(B \geqslant 1\). Inspecting the fine plaquettes touching a support adds at most four fine lattice steps. Their parents lie within five coarse steps, so replacing \(\ell\) by \(\ell + 5\) pays all these reads.

8.24.2From an anchor bound to a site bound

We give a general counting estimate. Suppose nonnegative weights \(b_i\), integer costs \(d_i \geqslant 0\), fine anchors \(o_i\), and coarse supports \(Y_i\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \sup_o \sum_{i : o_i = o} b_i e^{\sigma d_i} \leqslant \Gamma, \qquad c_B (Y_i) \leqslant v + v' d_i, \qquad y \in Y_i \Longrightarrow d (y, \pi o_i) \leqslant W (1 + d_i) .\)

Let \(k, W\) be nonnegative integers and \(0 \leqslant g_i \leqslant Cb_i (1 + d_i)^k\) with \(C \geqslant 0\), and assume

\(\displaystyle a \geqslant 0, \quad \varepsilon > 0, \quad av' + 2 \varepsilon \leqslant \sigma .\) (8.180)
