For a fixed coarse site \(y\), the fine anchors of labels with \(d_i = n\) and \(y \in Y_i\) have parents in a radius-\(W (1 + n)\) ball. There are at most \(L^4 b_W (1 + n)^4\) such anchors, because a parent has exactly \(L^4\) residues. The sum of \(g_i e^{ac_B (Y_i)}\) over these labels is at most

\(\displaystyle L^4 b_W \Gamma Ce^{av} (1 + n)^{4 + k} e^{- 2 \varepsilon n} .\)

Use the exponential series at \(\varepsilon (1 + n)\) to obtain

\(\displaystyle (1 + n)^{4 + k} \leqslant \varepsilon^{- (4 + k)} (4 + k) !e^{\varepsilon} e^{\varepsilon n} .\)

Summing the geometric series proves

\(\displaystyle \sup_y \sum_{i : y \in Y_i} g_i e^{ac_B (Y_i)} \leqslant e^{av} \Gamma CL^4 b_W \frac{\varepsilon^{- (4 + k)} (4 + k) !e^{\varepsilon}}{1 - e^{- \varepsilon}} .\) (8.181)

Restricting to \(d_i \geqslant n_0\) adds the factor \(e^{- \varepsilon n_0}\). The proof works with arbitrary label multiplicities, since the input already sums all labels at an anchor. Nonnegative finite partial sums justify the estimate also for a countable alphabet.

We need an additional factor \(e^{2 \varepsilon d_i}\) in the output bound. Apply the preceding argument to weights \(b_i e^{2 \varepsilon d_i}\) and exponent \(\sigma - 2 \varepsilon\). The sufficient condition becomes \(av' + 4 \varepsilon \leqslant \sigma\). For inherited supports use (8.178) with \(d_i = c_M (X_i)\) and \(W = (\ell + 20) B\). If \(4 \varepsilon < \sigma\), choose \(L\) large enough that \(a 10 P_{\ell} / L + 4 \varepsilon \leqslant \sigma\), before increasing \(x\). The incoming cover radius \(M = B (h)\) is eventually at most \(2 B (x)\): \(h = x + c_L\) with \(c_L\) fixed, and the logarithmic-square radius has \(B (x + c_L) / B (x) \to 1\). All factors in (8.181) other than \(\Gamma C\) then have upper exponent zero.

8.24.3Path lengths, including winding paths

For a bundle of \(r\) paths based at one fine anchor, let \(d_i\) be the sum of their true lengths, including their final plaquette walks. Dividing each walk into pieces of \(B\) steps shows that its visited sites have connected \(B\)-cover number at most \(1 + \text{length} / B\). All paths share the anchor, so their union with the anchor has cover at most \(1 + r + d_i / B\). Applying (8.178) at \(M = B\) gives

\(\displaystyle c_B (Y_i) \leqslant P_{\ell} + v_{\ell} (1 + r) + (v_{\ell} / B) d_i .\)

The support lies within \((\ell B + 1) (1 + d_i)\) of the parent anchor. Thus the preceding site estimate applies with \(v' = v_{\ell} / B\). For any fixed \(L\), this coefficient tends to zero, so the required strict exponential reserve is eventually available. No path length is replaced by its endpoint distance; in particular, winding and repeated edges retain their costs.

Suppose the path coefficients satisfy \(\sum_i b_i e^{\sigma d_i} \leqslant \Gamma\) at each translated anchor. An order-\(r\) atom evaluated with its original small-field mask has absolute value at most \(b_i \tau^r\): every one of its \(r\) curvature arguments has norm at most \(\tau\), while the unhealthy branch is zero. An external multiplier bounded by \(D (x)\) therefore has scalar bound \(D (x) \tau^r b_i\). The site conversion preserves the sum of the upper exponents of \(D\), \(\tau^r\), and \(\Gamma\), allowing any strictly larger power for the final bound. Applied to the actual families,

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{family} & \text{multiplier and coefficient budget} & \text{upper exponent}\\ \hline \text{cubic} & 2 h \hspace{0.17em} \tau^3 \cdot C & - 1 / 2\\ \text{quartic} & 2 h \hspace{0.17em} \tau^4 \cdot C & - 1\\ \text{quadratic loop} & \tau^2 \cdot 2 P (h) & - 1 \end{array}\)

Here \(C\) is the incoming fixed path bound, \(P (h)\) is the monitor, and the channel weight has absolute value at most one. The multiplier is the original \(\beta \leqslant 2 h\), wherever it occurs. The fixed number of signed orientations and plaquette axes is summed, not discarded.

For the tail of a squared path row, retain the ordered pair of path labels \((i, k)\). With coefficients \(a_i, a_k\) and lengths \(l_i, l_k\), its bound is

\(\displaystyle b_{ik} = 2 |a_i a_k | \hspace{0.17em} 1_{\{l_i d > R \text{or} \hspace{0.17em} l_k d > R\}},\)

where \(d = 1\) for the fine row and \(d = L\) for the coarse row. This bounds the actual half inner product because each unmasked transported curvature has norm at most two; the kept-row indicator can only decrease its magnitude. If \(\sum_i |a_i | e^{\sigma l_i} \leqslant C\), then

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i, k} b_{ik} e^{(\sigma / 2) (l_i + l_k)} \leqslant 2 e^{- \sigma R / (2 d)} C^2 .\) (8.182)

Indeed every retained pair has \(l_i + l_k \geqslant R / d\); extract \(e^{- \sigma R / (2 d)}\) and use the product of the two weighted row sums. The genuine read support includes both full paths and the kept-row test halo. After projection that extra halo is paid by replacing \(\ell\) by \(\ell + 2\). The site bound with its length reserve is valid if \(8 \varepsilon < \sigma\), since its available input exponent is now \(\sigma / 2\). As \(R = \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor\), the right side of (8.182) has every negative upper exponent, even after multiplication by \(2 h\), fixed powers of the true length and the site-conversion cost. For the coarse-level tail the parent map is the identity; its population factor is one, and the exponential still has fixed \(d = L\). The argument thus covers both tails without confusing their spatial scales.

8.24.4Inherited jets in the compensated variation

The incoming regular activity budget has upper exponent \(- 11 / 10\); the incoming forced activity budget has every negative upper exponent. The former bounds the original masked occurrence on its healthy domain, extended by zero, and the latter bounds the forced occurrence everywhere. Apply the anchor-to-site estimate to their original supports and masks. This preserves those exponents and all label multiplicities.

During the compensated variation the underlying fine field \(U_{\ast}\) is held fixed. Hence the regular, cubic, quartic, loop, fine-tail and forced values are constant in the coarse parameter. Their protective factors vary. On a coarse support \(Y\), each protective bond cutoff has amplitude one and derivative scale of upper exponent zero. Covering \(Y\) by its \(B\)-cover, its full bond read set contains at most \(b_B b_1 \rho_L c_B (Y)\) bonds. The product rule therefore gives the protector derivative scale \(v_0 (x) c_B (Y)\), with \(v_0\) of upper exponent zero. Multiplying a constant value by this protector preserves its scalar bound.

The coarse tail is the exception: its original coarse field varies. For a path of length \(l\), the unitary product argument gives its transported curvature jets amplitude \(|a_i | R_G^2\) and derivative scale \(2 R_G vl\). A product of two paths thus has scale \(2 R_G v (l_i + l_k)\), and its half inner product has amplitude at most \(|a_i a_k | R_G^4\). Multiplying by the original \(\beta\) and channel coefficient is paid by \(2 hR_G^4 b_{ik}\). The same tail estimate bounds these labels with every negative upper exponent, retaining the total length in the derivative scale. The function differentiated here is the unmasked extension of the coarse tail: the smooth path-pair expression with the fixed long-pair selector and with the coarse kept-row test omitted. The actual coarse tail carries that kept-row test, which is a threshold test in the coarse field and is not differentiated. At a base field under the funded-room condition whose full footprint of the term is healthy, the kept test holds on that footprint, so the unmasked extension equals the actual coarse tail in value there. The healthy-footprint hypothesis is the one supplied by the healthy family in Subsection 8.28. No derivative of the kept test is asserted, and the identification is made only at such base fields.

Choose \(- 1 / 2 < t < 0\). By the strict exponent slack, the six inherited logarithmic families together have a rooted bound at most \(h^t\) for sufficiently large \(x\), with the support and \(2 \varepsilon\) extra-cost reserves just proved. For the coarse tail add its differentiated bound to the scalar one; both are nonnegative and negligible, so this addition is paid by choosing an intermediate exponent between \(- 1 / 2\) and \(t\) first. The forced family has the same bound. Their common derivative scale can be taken as

\(\displaystyle v_1 (x) \{c_B (Y_i) + d_i \}, \qquad v_1 \text{ of upper exponent zero},\) (8.183)

where \(d_i\) is the old support cover or the true path length, as appropriate. The bounds are uniform over the held fine configuration and preserve its original labels and parameters.

8.25From a seed energy gain to packet estimates

We now supply the passage from local energy and chart estimates to the packet bound used in Subsection 8.2. There are three distinct costs. Extending the energy from a seed core to its full support introduces a small annular error. The density and normalization factors introduce a polynomial logarithmic expense. Differentiating the packet introduces a further polynomial factor. All three are paid from the same energy gain per seed.

Keep the scales \(h, P, d, \tau, B\) of (8.17), and fix the block length, chart, shell width and all other local parameters before sending \(x\) to infinity. All constants below are independent of the lattice period. For clarity, say that \(r (x)\) has upper exponent \(a\) if, for every \(b > a\), \(|r (x) | \leqslant h (x)^b\) eventually. This permits subpower losses. Products add upper exponents, and a fixed finite sum preserves a common upper exponent: in each case choose a strictly smaller exponent than the one finally required and absorb fixed constants using \(h \to \infty\). In particular \(B\), \(d\) and every fixed real power of \(P\) have upper exponent zero, while \(\tau\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\).

8.25.1Extending a core energy bound

Consider a packet with \(n\) seeds. In each signed channel let \(D_2 \subseteq D_{200}\) be the sets of local energy addresses within \(2 B\) and \(200 B\) of its seeds. Their sizes satisfy

\(\displaystyle |D_{200} | \leqslant n \hspace{0.17em} b_{200 B + 1} \hspace{0.17em} s_L,\)

where \(s_L\) bounds the number of local energy addresses per coarse site. Let \(e_p\) be the assigned energy at address \(p\) in that channel. Suppose the core energy is \(\sum_{p \in D_2} e_p\) and the annular terms satisfy \(e_p \geqslant - r (x)\) on \(D_{200} \setminus D_2\), with \(r (x) \geqslant 0\). Then

\(\displaystyle \sum_{p \in D_{200}} e_p \geqslant \sum_{p \in D_2} e_p - r (x) b_{200 B + 1} s_L n.\)

Average this inequality over signed channels with their normalized weights. If the averaged core energy is at least \(\mu \tau^2 n\), the averaged extended energy \(Q_A\) obeys

\(\displaystyle Q_A \geqslant (\mu \tau^2 - E (x)) n, \qquad E (x) = r (x) b_{200 B + 1} s_L .\) (8.184)

No energy gain from one channel is multiplied by a factor from another: the average is taken on the common configuration and common packet.

The local inputs in this application are a positive seed charge \(\mu\), the displayed core bound, and an annular remainder with upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). The latter is proved in Subsection 8.11 by comparing the fine and predictor energies with their extracted affine squares. On an address where no quadratic term was extracted, the fine and predictor energies are nonnegative; only the one-sided lower bound is needed there. On selected addresses, the local Taylor estimates bound the difference from the extracted quadratic energy in absolute value. The protected \(304 B\) fluctuation region and the \(300 B\) mask conditions allow those local estimates to be used throughout the \(200 B \setminus 2 B\) annulus. Multiplying \(r\) by the fixed-density ball count preserves its upper exponent, so \(E\) also has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\).

8.25.2The packet density and its normalization

Let \(A\) denote the packet. Its logarithmic density has the form

\(\displaystyle \Phi_A = - \beta (Q_A + Q_A^{\text{mean}}) + \sum_{i \in V_{200} (A)} c_i + \sum_{i \in V_{304} (A)} j_i - N_A \log m_{\beta} .\) (8.185)

Here \(Q_A^{\text{mean}} \geqslant 0\) is the extracted mean-square contribution; \(c_i\) are the local Gaussian normalization corrections; \(j_i\) are the protected chart corrections; and \(N_A\) counts the free chart bonds within distance \(10 B\) of the packet. The bond sets and this count satisfy, with constants fixed at \(L\),

\(\displaystyle |V_{200} (A) | \leqslant C_{200} d^4 n, \qquad |V_{304} (A) | \leqslant C_{304} d^4 n, \qquad N_A \leqslant C_{10} d^4 n.\)

Cover each seed neighbourhood by a lattice ball. There are \(L^4 \# \{ \text{axes} \}\) fine bonds per coarse site, so summing these bounds over seeds gives the claimed counts. Overlaps reduce the count.

The one-bond Gaussian normalization is

\(\displaystyle m_{\beta} = c_{\text{chart}} (\sqrt{2 \pi} \hspace{0.17em} \beta^{- 1 / 2})^q, \qquad c_{\text{chart}} > 0,\)

where \(q\) is the colour dimension. Thus

\(\displaystyle - \log m_{\beta} = - \log c_{\text{chart}} - \frac{q}{2} \log (2 \pi) + \frac{q}{2} \log \beta \leqslant C_{\text{norm}} d\)

for \(0 < \beta \leqslant 2 h\) and sufficiently large \(x\). The bound holds even when \(\beta\) is very small, since only an upper bound on \(\log \beta\) is used. Suppose also \(|c_i | \leqslant C_{\text{cov}}\), \(|j_i | \leqslant 1\), and that the likelihood-ticket product is bounded by \(\exp (C_{\text{tag}} d^4 n)\). These are uniform local analytic bounds. The other packet masks, enforcement factors and contractions take values in \([0, 1]\). Dropping the nonpositive mean-square term in (8.185), and using \(d \geqslant 1\), gives

\(\displaystyle \Phi_A + C_{\text{tag}} d^4 n \leqslant - \beta Q_A + Kd^5 n\) (8.186)

for a fixed \(K \geqslant 0\).

Here the ticket bound follows directly from the normalized tag probabilities. Let \(N_f\) and \(N_p\) be the numbers of frame and predictor tags in the fixed patch. A site likelihood is \(N_f\) times its tag probability, and a bond likelihood is \(N_p\) times its tag probability. The probabilities are nonnegative and at most one: in the minimizing frame branch the frame is the single selected minimizer and the tag probabilities are uniform on all frame tags; in the fallback branch they are uniform on the set of eligible tags and zero outside it; and the predictor weights are nonnegative with sum one. Thus each likelihood is at most \(N_f \leqslant e^{N_f}\) or \(N_p \leqslant e^{N_p}\), respectively. The radius-\(r\) seed neighbourhood contains at most \(nb_{r + 1}\) sites, and the bonds incident to it number at most \(nb_{r + 2} \# \{ \text{axes} \}\). Multiplying one factor at each such physical site and bond gives

\(\displaystyle \text{tickets}_r \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} [n\{b_{r + 1} N_f + b_{r + 2} \#\{ \text{axes} \}N_p \}] .\)

For \(r = 300 B\) this is the asserted \(\exp (C_{\text{tag}} d^4 n)\) bound. A tag used by several local tests is still charged only once in this product. There is no independent tag resampling at each use.

A packet is set to zero unless its seed support is contained in the sector seed support and its own flag set agrees with the actual coarse flags throughout its \(300 B\) neighbourhood. On a healthy packet neighbourhood, where there are no actual coarse flags, this flag test is equivalent to the packet's flag component being empty. The latter is the eligibility rule of the locally smooth stock extension \(\mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}}\) used in the local jet argument. The extension differs from \(\mathcal{P}_A\) in two respects: its eligibility test is the fixed one just described, and its action exponent is built from the unmasked rows rather than the clipped rows of \(\Phi_A\). The funded room and manifest-sector conditions and a healthy neighbourhood through radius at least \(500 B\) are used for both identifications below, and the second of them is also what gives the value bound of the extension itself. Indeed, every packet flag lies in its own support and hence in its \(300 B\) neighbourhood, so the flag agreement forces the packet flag set to be empty when that neighbourhood is healthy; the converse follows because both flag sets then vanish there. For the remaining factors, the \(500 B\) health region contains the \(200 B\) energy addresses together with their clean read neighbourhoods, as well as the \(300 B\) and \(304 B\) mask regions. The clean-hull agreement of the clipped and unmasked rows and the manifest-sector mean identity therefore identify the two exponents; the held fine field, likelihoods, masks and normalization factors are the same in both products. Neither rule requires the sector store's flag component to be globally empty. When the packet value is nonzero, every retained mask is nonzero. The mask support conditions then give the local hypotheses of (8.184), including the control of the hot fluctuation bonds. Therefore

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{P}_A | \leqslant \exp \{\Phi_A + C_{\text{tag}} d^4 n\} \leqslant \exp \{((Kd^5 + \beta E) - \beta \mu \tau^2) n\} .\)

Since \(\beta \leqslant 2 h\) and \(E\) has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\), the product \(\beta E\) tends to zero and is eventually at most \(1 \leqslant d^5\). We have proved

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{P}_A | \leqslant \exp \{(Cd^5 - \beta \mu \tau^2) n\}, \qquad C = K + 1.\) (8.187)

The estimate also holds for packets which vanish, since its right-hand side is positive. This argument retains the Gaussian normalization \(m_{\beta}\); it does not replace the original density by an unnormalized free density.

8.25.3Derivatives at the edge of a mask

For a \(C^j\) map \(F\) defined near a point \(y\) in a finite-dimensional real normed space, write

\(\displaystyle F \in \mathcal{B}_j (U, v ; y) \quad \text{if} \quad \|D^k F (y)\| \leqslant Uv^k \quad (0 \leqslant k \leqslant j), \qquad U, v \geqslant 0.\)

The zero-order bound is included. The product rule implies

\(\displaystyle F \in \mathcal{B}_j (U, v ; y), \quad G \in \mathcal{B}_j (V, w ; y) \Longrightarrow FG \in \mathcal{B}_j (UV, v + w ; y) .\) (8.188)

Indeed the norm of the \(k\)th derivative is bounded by \(UV \sum_{r = 0}^k \binom{k}{r} v^r w^{k - r} = UV (v + w)^k\). Repeated application gives a product bound with multiplied amplitudes and added derivative scales.

For the packet's stock-extension variation, the underlying fine configuration in its mask and likelihood factors is held fixed; the coarse field and its compensating fluctuation vary coherently. This matters at the boundary of a cutoff. If any of the retained mask products is identically zero on a neighbourhood of \(y\), the extension and all its derivatives through order \(j\) vanish at \(y\). In the complementary case, every relevant individual mask has points of nonzero value arbitrarily close to \(y\). Continuity of its argument therefore places that argument in the closed support of the mask at \(y\). For example, a cutoff which vanishes when \(\|u\| > 2 a \tau\) implies \(\|u (y)\| \leqslant 2 a \tau\) in this case. The same argument gives the closed fine and predictor threshold inequalities. It does not claim that the mask value at \(y\) is nonzero. The scalar estimate (8.186) and the core estimate must hold on these closed conditions; this is the closed support version of the local input used below.

For the local jet estimate, use the locally smooth stock extension \(\mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}}\) described above; its seed-eligibility indicator is fixed as the coarse field varies. The estimate is made at a base field \(y\) at which the funded-room and manifest-sector conditions hold and the packet's \(500 B\) neighbourhood is healthy. These hypotheses are used twice. They identify the unmasked rows in the exponent of \(\mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}}\) with the clipped rows of \(\Phi_A\), which is how the energy bound (8.184) gives the value bound of the extension's exponent (the last line of (8.189) below); and they give \(\mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}} (y) =\mathcal{P}_A (y)\) in value. No derivative of the physical packet's flag test is taken. Separate the extension as \(e^{\phi (v)} H (v)\), apart from its fixed indicator. The local differentiated estimates give

\(\displaystyle \ \begin{aligned} \phi - \phi (y) & \in \mathcal{B}_j (b (x) n, 1 ; y),\\ H & \in \mathcal{B}_j (e^{\ell n}, c (x) n ; y),\\ e^{\phi (y) + \ell n} & \leqslant e^{(Cd^5 - g) n}, \qquad g = \beta \mu \tau^2, \end{aligned}\) (8.189)

where \(b, c \geqslant 0\), \(b\) has upper exponent \(1\) and \(c\) upper exponent zero. Here \(\ell\) is the ticket expense per seed (not the support radius parameter of the next subsection), and in the next paragraph \(v\) denotes the coarse displacement scale, not the derivative-scale slot of \(\mathcal{B}_j\). The first bound is (8.164). To check the bound for \(H\), first consider a coarse noise cutoff. Its argument is the squared chord of \(V_e Q (U)_e^{- 1}\) divided by \((W \tau)^2\), with \(W > 0\) fixed and \(U\) held fixed. If the chord is strictly larger than \(2 W \tau\), the inner cutoff is locally zero. Otherwise the normalized matrix displacement has bounded value and derivatives of upper exponent zero: its positive derivatives are bounded using \(v / \tau\), where \(v\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) and \(\tau^{- 1}\) has upper exponent \(1 / 2\). Composition with the fixed smooth cutoff on \([0, 4]\) gives derivative scale of upper exponent zero. Its value is in \([0, 1]\). The complementary cutoff \(1 - \chi\) has the same property: its value is still at most one, and its positive derivatives differ only by a sign. At a boundary point use the closed chord inequality, as above. A fixed enlargement of the derivative scale absorbs the finite-order composition constants while keeping amplitude one.

The protected bond cutoffs have the corresponding bound by Subsection 8.18 and the compensated increment estimate. Fine masks, contractions and likelihoods evaluated at the held fine configuration are constants. The first two have absolute value at most one; the product of likelihoods has the stated bound \(e^{\ell n}\). Apply (8.188) to all these factors. There are at most \(nb_{300 B + 2} \# \{ \text{axes} \}\) coarse noise cutoffs and \(nb_{304 B + 2} \rho_L\) protected bond cutoffs, where \(\rho_L\) is the fine-bond density. Their derivative scales add, giving \(c (x) n\) with upper exponent zero, as asserted. The last inequality in (8.189) is the closed support version just explained, applied to the clipped exponent, which equals the extension's exponent at \(y\) under the healthy-neighbourhood, funded-room and manifest-sector hypotheses. Thus the differentiated local action, covariance and chart bounds proved in the preceding sections supply the required estimates.

We give the remaining derivative calculation. The repeated chain rule for \(e^{\phi}\) has one term for each partition \(\pi\) of the \(k\) derivative arguments:

\(\displaystyle D^k (e^{\phi}) (y) [v_1, \ldots, v_k] = e^{\phi (y)} \sum_{\pi} \prod_{C \in \pi} D^{|C|} \phi (y) [v_i : i \in C] .\)

This identity follows by induction: differentiation either adds the new argument as a singleton block or inserts it into one existing block. Each partition appears exactly once. There are at most \(k\)! partitions: send each partition to the permutation whose cycles list each block in increasing order. This map is injective. For unit arguments, (8.189) therefore gives, for \(1 \leqslant k \leqslant j\),

\(\displaystyle \|D^k (e^{\phi}) (y)\| \leqslant e^{\phi (y)} k! (1 + bn)^k \leqslant e^{\phi (y)} \{J_j (1 + bn)\}^k, \qquad J_j = \max \{1, j!\} .\)

The zero-order bound is equality. Using (8.188) and \(n \geqslant 1\), the stock extension \(\mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}}\) belongs to

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_j (e^{(Cd^5 - g) n}, a (x) n ; y), \qquad a (x) = J_j (1 + b (x)) + c (x) .\)

The function \(a\) has upper exponent \(1\), so \(a (x) \leqslant h^2\) eventually. As \(d \geqslant 1\) and \(d \geqslant \log h\) eventually, for \(n \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle a (x) \leqslant e^{2 d^5 n}, \qquad n \leqslant e^{d^5 n}, \qquad (a (x) n)^j \leqslant e^{3 jd^5 n} .\)

Enlarge \(a\) to at least one if necessary. Every derivative of order at most \(j\) is now bounded by the same number:

\(\displaystyle \|D^k \mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}} (y)\| \leqslant \exp \{((C + 3 j) d^5 - g) n\}, \qquad 0 \leqslant k \leqslant j.\) (8.190)

At \(k = 0\) this is also a bound for \(|\mathcal{P}_A (y) |\), since the two agree in value at \(y\). Thus finite differentiation changes the fixed coefficient of the logarithmic expense; it does not consume a fixed fraction of the energy gain. This argument applies, with the same constants, at every point of the variation at which the funded-room, manifest-sector and \(500 B\) healthy-neighbourhood hypotheses hold, the estimate being re-based at that point. It does not apply at other points of the variation: health is a closed condition, and manifestness is not preserved when only the coarse field is varied with the seeds held fixed.

8.25.4Absorbing the expense and the packet count

Here is the quantitative comparison used to turn these estimates into small connected-support bounds. Suppose \(\mu > 0\) and \(\beta \geqslant uh\) for some fixed \(u > 0\). Define

\(\displaystyle M = \mu uP^2, \qquad q_{\ast} = e^{- M / 2} .\)

Since \(h \tau^2 = P^2\), the gain satisfies \(g = \beta \mu \tau^2 \geqslant M\). For any fixed \(D \geqslant 0\), integer \(r \geqslant 0\) and \(z < 2\), the monitor comparison (8.20) gives

\(\displaystyle Dd^r P^z \leqslant M / 16 \quad \text{eventually} .\) (8.191)

Indeed apply that comparison with exponent \(2 - z\) and coefficient \(16 D / (\mu u)\), and multiply by \(\mu uP^z / 16\).

Fix a packet support radius parameter \(\ell\) and a support exponent \(\alpha \geqslant 0\). Let \(H_B\) and \(D_B\) be the root and adjacency counts from Subsection 8.2, and let \(c_{\ell}\) be its cover cost per seed. All are polynomial in \(B\) except the fixed \(c_{\ell}\). Consequently, for sufficiently large \(x\),

\(\displaystyle H_B \leqslant e^{M / 16}, \qquad D_B^2 \leqslant e^{M / 16}, \qquad \alpha c_{\ell} \leqslant M / 16, \qquad 2 \leqslant M / 16.\)

For example, bound both counts by fixed powers of \(d\), use (8.191), and then \(t \leqslant e^t\). Consider a nonnegative packet bound \(C_0 e^{(s - g) n}\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle s \leqslant Dd^r P^z, \qquad 0 \leqslant C_0 \leqslant e^{Dd^r P^z} .\)

Then \(C_0 \leqslant e^{M / 16}\) and the counting ratio obeys

\(\displaystyle D_B^2 e^{s - g + \alpha c_{\ell}} \leqslant e^{M / 16 + M / 16 - M + M / 16} \leqslant q_{\ast} .\)

In particular \(q_{\ast} \leqslant 1 / 2\). The packet counting estimate already proved therefore bounds the site sum over packets with at least \(n_0\) seeds by

\(\displaystyle H_B C_0 \frac{q_{\ast}^{\max (1, n_0)}}{1 - q_{\ast}} \leqslant \exp \{- M \max (1, n_0) / 4\} .\)

To verify the last inequality, put \(r_0 = \max (1, n_0)\). Since \((1 - q_{\ast})^{- 1} \leqslant 2 \leqslant e^{M / 16}\), its logarithm is bounded above by \(3 M / 16 - Mr_0 / 2 \leqslant - Mr_0 / 4\). This applies to (8.187) and (8.190) with \(z = 0\) and \(r = 5\). Hence once the stated local energy, chart and derivative inputs have been established, their full packet and support sums are small uniformly in the period. The scalar comparison selects a threshold after all fixed constants; it does not by itself construct the schedule that reaches that threshold at each renormalization step.

8.26Differentiating the projected Gaussian integrals

The Gaussian used in a local activity varies with the coarse field. Differentiating its expectation therefore involves both the activity and the Gaussian density. We first reduce to the coordinates read by that activity, and then use the compensated change of variables to put all nearby integrals on one reference probability space.

8.26.1Projection and the relative density

Let \(C\) be the positive covariance of Subsection 8.13, in counting colour coordinates. For a finite coordinate set \(I\), let \(C_I\) denote its principal submatrix. Projection of the centered Gaussian with covariance \(C\) is the centered Gaussian with covariance \(C_I\). Indeed its characteristic function at \(\xi \in \mathbb{R}^I\) is \(\exp (- \langle \xi, C_I \xi \rangle / 2)\), by extending \(\xi\) by zero. A function depending only on these coordinates can therefore be integrated against this marginal; the other coordinates need not be independent. The marginal density is

\(\displaystyle p_C (z) = (2 \pi)^{- N / 2} \exp \{- \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det C - \tfrac{1}{2} z^T C^{- 1} z\}, \qquad N = |I|,\) (8.192)

where here and below \(C\) denotes the projected covariance when there is no ambiguity. Positivity of a principal submatrix follows by extending a nonzero vector by zero. Formula (8.192) follows by diagonalizing \(C\) and changing variables in the product of \(N\) one-dimensional standard Gaussian densities. It is also valid in dimension zero, with determinant and density equal to one.

Fix a base coarse parameter \(y_0\) and write \(C_0 = C (y_0)\). For fixed frozen Haar variables, the compensated transformation induces a diffeomorphism \(T_y\) of the \(I\) coordinates with \(T_{y_0} z = z\) and positive Jacobian \(J_y (z)\). The exact expectation identity is

\(\displaystyle \int F_y (w) p_{C (y)} (w) \hspace{0.17em} dw = \int J_y (z) \frac{p_{C (y)} (T_y z)}{p_{C_0} (z)} F_y (T_y z) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0} (z) .\) (8.193)

The positive reference density cancels, leaving the ordinary change-of-variables formula on the right. The set \(I\) includes all colours of every bond in the activity's enlarged read support. The transformation acts bond by bond, and both it and its Jacobian on \(I\) depend only on the old coordinates in \(I\). Locality therefore identifies the transformed marginal integrand with the original activity evaluated on the transformed full field. This explains why the determinant in this formula is \(\det C_I\), rather than the determinant of the covariance on the full torus.

8.26.2Polynomial rank costs at the base point

There is a useful finite-order improvement over estimating a full determinant by its permutation expansion. If \(A (y_0) = 0\) is an \(N \times N\) matrix, multilinearity in the rows gives

\(\displaystyle \det (1 + A (y)) = \sum_{J \subseteq \{1, \ldots, N\}} \det A_J (y) .\) (8.194)

To verify the formula, in each row choose either the row of \(A\) or the corresponding identity row. The identity rows force their columns, leaving precisely the principal minor on the chosen set. Every term of \(\det A_J\) is a product of \(|J|\) functions vanishing at \(y_0\). Its derivatives of order less than \(|J|\) vanish, because at least one factor receives no derivative. Thus derivatives through order \(j\) in (8.194) use only \(|J| \leqslant j\).

Suppose every entry belongs to \(\mathcal{B}_j (v, 1 ; y_0)\), with \(v \geqslant 0\). A minor of size \(r \leqslant j\) has at most \(r\)! permutation terms. The product rule bounds each term by amplitude \((1 + v)^j\) and derivative scale \(j\). There are at most \((j + 1) (1 + N)^j\) subsets of size at most \(j\), since \(\binom{N}{r} \leqslant N^r\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle \det (1 + A) \in \mathcal{B}_j ((j + 1) (1 + N)^j j! (1 + v)^j, j ; y_0) .\)

For \(j = 0\) this just states the value one.

The relative density itself needs one more bookkeeping step, because the quadratic form must be priced without an operator norm that has not been estimated. We use the row-sum norm

\(\displaystyle \|K\|_{\infty} = \max_i \sum_k |K_{ik} |\)

of an \(N \times N\) matrix, which is the operator norm on \((\mathbb{R}^N, \| \cdot \|_{\infty})\) and is submultiplicative. It is bounded by every exponentially weighted row bound of Subsection 8.13, since the weights are at least one. For vectors \(x, x'\) and a matrix \(K\),

\(\displaystyle |x^T Kx' | \leqslant \sum_i |x_i | \sum_k |K_{ik} | |x'_k | \leqslant \|x\|_1 \|K\|_{\infty} \|x' \|_{\infty}, \qquad | \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} K| \leqslant N \|K\|_{\infty} .\) (8.195)

For a multilinear derivative \(D^b K (y_0)\) with matrix values, we write \(\|D^b K (y_0)\|_{\infty} \leqslant c\) if

\(\displaystyle \|D^b K (y_0) [h_1, \ldots, h_b]\|_{\infty} \leqslant c \|h_1 \| \cdots \|h_b \|\)

for all directions \(h_1, \ldots, h_b\); likewise for vector values and \(\| \cdot \|_1\).

Lemma 8.8. Let \(N = |I| \geqslant 1\), \(j \geqslant 0\), and let \(C (y)\) be a \(C^j\) family of symmetric positive definite \(N \times N\) matrices near \(y_0\), with \(C_0 = C (y_0)\). Let \(w (y) \in \mathbb{R}^N\) be \(C^j\) near \(y_0\), with \(w (y_0) = z\). Suppose that \(P, Q, v, W \geqslant 0\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \|C_0^{- 1} \|_{\infty} \leqslant P, \qquad \|D^b C (y_0)\|_{\infty} \leqslant Qv^b, \qquad |D^r w_i (y_0) [h_1, \ldots, h_r] | \leqslant W \|h_1 \| \cdots \|h_r \|\) (8.196)

for \(1 \leqslant b \leqslant j\), \(0 \leqslant r \leqslant j\) and every \(i \in I\). Put \(\kappa = \max (1, P) \max (1, Q)\). Then the relative density

\(\displaystyle R (y) = \frac{p_{C (y)} (w (y))}{p_{C_0} (z)}\)

satisfies

\(\displaystyle R \in \mathcal{B}_j \left( 1, \hspace{0.17em} K_j \hspace{0.17em} N (1 + W)^2 \kappa^3 \max (1, v) ; \hspace{0.17em} y_0 \right),\) (8.197)

where \(K_j \geqslant 1\) depends only on \(j\). For \(N = 0\) the ratio is identically one.

Proof. By (8.192), \(R = e^L\) with

\(\displaystyle L (y) = - \tfrac{1}{2} \{\log \det C (y) - \log \det C_0 \} - \tfrac{1}{2} \{w (y)^T \Gamma (y) w (y) - z^T \Gamma (y_0) z\}, \qquad \Gamma = C^{- 1},\)

and \(L (y_0) = 0\).

The case \(j = 0\). Here (8.197) asks only for \(|R (y_0) | \leqslant 1\), whatever the scale, and indeed \(R (y_0) = e^{L (y_0)} = 1\). So the lemma holds with \(K_0 = 1\), and no derivative of \(C\) or \(w\) enters. From now on \(j \geqslant 1\). Throughout, \(c_r, c'_r, c''_r\) denote constants depending only on \(r\), and \(1 \leqslant r \leqslant j\).

Inverse. Differentiating \(\Gamma C = 1\) gives \(D_h \Gamma = - \Gamma (D_h C) \Gamma\). Induction on \(r\) shows that

\(\displaystyle D^r \Gamma (y_0) [h_1, \ldots, h_r]\)

is a sum of at most \(c_r\) terms of the form

\(\displaystyle \pm \Gamma_0 (D^{b_1} C) \Gamma_0 (D^{b_2} C) \cdots (D^{b_m} C) \Gamma_0, \qquad b_1 + \cdots + b_m = r, b_i \geqslant 1,\)

in which the directions \(h_1, \ldots, h_r\) are distributed among the derivatives of \(C\), all evaluated at \(y_0\). (The induction step applies \(D_h\) either to one factor \(\Gamma_0\), producing \(- \Gamma_0 (D_h C) \Gamma_0\), or to one factor \(D^b C\), raising \(b\) by one; this bounds \(c_{r + 1}\) by \((2 r + 1) c_r\).) By submultiplicativity and (8.196), each term has row-sum norm at most \(P^{m + 1} Q^m v^r \leqslant \kappa^{r + 1} v^r\), since \(m \leqslant r\). Hence \(\|D^r \Gamma (y_0)\|_{\infty} \leqslant c_r \kappa^{r + 1} v^r\), and \(\| \Gamma (y_0)\|_{\infty} \leqslant \kappa\).

Determinant. Near \(y_0\), \(D_h \log \det C = \text{tr} (\Gamma \hspace{0.17em} D_h C)\). Differentiating this \(r - 1\) more times by Leibniz's rule gives at most \(2^{r - 1}\) terms \(\text{tr} (D^a \Gamma \cdot D^{b + 1} C)\) with \(a + b = r - 1\). By (8.195) and the inverse bound, each is at most \(N \cdot c_a \kappa^{a + 1} v^a \cdot Qv^{b + 1} \leqslant c_a N \kappa^{r + 1} v^r\). Thus

\(\displaystyle |D^r \log \det C (y_0) | \leqslant c'_r N \kappa^{r + 1} v^r .\)

Quadratic form. Leibniz's rule for the trilinear expression \(w^T \Gamma w\) writes \(D^r (w^T \Gamma w) (y_0)\) as a sum of at most \(3^r\) terms \((D^a w)^T (D^b \Gamma) (D^c w)\) with \(a + b + c = r\), all at \(y_0\). By (8.196), \(\|D^a w (y_0)\|_{\infty} \leqslant W\) and \(\|D^a w (y_0)\|_1 \leqslant NW\), including \(a = 0\), where \(D^0 w (y_0) = z\). Now (8.195) and the inverse bound give, for each term, the bound \(NW \cdot c_b \kappa^{b + 1} \max (1, v)^b \cdot W\). Hence

\(\displaystyle |D^r (w^T \Gamma w) (y_0) | \leqslant c''_r NW^2 \kappa^{r + 1} \max (1, v)^r .\)

The Euclidean norm of \(z\) and the operator norm of \(\Gamma\) on \(\ell^2\) never enter.

The exponent. Combining the two bounds, with \(Q \leqslant \kappa\) and \(W^2 \leqslant (1 + W)^2\),

\(\displaystyle |D^r L (y_0) | \leqslant K'_r N (1 + W)^2 \kappa^{r + 1} \max (1, v)^r, \qquad K'_r = \tfrac{1}{2} (c'_r + c''_r) .\)

Let \(K = \max_{1 \leqslant r \leqslant j} \max (1, K'_r)\) and \(S = KN (1 + W)^2 \kappa^3 \max (1, v)\). Every factor in \(S\) is at least one, because \(N \geqslant 1\). Therefore, for \(1 \leqslant r \leqslant j\), using \(\kappa^{r + 1} \leqslant \kappa^{3 r}\) and \(N (1 + W)^2 \leqslant \{N (1 + W)^2 \}^r\),

\(\displaystyle |D^r L (y_0) | \leqslant S^r .\)

This absorbs the constants \(K'_r\) into the scale.

Exponentiation. By the exponential partition formula used in Subsection 8.25, \(D^r e^L (y_0)\) is the sum over the set partitions of the \(r\) directions of \(e^{L (y_0)}\) times the product, over the blocks, of the derivatives of \(L\) of the block sizes. Since \(L (y_0) = 0\), each such product is at most \(S^r\), and there are at most \(r\)! partitions (the Bell number is at most \(r\)!). Hence \(|D^r R (y_0) | \leqslant r!S^r \leqslant (j!S)^r\) for \(1 \leqslant r \leqslant j\), while \(R (y_0) = 1\). This is (8.197) with \(K_j = \max (1, j!) K\).\(\Box\)

In the application \(w = T_y z\), the coordinate bound \(W\) is that of Subsection 8.27, of the form \(W \leqslant \sqrt{\beta} \{u (x) + 2 m (x)\} + F_I (z)\), and the quantities \(P, Q, v\) are the weighted row bounds of the principal covariance, its inverse and their derivatives from Sections 8.13 and 8.19. Since \(1 \leqslant F_I (z)\) and \(N \leqslant F_I (z)\), and \(1 + a + F \leqslant 2 (1 + a) F\) for \(a \geqslant 0\), \(F \geqslant 1\), the scale in (8.197) is at most \(D' (x) F_I (z)^3\) with \(D' = 4 K_j \{1 + \sqrt{\beta} (u + 2 m)\}^2 \kappa^3 \max (1, v)\), which has upper exponent zero when \(\kappa\) and \(v\) do. This is the form (8.205) used there, before the Jacobian factor is multiplied in. This is a finite-rank derivative estimate at the base point. It does not yet justify taking derivatives under an integral over unbounded \(z\); that is the purpose of the next subsection.

8.26.3An integrable bound on one open neighbourhood

We prove the additional assertion needed for that interchange. Let \(y\) range over a finite-dimensional real parameter space and let \(C (y)\) be a \(C^j\) family of symmetric matrices near \(y_0\), with \(C_0\) positive definite. Suppose

\(\displaystyle C_0^{- 1} \geqslant \lambda 1, \qquad \lambda > 0.\)

Let \(z\) be an arbitrary sample and let \(w_z (y)\) be a family of \(C^j\) maps such that, on one neighbourhood of \(y_0\), uniformly in \(z\) and in any auxiliary frozen variables,

\(\displaystyle w_z (y) - z \in \mathcal{B}_j (B, 1 ; y), \qquad B \geqslant 0.\) (8.198)

Then there is a nonnegative function \(M\), integrable for \(\gamma_{C_0}\), which bounds every derivative through order \(j\) of \(p_{C (y)} (w_z (y)) / p_{C_0} (z)\) on a possibly smaller common neighbourhood.

By continuity and finite dimension, shrink the neighbourhood so that \(C (y)\) is positive definite and \(C (y)^{- 1} \geqslant (\lambda / 2) 1\). For an explicit verification of the last inequality, make every entry of \(C (y)^{- 1} - C_0^{- 1}\) at most \(\lambda / (2 (1 + N))\) in absolute value. Its row and column sums are at most \(\lambda / 2\), so its quadratic form is bounded in absolute value by \((\lambda / 2) \|z\|^2\). On a smaller closed neighbourhood, all required derivatives of \(C^{- 1}\) and \(\log \det C\) have finite bounds. These bounds may depend on \(N\) and the fixed period.

The derivatives of \(w_z\) have amplitude at most \(B + \|z\|\). Thus the logarithm of the numerator has derivatives bounded by \(K (1 +\|z\|)^2\) for a fixed \(K\). Moreover

\(\displaystyle \|z\|^2 \leqslant 2\|w_z (y) \|^2 + 2 B^2,\)

and hence

\(\displaystyle - \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det C (y) - \tfrac{1}{2} w_z (y)^T C (y)^{- 1} w_z (y) \leqslant K_0 + \lambda B^2 / 4 - \lambda \|z\|^2 / 8.\)

The repeated exponential chain rule now bounds every derivative through order \(j\) of the relative density by

\(\displaystyle M (z) = K_1 \frac{e^{- \lambda \|z\|^2 / 8} (1 +\|z\|)^{2 j}}{p_{C_0} (z)} .\) (8.199)

This is integrable against \(\gamma_{C_0}\): multiplication by its density cancels the denominator, and a polynomial times a strictly decaying Gaussian is integrable in finite dimension. Explicitly, \((1 + t)^{2 j} \leqslant e^{2 jt}\) for \(t \geqslant 0\), and completing the square absorbs \(2 jt\) into half the negative quadratic exponent. The remaining integral factors into \(N\) finite one-dimensional Gaussian integrals. The same proof works when \(z\) is a measurable function of a larger probability space with law \(\gamma_{C_0}\).

8.26.4Why the activity satisfies the neighbourhood hypotheses

For packet factors we use the locally smooth stock extension of Subsection 8.25, with its fixed seed-eligibility indicator. Under the stated healthy-footprint and sector conditions, its value at the base field equals the physical packet. We do not differentiate the physical packet's varying flag test.

For the compensated map of Subsection 8.20, write \(u_0 = \nu_0 + \beta^{- 1 / 2} z\) and \(F_M (u) = u + \psi (M, u)\). Its displacement in Gaussian coordinates is

\(\displaystyle T_y z - z = \sqrt{\beta} \hspace{0.17em} \{\psi (M (y), u_0) + \nu_0 - \nu (y)\} .\) (8.200)

Here the fixed coupling satisfies \(\beta > 0\). The perturbation \(\psi\) and all its parameter derivatives are uniformly bounded for all \(u_0\): outside the fixed radius-\(2 d_{\ast}\) ball of Subsection 8.20 it is zero, and inside that ball compactness applies. The unmasked mean and its derivatives are bounded on a common neighbourhood uniformly in the frozen Haar variables. Indeed the frozen cut logarithms are bounded smooth functions of the coarse variation; the finitely many source sums, row matrices and principal inverses have uniform derivative bounds there. All principal determinants stay nonzero after one common shrink. Formula (8.200) proves (8.198). The same compact argument and the finite product rule bound the Jacobian derivatives uniformly.

The remaining activity integrand also has bounded derivatives on one neighbourhood, uniformly in the Gaussian sample. The required localization is worth spelling out. Compare two fluctuation arrays \(u, \tilde{u}\) such that at each bond either they agree or both have norm greater than \(4 a \tau\). Every cutoff of width at most \(2 a \tau\) is then equal on the two arrays, and if that cutoff is nonzero the arrays agree at that bond. A masked chart logarithm therefore agrees as well. A packet's nonzero protected product forces agreement throughout its \(304 B\) region, which contains the read stencils of all its \(200 B\) action terms. Thus its logarithmic exponent and its complete value agree.

For a linear Gaussian ratio term, the score vanishes on inactive bonds and its hot endpoint carries the doubled cutoff. For a quadratic ratio term, every nonzero matrix itinerary has two hot endpoints. To check this, \(AC - 1\) is zero if either coordinate is inactive, since both \(A\) and \(C\) equal the identity on that coordinate. In a product of copies of \(- (AC - 1)\) followed by \(A\), the first nonzero factor forces the initial endpoint hot, and the last factor cannot join a hot coordinate to an inactive one. Both endpoint cutoffs therefore force equality of the coordinates actually used by the quadratic term. This argument holds nearby as long as the principal inverses exist; it does not require the nearby point to satisfy the original sector inequalities.

It follows that every fixed activity factor is unchanged when large initial fluctuation coordinates are replaced by bounded ones, provided this comparison is made through the compensated map and the fine field is held fixed. Apply radial truncation at radius \(3 d_{\ast}\) to the initial fluctuation coordinates \(u_0 = \nu_0 + \beta^{- 1 / 2} z\) of each bond. These do not depend on the parameter \(y\), so the lack of smoothness of radial truncation in its argument plays no role. The truncation leaves the closed radius-\(3 d_{\ast}\) ball unchanged, and in particular the radius-\(2 d_{\ast}\) ball, which is all that is used. Outside that ball \(F_M\) is the identity; both the old and truncated coordinates then remain beyond the cutoff threshold, since \(8 a \tau \leqslant d_{\ast} / 2\). Inside it, the coordinates agree. The resulting bounded input family has uniform parameter derivatives by the compact chart construction. The frozen fine factors remain merely bounded measurable functions: their regular activities, forced activities, likelihoods and contractions use the supplied finite-volume bounds for the chosen labels. No differentiation of these frozen factors is required.

For each fixed finite product of activity factors, finite sums, products and smooth compositions on bounded sets now give a common bound on all derivatives through order \(j\). The local action, normalization and chart functions used here are the unmasked smooth extensions already constructed. Hard selection indicators are fixed. Together with (8.199) and the Jacobian bound, this gives an integrable envelope for the entire right-hand side of (8.193), also after integrating the frozen variables against their Haar probability law.

8.26.5Interchange and quantitative use

Here is the precise final differentiation argument. Suppose \(G (y, \omega)\) is measurable in \(\omega\), \(C^j\) in \(y\) on an open neighbourhood, and for each \(r \leqslant j\) its \(r\)th derivative is bounded there by an integrable function \(M_r (\omega)\). Then

\(\displaystyle D^r \int G (y, \omega) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu (\omega) = \int D_y^r G (y, \omega) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu (\omega), \qquad r \leqslant j.\) (8.201)

Derivative measurability follows in a fixed finite basis by taking limits of difference quotients of measurable functions. Dominated convergence first gives continuity of each derivative integral. For the induction step, the fundamental theorem of calculus on a short parameter segment writes its difference quotient as the integral of the next derivative along that segment. The bound \(M_{r + 1}\) permits dominated convergence as the segment shrinks. Starting at order zero proves the derivative identity and its continuity successively through order \(j\).

Apply this to the common reference expression above. It proves smoothness of each projected activity integral. If a sharper estimate at the base point gives \(G (\cdot, \omega) \in \mathcal{B}_j (q (\omega), D ; y_0)\) and \(\int q \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \leqslant Q\), then (8.201) and the triangle inequality give

\(\displaystyle \int G (\cdot, \omega) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu (\omega) \in \mathcal{B}_j (Q, D ; y_0) .\)

The neighbourhood envelope establishes the validity of differentiation; the sharper moment estimate supplies its period-independent bound. Those moment estimates must be paid separately. In particular the period-dependent constants used to justify (8.201) are never used as uniform renormalization bounds.

8.27Uniform moment bounds after changing Gaussian coordinates

For real centered Gaussian coordinates we use the Wick–Isserlis pairing rule [MVV25, Theorem 1]. For singular covariance, add independent centered Gaussian noise of covariance \(\epsilon I\) and let \(\epsilon \downarrow 0\) in the finitely many polynomial moments. Repeated coordinates are allowed, and complex linear combinations follow by multilinearity.

We now estimate the polynomial in the Gaussian sample introduced by differentiation of the projected density. The covariance may couple every coordinate to every other coordinate. Only its uniform diagonal bound is needed for this step.

8.27.1A polynomial moment bound without independence

Let \(Z\) be a centered Gaussian vector with covariance \(C \geqslant 0\) and \(C_{ii} \leqslant b\). Each coordinate has exponential moment

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}e^{tZ_i} = e^{C_{ii} t^2 / 2} \leqslant e^{bt^2 / 2} .\)

This follows directly by completing the square in its one-dimensional Gaussian law, including the zero-variance case. For an integer \(r \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle (1 + |z|)^r \leqslant e^{r |z|} \leqslant e^{rz} + e^{- rz} .\)

Hence

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} (1 + |Z_i |)^r \leqslant 2 e^{br^2 / 2} .\) (8.202)

Let \(I\) be a set of physical bonds, let \(I_{\text{col}}\) contain all their colour coordinates, and put \(n = |I|\), \(N = |I_{\text{col}} |\). Define

\(\displaystyle F_I (z) = 1 + n + N + \sum_{i \in I_{\text{col}}} |z_i |, \qquad Y_I = 1 + n + 2 N.\)

Then

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}F_I (Z)^r \leqslant Y_I^r \{1 + 2 Ne^{br^2 / 2} \} .\) (8.203)

For nonempty \(I_{\text{col}}\) choose a coordinate at which \(|z_i |\) is maximal. The definition gives \(F_I (z) \leqslant Y_I (1 + |z_i |)\), and therefore

\(\displaystyle F_I (z)^r \leqslant Y_I^r \left( 1 + \sum_{i \in I_{\text{col}}} (1 + |z_i |)^r \right) .\)

For an empty coordinate set the same inequality is immediate. Take expectations and use (8.202). In particular all these moments are integrable. This argument uses one-coordinate marginals and a finite sum, without factoring a joint moment.

For the local covariance, the weighted matrix bounds of Subsection 8.13 supply a fixed \(b\), independent of the period. Suppose an activity has nonnegative support bound \(s\), equal to the sum of its covering numbers and nonnegative length charges. A cover by radius-\(B\) balls gives

\(\displaystyle | \text{supp} | \leqslant s \hspace{0.17em} b_B, \qquad |I| \leqslant s \hspace{0.17em} b_B \rho_L, \qquad N = q |I|,\)

where \(q\) is the colour dimension and \(\rho_L\) the fixed bond density. The first inequality follows by summing the covering balls over all factors; overlaps can only decrease the union. The second uses that every bond of \(I\) has its anchor in that union. Since \(b_B\) has upper exponent zero, (8.203) implies, for every fixed \(r\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}F_I (Z)^r \leqslant A_r (x) (1 + s)^{m_r},\) (8.204)

for an integer \(m_r\) and a nonnegative \(A_r\) of upper exponent zero. Indeed insert the displayed bounds for \(n, N\) into (8.203) and expand the resulting fixed polynomial.

8.27.2The derivative of the density and Jacobian

At the base parameter the compensated transformation is the identity. The coordinate derivative bound following from (8.200) is

\(\displaystyle \|D^r (T_y z)_i |_{y = y_0} \| \leqslant \sqrt{\beta} \{u (x) + 2 m (x)\} + F_I (z), \qquad 0 \leqslant r \leqslant j,\)

where \(u, m\) bound respectively the compensation increment and mean derivatives and have upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). For the value, use \(|z_i | \leqslant F_I (z)\); for positive orders differentiate the compensated displacement. As \(\beta \leqslant 2 h\), the first term has upper exponent zero.

The principal covariance and its inverse inherit the weighted derivative bounds proved earlier. The finite-order relative determinant argument of Subsection 8.26 therefore gives a derivative scale polynomial in \(N\), \(F_I (z)\) and functions of upper exponent zero. The Jacobian is a product of \(n\) one-bond factors. Each has value one at the base parameter and derivative scale of upper exponent zero, by Subsection 8.20. Its product has amplitude one and the sum of these scales. Since \(1, n, N, |z_i | \leqslant F_I (z)\), every term of the fixed polynomial is bounded by one common power of \(F_I (z)\). We obtain an integer \(k\) and a nonnegative \(D (x)\) of upper exponent zero such that the complete density ratio times Jacobian, denoted \(R_y (z)\), satisfies

\(\displaystyle R_{\cdot} (z) \in \mathcal{B}_j (1, D (x) F_I (z)^k ; y_0) .\) (8.205)

The exponent \(k\) depends on the derivative order and fixed construction data, not on the support size or period.

8.27.3Retaining the warning gain under integration

Consider a finite product of local activity factors indexed by \(\mathcal{P}\). Write \(t_i \geqslant 0\) for their pointwise bounds and \(s\) for their total support bound. Suppose their proved local derivative estimates, under the coherent variation, give

\(\displaystyle \Phi_{\cdot} (z) \in \mathcal{B}_j \left( \boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{E}} (z) \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} t_i, \hspace{0.17em} 2 r (x) s ; y_0 \right), \qquad r \geqslant 1,\) (8.206)

where \(r\) has upper exponent zero and \(\mathcal{E}\) is the joint warning event for the \(m\) warning factors. The displayed estimate implies that all derivatives through order \(j\) vanish outside \(\mathcal{E}\). In this application the stronger local-vanishing property holds: an unsatisfied warning factor is zero on a neighbourhood of \(y_0\), as in the closed-support argument for packet masks. For the other factors, their derivative scales add: each nonzero supported factor has covering number at least one, so its unit scale and length scale together cost at most twice its assigned support bound. This proves the factor \(2 s\) in (8.206) from the individual bounds. The individual estimates for all species remain necessary inputs to this product step.

Multiply (8.206) by (8.205). Since \(F_I \geqslant 1\) and \(1 + s \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle 1 + DF_I^k + 2 rs \leqslant (1 + D + 2 r) (1 + s) F_I^k .\)

Converting to derivative scale one therefore bounds all derivatives through order \(j\) of the transformed integrand by

\(\displaystyle \boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{E}} (z) \left( \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} t_i \right) (1 + D + 2 r)^j (1 + s)^j F_I (z)^{kj} .\) (8.207)

Suppose the simultaneous-warning estimate of Subsection 8.3 gives \(\mathbb{P} (\mathcal{E}) \leqslant \rho^{2 m}\) with \(\rho \geqslant 0\). By Cauchy–Schwarz and (8.204) at order \(2 kj\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{E}} F_I^{kj}] \leqslant \mathbb{P} (\mathcal{E})^{1 / 2} (\mathbb{E}F_I^{2 kj})^{1 / 2} \leqslant E (x) (1 + s)^a \rho^m,\)

for a nonnegative upper-exponent-zero function \(E\) and an integer \(a\). For example replace the square root of the moment bound by \((1 + A_{2 kj}) (1 + s)^{m_{2 kj}}\). This also proves integrability of (8.207). Integrating any frozen Haar variables adds no factor, since their total mass is one and the estimate is uniform in them.

Consequently the integrated derivatives obey

\(\displaystyle \left| D^r \int R_y \Phi_y \hspace{0.17em} d \mu |_{y = y_0} \right| \leqslant E (x) (1 + D + 2 r (x))^j (1 + s)^{a + j} \rho^m \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} t_i, \qquad 0 \leqslant r \leqslant j.\) (8.208)

The derivative interchange is justified by the separate common neighbourhood bound of Subsection 8.26. Absorb one factor \(\rho\) into the bound of each warning factor. The right-hand side then has exactly the product-bound form used by the connected support summation, with one fixed polynomial in \(1 + s\). The support-estimate of Subsection 8.2 allows such a fixed polynomial through its exponential reserve. Thus Gaussian differentiation preserves the available warning gain while adding only upper-exponent-zero and polynomial support costs. No independence of warning events or Gaussian coordinates has been used.

8.28The common differentiated activity bound

We assemble the estimates on the actual four-part alphabet: connected seed packets, forced activities, exponentiated logarithmic activities, and warnings. All factors use the same sector, fine representation, tags, Gaussian law and compensated variation. Besides the hypotheses of the preceding local estimates, retain the deterministic safety and plateau inequalities of Subsection 8.3, whose safety conditions are produced in Subsection 8.15. Their construction is an incoming requirement at this stage. The argument below explains both their derivative use and the common bound they yield.

8.28.1Exponentiating the logarithmic activities

If \(f \in \mathcal{B}_j (w, d ; y_0)\) with \(0 \leqslant w \leqslant 1\), then

\(\displaystyle e^f - 1 \in \mathcal{B}_j (C_j w, d ; y_0)\) (8.209)

for a constant depending only on \(j\). For the value, the mean value theorem gives \(|e^{f (y_0)} - 1| \leqslant ew\). For a positive derivative order \(k \leqslant j\), use the partition formula for the exponential. A partition with \(m \geqslant 1\) blocks contributes at most \(ew^m d^k \leqslant ewd^k\). There are at most \(k\)! partitions, so \(C_j = e \max (1, j!)\) suffices. This retains the small amplitude and does not require that the parameter variation stay within the small-amplitude bound on a whole neighbourhood.

Fix \(- 1 / 2 < t < 0\). The inherited logarithmic bounds of Subsection 8.24, the three short bounds of Subsection 8.22, and the correction chain bounds of Subsection 8.23 have a common rooted bound \(J (x)\) of upper exponent \(t\), after an arbitrarily small strict exponent loss. Their supports are nonempty. Since the support exponent and extra-cost weight are nonnegative, each individual bound is at most \(J (x)\): choose one site in its support and retain that summand in the rooted bound. Eventually \(J (x) \leqslant 1\). Thus (8.209) applies to every logarithmic label with the same \(C_j\) and the same threshold, even when the alphabet is countably infinite. Multiplying all its bounds by \(C_j\) preserves their upper exponent.

The forced family needs no exponentiation. By enlarging a nonnegative function \(r (x) \geqslant 1\) of upper exponent zero, both families have jets with derivative scale

\(\displaystyle r (x) \{1 + c_B (S_i) + d_i \} .\) (8.210)

Here \(S_i\) is the full assigned support and \(d_i\) its old-cover or path-length charge; for a correction itinerary it is the correction order. The short terms have \(d_i = 0\). All earlier scale bounds are dominated by this expression.

8.28.2Warnings vanish as germs at a reduced threshold

Let \(\xi > 0\) be the safety margin and use the smaller threshold \(\xi / 2\) in the Gaussian event. Suppose every coordinate within distance \(2 B\) of a warning anchor satisfies

\(\displaystyle |z_i | \leqslant (\xi / 2) \tau \sqrt{\beta} .\)

The deterministic safety estimate at margin \(\xi\) implies that the warning factor is zero on a neighbourhood of the base parameter. We check the different dependencies, since a value equal to zero by itself would not imply vanishing derivatives.

A warning in its mandatory seed neighbourhood is identically zero by its frozen indicator. Outside that neighbourhood, the safety estimate makes every relevant fine plaquette reading strictly smaller than \(\tau / 4\), and every predictor chord strictly smaller than \(W \tau\). The fine mask, site likelihood, bond likelihood and contraction test are evaluated at the held fine configuration \(U_{\ast}\). Their values are consequently constant under the variation and equal to one. The contraction statement uses the fine plaquette estimates at all sites in its read set, which fits in the same \(2 B\) event window. The predictor chord depends continuously on the varied coarse field, with its fine predictor held fixed. Its strict inequality persists on a neighbourhood, so its cutoff also remains one there.

For a fine-bond cutoff, the reduced threshold bounds the base increment by \((C + q \xi / 2) \tau\). The safety constants satisfy \(C + q \xi / 2 < a\), where \(a \tau\) is its plateau radius. The compensated increment is continuous, so this strict inequality also persists locally. Its cutoff-minus-one is thus a zero germ. Intersecting the finitely many neighbourhoods needed by a given factor proves the assertion. In particular, every nonzero jet of a warning is supported on the reduced-margin excursion event; no assertion about differentiation of an indicator of that event is needed.

The warning amplitude is bounded by the fixed constant

\(\displaystyle T = 1 +\# \{ \text{frame tags} \} +\# \{ \text{predictor tags} \} .\)

Indeed the held likelihoods are bounded by their tag counts, all masks lie in \([0, 1]\), and the warning is a protected difference from one. Its derivative scale has the form (8.210) with \(d_i = 0\). For a varying noise cutoff, normalize its chord by \(W \tau\); the positive derivatives cost the coarse scale divided by \(\tau\), of upper exponent zero. For a bond cutoff use the corresponding normalized compensated increment. A cutoff outside its closed support is a zero germ; on the closed support the smooth scalar cutoff has bounded derivatives. Multiplying over its protective read set adds at most a function of upper exponent zero times its support cover. This proves the asserted scale in all cases, including boundary points.

For every \(t < 0\), the Gaussian estimate at margin \(\xi / 2\) gives, after increasing the threshold in \(x\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{P} (\text{all $m$ reduced-margin warnings}) \leqslant (h^{2 t})^m .\) (8.211)

This follows from the faster-than-every-power bound already proved, applied at an exponent strictly below \(2 t\). Set \(\rho = h^t\). The Gaussian moment argument consumes the square root of (8.211), leaving the bound \(T \rho\) per distinct warning. Its site sum has upper exponent \(t\): warning addresses have fixed density, each support lies in a fixed-multiple-\(B\) ball, its cover is bounded by a fixed constant, and the number of such supports through a site is polynomial in \(B\).

8.28.3Packets and the product estimate

The jets of the packet's stock extension \(\mathcal{P}_A^{\text{st}}\) at a base field satisfying the hypotheses of Subsection 8.25 have bound

\(\displaystyle p_A = \exp \{((C + 3 j) d^5 - \beta \mu \tau^2) n (A)\}\)

and derivative scale one by (8.190). The same energy gain pays their root counts, support exponents and any prescribed fixed seed marks by the absorption argument in Subsection 8.25. Thus their available weighted site bound is smaller than every fixed negative power of \(h\). A disconnected packet has factor and bound zero. All thresholds are chosen after the fixed derivative order, halo, support and length reserves.

Let \(\mathcal{P}\) be a finite family of distinct labels of the four types whose assigned supports are healthy for the base coarse field: no coarse flag at the threshold \(\text{fieldCut} (x)\) lies in any \(S_i\). In particular, each packet's full enlarged support must have this property to apply its packet jet estimate. This is a separate hypothesis on the tested family; the sector's manifest flag identity alone does not imply it. For packet jets we use the locally smooth stock extension of Subsection 8.25; the healthy \(500 B\) neighbourhood, together with the funded-room and manifest-sector conditions, identifies its value with the physical packet at the base field. Likewise, for the coarse tail of the inherited logarithmic activities we differentiate the unmasked extension of Subsection 8.24, with the fixed long-pair selector and without the kept-row test; on a healthy footprint and under the funded-room condition it equals the actual coarse tail in value at the base field. No derivative of the physical flag test or of the kept-row test is asserted. Put

\(\displaystyle s = \sum_{i \in \mathcal{P}} \{c_B (S_i) + d_i \} .\)

Every nonzero factor has nonempty support, hence \(c_B (S_i) \geqslant 1\). The derivative scales in (8.210) therefore sum to at most \(2 r (x) s\), also covering the unit packet scales. Let \(a_i\) denote the packet, forced or exponentiated-log bound, and put \(a_i = T\) for a warning. The product rule and the zero-germ property give exactly

\(\displaystyle \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} f_i \in \mathcal{B}_j \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{C}} (z) \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} a_i, 2 r (x) s ; y_0 \right),\)

where \(\mathcal{C}\) is the joint reduced-margin warning event. This verifies (8.206) for all species on the same variation. Applying the Gaussian differentiation and moment estimates yields

\(\displaystyle \left| D^k \int \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} f_i \right| \leqslant A_j (x) (1 + s)^{m_j} \prod_{i \in \mathcal{P}} \tilde{a}_i, \qquad 0 \leqslant k \leqslant j,\) (8.212)

with \(A_j\) of upper exponent zero and \(\tilde{a}_i = a_i\) except that a warning has bound \(T \rho\). The integral includes the Gaussian dependence on the varied field and the frozen Haar variables, as in that argument.

The combined optional site sum has upper exponent \(t\), and the packet site sum is negligible. For a desired output exponent \(t_{\text{out}} \in (- 1 / 2, 0)\), choose \(t \in (- 1 / 2, t_{\text{out}})\). Fixed upper-exponent-zero factors and the fixed polynomial in \(1 + s\) are then paid by the strict exponent slack and the support reserve of Subsection 8.2. Its tree degree condition holds eventually because the geometric neighbour count has upper exponent zero whereas the vertex bound has negative upper exponent. This supplies the differentiated product input for the connected-support estimates. It preserves the incoming representation and its selected history; it does not construct or reset their parameters.

8.29Recovering the block integral from the activities

The preceding estimates concern a dictionary of local factors. We now verify that its absolutely convergent sum is the original block integral. This verification fixes the scalar normalization and the incidence conditions before any logarithm of a gas is taken. Throughout, fix the coarse field, forest, allocation of seeds, and tag array. The funded sector hypotheses, the chart-access conclusion and the locality statements used in the preceding sections are retained. In particular, all precisions are positive, the covariance has its specified finite range, and the same assigned supports include every variable read by their factors.

8.29.1The Gaussian density and the scalar factor

Write \(A\) for the completed sector precision, \(A_0\) for its flat reference, and \(C\) for the localized covariance. The reference \(A_0\) is formed at the identity coarse field with no seeds and the same forest: all free bonds are active and only tree coordinates receive identity completion. The precision \(A\) comes from the selected affine row square with an identity completion on inactive coordinates; \(C\) is a positive finite-range approximation to its inverse. We retain the correction \(C^{- 1} - A\) below, rather than identifying the two. In particular, this affine precision is not asserted to be the Hessian of the full nonlinear effective action. All three act on the full real coordinate space, of dimension \(D\). Let \(q\) be the colour dimension, \(N\) the number of free forest bonds, and \(n\) the number of active free bonds. The other \(N - n\) free bonds are integrated in their original Haar coordinates. Put

\(\displaystyle g = \beta^{- 1 / 2}, \qquad b = \sqrt{2 \pi}, \qquad m = c_{\text{H}} (bg)^q,\)

where \(c_{\text{H}} > 0\) is the constant in the local probability-Haar density of Subsection 8.18. The mean \(\mu\) is the physical field associated with \(- P^{\ast} \ell\), where \(P\) is the padded, column-dependent principal inverse and \(\ell\) the linear row source of Subsection 8.14. This uses \(P^{\ast}\), rather than \(C\) or \(A^{- 1}\). Its inactive coordinates vanish: the precision has an inactive identity block, every principal inverse preserves that block, and the inactive source coordinates are zero. With \(u = \mu + gz\), the reference Gaussian has density

\(\displaystyle \vartheta_C (z) = b^{- D} \exp \left( - \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det C - \tfrac{1}{2} z^T C^{- 1} z \right) .\) (8.213)

Inactive coordinates are completed by independent standard Gaussians. Their energy is \(\mathscr{G} (z) = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i \text{inactive}} z_i^2\). There are \(D - nq\) such coordinates, including the tree coordinates. Consequently the change of variables in the active Haar coordinates, with the dummy Gaussian integrals inserted, gives the multiplier

\(\displaystyle b^{- (D - nq)} g^{nq} c_{\text{H}}^n \exp \left( - \mathscr{G} (z) + \sum_{e \text{active}} j_e (u) \right) .\) (8.214)

Here \(j_e\) is the localized logarithmic Haar correction; its auxiliary cutoff agrees with the actual correction wherever the smaller chart mask is nonzero. The constant in (8.214) is exactly

\(\displaystyle b^{- D} m^N \exp (- (N - n) \log m) .\) (8.215)

Indeed the right side is \(b^{- D} m^n = b^{- (D - nq)} g^{nq} c_{\text{H}}^n\). This uses \(n + (N - n) = N\), not the number of active coordinates as a substitute for the number of free bonds.

The dummy insertion is exact even though the active integrand depends on the remaining Haar coordinates. Split the latter product measure into active and inactive free bonds and freeze the inactive variables. The active affine substitution has Jacobian \(g^{nq}\). In the orthogonal coordinate decomposition into active and dummy real variables, the dummy density integrates to one. Fubini therefore proves the identity for each fixed inactive configuration and then for its Haar integral. Chart access guarantees that the nonzero original integrand is covered by this substitution; the smaller mask can be inserted there with value one. No assertion that the entire Haar group lies in one chart is used.

Let \(\mathscr{L}\) denote the original positive prefactor: it is the noise normalization times \(\exp (v| \Lambda_f |)\) times the coarse-field scalar extracted from the inherited exponent. The scalar retained outside all activities is

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{O} = \mathscr{L} \hspace{0.17em} m^N (\det A_0)^{- 1 / 2} > 0.\) (8.216)

The flat reference uses the very same forest. Its determinant is not replaced by a componentwise determinant, nor is (8.216) charged to every component.

8.29.2Restoring the masks and the exponent

We spell out the cancellations that identify the density. Let \(\mathscr{E} (u)\) be the whole quadratic-plus-remainder block energy, and let \(r_j (u)\) be the inherited logarithms. Let \(d_p (u)\) be the individual nonlinear remainders, so the signed channel average satisfies

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{E} (u) = \langle \sum_p d_p (u) \rangle + Q (u),\)

where \(Q (u) = \frac{1}{2} \|Mu + b_{\text{row}} \|^2\) is the physical quadratic expression. The selected row matrix \(M\) has zero inactive columns; \(A = M^T M + I_{\text{inactive}}\) and \(\ell = M^T b_{\text{row}}\). The inactive coordinates of \(\mu\) and \(\ell\) are zero. This is the rowwise square expansion used in Sections 8.21 and 8.22: each row is assigned either to its seed neighbourhood or to the remote remainder, with no omitted row. Write \(c_e = \frac{1}{2} (J_e (A_0) - J_e (A))\) for the local normalization correction.

The compulsory packet exponent, summed over the packet layout, is

\(\displaystyle s (u) = - \beta (D_{\text{near}} (u) + Q_{\text{seed}} (\mu)) + \sum_{e \text{near}} c_e + \sum_{e \text{near}} j_e (u) - (N - n) \log m.\) (8.217)

Here \(D_{\text{near}} (u)\) is the signed average of \(d_p (u)\) over the posted rows at radius \(200 B\), and \(Q_{\text{seed}} (\mu)\) is the signed average of the extracted row squares at \(\mu\) over those same posted rows. They are not complementary row sets. The correction sum \(\sum c_e\) uses radius \(200 B\), whereas the Haar correction sum \(\sum j_e\) uses radius \(304 B\); they are different sets. The packet layout uses disjoint halos of radius at least \(500 B\), so all reads through \(304 B\), including the extra two lattice steps, belong to a unique packet. Thus summing the packet exponents gives exactly (8.217). Likewise the packet products reproduce the compulsory likelihoods, flag tests and chart masks once each. A test whose location belongs to a packet has the same seed membership in that packet as in the full allocation: a seed changing the test would meet that packet's halo, and hence would lie in the same component.

Let \(f_i (z)\) be the optional logarithms in the complete dictionary. On the nonzero smaller chart mask their sum is

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \sum_i f_i (z) = & \sum_j r_j (u) + \sum_p h_p (u) + \sum_{e \text{remote}} c_e + \sum_{e \text{remote}} j_e (u) + \mathscr{R} (z), \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.218)} \\ \mathscr{R} (z) = & \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \det C + \log \det A_0) - \sum_e c_e + z^T \hat{h} + \tfrac{1}{2} z^T (C^{- 1} - A) z. \end{array}\)

The remote index sets here are the exact complements of the two near sets above. The determinant and inverse series giving \(\mathscr{R}\) are those of Sections 8.13 and 8.23. Their three determinant contributions are

\(\displaystyle - \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \det A - \sum_e J_e (A)), \quad \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \det A_0 - \sum_e J_e (A_0)), \quad \tfrac{1}{2} (\log \det A + \log \det C) .\)

Their sum is the determinant part of \(\mathscr{R}\). The linear itineraries sum to \(z^T \hat{h}\) and the quadratic itineraries sum to \(\frac{1}{2} z^T (C^{- 1} - A) z\). Their endpoint protectors equal one on the smaller full mask. Absolute summability was proved before this recombination, so these are identities of convergent series.

The remote action terms obey

\(\displaystyle \sum_p h_p (u) - \beta (D_{\text{near}} (u) + Q_{\text{seed}} (\mu)) = - \beta \left( \langle \left. \sum_p d_p (u) \rangle + Q (\mu) \right) . \right.\) (8.219)

For a near row this follows from its assigned debit and mean-square term. For a remote row it is precisely the unmasked local-square identity: its protecting cutoff equals one on the smaller full mask. Summing first over rows and then over the finite signed channels proves (8.219), including rows with zero factors.

Finally, if \(\ell\) is the linear coefficient of \(Q\), then \(\hat{h} = - g^{- 1} (A \mu + \ell)\) and \(\beta g^2 = 1\) give

\(\displaystyle - \beta Q (u) - \mathscr{G} (z) = - \beta Q (\mu) + z^T \hat{h} + \tfrac{1}{2} z^T (C^{- 1} - A) z - \tfrac{1}{2} z^T C^{- 1} z.\) (8.220)

To verify this, expand \(Q (\mu + gz)\), use that the inactive mean and linear coefficient vanish, and add the inactive identity quadratic form supplied by \(\mathscr{G}\). This is an algebraic identity, valid also where the masks vanish.

Combining (8.217)–(8.220) cancels both local sums \(\sum c_e\) and both full determinants. It gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & - \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det A_0 + s (u) + \sum_i f_i (z) - \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det C - \tfrac{1}{2} z^T C^{- 1} z\\ & \qquad = \sum_j r_j (u) - \beta \mathscr{E} (u) - \mathscr{G} (z) + \sum_{e \text{active}} j_e (u) - (N - n) \log m. \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.221)} \end{array}\)

Multiplication by \(b^{- D} m^N \mathscr{L}\) and (8.215) now recover the exact original density and its Haar Jacobian.

There is also a finite product identity for the masks. Write \(a_p\) for a compulsory test and \(w_p\) for its optional warning. At a compulsory position \(w_p = 0\) and its packet supplies \(a_p\); at every other position the optional factor is \(1 + w_p = a_p\) on the smaller full mask. The auxiliary mask warnings restore that full mask from the packet masks. If it vanishes, the entire product on both sides is zero. If it is nonzero, every doubled mask is one and the preceding identities apply literally. The complete product of physical tag likelihoods equals the number of tag choices times the original normalized likelihood. Thus a single factor \(|\mathcal{T}|^{- 1}\), where \(\mathcal{T}\) is the tag-array space, remains outside the product. This is the uniform tag average, not a new likelihood for each use of a tag.

8.29.3Absolute expansion and return to probability Haar

Fix a finite old forced-label set \(K\) and a packet layout \(\mathcal{P}\). For finite sets \(I\) of logarithmic labels and \(J\) of warning labels, the expanded integrand is

\(\displaystyle \Phi_{K, I, J} = \prod_{P \in \mathcal{P}} P (u) \prod_{k \in K} K_k (u) \prod_{i \in I} (e^{f_i (z)} - 1) \prod_{j \in J} w_j (u) .\) (8.222)

The four alphabets form a disjoint union; coincident physical locations do not identify their labels. On the finite lattice the nonempty supports and rooted estimates imply \(\sum_i p_i < \infty\) for the whole alphabet. Separately, the all-argument logarithm estimates of Sections 8.22–8.24 give envelopes \(|f_i (z) | \leqslant a_i\) with \(\sum_i a_i < \infty\). Their endpoint cutoffs are retained in this assertion. Thus \(\sum_i |f_i (z) | < \infty\), and

\(\displaystyle \exp \left( \sum_i f_i (z) \right) = \sum_{I \text{finite}} \prod_{i \in I} (e^{f_i (z)} - 1) .\)

For a proof, exhaust the countable alphabet by finite subsets and expand each finite product. Absolute convergence follows from \(\sum_i |e^{f_i} - 1| < \infty\): outside finitely many indices, \(|e^{f_i} - 1| \leqslant 2 |f_i |\). The warning alphabet is finite and its expansion is the ordinary identity for \(\prod_j (1 + w_j)\).

The simultaneous product estimate (8.22) gives, with the original probability Haar variables \(y\) retained,

\(\displaystyle \int | \Phi_{K, I, J} (y, z) | \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_F (y) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_C (z) \leqslant \prod_{v \in \mathcal{P} \sqcup K \sqcup I \sqcup J} p_v .\)

Summing the right side over all finite label sets gives at most \(\prod_v (1 + p_v) \leqslant \exp (\sum_v p_v)\). Distinct triples \((K, I, J)\) give distinct label sets. Tonelli applied to the absolute values therefore justifies every exchange between these sums and integrals. The same argument covers the subsequent finite sum over seed allocations and tag arrays. There is no division by the value of a vacuum or flagged term.

An allocation contributes only when its flags match the coarse field, the old forced labels are admissible, and their inventories have the required incidence with the seeds. These conditions are necessary for a nonzero original integrand: the profile enforces its exact flags, and a nonzero old gas term enforces its admissibility and incidence. Set the entire integral to zero when any condition fails. On a valid allocation the density calculation above applies; on an invalid one both sides vanish. Hence the absolutely convergent sum, multiplied by \(|\mathcal{T}|^{- 1} \mathscr{O}\), equals the original contribution for the fixed tags and seed allocation. Summing the old forced sets, then the tags and seed allocations, recovers the probability-Haar block push with scalar factor (8.216).

8.29.4Factorization into connected activities

It remains to show that the integral of a molecule factors over its connected components. Connectivity is defined by intersection of the full assigned supports. For fixed tags, different components read disjoint Haar coordinates. Their Gaussian coordinates may be larger than their physical read sets; the assigned supports include the full covariance-range halo around every Gaussian coordinate read. If two components have disjoint assigned supports, any two Gaussian coordinates read by them are farther apart than the range of \(C\). Their cross covariance is zero. The Gaussian characteristic function consequently splits into the product of its component characteristic functions, proving independence of these coordinate projections. It follows that the Gaussian integral of the product factors. The remaining product-Haar integral factors by its disjoint read sets. All integrands are measurable and integrable by the preceding absolute bounds.

Lastly average over the tags. A component integral depends only on the site tags and on the bond tags anchored in its assigned support. These coordinate sets are disjoint for different components. The uniform measure on tag arrays is a product measure, so averaging first the bond tags and then the site tags proves the same factorization for the averaged integrals.
