Averages are taken after each component has been identified, using the common original tag space.

For each connected label set \(M\), define its activity \(a_M (V)\) by this averaged integral with the exact local incidence tests: its inventory must equal the flags of \(V\) inside its assigned support, and all its forced-label and packet tests must hold. The integral uses the globally flagged sector with the same local data. Locality shows that its value agrees with the restriction of the full allocation: on this support the seeds, tags, forced labels and coarse field coincide. The flags outside the support cannot change its value.

Taking connected components and taking their union are inverse on admissible label families. Indeed every label has nonempty support, so distinct components cannot share a label. Their supports are disjoint by definition. Exact global flag coverage is equivalent to exact local inventory equality in each component together with coverage by all inventories. This proves the weight-preserving factorization, including the empty family. Reindexing the absolutely convergent sum gives

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{B}R) (V) = \mathscr{O} (V) \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} \mathcal{A} \text{finite, compatible}\\ \bigcup_{M \in \mathcal{A}} I_M = \text{Flags} (V) \end{array}$}}} \prod_{M \in \mathcal{A}} a_M (V) .\) (8.223)

Thus the local bounds an exact representation of the original block push. The later separation of flag-free logarithms and the iteration of the resulting bounds still use their stated construction inputs; neither follows merely from (8.223).

8.30Averaging the forests and separating the regular gas

For maximal-tree gauge fixing on a lattice, see Creutz [Cre77]. The comparisons below track changes between the particular rooted forests used in the block construction and the associated chart factors.

The exact block gas of Subsection 8.29 was constructed with a fixed forest. To restore the lattice symmetries we average over forests before taking the logarithm of the flag-free gas. We first show that this operation leaves the scalar normalization unchanged, and then choose an average compatible with local factorization.

8.30.1The flat determinant is independent of the forest

Every block forest has one outgoing edge from each nonroot vertex, directed towards the root. Distinct nonroot vertices give distinct unoriented positive-bond representatives of these edges. If two outgoing edges represented the same bond with opposite orientations, the forest height would strictly decrease in both directions, which is impossible. Thus the tree bonds are in bijection with the nonroot vertices. In particular all forests have the same number of tree bonds and the same number \(N\) of free bonds.

Let \(F, H\) be two forests and choose a bijection \(t\) between their tree bonds. We construct an integer change of basis on all bond coordinates, including the dummy tree coordinates. For a bond cochain \(u\), let \(D_F u\) set its \(F\)-tree coordinates to zero. Let \(\alpha_F u (x)\) be the signed sum of \(u\) along the forest path from \(x\) to its block root, and put

\(\displaystyle B_F u = u + \nabla \alpha_F u.\)

Telescoping along a path proves the following four facts: \(B_F u\) is zero on the \(F\)-tree bonds; \(B_F u = u\) if \(u\) was already zero there; \(\alpha_F u\) is zero at each root; and \(B_F \nabla v = 0\) when \(v\) is zero at all roots. These assertions hold over any abelian group, since they involve only signed sums.

Define \(T_{FH} u\) on the \(H\)-tree bonds by transporting the old \(F\)-tree coordinates through \(t\), and on the other bonds by \(B_H D_F u\). Then

\(\displaystyle D_H T_{FH} u = B_H D_F u.\)

The reverse construction uses \(t^{- 1}\). On tree coordinates it plainly recovers \(u\). On the complementary coordinates,

\(\displaystyle B_F D_H T_{FH} u = B_F (D_F u + \nabla \alpha_H D_F u) = B_F D_F u = D_F u,\)

because \(\alpha_H D_F u\) vanishes at the roots. Thus \(T_{HF} T_{FH} = 1\); reversing the argument gives the other inverse identity. Its matrix and inverse have integer entries. Their integer determinants multiply to one, so \(| \det T_{FH} | = 1\). Apply this same construction to every colour coordinate.

Let \(M_F, M_H\) be the flat selected row matrices, and let \(D_F^0, D_H^0\) be the diagonal projections onto their tree coordinates. There is one forest-independent flat row map \(M\), with \(M_F = MD_F\) and \(M_H = MD_H\). Flat rows annihilate gradients of root-zero functions. For a fine row this is the telescoping identity around its plaquette, preserved by each linear path coefficient. For a predictor row the coarse sampling operator sends a gradient to the coarse gradient of its root values; these values are zero. Signed coordinate changes preserve gradients, so the statement holds simultaneously in all signed channels. Hence

\(\displaystyle M_H T_{FH} = M_F, \qquad T_{FH}^T D_H^0 T_{FH} = D_F^0 .\)

The second identity follows because the tree coordinates are simply permuted. For the completed flat precisions we obtain

\(\displaystyle A_F^0 = M_F^T M_F + D_F^0 = T_{FH}^T A_H^0 T_{FH}, \qquad \det A_F^0 = \det A_H^0 .\)

Together with the common free-bond count, this proves that the scalar \(\mathscr{O}\) in (8.216) is forest independent.

8.30.2An independent choice at each coarse site

There are finitely many allowed forests on a finite torus: each vertex chooses one of finitely many oriented neighbouring edges and one of finitely many heights. The set is nonempty by the block-tree construction. At each coarse site \(x\), choose independently a uniform forest \(F_x\) from this finite set. On the block with root \(x\), use only the restriction of \(F_x\). The assembled object is a valid forest, since each original forest edge remains in its parent block and its height decreases along that edge. Repeated global forest choices producing the same assembled forest cause no difficulty; the average is defined on the product space of choices.

For a connected component \(M\), its integrated activity depends on these choices only at coarse sites in its full assigned support \(X_M\). This follows from the local reads of the rows, inverse windows, tags and chart factors: agreement of the tree edges in that support gives equality of each such read and of its Gaussian marginal. To compare the Haar factors, realize both local charts as pullbacks of the same full product Haar measure; agreement of the principal Gaussian marginals then gives equality of the integrals. In particular changing a forest outside \(X_M\) does not alter the component integral. Compatible components have disjoint \(X_M\). Independence of the site choices therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_F \prod_{M \in \mathcal{A}} a_M^F (V) = \prod_{M \in \mathcal{A}} \mathbb{E}_F a_M^F (V) .\) (8.224)

This is the factorization of a product measure over disjoint coordinate sets; it does not hold for an arbitrary average over one global forest.

Set \(\bar{a}_M (V) =\mathbb{E}_F a_M^F (V)\). The bounds on \(a_M^F\) are uniform in \(F\), so the same bounds \(\bar{a}_M\). The finite average commutes with the absolutely convergent gas sum, and the forest-independent \(\mathscr{O}\) factors out. Thus (8.223) holds with \(\bar{a}_M\) in place of \(a_M^F\). A signed lattice transformation permutes the site choices and transforms every chosen forest by a bijection. Its product uniform measure is unchanged. The covariance of the individual sector formulas consequently gives the same covariance for \(\bar{a}_M\), with the original transformed activity addresses.

8.30.3The cost of excluding regular activities

Separate the connected labels into regular labels \(i\) with empty inventory and forced labels \(j\) with nonempty inventory. Write their values as \(w_i (V)\) and \(k_j (V)\), and their supports as \(X_i, Y_j\). A nonzero regular value has no flag in its support. Fix a compatible finite forced family \(A\) whose inventories cover all flags, and put \(Y_A = \bigcup_{j \in A} Y_j\). The remaining sum is the regular gas restricted to labels whose supports avoid \(Y_A\):

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{B}R) (V) = \mathscr{O} (V) \sum_{A \text{admissible}} \prod_{j \in A} k_j (V) \hspace{0.17em} Z_{Y_A} (V) .\) (8.225)

The reindexing is exact: a compatible family splits uniquely into its regular and forced parts, and cross compatibility means precisely \(X_i \cap Y_A = \varnothing\) for every retained regular label. Absolute convergence justifies summing in this order.

Assume the regular bounds satisfy the connected-cluster bound of Subsection 8.1, with nonnegative support weight, rooted allowance \(\gamma\) and tail ratio \(q_0 < 1\). Let \(c_{\Gamma}\) be the corresponding logarithmic term, indexed by a finite ordered tuple of occurrences, with its Ursell coefficient divided by the tuple factorial. Labels may repeat in this tuple. Then

\(\displaystyle Z = \exp \left( \sum_{\Gamma} c_{\Gamma} \right) > 0.\)

For a finite set \(Y\) define

\(\displaystyle P_Y = \sum_{\Gamma : \hspace{0.17em} X_{\Gamma} \cap Y \ne \varnothing} c_{\Gamma}, \qquad X_{\Gamma} = \bigcup_{i \in \Gamma} X_i .\)

Deleting labels that meet \(Y\) replaces \(c_{\Gamma}\) by zero precisely when its union support meets \(Y\). Otherwise it leaves the term unchanged. The same absolute majorant applies to the deleted gas. Subtracting the two absolutely convergent logarithmic series yields

\(\displaystyle Z_Y = Ze^{- P_Y} .\) (8.226)

The rooted cluster estimate, summed over \(x \in Y\), gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} \Gamma : \hspace{0.17em} X_{\Gamma} \cap Y \ne \varnothing\\ | \Gamma | - 1 \geqslant m \end{array}$}}} |c_{\Gamma} | \leqslant |Y| \frac{\gamma q_0^m}{1 - q_0} .\) (8.227)

Indeed each term on the left is counted by at least one site of \(Y \cap X_{\Gamma}\); overcounting is harmless in this nonnegative sum. In particular, with \(C_Y = |Y| \gamma / (1 - q_0)\),

\(\displaystyle Ze^{- C_Y} \leqslant Z_Y \leqslant Ze^{C_Y} .\)

The correction \(P_Y\) retains its actual sign; only its absolute estimate uses \(C_Y\). Combining (8.225) and (8.226) gives the exact separation

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{B}R) (V) = \mathscr{O} (V) Z (V) \sum_{A \text{admissible}} \prod_{j \in A} k_j (V) e^{- P_{Y_A} (V)} .\) (8.228)

For each fixed \(A\), the ratio is finite and positive. The original absolute family sum implies absolute summability of this outer series: its term is exactly the signed forced product times the restricted regular gas, whose absolute value is bounded by the corresponding sum of absolute original terms. No volume-independent bound on the extensive scalar \(Z\) is asserted.

8.30.4Local regular functions and their derivatives

For each regular component, the integrated construction gives a measurable local extension \(f_i\), smooth through the purchased finite order at configurations healthy on \(X_i\), with

\(\displaystyle w_i (V) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{X_i \cap \text{Flags} (V) = \varnothing\}} f_i (V) .\)

The forest average preserves locality, gauge invariance, measurability and each uniform derivative bound, by linearity and the triangle inequality for the finite average. For a cluster tuple \(\Gamma = (i_1, \ldots, i_n)\), define

\(\displaystyle F_{\Gamma} (V) = \frac{u (i_1, \ldots, i_n)}{n!} \prod_{r = 1}^n f_{i_r} (V) .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle c_{\Gamma} (V) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{X_{\Gamma} \cap \text{Flags} (V) = \varnothing\}} F_{\Gamma} (V) .\) (8.229)

If the union is healthy, every constituent mask is one. If it is not, a flagged site belongs to some constituent support, so that factor vanishes. This proves the equality even for repeated labels.

Suppose the local extension of the \(r\)th occurrence has derivatives through order \(j\) bounded by \(p_{i_r}\) at derivative scale one at configurations healthy on its support. Restrict throughout the following derivative estimates to configurations healthy on \(X_{\Gamma}\); every constituent hypothesis then holds. Leibniz's formula gives product amplitude \(\prod_r p_{i_r}\) at derivative scale \(n\). Thus its derivatives through order \(j\) are bounded by

\(\displaystyle \frac{|u (i_1, \ldots, i_n) |}{n!} (1 + n)^j \prod_r p_{i_r} \leqslant \frac{|u (i_1, \ldots, i_n) |}{n!} \prod_r (2^j p_{i_r}),\)

since \(1 + n \leqslant 2^n\) for \(n \geqslant 1\). Buying the regular cluster bound with the inflated bounds \(2^j p_i\) therefore pays these derivatives on the same union support. A spatial derivative weight is also local: from any anchor in \(X_{\Gamma}\), the connected-cover diameter bound places every read within a fixed multiple of \((B + 1) (1 + \text{cover}_B \hspace{0.17em} X_{\Gamma})\). Its polynomial cost is paid by the reserved exponential support weight as in Subsection 8.2.

Equations (8.228) and (8.229) supply the regular logarithm and the exact exclusion correction of the nonlinear step. The exclusion exponential must still be regrouped with the forced labels using its occurrence multiplicities; it cannot be dropped or replaced by an independent probability. Its quantitative allowance is the rooted bound (8.227).

8.31Regrouping the exclusion correction

The regular gas in (8.228) has been extracted, but the forced family still carries its exclusion factor. We now put this factor into local forced activities. The factorials and signs of the repeated logarithmic terms are retained throughout.

Fix a coarse configuration \(V\). Continue to write \(k_j\) for a forced activity with support \(Y_j\) and nonempty inventory \(I_j \subseteq Y_j\), and \(c_{\Gamma}\) for a regular logarithmic cluster term with nonempty support \(X_{\Gamma}\). We have summable majorants

\(\displaystyle |k_j | \leqslant p_j, \qquad |c_{\Gamma} | \leqslant q_{\Gamma}, \qquad \sum_j p_j + \sum_{\Gamma} q_{\Gamma} < \infty .\)

These follow on a finite lattice from the previously purchased rooted bounds. Here \(q_{\Gamma}\) is specifically the absolute Ursell coefficient divided by the tuple factorial, multiplied by the purchased regular constituent bounds. Thus the rooted cluster estimates apply to this majorant itself, not merely to \(|c_{\Gamma} |\). All component addresses, support sets and eligibility tests are fixed before their values at \(V\) are evaluated.

For a finite admissible forced family \(A\), the exclusion correction has the absolutely convergent expansion

\(\displaystyle e^{- P_{Y_A}} = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} n : \text{finite support in the cluster alphabet}\\ n_{\Gamma} > 0 \Rightarrow X_{\Gamma} \cap Y_A \ne \varnothing \end{array}$}}} \prod_{\Gamma : n_{\Gamma} > 0} \frac{(- c_{\Gamma})^{n_{\Gamma}}}{n_{\Gamma} !}, \qquad n_{\Gamma} \in \mathbb{N}.\) (8.230)

For a finite cluster alphabet this is the product of the ordinary exponential series. For the whole alphabet, the sum of the absolute products is at most \(\exp (\sum_{\Gamma} q_{\Gamma})\); increasing finite subsets and dominated convergence prove the identity. Moreover the sum after multiplication by \(\prod_{j \in A} k_j\) and summation over \(A\) is absolutely dominated by

\(\displaystyle \prod_j (1 + p_j) \exp \left( \sum_{\Gamma} q_{\Gamma} \right) .\) (8.231)

The restriction that every retained cluster meet \(Y_A\) is essential: the correction deletes exactly those regular clusters.

A finite decorated family consists of a finite set of forced labels and a finite set of cluster labels, with a positive integer multiplicity attached to each cluster label. Give a forced label its support \(Y_j\) and multiplicity one, and a cluster label its support \(X_{\Gamma}\). Its graph has one vertex per distinct label, with edges for intersecting supports. A new forced label \(D\) is a connected decorated family such that the old forced supports are pairwise disjoint and every cluster support meets the union of the old forced supports in \(D\). Write \(Z_D\) for its union support and \(I_D = \bigcup_{j \in D \text{forced}} I_j\) for its inventory. In particular \(D\) contains a forced label and \(I_D \ne \varnothing\).

Define its value by

\(\displaystyle \tilde{k}_D (V) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{ \text{Flags} (V) \cap Z_D = I_D \}} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{ \text{the old local eligibility tests hold} \}} \prod_{j \in D \text{forced}} k_j (V) \prod_{\Gamma \in D \text{cluster}} \frac{(- c_{\Gamma} (V))^{n_{\Gamma}}}{n_{\Gamma} !} .\) (8.232)

The cluster label itself already includes its original tuple factorial in \(c_{\Gamma}\). The additional \(n_{\Gamma}\)! belongs to the exponential in (8.230); these are two different factorials.

Consider a nonzero term in the expansion for \((A, n)\). Split its support graph into connected components. Every retained cluster meets a forced support in \(A\), and that forced label is in its component. Thus each component is a new forced label as just defined. Its inventory equals the flags inside its union support: a flag there belongs to some old inventory because \(A\) covers all flags, and the corresponding old forced support meets this component, so belongs to it. The reverse inclusion follows from the original inventory condition. Old eligibility tests hold for each nonzero factor. Hence the product of (8.232) over the components equals the original term, with every sign and factorial.

Conversely, flatten a finite compatible family of new labels whose inventories cover all flags and whose product is nonzero. Its old forced labels are pairwise compatible, both within and between the new labels. They satisfy all eligibility and exact flag-coverage conditions. A cluster label cannot occur in two new components: its nonempty support would intersect both of their supposedly disjoint union supports. Its multiplicity is therefore recovered unambiguously. Each retained cluster meets a forced support in its own component, as required in (8.230). The connected components of the flattened graph are exactly the original new labels. Flattening and taking components are inverse.

This weight-preserving bijection and (8.231) give

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{B}R) (V) = \mathscr{O} (V) \exp \left( \sum_{\Gamma} c_{\Gamma} (V) \right) \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} \mathcal{D} \text{finite, compatible}\\ \bigcup_{D \in \mathcal{D}} I_D = \text{Flags} (V) \end{array}$}}} \prod_{D \in \mathcal{D}} \tilde{k}_D (V) .\) (8.233)

If there are no flags, no nonzero forced component is possible and the empty family contributes one. If there are flags, every nonzero component has a nonempty inventory. This is the required separation into a regular exponential and a forced gas.

The new values are measurable and gauge invariant: each factor and eligibility predicate is, and the products are finite. They use only the final coarse field and its flags on \(Z_D\), by locality of the constituents and of the inventory test. Under a signed lattice transformation the old labels and the cluster occurrence tuples are permuted, with their multiplicities unchanged. Thus the value, support and inventory transform together. A common countable enumeration may then be chosen for the new labels, assigning zero to any unused addresses. Such an enumeration only reindexes the absolutely convergent gas and changes none of its values or support conditions.

For later bounds the same construction has the explicit nonnegative majorant

\(\displaystyle \tilde{p}_D = \prod_{j \in D \text{forced}} p_j \prod_{\Gamma \in D \text{cluster}} \frac{q_{\Gamma}^{n_{\Gamma}}}{n_{\Gamma} !} .\) (8.234)

Taking absolute values in (8.232) proves the bound by (8.234). We next prove the uniform rooted estimate for these majorants.

8.31.1Keeping one compulsory gain through the second expansion

Let \(a \geqslant 0\) be the desired support exponent and \(\varepsilon > 0\) a reserve. Suppose the regular constituent bounds obey the cluster criterion at support exponent \(a + \varepsilon\), rooted allowance \(u > 0\) and tail ratio \(1 / 2\). Suppose also that the old forced bounds obey

\(\displaystyle \sup_x \sum_{j : x \in Y_j} p_j e^{(a + \varepsilon) c_B (Y_j)} \leqslant ue^{- M}, \qquad M \geqslant 0.\)

The regular cluster bound then gives

\(\displaystyle \sup_x \sum_{\Gamma : x \in X_{\Gamma}} q_{\Gamma} e^{(a + \varepsilon) c_B (X_{\Gamma})} \leqslant 2 u.\)

Set \(\eta = 20 u\) and assume

\(\displaystyle \eta \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \qquad \frac{4 b_B \eta}{\varepsilon} \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \qquad b_B = (2 B + 1)^4 .\) (8.235)

Here is the distinct-set form of the connected-tree bound used below. If the site bound of nonnegative weights \(w_i\) at exponent \(a + \varepsilon\) is at most \(\gamma\), the sum at exponent \(a\) over distinct connected sets of \(m + 1\) labels meeting a given site is at most \(\gamma (4 b_B \gamma / \varepsilon)^m\). Indeed, choose a root whose support contains the site. Enumerate the other \(m\) labels in each of their \(m\)! orders and choose a spanning tree oriented toward that root. This gives at least \(m\)! terms in the rooted forest sum, with root weights restricted to the given site, and the factorial in (8.3) cancels them. The covering number of the union is at most the sum of the individual covering numbers, so the same bound applies with the union weight. The second condition in (8.235) is this degree bound with \(\gamma = \eta\).

We prove that, for every site \(x\) and integer \(m \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} D : \hspace{0.17em} x \in Z_D\\ |D|_{\text{copies}} \geqslant m \end{array}$}}} \tilde{p}_D e^{ac_B (Z_D)} \leqslant 2^{- m} e^{- M} \hspace{0.17em} 2 \eta .\) (8.236)

For an upper bound forget the old compatibility tests, allow forced labels to repeat as well, and retain only connectedness and the presence of at least one forced label. On the resulting combined alphabet put \(w_j = p_j\), \(w_{\Gamma} = q_{\Gamma}\), and give each forced occurrence a mark \(M\), each regular occurrence a mark zero. Inflate a bound to

\(\displaystyle v_i = 2 w_i e^{e_i}, \qquad e_j = M, \quad e_{\Gamma} = 0.\)

The rooted bound for \(2 v_i\) at exponent \(a + \varepsilon\) is at most \(4 e^M ue^{- M} + 4 (2 u) = 12 u \leqslant \eta\). Thus these bounds meet the distinct connected-set criterion with allowance \(\eta\) and tail ratio \(1 / 2\), by (8.235).

To include multiplicities, first charge the desired support weight to each occurrence by setting \(v_i' = v_i e^{ac_B (X_i)}\), with \(X_i\) denoting either kind of support. Since \(X_i \ne \varnothing\), choose a site in \(X_i\) and use the single term of the rooted bound for \(2 v_i\) to obtain \(v_i' \leqslant \eta \leqslant 1 / 2\). For a fixed distinct set of labels \(S\), summing all positive multiplicities gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_{(n_i \geqslant 1)_{i \in S}} \prod_{i \in S} \frac{(v_i')^{n_i}}{n_i !} \leqslant \prod_{i \in S} \sum_{n \geqslant 1} v_i' (1 / 2)^{n - 1} = \prod_{i \in S} 2 v_i' .\)

Here we used \(n! \geqslant 1\) and \(v_i' \leqslant 1 / 2\). Connectedness depends only on the distinct supports, so the distinct connected-set bound now shows that the sum over all rooted connected multiplicity families is at most \(\eta / (1 - 1 / 2) = 2 \eta\). Cover subadditivity on a connected set gives \(c_B (\bigcup_{i \in S} X_i) \leqslant \sum_{i \in S} c_B (X_i) \leqslant \sum_i n_i c_B (X_i)\), justifying the charge per occurrence.

Finally the inflated product differs from the original one by \(2^{\sum_i n_i} \exp (\sum_i n_i e_i)\). For a retained family these two factors are at least \(2^m\) and \(e^M\), respectively. Extracting them proves (8.236). Restricting back to the actual new labels only decreases the nonnegative sum. This argument preserves a compulsory gain even after arbitrary repeated regular corrections have been attached.

In the selected scale estimates, \(u (x)\) has a fixed negative upper exponent and \(B (x)\) has upper exponent zero. Both inequalities in (8.235), and the first regular degree bound with its own fixed positive reserve, therefore hold eventually. The compulsory mark is

\(\displaystyle M (x) = \frac{\mu}{4} \text{fineMonitor} (x)^2, \qquad \mu > 0.\)

Thus (8.236) is precisely \(40 u (x) 2^{- m} e^{- M (x)}\). It pays every fixed outgoing gain \(\exp (- A_0 P (x)^{\delta})\) with \(A_0 \geqslant 0\) and \(0 \leqslant \delta < 2\), where \(P (x) = \exp ((\log \log x)^2)\) is the unshifted monitor. Indeed \(P (x) \leqslant \text{fineMonitor} (x)\) eventually, the ratio \(\text{fineMonitor} (x)^{\delta} / \text{fineMonitor} (x)^2\) tends to zero, and \(40 u (x) 2^{- m} \leqslant 1\) eventually. This proves the outgoing forced allowance from the incoming regular/forced bounds, with the same scale and history.

8.32Absorbing localized logarithms into the forced gas

The two exponential shifts in (9.6) arise from an exact regrouping of the forced activities with localized logarithms. We prove this regrouping, including its flag constraints and the derivative bound needed for a tilted family.

All activities and logarithms in this section are real valued. Fix a coarse configuration \(U\) and let \(F (U)\) be its set of flagged sites. A hard activity \(K_i (U)\) has a nonempty support \(X_i\), a nonempty inventory \(I_i \subseteq X_i\), and a fixed eligibility condition. The hard factors and their eligibility predicates are measurable, gauge invariant and local on \(X_i\), with the same final-flag convention as the logarithms below. Nonzero hard values satisfy their eligibility condition. The hard gas sums over finite families \(A\) of pairwise disjoint supports with \(\bigcup_{i \in A} I_i = F (U)\):

\(\displaystyle G (U) = \sum_{A \text{admissible}} \prod_{i \in A} K_i (U) .\)

The empty family is allowed precisely when \(F (U) = \varnothing\). Each logarithmic term \(\ell_j (U)\) has nonempty support \(Y_j\), is measurable and gauge invariant, and is local with the same threshold convention as the hard activities. Assume

\(\displaystyle Y_j \cap F (U) = \varnothing \Longrightarrow \ell_j (U) = 0.\) (8.237)

Suppose there are summable uniform majorants for both \(K_i\) and \(\ell_j\). These hypotheses will also imply absolute convergence of the new gas; it need not be assumed separately.

Use a disjoint union of the hard and logarithmic labels, assigning the factors

\(\displaystyle r_i = K_i, \qquad r_j = e^{\ell_j} - 1\)

and the supports \(X_i\), \(Y_j\), respectively. A new activity label \(C\) is a finite connected set of these labels which contains at least one hard label and whose hard supports are pairwise disjoint. Write \(Z_C\) for the union of all its supports and \(J_C = \bigcup_{i \in C \text{hard}} I_i\) for its inventory. The new activity is

\(\displaystyle \hat{K}_C (U) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{Z_C \cap F (U) = J_C \}} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{ \text{all hard labels in } C \text{ are eligible at } U\}} \prod_{v \in C} r_v (U) .\) (8.238)

Its support and inventory are fixed by \(C\) before \(U\) is chosen. Its inventory is nonempty. The extra eligibility test is exactly the conjunction of the original tests, not a relaxed test. The new gas \(\hat{G}\) uses pairwise disjoint \(Z_C\) and exact coverage of \(F (U)\) by the \(J_C\).

8.32.1The exact compatible-family bijection

The claimed identity is

\(\displaystyle \hat{G} (U) = G (U) \exp \left( \sum_j \ell_j (U) \right) .\) (8.239)

First expand the right side as a sum over a finite admissible hard family \(A\) and a finite set \(B\) of logarithmic labels:

\(\displaystyle \sum_{A \text{admissible}} \sum_{B \text{finite}} \prod_{i \in A} K_i (U) \prod_{j \in B} (e^{\ell_j (U)} - 1) .\) (8.240)

To justify this, absolute summability of \(\ell_j (U)\) implies absolute summability of \(e^{\ell_j (U)} - 1\). Outside finitely many indices \(| \ell_j (U) | \leqslant 1\), and \(|e^x - 1| \leqslant 2 |x|\) there. Finite products \(\prod_j [1 + (e^{\ell_j} - 1)]\) equal the corresponding exponentials; their expansions converge absolutely, dominated by \(\prod_j (1 + |e^{\ell_j} - 1|)\). Passing to the limit proves the logarithmic part of (8.240). The hard sum is absolutely dominated by \(\prod_i (1 + |K_i |)\), so the double sum is also absolutely convergent.

Consider a nonzero term of (8.240) and split \(A \sqcup B\) into connected components of its support intersection graph. Every component contains a hard label. In fact, if it contains a logarithmic label \(j\), its nonzero factor implies \(\ell_j (U) \ne 0\). By (8.237), some flagged site lies in \(Y_j\). Exact coverage of all flags by the old hard inventories places that site in some \(I_i\), \(i \in A\). Since \(I_i \subseteq X_i\), the labels \(i, j\) have overlapping supports and lie in the same component. Each component is therefore an allowed new label.

Distinct components have disjoint supports. For a component \(C\), any flag in \(Z_C\) belongs to an old inventory \(I_i\); its hard support then meets \(Z_C\) and so \(i\) lies in \(C\). Conversely every \(I_i\) in \(C\) consists of flags and lies in \(Z_C\). Thus \(Z_C \cap F (U) = J_C\). Every old hard factor in the nonzero term is eligible. The product of the new activities over the components consequently equals the original term, and their inventories cover \(F (U)\).

Conversely, take an admissible new family with nonzero product and flatten its labels. Within each component the old hard supports are disjoint by definition; between components they are disjoint because the whole component supports are disjoint. The old hard inventories cover \(F (U)\), and the local eligibility indicators in (8.238) equal one. Flattening therefore gives an admissible hard family and a finite set of logarithmic labels. No label is duplicated between components, because its support is nonempty and would otherwise meet both disjoint component supports. The connected components of the flattened support graph are exactly the original new labels. This proves that flattening and taking components are inverse on nonzero terms.

The identity follows by this weight-preserving bijection and absolute convergence of (8.240). In particular the bijection transports its absolute sum to that of the new gas. It also includes the empty-family case: when there are no flags, every logarithm vanishes and no nonzero new component can occur.

The new activities have the required locality and symmetry. Products inherit gauge invariance; flags and all old eligibility predicates are gauge invariant, so the two indicators are also invariant. Measurability follows from measurable predicates and finite products. For locality on \(Z_C\), agreement of the configurations and their flag data there gives agreement on every constituent support and on every eligibility test. Finally the first indicator enforces exact local inventory equality. These arguments apply to the original addresses and predicates throughout.

8.32.2Preserving the derivative allowance

Now let the hard factors be \(K_i (\theta, U)\), with the logarithmic terms independent of \(\theta\). All addresses and eligibility tests remain fixed. Let \(D \geqslant 0\), and suppose the hard factors have the joint bound of (9.1) with majorants \(p_i\) on \(| \theta | D \leqslant 1\), and are independent of \(\theta\) whenever their support does not contain a fixed anchor \(o\). Suppose also that \(| \ell_j (U) | \leqslant v_j \leqslant 1\), and, for an exponent \(u \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_x \sum_{i : x \in X_i} p_i e^{(u + 1) c (X_i)} \leqslant b, \qquad \sup_x \sum_{j : x \in Y_j} v_j e^{(u + 1) c (Y_j)} \leqslant w.\) (8.241)

Choose \(\kappa \geqslant 0\) and define inflated constituent bounds

\(\displaystyle a_i = e^{\kappa} p_i \quad (i \text{hard}), \qquad a_j = 2 v_j \quad (j \text{logarithmic}), \qquad \Gamma = e^{\kappa} b + 2 w.\)

For a logarithmic factor the constant joint bound is \(2 v_j\). For a hard factor the joint bound is \(e^{- \kappa} a_i\). By the product rule for joint bounds, the product in a new label \(C\) has joint bound

\(\displaystyle e^{- \kappa \# \{i \in C : i \text{hard} \}} \prod_{v \in C} a_v \leqslant e^{- \kappa} \prod_{v \in C} a_v = : p_C .\)

The two indicators in (8.238) are independent of \(\theta\), so the same bound holds for \(\hat{K}_C\). It is independent of \(\theta\) outside the anchor support as well: if \(o \not\in Z_C\), each hard constituent is independent of \(\theta\).

The constituent site bound at exponent \(u + 1\) is at most \(\Gamma\). If \(h_B \Gamma \leqslant 1\), the distinct connected-set estimate (7.35), with reserve one, gives

\(\displaystyle \sup_x \sum_{C : x \in Z_C} p_C e^{uc (Z_C)} \leqslant e^{- \kappa} (e^{\Gamma} - 1) .\) (8.242)

Restricting to labels with nonempty hard part and separated hard supports only decreases the nonnegative sum. Thus an allowance \(A\) is sufficient whenever

\(\displaystyle e^{- \kappa} (e^{\Gamma} - 1) \leqslant A.\)

All the properties in (9.3) are preserved, with the same anchor and insertion debt \(D\) and with new site bound \(A\). Applying Subsection 9.1 then gives marked forced cost \(DA\) on the fused alphabet. The original derivative parameter is never rescaled when a logarithmic contribution is absorbed.

For uniform absolute convergence in this argument, the finite lattice and nonempty supports imply summability of \(p_i\) and \(v_j\) from (8.241). The same estimate and (8.242) give summability of \(p_C\). Thus the scalar bijection, the parameter derivatives, and the site bounds refer to the same series. Applying this construction successively to the two localized logarithmic corrections proves the two gas shift identities used in (9.6), provided their actual corrections satisfy (8.237), the fixed-address conditions and the purchased bounds above. Producing those corrections from the nonlinear block integral, and paying these inequalities on the selected history, remain the analytic inputs.

8.33The marked block integral on the original activity addresses

We now obtain the exact tilted identity used in Subsection 9.1. The insertion is transported through the same integral and the same connected-component decomposition as the background. There is no division by a forced activity, which may vanish, and the distinguished activity address remains fixed.

8.33.1The fine inserted density

Write the fine density as

\(\displaystyle \rho (U) = e^{E (U)} \sum_{A \text{admissible}} \prod_{i \in A} K_i (U) .\)

Choose a bounded real measurable gauge-invariant function \(h\), with \(|h| \leqslant w\), supported on the eligibility condition of one fixed activity address. Its dependence on the configuration is confined to that address's full inspected support. There are two possibilities. For a regular address \(b\), its background regular activity is identically zero and \(h\) vanishes outside the healthy set for its support. For a forced address \(b\), \(h\) vanishes whenever that forced address fails its original eligibility test. Define

\(\displaystyle f_h (U) = e^{E (U)} \sum_{A \text{admissible}} B_h (A, U), \qquad B_h (A, U) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} h (U) \prod_{i \in A} K_i (U), & b \text{ regular},\\ \boldsymbol{1}_{\{b \in A\}} h (U) \prod_{i \in A \setminus \{b\}} K_i (U), & b \text{ forced} . \end{array} \right.\) (8.243)

The admissibility tests, including exact flag coverage, are those of the background. In the forced case the address is replaced even when its original value \(K_b (U)\) is zero.

Here and below use the same funded sector data and all-species integrated product bounds as in Subsection 8.29. On the fixed finite lattice, choose summable uniform bounds \(p_i \geqslant |K_i (U) |\). In the regular case the absolute family sum in (8.243) is bounded by \(w \prod_i (1 + p_i)\). In the forced case replace \(p_b\) by \(w\) and use the same product bound. Thus the series converges uniformly and absolutely. The fine exponent has a bounded measurable envelope, so \(f_h\) is bounded and measurable. Gauge invariance follows termwise from the invariance of \(h\), the activities and the flag tests. In particular the fine Haar integral and every fixed transition slice integral exist. This argument does not require a sign for \(h\).

8.33.2One marked factor in the Gaussian expansion

Denote the elementary alphabet of the unmarked expansion by \(\mathcal{I}\) and the letter assigned to \(b\) by \(\star\). Its support is the same enlarged inspected support as the original address. For one sector, one tag assignment and one old Haar configuration, let \(v_i (z)\) be the elementary factors of the Gaussian expansion. The marked factor is

\(\displaystyle H (z) = h (U (z)) \hspace{0.17em} \zeta_{\star} (z), \qquad 0 \leqslant \zeta_{\star} \leqslant 1,\)

where \(\zeta_{\star}\) is the larger local fluctuation cutoff on the marked support. For a finite set of letters \(P\) put

\(\displaystyle \Psi_h (P, z) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{\star \in P\}} H (z) \prod_{i \in P \setminus \{\star\}} v_i (z) .\) (8.244)

The cutoff makes the marked factor part of the same local integral construction. On the nonzero support of the genuine common chart cutoff it equals one: the stronger common small-fluctuation bound places each marked coordinate in its plateau. If the common cutoff is zero, both sides of the chart identity are zero. Hence inserting \(\zeta_{\star}\) changes none of the represented fine integral.

The regular and forced addresses give two slightly different expansion identities. At a blank regular address the old exponential-minus-one factor \(v_{\star}\) is zero. Every old term containing that letter therefore vanishes. Insert \(\star\) into each remaining finite letter set and replace its zero factor by \(H\). Erasing \(\star\) is the inverse map, so the resulting sum is \(H\) times the original sum. In the forced case, a family not containing the address contributes zero. For a family containing it, erase just that forced factor and put \(H\) in its place. The optional logarithmic and warning expansions are unchanged. These two identities give precisely the two cases of (8.243); there is no extra multiplicity.

To justify integration of these expansions, let \(r_i \geqslant 0\) be the summable elementary integrated bounds already established for the unmarked product construction. Its bound applies to every finite set of letters, in particular to \(P \setminus \{\star\}\). Since \(|H| \leqslant w\), it gives

\(\displaystyle \int | \Psi_h (P, z) | \hspace{0.17em} d \nu (z) \leqslant \boldsymbol{1}_{\{\star \in P\}} w \prod_{i \in P \setminus \{\star\}} r_i,\) (8.245)

where \(\nu\) includes the old probability Haar variables and the Gaussian variables. The right side is summable over \(P\): erasure maps the sets containing \(\star\) injectively to all finite sets, whose product-bound sum is at most \(\exp (\sum_i r_i)\). The separate uniform envelope bounds give measurability and an integrable envelope exactly as in the unmarked construction. Thus Fubini and absolute summation justify every exchange here.

For clarity, the chart and Gaussian normalization in this argument are unchanged. First split the free Haar coordinates into the active chart coordinates and their complement. On a nonzero sector the chart-support lemma puts every active bond in the original exponential chart. The product change of variables contributes \(c_H^{N_{\text{active}}} \prod J (u_i)\); the affine rescaling contributes \(g^{qN_{\text{active}}}\); the unused Gaussian coordinates integrate to one with their normalized density. The quadratic completion and logarithmic cancellation from Subsection 8.29 then apply with the extra factor \(h (U)\) untouched. Its local cutoff has just been restored to one on this support. Summing the sector partition and averaging the original tags consequently recovers \(\mathcal{T}f_h\), with the same scalar overhead \(\mathscr{O}\) as for \(\mathcal{T} \rho\).

8.33.3The distinguished connected component

Split a finite elementary letter set into its connected components, using their full assigned supports. If it contains \(\star\), exactly one component \(C\) contains that letter. Formula (8.244) factors into the marked product on \(C\) and the ordinary products on the other components. The Gaussian cross covariances between their read sets vanish; the Haar and tag read sets are disjoint. Thus the product integral factors. This is the same finite-range independence argument used in the unmarked case, now with one bounded local factor substituted.

Let \(a_C^F (V)\) be an ordinary integrated component value and \(b_C^F (V)\) the marked value, set to zero unless \(\star \in C\). The component and flattening maps are inverse on the permitted families. The sector and eligibility masks are identical on both sides: if a component fails its local test, its corresponding ordinary or marked value is zero; on a nonzero family the union of its inventories is exactly the set of flags. Absolute convergence from (8.245) therefore proves

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T}f_h (V) = \mathscr{O} \sum_{\mathcal{A} \text{admissible}} \sum_{C \in \mathcal{A}} b_C^F (V) \prod_{D \in \mathcal{A} \setminus \{C\}} a_D^F (V) .\) (8.246)

The marked value has the same locality in the forest as the ordinary one. Indeed \(h\) reads only its inspected support, and its added cutoff reads only the corresponding buffered coordinates. Forests agreeing there give equal values after pulling their free Haar variables back to the common full Haar space. Their principal Gaussian covariances agree, so integration gives equality too. We may therefore average independent forest choices at coarse sites as in Subsection 8.30. For a compatible family the marked component and all the ordinary components use disjoint sets of site choices, so the average of their product is the product of their averages. Set

\(\displaystyle a_C =\mathbb{E}_F a_C^F, \qquad b_C =\mathbb{E}_F b_C^F .\)

Equation (8.246) holds with these averaged values. The same absolute majorants apply to this finite average.

Two distinct compatible components cannot both have nonzero marked value, since both would contain \(\star\). Hence, for every real \(\theta\) and every compatible family,

\(\displaystyle \prod_{C \in \mathcal{A}} (a_C + \theta b_C) = \prod_{C \in \mathcal{A}} a_C + \theta \sum_{C \in \mathcal{A}} b_C \prod_{D \ne C} a_D .\)

The terms of degree at least two vanish individually. Together with the unmarked identity this proves the exact integral formula

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T} \rho + \theta \mathcal{T}f_h = \mathscr{O} \sum_{\mathcal{A} \text{admissible}} \prod_{C \in \mathcal{A}} (a_C + \theta b_C) .\) (8.247)

The right side is affine despite its product notation. The masks, support assignments, inventories and distinguished fine address have never changed.

8.33.4Passing the mark through the two regroupings

For \(\theta\) in the interval allowed by the purchased marked bounds, apply the regular cluster expansion and forced exclusion regrouping to the values \(a_C + \theta b_C\). Denote their regular exponential by \(a (\theta)\) and their forced gas by \(G_{\theta}\). The exact identities of Sections 8.30 and 8.31 give

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T} \rho + \theta \mathcal{T}f_h = \mathscr{O} a (\theta) G_{\theta} .\)

The absolute product and cluster majorants apply uniformly on this interval. Indeed if \(|a_C | \leqslant A_C\), \(|b_C | \leqslant DB_C\) and \(| \theta | D \leqslant 1\), the affine factor has the joint value/derivative bound \(A_C + 2 B_C\) of Subsection 9.1. Products, cluster coefficients and exponential multiplicities retain that joint bound by the product rule proved there. This uses the marked-bound purchases; the unmarked bounds alone do not supply the derivative bound.

Finally let \(R_0, S_0\) be the two fixed local logarithmic corrections absorbed into the forced gas in Subsection 8.32. That regrouping uses the same fixed background and gives \(b (\theta) = G_{\theta} e^{R_0 + S_0}\).

We identify the coarse exponent on the same configuration, background, path and clipping threshold as the integral above. The regular families have the summability, symmetry and local reset hypotheses of Subsection 12.10, including its side-size and clipping conditions. Let \(F\) be the exponent of the intermediate representation with regular terms frozen, and let \(P\) be the path contribution subtracted with that quadratic coefficient included in it. We shall prove

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} E_{\text{coarse}} + S_0 & = F - P, \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.248)} \\ e^{F + R_0 - P} & = \mathscr{O} a (0) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(8.249)} \end{array}\)

Here are the two calculations. All sums below are over the same finite coarse lattice and, for the local entries, over absolutely summable families. Write the frozen exponent as \(F = \tau N - K_{\beta} - A\), where \(N\) is the number of coarse sites, \(K_{\beta}\) is the retained path exponent before the coupling reset, and \(A\) is the frozen regular sum. In the second regrouping let \(v\) be the vacuum subtraction, \(d\) the sum of the discarded regular terms, \(a\) the sum of the untagged terms, \(H\) the quadratic proxy sum, and \(L_0\) the sum of the path loans. Let \(\Delta\) be the extracted quadratic coefficient. The splitting of the regular entries and the path ledger take the form

\(\displaystyle A = v + \Delta H - d + a, \qquad K_{\beta + \gamma + \Delta} - K_{\beta} + J = P + \Delta H + L_0,\)

where \(J\) is the sum of the lower and upper path corrections added to the regular terms, and \(P\) is \(\gamma\) times the masked path receipt. These are precisely (12.68) and (12.71): in that section's notation the present \((v, H, d, a, J, L_0, \gamma)\) are \((c_0, h, D, R_0, F, J, \alpha)\). The kinetic difference is \((\gamma + \Delta) \mathcal{K}\), since only its coupling changes; the loop term is unchanged. Thus these identities hold on every field, including unhealthy ones. The reset uses vacuum \(\tau - v / N\), regular sum \(a + J\) and logarithmic sum \(S_0 = d + L_0\). Collecting the terms gives

\(\displaystyle E_{\text{coarse}} + S_0 = \tau N - K_{\beta} - (v + a + \Delta H - d) - P = F - P.\)

The final regrouping preserves this exponent because it preserves the sum of the regular entries, leaving the vacuum and path terms unchanged.

For completeness, the first transfer can be checked directly on its entries. Let \(A_{\ast}\) be the original regular sum and \(A_{\text{rem}}\) the sum after removing its selected finite set \(I_0\) of entries. Distribute each selected masked entry \(f_i\) over sites with its allocation weights \(\lambda_{oi}\), for which \(\sum_o \lambda_{oi} = 1\), and put \(e_o = \sum_{i \in I_0} \lambda_{oi} f_i\). Hence \(A_{\ast} = A_{\text{rem}} + \sum_o e_o\). Let \(c_{\ast}\) be the common flat value of the allocated local reference (independent of \(o\) by translation), let \(g_o\) be its local replacement, and write \(\chi_o\) for that replacement's healthy mask. The discrepancy used by the transfer is

\(\displaystyle t_o = e_o - c_{\ast} - \chi_o g_o .\)

Thus \(A_{\ast} = A_{\text{rem}} + Nc_{\ast} + \sum_o \chi_o g_o + \sum_o t_o\). This uses the actual masked entries and is valid on unhealthy fields too. The local replacement makes \(t_o\) vanish on its larger healthy support, as required for absorbing \(- t_o\) into the gas.

Write \(\langle \cdot \rangle\) for the uniform average over signed translations of the coarse lattice. The original and remaining regular sums are invariant under this average: their label permutations preserve values and masks, and their series converge absolutely. The new regular sum, vacuum and logarithmic correction are

\(\displaystyle A = A_{\text{rem}} + \left\langle \sum_o \chi_o g_o \right\rangle, \qquad \tau = \tau_{\ast} - c_{\ast}, \qquad R_0 = - \left\langle \sum_o t_o \right\rangle .\)

The averaging factor is the reciprocal of the number of signed translations, so the constant \(Nc_{\ast}\) is unchanged. Substitution gives \(F + R_0 = N \tau_{\ast} - K_{\ast} - A_{\ast}\). Enumerating the two disjoint label families changes no sum, so this also proves the identity for the actual enumerated arrays.

We next identify \(A_{\ast}\). Write \(c_{\Gamma} (U)\) for the absolutely summable flag-free cluster logarithms of Subsection 8.30. Their sum is \(\log a (0)\) and \(a (0) = \exp (\sum_{\Gamma} c_{\Gamma}) > 0\). The first part of the regular alphabet has values \(- c_{\Gamma}\), transported to fixed addresses with the same full supports. Its assigned healthy mask changes no value, because \(c_{\Gamma}\) already vanishes off that healthy set. The transport is a bijection, so this part sums to \(- \log a (0)\) without a multiplicity.

The other part consists of the low-path receipts with coefficient \(- \gamma\), the cubic and quartic atoms with coefficient \(- \beta\), and the loop atoms with coefficient \(- 1\). Each retains its original mask and signed-channel weight. The low channels sum to \(- P\) and the other three families sum to \(- B_{\ast}\), where

\(\displaystyle B_{\ast} = \beta (\mathcal{B}_3 +\mathcal{B}_4) +\mathcal{B}_{\text{loop}}, \qquad K_{\ast} = K_{\text{base}} + B_{\ast} .\)

Each \(\mathcal{B}\) is the signed average of its translated masked path atoms. Partitioning the path alphabet proves these equalities; the path bounds give absolute summability. Translation and signed averaging therefore commute with the sums. The cluster sum is invariant under the same symmetries by their bijections of cluster addresses. The further uniform signed-translation average of the whole regular alphabet leaves its sum unchanged. This proves \(A_{\ast} = - \log a (0) - P - B_{\ast}\).

Finally let \(T_{\ast} > 0\) be the field-independent factor in (8.216), evaluated at the identity with the same forest and incoming vacuum. The extracted field-dependent part of the inherited exponent is \(\exp (- K_{\text{base}})\), whence \(\mathscr{O} = T_{\ast} \exp (- K_{\text{base}})\). The vacuum is defined by \(\tau_{\ast} = N^{- 1} \log T_{\ast}\); \(N > 0\) and \(T_{\ast} > 0\) justify this definition. Writing \(v_{\ast} = N \tau_{\ast}\) now gives

\(\displaystyle F + R_0 - P = v_{\ast} - K_{\text{base}} - B_{\ast} - A_{\ast} - P = v_{\ast} - K_{\text{base}} + \log a (0) .\)

Exponentiating proves (8.249). Every reindexing above preserves the original threshold and path; in particular the receipt \(P\) used in the reset is the one just summed in the regular alphabet.

Indeed, adding \(R_0\) to (8.248) and exponentiating, then using (8.249), gives

\(\displaystyle e^{E_{\text{coarse}} + R_0 + S_0} = \mathscr{O} a (0) .\) (8.250)

With this normalization, substitution proves

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T} \rho + \theta \mathcal{T}f_h = e^{E_{\text{coarse}}} \frac{a (\theta)}{a (0)} b (\theta),\)

which is (9.6) for the actual insertion (8.243). Differentiation is justified by the joint summable bounds, not by a formal manipulation of the series. This establishes the exact marked integral underlying that argument. The remaining quantitative task is to purchase its marked local derivative bounds and principal-term estimate on the selected history; the density identity alone does not provide those estimates.

8.34Bounds and local derivatives of the marked activities

The marked integral permits a bounded measurable insertion. Its coarse local extension nevertheless has the derivatives needed for the projection argument. The reason is the compensated transport: it holds the fine field read by the insertion fixed. Only its smooth cutoff and the surrounding integral are differentiated.

8.34.1Paying for the distinguished letter

Let \(X\) be the fine insertion support, \(c = C_{B_f} (X)\) its fine cover number, \(s\) the fine support exponent, and \(w\) its uniform bound. Its fine mass is \(m = e^{sc} w\). Fix the support reserve \(\varepsilon > 0\), with \(s > 4 \varepsilon\), and let \(g > 0\) be the normalization gain. Introduce

\(\displaystyle D = \frac{me^{- \varepsilon c}}{g}, \qquad J = ge^{- (s - \varepsilon) c}, \qquad DJ = w.\) (8.251)

The last equality follows by cancellation of the two exponentials. It also covers \(w = 0\). We give the marked letter bound \(J\) in the mixed component estimate and keep the factor \(D\) outside it.

Let \(X'\) be its full enlarged coarse support. The footprint estimate of Subsection 8.24 has the form

\(\displaystyle C_B (X') \leqslant C_0 + \frac{10 C_0}{L} c,\)

where \(C_0\) depends only on the fixed enlargement depth. Choose the block ratio so that \(10 uC_0 / L \leqslant \varepsilon\), where \(u\) is the mixed estimate's support exponent. The marked contribution to the elementary bound, including the two length reserves, then obeys

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} Je^{2 \varepsilon c} e^{uC_B (X')} & \leqslant ge^{uC_0} e^{- (s - 4 \varepsilon) c}\\ & \leqslant ge^{uC_0} . \end{array}\)

At any root there is at most this one new letter. Thus replacing the old bound at that address by \(J\) increases both the optional root bound and the total root bound by at most \(ge^{uC_0}\). All other bounds and all supports remain unchanged. Applying the mixed connected-component estimate of Subsection 8.2 to this enlarged bound family bounds the marked components, including any excess-cardinality, support-size and forced-count restrictions required there.

This enlargement is purchased at the same scale as the background. If its original root bound and \(g\) have negative upper exponent \(q < 0\), their sum has that exponent too. The geometric degree factor has upper exponent zero. It is therefore eventually at most \(\beta_f^{q / 2}\); this quantity tends to zero and may be used as the new tree ratio. This follows directly from the strict exponent comparison in Subsection 9.1. It makes no new choice of a history or a fine-to-coarse transition.

8.34.2Holding a merely measurable insertion fixed

Fix a component \(P\) containing the marked letter, a healthy coarse configuration on its full support, and a smooth coarse probe based there. Use the compensated transport from Subsection 8.20. For fixed old Haar variables and Gaussian point \(z\), let \(U_0 (z)\) be the physical fine field at the base point. On the nonzero support of the transported marked cutoff, the transport identity gives agreement with \(U_0 (z)\) on the inspected support of \(h\). Since \(h\) is local, the marked factor becomes

\(\displaystyle h (U_0 (z)) \hspace{0.17em} \zeta_{\star} (y, z) .\) (8.252)

If the cutoff is zero, both expressions are zero. The identity holds as a germ in the coarse probe parameter \(y\). The truncation in the compensated transport preserves this germ, because its threshold is larger than the cutoff support. This is exactly the cutoff comparison used for the unmarked transported factors.

The function \(h (U_0 (z))\) is constant in \(y\) and has absolute value at most \(w\). The cutoff derivative estimate therefore gives amplitude \(w\) and derivative scale at most \(J_0 \sigma_{\star}\), where \(\sigma_{\star}\) is the marked letter's support scale and \(J_0\) has upper exponent zero. No derivative of \(h\) has been taken. This observation also gives the locally uniform integrable derivative envelopes: multiply the existing smooth cutoff envelopes by the bounded fixed function \(h (U_0 (z))\).

Write \(a_i\) for the unmarked elementary jet amplitudes and \(r\) for their common scale factor. Set \(\sigma_i = 1 + c_B (S_i) + d_i\), where \(d_i\) is its length cost, and \(W_P = \sum_{i \in P} (c_B (S_i) + d_i)\). Since \(c_B (S_i) \geqslant 1\) for a nonzero factor, \(\sum_{i \in P} \sigma_i \leqslant 2 W_P\), as in (8.210) and the product argument preceding (8.212). Leibniz's rule applied to (8.252) and the unmarked factors gives amplitude

\(\displaystyle A_P = w \prod_{i \in P \setminus \{\star\}} a_i\)

and derivative scale at most \(2 (r + J_0) W_P\). The same joint warning event can be retained. Outside that event, one warning factor has the zero germ supplied by the strict small-fluctuation plateau. The marked letter is a regular or forced insertion letter, never a warning letter; erasing it leaves the warning set unchanged. Thus the zero germ still occurs among the remaining factors.

Now include the Jacobian and projected Gaussian density of (8.205). Their joint derivative scale is bounded by \(D_0 \mathcal{F}_I (z)^k\), with amplitude one. Here \(D_0\) is the upper-exponent-zero density scale in that estimate, and \(\mathcal{F}_I \geqslant 1\) is the Gaussian coordinate polynomial on the actual local read set \(I\). For derivatives through order \(j\), the full integrand has the bound

\(\displaystyle \boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{W}_P} (z) A_P [1 + D_0 + 2 (r + J_0)]^j (1 + W_P)^j \mathcal{F}_I (z)^{kj} .\) (8.253)

To verify the scale conversion, use

\(\displaystyle 1 + D_0 \mathcal{F}_I^k + 2 (r + J_0) W_P \leqslant [1 + D_0 + 2 (r + J_0)] (1 + W_P) \mathcal{F}_I^k\)

and raise both nonnegative sides to the integer power \(j\). The estimates concern the unmasked extension on a neighborhood of the healthy base point; they do not differentiate the flag mask.

8.34.3Integrating the derivative bound

The Gaussian moment and warning estimate of Subsection 8.27 gives, for some fixed integer \(K\) and scalar \(F\) of upper exponent zero,

\(\displaystyle \int \boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{W}_P} \mathcal{F}_I^{kj} \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma \leqslant (1 + |F|) (1 + W_P)^K \rho^{N_P} .\)

Here \(N_P\) is the number of warning letters and \(\rho\) is the half-warning factor of that estimate. Explicitly, Cauchy–Schwarz uses the moment of degree \(2 kj\) and the square root of the joint warning probability. This explains both the doubled moment degree and the preserved half-warning gain. The old Haar measure has total mass one, so introduces no further volume factor.

Integrating (8.253) therefore bounds the local derivative amplitude by

\(\displaystyle B_{\ast} A_P \rho^{N_P} (1 + W_P)^{K + j}, \qquad B_{\ast} = (1 + |F|) [1 + D_0 + 2 (r + J_0)]^j .\) (8.254)

The finite-order differentiation-under-the-integral argument of Subsection 8.26 applies: the relative Gaussian density supplies an integrable local envelope, the transported insertion is bounded and constant in the probe, and all remaining factors have the already proved smooth envelopes. Thus this is a bound for derivatives of the actual local integral. The constants \(j, k, K\) are fixed before the coupling limit. Products, sums and fixed powers show that \(B_{\ast}\) has upper exponent zero.

Let \(v_P\) be the mixed component bound with the marked elementary bound replaced by \(J\), with the warning gain and the size weight \((1 + W_P)^{K + j}\) included. The exact identity \(DJ = w\) from (8.251) rewrites (8.254) as \(DB_{\ast} v_P\). This proves the marked analogue of the unmarked local jet estimate from the same sector data, rather than postulating a new regularity property of \(h\).

To make a globally measurable gauge-invariant extension, first average the local projected integral over the original finite tag choices, then average its gauge translates against product probability Haar measure. Locality is preserved because only the gauge coordinates incident to its read support matter. Haar invariance makes the result gauge invariant. At a healthy base point, the local covariance and chart bounds persist on a probe neighborhood. Change of variables in the local integral shows that it is already gauge invariant there. Hence the gauge average agrees with that local integral as a germ, and the derivative bound is unchanged. Independent forest averaging also preserves the bound. We have constructed a measurable local extension \(g_P\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle b_P (V) =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{X_P \cap \text{Flags} (V) = \varnothing\}} g_P (V)\)

for regular components, with derivatives through the purchased order bounded by \(DB_{\ast} v_P\) at healthy configurations. This is finite-order smoothness on the stated domain.

8.34.4The logarithmic derivative and its cluster bounds

Let \(f_P\) be the unmarked local extension. For a regular cluster tuple \(\Gamma = (P_1, \ldots, P_n)\) its unmasked marked logarithmic term is

\(\displaystyle h_{\Gamma} = \frac{u (P_1, \ldots, P_n)}{n!} \sum_{r = 1}^n g_{P_r} \prod_{s \ne r} f_{P_s} .\) (8.255)

This is the derivative at zero of the corresponding product of affine tilted factors. All terms have the mask of the union support. Indeed on a healthy union every constituent mask is one; if the union contains a flag, each summand has a zero factor, either its marked factor or one of its ordinary factors.

Suppose \(f_P\) has local amplitude \(A_P^0\) and \(g_P\) has amplitude \(DA_P^1\), both at derivative scale one. Put \(A_P = A_P^0 + 2 A_P^1\) and inflate this bound to \(\hat{A}_P = 2^{j + 2} A_P\). On a healthy union each summand in (8.255) has amplitude at most \(D \prod_r A_{P_r}\) and derivative scale \(n\). Its derivatives through order \(j\) are consequently bounded by

\(\displaystyle D \frac{|u (P_1, \ldots, P_n) |}{n!} n (1 + n)^j \prod_r A_{P_r} \leqslant D \frac{|u (P_1, \ldots, P_n) |}{n!} \prod_r \hat{A}_{P_r} .\)

For the last inequality use \(1 + n \leqslant 2^n\) and \(n \leqslant 2^{2 n}\). Thus the canonical cluster majorant at the inflated bounds pays all these derivatives as well as the scalar tilt derivative. The rooted cluster estimate and its tail then give (9.8). The marked mixed-component estimate proved above supplies its order-zero counterpart, with the excess-cardinality restriction for nonprincipal terms. The polynomial support-weight conversion and strict exponent reserve in Subsection 9.1 therefore apply to these actual local terms.

For forced components use their absolute marked integral bound from (8.245) and the same mixed estimate. The affine joint bounds propagate through the exclusion regrouping by the product rule, keeping the exponential multiplicity factorials. Subsection 8.31's rooted argument applies to these inflated bounds and gives the summable joint forced-bound family required in (9.3). Every nonzero marked component contains the projected insertion anchor, so the derivative vanishes at all other supports. This proves the structural and local analytic inputs to the marked differentiation. Their simultaneous scale purchases are made as follows.

8.34.5One coupling threshold for the marked estimates

Fix the derivative and spatial orders, the target support exponent \(a \geqslant 0\), and a reserve \(e > 0\) with \(a + 2 e\) no larger than the available mixed support exponent. Let \(\mathcal{O}_n\) be the unmarked rooted component bound with size weight \(n\), and \(C_n\) the corresponding marked mixed bound after adjoining \(J\). Both have upper exponent \(q < 0\). Write \(C_n^{(1)}\) for the marked bound with excess cardinality at least one; its extra tree factor gives upper exponent \(3 q / 2\). Put

\(\displaystyle t = (1 + 2^{j + 2} B_{\ast}) (\mathcal{O}_n + 2 C_n), \qquad v =\mathcal{O}_n + 2 C_0, \qquad u = 10 (t + v), \qquad \gamma = \beta_f^{q / 2} .\)

These quantities are positive in the selected construction. The inflated regular bounds above have rooted allowance at most \(t\). The forced bounds have allowance at most \(ve^{- M}\), where \(M\) is the compulsory forced mark. These assertions follow by adding the unmarked and marked mixed bounds, then multiplying the regular bound by \(2^{j + 2} B_{\ast} \leqslant 1 + 2^{j + 2} B_{\ast}\).

The functions \(t, v, u\) have upper exponent \(q\). Since the geometric degree factor \(4 (2 B + 1)^4 / e\) has upper exponent zero, eventually

\(\displaystyle \frac{4 (2 B + 1)^4 t}{e} \leqslant \gamma, \qquad \frac{4 (2 B + 1)^4 u}{e} \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \qquad \gamma \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \qquad u \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} .\)

At the same scale the shifted fine radius is at most twice the coarse radius, by the radius estimate of Subsection 3.15. There are finitely many eventual inequalities at fixed orders; choose their common threshold before choosing the finite torus or insertion. The rooted bounds are uniform in both, so this threshold works for every permitted insertion on every such torus.

For the second expansion the two rooted contributions, after inflating copies and extracting the compulsory mark, are bounded by

\(\displaystyle 4 e^M (ve^{- M}) + 4 t (1 - \gamma)^{- 1} \leqslant 4 v + 8 t \leqslant 10 (t + v) = u.\)

The connected multiplicity argument in Subsection 8.31 thus gives forced joint bound at most \(2 ue^{- M}\). Its differentiated bound is \(D\) times this quantity, as required in Subsection 9.1.

Finally, let \(A_{e, r}\) be the polynomial support-weight bound in that section. It has upper exponent zero at fixed orders. The nonprincipal regular tail divided by the fine mass reserve is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \frac{A_{e, r}}{g} \left( B_{\ast} C_n^{(1)} + t \frac{\gamma}{1 - \gamma} \right) .\)

Here \(g = \beta_f^q\), so \(g^{- 1}\) has upper exponent \(- q\). Both numerator terms have upper exponent \(3 q / 2\), since \((1 - \gamma)^{- 1} \leqslant 2\). The quotient therefore has upper exponent \(q / 2\) and is eventually at most \(\beta_f^{q / 4}\). This purchases the tail and joint forced bounds used by the actual marked expansion. The final principal-term and projection allowances must still be compared with the selected outgoing budget, using the same already chosen scale.
